PREFACE 

While serving as an instructor in real property subjects at Suffolk Law School, the writer collected for classroom use a brief set of notes covering the subjects of Deeds, Mortgages and Easements. In 1922 those notes were printed. No attempt was made to include the fundamentals of the law of Real Property, as such matters were covered by an independent earlier course, and a school textbook therefor was in preparation. Accordingly the material was confined to matters connected with practical conveyancing. Following the writer's resignation from the faculty, the School has continued the use of the notes as a textbook. The gentlemen who have since carried on the course have felt that better results could be achieved if the material could be amplified by the addition of further illustrations and discussion of cases, by adding a few new topics, and by including some of the forms most commonly used in conveyancing. As a result of those suggestions this book has been prepared. The material now included, as in the former edition, deals exclusively with Massachusetts law. Much of this material has been selected from Crocker's Notes on Common Forms. If due credit has not always been given, the omission was unintentional.

The notes on Tenancies by the Entirety appearing in section 26 are included by the permission of Arthur V. Getchell, Esq., who collected and published this material. 

Decisions of the Supreme Judicial Court down to September 1, 1930, have been considered, and also new legislation to and including that of the year 1931. 

WATERTOWN, July, 1931. 

A. L. P.

1. THE FEUDAL SYSTEM. 

William the Conqueror, the first of the Norman line, effected the conquest of England in 1066. It is probably incorrect to say that the Normans created the feudal system in England. It is a fact, however, that they nourished the system and brought it to a point where it had a direct and far-reaching influence upon land titles. With the advent of the first Norman king, the land to which he became entitled as the successor of the Anglo-Saxon kings and the great amount of land held by those who had resisted him, which by the customary law of both Normandy and England was forfeited to the king, became alike terra regis. This land the king was free to grant and did grant to his followers in return for the tenant doing homage and binding himself to perform and furnish military service. Thus was laid the foundation of the feudal system with its multiplicity of tenures.

Concerning the feudal system, Professor Reeves says in

his treatise on the Law of Real Property, Vol. 1, page 341: 

"The primary object of the feudal system, as it was elaborated in England, was to have all the king's subjects who could carry arms bound by ties of the strongest self-interest to be ready, at a moment's notice, to form or provide an army for any and all sorts of military service. It did this by making the landowner's holding of his property dependent upon his obligation and readiness to render services to a superior lord. Its fundamental principle was that the king was the owner of all the lands within his realm. He parcelled out large tracts of this property to individuals, or religious bodies, to hold as the vassals or tenants of the crown. These holders in their turn subparcelled, or subinfeudated, their respective portions to others below themselves, to hold as their vassals or tenants; and the latter, again, often brought in others as holders under themselves. . . . At the top of this series is the king, who is designated the lord paramount. Those who hold immediately of him, as his tenants, or vassals, are called tenants in capite, or in chief. 

Those at the bottom of the scale, who cultivate or otherwise make actual use of the land, hold in demesne as the tenants paravail, the tenants who make the avail or profits out of the land itself. And those standing between these last and the king, or lord paramount, are the vassals of those above and the lords of those below themselves. . . . To take an actual case, during the reign of Edward I, Roger of St. German made the proceeds of land at Paxton in Huntingtonshire which he held of Robert of Bedford; the latter held it of Richard of Ilchester, who held of Alan of Chartres, who held of William Le Boteler, who held of Gilbert Neville, who held of Devorguil Balliol, who held of the king of Scotland, who held of the king of England. Roger of St. German, who held the land in demesne as tenant paravail, looked up to Robert of Bedford as the lord to whom he was immediately responsible, and through him and the other mesne lords to the king of England as lord paramount; while the king of 

Scotland, as tenant in capite, looked upward to the king of England as his only lord, and downward to Devorguil Balliol as his tenant or vassal. Every such ladder of ownerships - 

---

DEEDS. 

3 

and there was not an acre of land in the kingdom that did not have one of them, with at least two rungs, and often, as in the above illustration, with many more had connected with it bonds of honor, self-interest, and even self-preservation, which bound the different parts almost indissolubly together." 

2. FEOFFMENT AND LIVERY OF SEISIN. 

The manner of conveying real property, to be thus held of a superior lord, was by words of pure donation, "do et concedo" (I give and grant); and these are still retained as operative words of conveyance in many forms of modern deeds. (The granting words employed in the original Massachusetts warranty deed were "give, grant, bargain, sell and convey.")

Originally, before feudalism had developed, land so given was to be held at the will of the donor. Later the grant came to be made for a certain and determined period, as for one or more years, and later still for the life of the grantor or grantee. 

But after passing through these stages, and still another period in which it was the well-recognized custom for the land to be granted after the death of the vassal to his son or sons, these interests became inheritable, and were so created and transferred that, when the first taker died, his heir should have the property in his stead, and upon the death of such heir it should pass to his heirs, and so on ad infinitum. It then, with this descendible characteristic, came to be denominated a feud, feod, fief or fee, and generally, in this day, to own land "in fee" is to have it in such manner that the law will cast the title upon the heir of the owner who dies intestate. 

In Richardson v. Lane, 226 Mass. 224, the Court said: "The words 'fee simple' relate to the quantity or extent of the interest in land; it is the largest estate and greatest interest that can be enjoyed in land. A fee simple is an absolute estate of inheritance."

Concerning feoffment, Chancellor Kent says in his Commentaries, Vol. 4, page 480: "Feoffment was the mode of conveyance in the earliest periods of the common law. It signi-

fied, originally, the grant of a feud or fee; but it came, in time, to signify the grant of a free inheritance in fee, respect being had to the perpetuity of the estate granted, rather than to the feudal tenure. The feoffment was likewise accompanied with actual delivery of possession of the land, termed livery of seisin. The notoriety and solemnity of the livery were well adapted to the simplicity of unlettered ages, by making known the change of owners, and preventing all obscurity and dispute concerning the title. The actual livery was performed by entry of the feoffor upon the land, with the charter of feoffment, and delivering a clod, turf or twig, or the latch of the door, in the name of seisin of all the lands contained in the deed. The ceremony was performed in the presence of the peers, or freeholders of the neighborhood, who were the vassals of the feudal lord, and who might afterwards be called on to attest the certainty of the livery of seisin." 

In connection with the foregoing it should be said that while the written instrument, the charter of feoffment, so called, at an early date came to be executed and delivered as a part of the transfer, it was not the written instrument which effected the conveyance. It was the actual putting of the vassal into possession of the land, the making to him livery of seisin, which was the delivery of the possession, thereby doing as much as a man could do, although he could not physically deliver the property as he could a bale of goods. See Goodwin on Real Property, page 6. That the charter of feoffment of early days was commemorative only and not operative is shown by the fact that the granting words are in the past tense, namely, "I have given and granted," not "I give and grant. And it was not until the year 1677 that the passage of the Statute of Frauds required feoffments to be evidenced by a writing. 

3. GRANT.

Incorporeal hereditaments could not be transferred by feoffment; they supplied no symbol that could be handed over in the process of livery of seisin; and, therefore, the common law created a symbol, in the form of the deed of grant. Hence incorporeal hereditaments are said to “lie in grant," and not in

DEEDS. LO livery; and the deed of grant is the instrument by which they have always been conveyed. See Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 1384.

The deed of grant differed but little from a charter of feoffment except in its subject matter; for the operative words therein commonly used were "dedi et concessi" (have given and granted). 2 Blackstone's Commentaries, page 317. 

4. SEISIN. 

Having established that a conveyance of land at the early common law necessitated delivery of seisin, it behooves us to consider the feudal idea of seisin which, as Professor Reeves says, is so inwrought into the structure of the law of real property that it is very difficult to understand and apply the reasoning of the courts, either ancient or modern, upon the subject, without an understanding of the unfolding and nature of that idea. 

In Slater v. Rawson, 6 Met. 439, 444, the Court said: "According to the modern authorities, there seems to be no legal difference between the words seizin and possession." And again: "Lord Coke says seizin signifies, in the common law, possession." If we stop with these statements, we are in difficulty immediately because it is well understood that a tenant for years has not, and never had, seisin, although he commonly has exclusive possession. The difficulty with the above statements is that they omit the information that the idea of seisin was never involved in any estate less than a freehold. A freehold estate is an estate for life or greater. "It will conduce to clearness to explain here that the important word 'seisin' embraces not only the thought of possession, or right of possession, of real property, but also that of a claim of a freehold estate therein, an estate either for life or that may descend to the owner's heirs. Thus, seisin in fact is the actual possession of realty, coupled with a claim of a freehold estate therein; and seisin in law is the right to the possession of, and the ownership of, a freehold estate in real property which no one else is holding adversely. A life owner, or an owner in

.co 

fee, of land, who has possession, is seised in fact; an heir, who has inherited vacant land of which he has not yet taken possession, is seised in law; but a tenant for years, since his estate is less than one for life, has only possession and not seisin.”. 

Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 1, page 78.

In a statement much similar to the foregoing in Stearns on Real Actions, page 2, the author includes a devisee under a will who has not entered as one having a seisin in law as distinguished from seisin in fact.

Now common law pleading was a fearful and wonderful system, and the pleadings attendant upon real actions were the most technical and intricate of all. There was the writ of right and various writs of entry, and the form of action which would lie for the recovery of land depended, at the time when the common law rules of pleading were adhered to, upon the quality of the demandant's seisin. "That, at common law, a seisin in deed, in contradistinction to a seisin in law, was necessary to maintain a writ of right, is established by a long and unbroken chain of authorities." Ward v. Fuller, 15 Pick. 185, 191.

And while an heir might have prevailed in certain of these actions which required an actual seisin, because he was permitted to declare upon the seisin of his ancestor, a devisee was obliged first to enter before action. Brown v. Wood, 17 Mass. 

67; Green v. Chelsea, 24 Pick. 71. 

But the Revised Statutes, chap. 101, sec. 51, provided that all writs of right, and all writs of entry, except that which was allowed and provided for in that chapter, should be abolished from and after the thirty-first day of December, 1839. 

Jurisdiction of the writ of entry which was thus retained was transferred from the Superior Court to the Land Court in 1904. 

As to this action the present statute is as follows:

G. L. c. 237: SECTION 1. All estates of freehold in fee simple, fee tail or for life may be recovered by a writ of entry upon disseisin, unless a different action is provided. 

SECTION 4. The demandant need not prove an actual entry under his title, but proof that he is entitled to such an estate as he claims in the land and that he has a right of entry therein shall be sufficient to prove his seisin. No writ of entry shall be

DEEDS. 

maintained unless the demandant has at the time of commencing his action a right of entry into the land demanded. 

Thus was eliminated, and properly, so far as real actions are concerned, much of the technicalities of the doctrine of seisin. 

It should be noted that the text writers, and, indeed, the courts, often employ the expression "constructive seisin" in place of "seisin in law." For this there is no necessity, and the expression "constructive seisin" should have been retained as descriptive of a seisin constructively held, as where, after the death of the ancestor, a tenant remains in possession but acknowledges that he holds under the heir. In such a situation the heir has a constructive seisin in deed. "When the country was first settled, the ceremony of livery of seisin was in use. In the District of Maine, livery of seisin was given by the attorney of the Council of Plymouth, to Vines & Oldham, in the year 1629, of their patent on Saco River. 

From that time up to the year 1692, as appears by the records of deeds in the county of York, the ceremony of livery of seisin was attended to generally in that part of the country. But the law of 1642 in the Colony of Massachusetts, rendered it useless here.” History of Land Titles in Massachusetts by 

James Sullivan, published in 1801, page 88. [The date 1642 is error; 1652 is the correct date.] "As early as 1640 it was provided by statute in the Massachusetts colony that conveyances of real estate, where the grantor remained in possession, should, unless acknowledged and recorded, be valid only as against the grantor and his heirs. Soon afterwards, in 1652, with the view of preventing the then common evil of transfers of real estate without deed, it was provided that no conveyance should be valid, unless 'by deed in writing under hand and seal,' accompanied either by livery of seisin or by the acknowledgment and recording of the deed. In 1692 it was provided that all leases and other estates in land made and created by livery and seisin only, or by parole, and not put in writing and signed by the parties so making or creating the same, or their agents thereunto lawfully authorized by writing,' should have the force and effect of leases and estates at will only. And in 1697 what had been implied in the statute of 1652 was distinctly enacted, namely,

that a deed recorded at length should 'be valid to pass' real estate 'without any other act or ceremony in the law whatsoever,' and it was further provided that, unless acknowledged and recorded, a deed should not be good except as against the grantor and his heirs. This left the law substantially as we find it at present; the only material changes having been that the St. 1783, c. 37, § 4, extended the application of the law to leases for more than seven years, and that the Revised Statutes of 1836 included, in the same exception with the heirs, the devisees of the grantor, and also (adopting a judicial determination to that effect) persons having actual notice of the deed." -Crocker's Notes on Common Forms, page 3.

For a further statement of the history of the law relative to conveyances of real estate, see Pidge v. Tyler, 4 Mass. 541. 

In Ward v. Fuller, 15 Pick. 185, the Court said: "What is the operation and legal effect of a deed duly acknowledged and recorded? The statute of 1783, c. 37, § 4, provides that all deeds, etc., signed and sealed by the grantor, having good and lawful right and authority thereunto, and acknowledged by such grantor and recorded at length in the registry of deeds, shall be valid to pass the estate conveyed, without any other act or ceremony in the law whatsoever. This was intended to be a substitute for the old and less convenient mode of conveyance by feoffment and livery of sèisin. The registry gives more certain and extensive information of the alienation of estates than the notoriety derived from livery of seisin. It enables every person, by a proper use of known and convenient means, to ascertain the legal verity of all titles. Wells v. Prince, 4 Mass. 68; Pidge v. Tyler, 4 Mass. 541. Nothing more was to be done; no delivery of possession, nor 'any other act or ceremony' was required, to perfect the transfer of estates. 'A conveyance by deed, duly acknowledged and registered, is, by our statute of enrolments, equivalent to livery of seisin.' Higbee v. Rice, 5 Mass. 352. It gives to the grantee legal investiture of the land conveyed, and has the same effect as if the grantor entered upon the land and gave actual seisin by the formal delivery of turf and twig. Warren v. Childs, 11 Mass. 225; Goodwin v. Hubbard, 15 Mass. 214.”

It will be sufficient to conclude this topic by saying that

DEEDS. 

while the doctrine of seisin has not been eliminated from the law, many of the common law refinements of the doctrine have become obsolete, and by quoting from Professor Reeves upon the common law conception of an estate in fee simple. "Undoubtedly the jurists who made our common law of real property conceived of an estate in fee simple, as the philosophers taught them to think of time, as a straight line stretching away to infinity. Every minutest piece of land or tenement must be the starting point of such a line, which must always be continuous and unbroken. If the owner disposed of a part of it only, the law at once invested him, or if he had died, his heirs, with the residue, which was still infinite in extent. If he cut it up into numerous pieces, disposing of them consecutively to different persons, and ultimately transferred the fee, the endless piece, -the line in all its straightness and continuity was unaffected, but had simply passed over to a number of successive owners. If the expression be permissible with reference to a line, each part then rested for support upon that which immediately preceded it, the first or present one starting at the land, the next resting upon it, and so on to the last, which looked to all the others for support and was still infinite in extent. There could be no hiatus, no deviation. And though some of the successive owners might be yet unborn or unascertainable, yet the unbroken, continuous, straight line, with its parts thus upheld, must always remain and extend to infinity."- Reeves, page 1135. "The common law, unaffected by statute, will never permit the seisin of real property to be lost or in abeyance, for an instant. There must always be someone in being, in whom the seisin resides. This was because there must always be someone, who was a freeholder, to render the services due to the lord; and also because there must always be a freeholder to answer in any real action which might be brought for the recovery of the property." Reeves, page 381.

The foregoing has bearing upon numerous phases of the law of real property, and has been inserted for the purpose of directing attention to the fact that here lies the source of the inflexibility and harshness of the common law as to transfers of real property which was relieved in part by the advent of

uses in real property and the enactment of the Statute of Uses which is discussed briefly later on, and which has been further modified by certain modern statutes. 

5. DISSEISIN TITLE BY ADVERSE POSSESSION. 

"The act of ousting a person from land and depriving him of seisin is a disseisin. It involves not only dispossession, but also the claim (whether well founded or not) by the disseisor of a freehold estate in land. It is the act which lays the foundation for the acquisition of title by adverse possession under modern statutes." - Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 1, page 382.

A definition and theory differing slightly from the foregoing appears in Tiffany on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 1923: "Disseisin, as the expression was used in the old books, means a wrongful dispossession of one who has a freehold estate in the land. . . . Since then disseisin, generally speaking, meant the wrongful dispossession of one person by another, and the statute of limitations runs by reason of such wrongful dispossession, it would ordinarily be correct to say that the statute runs in favor of a disseisor as against a disseisee. But it also runs under circumstances which did not involve a disseisin at common law, as for instance, when a tenant per autre vie holds over after the death of cestui que vie, or a tenant for years disclaims the title of his landlord." 

Title by adverse possession may be made out, although the occupant does not know that he is in possession of land belonging to another. Van Allen v. Sweet, 239 Mass. 571.. "The common law, unaided by legislation, knows nothing of the loss of lands, corporeal hereditaments, by one man, and their passing to another, by any length of mere adverse holding or enjoyment. Acquisition of tangible realty by adverse possession must rest on statute.' ."-Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 1354.

It is important that a student should distinguish between the terms adverse possession and prescription, which often are used as though synonymous. Technically and properly, prescription, which will be referred to later in the notes on the law

DEEDS. 

11 of easements, is applicable only to incorporeal hereditaments, and does not extend to land or corporeal property. The common law fixed the length of enjoyment of an incorporeal hereditament, such as a way or a watercourse, which should be deemed sufficient evidence of an ownership of the right, and upon proof of use for the required period, declared that there was a presumption that a deed had been given and that it had been lost. In modern times the period so required is uniformly made to conform to the period fixed by the statute of limitations of real actions which in Massachusetts is twenty years.

On the other hand, title to land itself by adverse possession rests squarely on the statute of limitations without the necessity of introducing any fiction relative to a lost deed, although statements to the effect that such a presumption exists may be found in the cases, witness the case of White v. Loring, 24 

Pick. 319 at 322, where the Court said: "And notwithstanding the dictum in Holyoke v. Haskins, 5 Pick. 27, which when taken in connection with the context, means no more than that a grant of land will not be presumed from a mere possession twenty years, it has repeatedly and recently been solemnly decided by this Court, that the presumption extends as well to corporeal, as incorporeal hereditaments." See also Tinker v. Bessel, 213 Mass. 74.

The Massachusetts statute of limitations of real actions is 

G. L. c. 260, §§ 21 to 31. 

SECTION 21. An action for the recovery of land shall be com menced, or an entry made thereon, only within twenty years after the right of action or of entry first accrued, or within twenty years after the demandant or the person making the entry, or those under whom they claim, have been seized or possessed of the premises, except as hereinafter provided. 

A possessory title, while the statute is running, is valid against all the world except the true owner, and, after the statute has run, it cannot be disturbed by any one. 

While our statute does not expressly provide that the running thereof shall operate to transfer title to the person in possession, that result follows nevertheless. 

In Hewett v. Peterson, 253 Mass. 92, the Court said: "The

plaintiff's real trouble is that to acquire title to another's land by adverse possession, one must have held it full twenty years and not nineteen years and some months."

Let us now consider what manner of occupancy will give a disseisor the benefit of the statute and preclude the former owner from having an action for recovery. "It is incumbent on the person claiming such title to show an actual disseisin of the real owner, so as to turn his estate into a right of action; and it is not sufficient to show such acts as the owner might elect to consider as a disseisin for the sake of the remedy." McDonough v. Everett, 237 Mass. 378. 

In Cook v. Babcock, 11 Cush. 206, the Court said: "Where a party claims by a disseisin, ripened into a good title by lapse of time as against the legal owner, he must show an actual, open, exclusive and adverse possession of the land. All these elements are essential to be proved, and the failure to establish any one of them is fatal to the validity of the claim. In weighing and applying the evidence in support of such a title, the acts of the wrongdoer are to be construed strictly, and the true owner is not to be barred of his right except upon clear proof of an actual occupancy, clear, definite, positive and notorious."

It is submitted that the word "adverse" is out of place in a statement of the elements essential to making a title by proof of adverse possession. To say that one has title by adverse possession because he holds adversely is to argue in a circle. 

Hostile" or "under claim of right" would be understood more readily. See Ashley v. Ashley, 4 Gray, 197 at 200. 

In Barker v. Kennard, 226 Mass. 586, it was said: "The respondent was possessed of it [land] in fact, and his occupation was open, notorious, exclusive and continuous for a period of more than twenty years prior to the filing of the petition of the petitioner for registration. These acts, if found by the jury, clearly amount to a disseisin and adverse holding against the true owner.' 

In Curtis v. Goodwin, 232 Mass. 538 it was said: "It is elementary that a possession which will constitute a disseisin of the true owner must be not only open and notorious, but adverse, 'with an intention to appropriate and hold the same as

DEEDS. 

13 owner, and to the exclusion, rightfully or wrongfully, of every one else.' Bond v. O'Gara, 177 Mass. 139, 144; Duff v. Leary, 146 Mass. 533." "Confessedly the record title to the locus is in the petitioner. The respondent disputes the validity of that title by the contention that she has acquired a superior title by adverse possession. That is an affirmative proposition. The burden of proof rested upon her. Title of that nature can be sustained only by proof of open, adverse, exclusive, continuous and uninterrupted possession under claim of title for the requisite period of twenty years. Whether there is proof of these matters is a pure question of fact. Findings of fact by the trial judge (Land Court) upon unreported evidence and a view cannot be reversed, but must be accepted as final. The circumstance that the respondent entered upon the disputed parcel under color of title by virtue of the deed to her from Flagg does not affect in any degree the rule of law that title by adverse possession is wholly a question of fact. Entry made under color of title might be evidence or even proof as to the extent of possession if and when the requisite possession is shown." Nantucket v. Mitchell, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 733. 

A deed of wild land executed and acknowledged by a grantor who had no right to the land, and duly recorded in the registry of deeds, and a mere entry by the grantee without an open exclusive occupation manifested by fencing or otherwise, do not amount to a disseisin of the true owner. 

Bates v. 

Norcross, 14 Pick. 224. 

6. ELEMENTS OF ADVERSE POSSESSION. 

Now to consider the necessary elements stated in Cook v. Babcock. (a) "Actual." 

Of course continued residence on the land is generally accepted as constituting actual possession, and in this connection it should be noted that possession of leased premises by a tenant commonly inures to the benefit of the landlord. Mur-

phy v. Commonwealth, 187 Mass. 361; Attorney General v. 

Ellis, 198 Mass. 91; Melvin v. Proprietors, 5 Met. 15. But the use by a tenant of land not included in the lease will not inure to the landlord's benefit. This was brought out in 

Brooks v. Rosenbaum, 217 Mass. 172, which was a suit by a landowner against the owner of adjoining land and his lessee, to enjoin the defendants from committing continuing trespasses on the plaintiff's land by maintaining foundation stones projecting therein placed there by the defendant lessee in constructing a basement. It was held that the plaintiff was not entitled to an order against the defendant landowner directing him to execute an instrument releasing to the plaintiff any interest in the plaintiff's land and disclaiming any right to an easement therein, because the defendant landowner by the wrongful acts of his lessee, which were beyond his control, could acquire no interest in the plaintiff's land that could ripen into an easement. The Court said: "The defendant as lessor cannot derive any title under the lease, nor can it enforce any interest acquired by the several lessees under a wrongful appropriation for the use of the leasehold itself. The benefit, if any, is coexistent only with the term." 

Foster v. Lee, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 943, was a petition to register title to land which was conveyed to the respondent's predecessor in title by deed which reserved "all the rocks that are lying on the southern part of said land," together with a right to pass and repass to take away the rocks. In 1869 the petitioners, being the widow and heirs of the original grantor, gave to respondent's predecessor a lease of the same land for fifty years, "to use the same for bathing, boating, and fishing purposes and no other." Rent was paid by the lessee and his successors until 1919. Petitioners contended that they had title by adverse possession because of the lease, but the Court said that while it is possible under certain conditions for a lessor to gain title by adverse possession through the occupancy of his lessee or tenant, the Land Court had said that while, if record title had been in a third party, possession by respondent under the lease would have resulted in acquisition of title by adverse possession, in fact title was in the respondent's predecessor and he was in possession under his deeds,

DEEDS. 

15 and his continued possession and that of his successors in title was not adverse to themselves, and this statement the Supreme Court took as being "not a ruling of law but a finding of fact." Petition denied.

Cultivation or otherwise improving the land is generally considered very strong evidence in favor of one who claims title by adverse possession, and such proof is strengthened by evidence of the erection and maintenance of a fence around the land. On the other hand, a merely occasional use of the land, an occasional entry to cut timber or grass, or to appropriate other products or profits of the land, is commonly held insufficient. Parker v. Parker, 1 Allen, 245; McDonough v. 

Everett, 237 Mass. 378. 

Slater v. Jepherson, 6 Cush. 129, was a writ of entry to recover woodland. The tenant showed acts of occupation for more than twenty years by cutting wood and timber for use and sale; the cutting off at one time of all the wood and timber; clearing a portion of the land with a view to cultivating it; the sale of part of the premises; the running of a line between the premises and the demandant's other land and the marking of the land by lopping trees; and that such occupation was known to the demandants. Held, that the lower court was right in directing a verdict for the demandant be-. cause there was no evidence offered by the tenant to show that he had ever inclosed the land by fences, or built upon it, or cultivated it. "A soapstone ledge one hundred and sixty feet long and seventy-five feet wide, which could be definitely located on a certain farm, was excepted from a conveyance of the farm. 

For fifty-nine years after the conveyance neither the grantor of the farm nor his successors in title quarried the ledge, and there was no way from it to a public road. The grantee of the farm and his successors in title built and maintained a fence around the entire farm during that whole period. Held, that such facts did not show a title to the ledge by adverse possession in a successor of the grantee of the farm." 

Shippee, 216 Mass. 23. 

White v.

The fact that taxes were assessed to and paid by the occupant is admissible in support of his claim (Elwell v. Hinckley,

138 Mass. 225), but commonly is not given much weight. See also Whitman v. Shaw, 166 Mass. 451.

In Amee v. Boston & Albany Railroad Company, 212 Mass. 

421, the Court passed upon the admissibility of several distinct lines of evidence two of which are out of the ordinary. (b) "Open." 

The word "notorious" is often linked with "open" in the decisions, the requirement in this regard being that the possession must be of such a character that the owner may have opportunity to learn of the adverse claim and to protect his rights. It is generally accepted, however, that actual knowledge of the possession on the part of the true owner is not necessary, it being sufficient that he could have learned thereof by the exercise of ordinary diligence. See Samuels v. Borrowscale, 104 Mass. 207.

In fact, if the requirements of actual possession are proven, it commonly follows that the possession was also "open," and it was held in Cook v. Babcock, supra, that if acts of ownership relied upon as proof of a title by disseisin are not of a nature to work a disseisin, they cannot be made more effectual for that purpose by proof that they were known and not objected to by the legal owner, but the Court also said: "and on the other hand, evidence of actual, exclusive and adverse possession will not avail as proof of title, unless it is shown to have been open and notorious or actually brought home to the knowledge of the person holding the legal title." See Endicott v. Haviland, 220 Mass. 48; Dow v. Dow, 243 Mass. 587 at 593. 

Here it should be noted that the recording of a deed of land, executed by a grantor who had no title, is not constructive notice to the true owner that such conveyance has been made, the recording of a deed being constructive notice only to afterpurchasers under the same grantor, and it was held accordingly in Bates v. Norcross, 14 Pick. 224, that a mere entry on wild land by the grantee named in such a deed without an open, exclusive occupation manifested by fencing or otherwise did not amount to a disseisin of the true owner.

DEEDS. 

17 (c) "Exclusive."

Of course, the possession must be exclusive of the true owner, for if the owner is on the land as owner, the possession is in him. Bellis v. Bellis, 122 Mass. 414. The requirement that the possession be exclusive of third persons also appears to follow from the nature of legal possession. If two or more persons are on land, neither having title thereto, each claiming possession independently of the other, neither can be regarded as in legal possession. Of course, two or more persons claiming title as co-owners may together gain title by adverse possession, but again their possession must be exclusive of the true owner and of all other persons.

It is often said loosely that one tenant in common cannot gain title against his co-tenant by adverse possession. This is not the law in Massachusetts, however. See Joyce v. Dyer, 189 Mass. 64 and cases cited; Inhabitants of Ipswich v. Proprietors, 218 Mass. 487; Nickerson v. Nickerson, 235 Mass. 348.

The requirements in such a case are somewhat stricter than in the ordinary case of possession against a stranger, for the reason that the occupation of one tenant in common will be deemed to be in conformity to his right and title and not adverse, but possession exclusively maintained by one without any claim of right to possession or any demand for rents and profits by the other is evidence from which a jury may and ought to infer an actual ouster and adverse possession. (d) "Adverse" or "Hostile."

The requirement that the possession must be "adverse" to the true owner especially demands that it must not be permissive. "Permission" and "claim of right" are the opposed terms, and it was held in Hall v. Stevens, 9 Met. 418, that when a party's first entry upon land, and his occupation thereof, were by permission of the owner, the legal presumption is that his subsequent occupation was also permissive, and not adverse, unless some act of his is proved which constitutes a disseisin of the owner. See also Curtis v. Goodwin, 232 Mass. 538.

Cases come to light wherein it seems to appear that an owner of land has acquired title by adverse possession to land belonging to an adjoining owner, but often, upon analysis of the evidence and considering the relations of the parties and their acts and statements, it develops that the use set up was never adverse or under claim of right, but was begun and continued by "neighborly permission." Robert v. Perron, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 19, involved title to a portion of a passageway adjoining land conveyed to L by the Holyoke Water Power Company in 1886. Within two months after getting his deed, L encroached on the passageway. The 

Master reported that he would have found that defendant had sustained the burden of proving that the enclosure and occupancy was under claim of right and adverse, had not defendant testified that her father, L, just before he built a fence in the position complained of, told her that someone from the Power Company had told him that he could take the waste land and use it he could close that piece in it was no good . . . he could fence the land in and use it up.

The Master also found that L acted in the belief, based in part on what had been told him by this person, that he had a right to fence in, grade and occupy the extra land. 

The trial court ruled that the possession was adverse. On appeal from a decree based on this finding, the decree was affirmed. The Court said that the judge was justified in finding that L was acting with the intent to assert a right in himself inconsistent with the right of the Power Company to interfere with him, not pursuant to a permission, which implied a continued recognition on his part of a right in the Company, and permissive only in the sense that the Company did not interfere when he began, and that he understood that it would not interfere at any time. It was also said, "a holding by one in such a state of mind is adverse . . the cases cited by the plaintiff which hold that a use beginning by permission must be assumed to continue to be permissive until evidence is introduced which changes its character, and until changed, cannot be adverse, have no application here. The use here did not begin by permission nor was it permissive within the meaning of those decisions."

DEEDS. 

19 7. 

INTERRUPTION OF POSSESSION. 

Of course the running of the statute will be interrupted if the disseisee regains possession, but it has been held that an entry by a person disseised merely for the purpose of seeing if there is any evidence of an adverse occupation, is not, as a matter of law, conclusive evidence of an interruption of the disseisor's adverse possession. Bowen v. Guild, 130 Mass. 121. The bringing of a writ of entry before the expiration of the twenty-year period, if prosecuted to judgment in favor of the demandant, will interrupt the possession of a disseisor (Butrick, Petitioner, 185 Mass. 107), and so will a petition to the 

Land Court for registration, or an action in equity to prevent continuing trespass.

If the disseisor at any time recognizes the title of the disseisee in any formal manner, there is an interruption for the reason that it thereby results that the possession has not been "hostile" throughout the required period. There was a case in the Land Court where the land had been occupied by three generations of a family who originally entered under a lease. 

On the face of things it seemed probable that the tenants, as they contended, had been holding adversely to the lessor, and under a claim of right for nearly forty years, but evidence was produced which established that the occupant for the time being had upon one occasion and another admitted that the lessor should be consulted upon questions involving rights in the land, with the result that no period of twenty years. was established during which the tenants had persisted in a claim of right, and accordingly it was decided that title by adverse possession had not been made out. "Adverse possession is pure matter of fact, to be interrupted only by interrupting the possessor's exclusion of adverse claimants, an abandonment of his claim, or a change in his intent. . . . In general also the effect of the adverse possession will not be abridged by a change of title. The adverse possessor ex hypothesi is a wrongdoer until the twenty years has elapsed. Commonly, at least, if not necessarily, his claim is adverse to all the world, and probably any dealings

among the excluded parties, even where a deed by a disseisee is valid, would not affect him." Harrison v. Dolan, 172 

Mass. 395. 

8. TACKING. 

It is generally conceded that an heir may tack the possession of his ancestor to his own in order to make out the statutory period, and so may a devisee tack the possession of his testator, and a grantee the possession of his grantor. 

Sawyer v. Kendall, 10 Cush. 241; Frost v. Courtis, 172 Mass. 

401. And in Wishart v. McKnight, 178 Mass. 356, it was held: "Successive grantors may transfer their possession of a strip of land successively and continuously occupied as part of the granted premises but not included in the description in any of the deeds, and by such continuity of possession for twenty years a title by limitation may be acquired." 

9. COLOR OF TITLE. 

In this country there is a doctrine generally recognized that if one enters under a deed or other written instrument, and occupies and improves a part only of the land described therein, nevertheless the limits and extent of his legal possession will be defined by the boundaries contained in such deed or instrument, although it has no validity in conveying title. In Massachusetts this doctrine was recognized at an early date (see 

Stearns v. Palmer, 10 Met. 32 at 36), and was repeated and affirmed in the case of Dow v. Dow, 243 Mass. 587. In the latter case it was pointed out that the principle applies only when there has been an entry of disseisin upon a single parcel of land, and was not applicable to include three separate and not contiguous parcels described in a sheriff's deed which was relied on as "color of title," entry having been made on only one of the parcels. 66 'Color of title" is admissible to show the nature and extent of the possession, but it is not in itself evidence of user. See 

Nantucket v. Mitchell, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 733.

DEEDS. 

21 10. 

DISABILITIES. 

As title by adverse possession depends upon failure to assert title for the period prescribed by the statute of limitations of real actions, it follows that title cannot be acquired in this manner against one who is under a disability which prevents the statute from running against him. 

G. L. c. 260, § 25: If, when such right of entry or of action first accrues, the person entitled thereto is a minor, or is insane, imprisoned or absent from the United States, he, or a person claiming under him, may make the entry or commence the action within ten years after such disability is removed. 11.

MISCELLANEOUS NOTES ON ADVERSE POSSESSION. 

The rule is established in Massachusetts that the statute of limitations will run in favor of a trustee as against his beneficiary, provided the trust has been repudiated or otherwise terminated to the knowledge of the beneficiary. Schmidt v. 

Schmidt, 216 Mass. 572 at 577; Young v. Walker, 224 Mass. 

491 at 493. The decisions for the most part concern trusts of personal property, and no Massachusetts case has been found wherein a repudiation of a trust concerning land was made out, but the rule is stated as applicable to such trusts in Perry on Trusts, 6th Ed. Vol. 2, § 863, where it is said that the trustee must clearly repudiate the trust and assume an adverse position, with notice to the cestui, before the statute can begin to run, and that when these facts exist for twenty years an action to recover the land is barred. In Amory v. Amherst 

College, 229 Mass. 374, it was said: “As to an express trust, the statute [of limitations] will not run against the cestui que trust in favor of the trustee, unless the latter had openly and notoriously repudiated the claim of the former; while in the case of an implied trust the rule ordinarily is different." 

Many cases where title by adverse possession is pleaded break down because, although the claimant has made some use or occupation of the land during the full statutory period, it develops upon analyzing his acts that the use set up did not at

first satisfy the rules, and that accordingly he has not had adverse possession throughout the required period. For example, suppose that in each year for five successive years a man cut and hauled away a small amount of wood from a wood lot containing ten acres which belonged to another. At the beginning of the sixth year he built and occupied a house in one corner of the lot and cultivated a small amount of land surrounding it. 

This continued through the second five years. He then fenced in an acre of land on which the house was situated, and during the third five-year period cultivated the whole acre so inclosed. 

He then fenced the ten-acre parcel, and during the fourth fiveyear period cut off the greater part of the wood and cleared more land. Under these circumstances, although he has made some use of the land continuously during a period of twenty years, he probably has acquired title to nothing. The statute may have run in his favor for fifteen years as to the land occupied by the house and the adjoining land continuously cultivated, it probably has run ten years as to the one acre first. fenced, and very likely it has run for five years as to the whole parcel, and yet he has acquired title to nothing as against the real owner.

In the Land Court, when a break in the record title appears, the Court often requires the petitioner to procure and file affidavits tending to establish title by occupancy, that is to say, by adverse possession. Often, in response to such requirement, the petitioner files an affidavit which merely states that he and the person under whom he claims title have been in actual possession of the land, openly, exclusively and under claim of right for more than twenty years. This form is not in favor. 

The affidavit should set forth the period and the manner of occupancy, with such details as go to establish the elements requisite to adverse possession, leaving the Court to say whether or not the requirements have been satisfied.

Title to land which has been registered under the provisions of chapter 185 of the General Laws cannot be acquired by adverse possession.

G. L. c. 185, § 53, as amended by chapter 117 of the Acts of 1921: No title to registered land, or easement or other right therein, in derogation of the title of the registered owner, shall

DEEDS. 

23 be acquired by prescription or adverse possession. Nor shall a right of way by necessity be implied under a conveyance of registered land. 

12. CONTINUING TRESPASS. 

A trespass, in the ordinary sense, is a violation of a property right which ordinarily gives the injured party the right to recover damages at law. There is a class of cases, however, where the trespass complained of is of a permanent nature, a "continuing trespass," such as the erection of a building or part of a building or other structure on land belonging to another. In a proceeding in equity in which the owner of the land which has been encroached upon asks for a mandatory injunction directing the removal of the structure, the defendant commonly contends that the cost of removal would be so out of proportion to the damage suffered by the plaintiff that the injunction should be denied and only money damages awarded. This contention may prevail where the encroachment has resulted from innocent mistake, or where the plaintiff has slept on his rights, but the trend of the later Massachusetts cases seems to be against the offender and in favor of the injured party. But see Loughlin v. Wright Machine Co., Adv. Sh. 1930, page 2187.

In Szathmary v. B. & A. R.R. Co., 214 Mass. 42, which was a suit in equity to enjoin a continuing trespass committed by defendant by the maintenance of a retaining wall, the foundation of which projected underground from five to fifteen inches into the land of the plaintiff, it appeared that the boundary line between the plaintiff's land and the defendant's location was marked correctly by a fence that had existed for more than twenty years, that the plaintiff before the wall was built sent an engineer to the defendant to protest against any encroachment on his land, and also had served on the defendant a notice of warning through an attorney. The defendant contended that the plaintiff's remedy should be restricted to compensation in damages, because the plaintiff had suffered no substantial injury from the encroachment, and the removal of the wall would subject the defendant to great inconvenience and loss. Held, that the plaintiff was entitled to a mandatory

injunction ordering the removal of the wall, and that under the circumstances he should not be deprived of this right merely because such an injunction might subject the defendant to an expense and inconvenience disproportionate to the benefit afforded to the plaintiff. The Court said: "The general principle, that when one without right attempts to appropriate the property of another by conduct which will ripen into an easement, a court of equity will compel the trespasser to undo as far as possible what he has wrongfully done, is too well established for discussion. Curtis Manuf. Co. v. Spencer 

Wire Co., 203 Mass. 448; Kershishian v. Johnson, 210 Mass. 

135, and cases cited. The defendant's contention is that this case is one of the exceptions to the general rule, where the plaintiff suffers no substantial injury from the encroachment, and the removal of the wall would subject the defendant to so great inconvenience and loss as to be grossly inequitable; and where compensation in damages is the suitable remedy. 

See Lynch v. Union Instn. for Savings, 159 Mass. 306; Harrington v. McCarthy, 169 Mass. 492; Levi v. Worcester Consolidated St. Ry., 193 Mass. 116; Methodist Episcopal Society v. Akers, 167 Mass. 560. In these exceptional cases there always appears some element, such as an innocent mistake or a bona fide claim of right on the part of the defendant, laches on the part of the plaintiff, or the presence of some other incident, that would render inequitable the issuance of a mandatory injunction. See also Boston and Albany R.R. Co. v. 

Terminal Realty Corporation, 252 Mass. 165. وو In Congregation Beth Israel v. Heller, 231 Mass. 527, it was held that the owner of an easement in land is entitled to a mandatory injunction compelling the removal of structures that violate his right, although he has suffered no pecuniary damage. 

In Marcus v. Brody, 254 Mass. 152, the plaintiff and defendant were owners of adjoining lots. In March, 1924, defendant began the erection of a garage on the rear of his lot. 

The garage was finished in May, 1924. In April the plaintiff had her lot surveyed. It was found by the trial judge that the rear of the garage extended on to the plaintiff's lot, the foundations four and one-fourth inches, and the wall two and

DEEDS. 

25 one-eighth inches; that the foundations at the front extended over two and one-half inches on to plaintiff's land, and the wall at this point one-half inch; that the concrete driveway was over two and one-half inches in front of the garage running to nothing at a point near the rear of the house. It was found that the encroachment by the defendant was innocent and not intentional, and was not known to him until the survey was made and his attention was directed to it by the plaintiff. Defendant refused upon notice to remove the encroachment and completed the garage.

It was found that the land encroached on was worth $5; that the cost of removing the encroachment would be $500; and that cost of removing the obstructions when first discovered would have been $300. Defendant was ordered to remove the encroachments. The Court said: "Injunctive relief may impose upon the defendant an expense out of proportion to the apparent benefit to the plaintiff, but this is not enough to deprive her of the right to an injunction." 

In Crosby v. Blomerth, 258 Mass. 221, defendant sold to plaintiff a lot of land in a development tract, and proceeded to erect a house on his remaining adjoining land. The master found that the value of the house erected by defendant was $7,500, and that it would cost $1,900 to move it, as prayed for. The parties agreed that the foundation encroached on the land of the plaintiff to the extent of five hundred square inches, a total land value of 45 cents, and that the eaves encroachment amounted to forty-eight square feet with a land value of $6.10. A master found that the plaintiffs knew of the contemplated erection of a house by the defendant from the beginning, but had reason to believe that the cellar wall was built exactly on the line, and that the defendant did not use 'the necessary care in laying out his foundation. The 

Court said: "On these facts there is absolutely nothing in the defence of unreasonable delay and laches. Although the plaintiffs lived within fifty feet of the building and daily observed the progress being made they 'had reason to believe that the cellar wall was built exactly on the line.' They owed defendant no duty to make an investigation which defendant did not care to make for himself to ascertain whether or not he was trespassing on their premises. . . . The final contention

1 of the defendant is, that the decree is inequitable because the injury to the plaintiffs is not serious or substantial, and may be readily compensated in damages, while to restore things as they were before the act complained of would subject defendant to great inconvenience and loss. The fact that the pecuniary damage in land value is not serious or substantial is of no consequence. Congregation Beth Israel v. Heller, 231 

Mass. 527, 529. The walls of the cellar and the overhanging eaves constituted a continuing trespass and nuisance upon the adjoining premises of the plaintiff. In the circumstances of the case to compel the defendant to restore things to their former condition is not inequitable or oppressive."

13. IMPORTANT STATUTORY PROVISIONS. 

(GENERAL LAWS, CHAPTER 183.) SECTION 1. A deed executed and delivered by the person, or by the attorney of the person, having authority therefor, shall, subject to the limitations of section four, be sufficient, without any other act or ceremony, to convey land. 

SECTION 2. A deed of quitclaim and release shall be suffi cient to convey all the estate which could lawfully be conveyed by a deed of bargain and sale.

SECTION 3. An estate or interest in land created without an instrument in writing,signed by the grantor or by his attorney shall have the force and effect of an estate at will only, and no estate or interest in land shall be assigned, granted or surrendered unless by such writing or by operation of law. 

SECTION 4. A conveyance of an estate in fee simple, fee tail or for life, or a lease for more than seven years from the making thereof, shall not be valid as against any person, except the grantor or lessor, his heirs and devisees and persons having actual notice of it, unless it, or an office copy as provided in section thirteen of chapter thirty-six, is recorded in the registry of deeds for the county or district in which the land to which it relates lies.

SECTION 5. The record of a deed, lease, power of attorney or other instrument, duly acknowledged or proved as provided in this chapter, and purporting to affect the title to land, shall be conclusive evidence of the delivery of such instrument, in favor of purchasers for value without notice claiming thereunder. 

SECTION 5A. (Chapter 227 of the Acts of 1924.) A state ment of a person's married or unmarried status, kinship or

lack of kinship, or of the date of his birth or death, which relates or purports to relate to the title to land and is sworn to before any officer authorized by law to administer oaths may be filed for record and shall be recorded in the registry of deeds for the county where the land or any part thereof lies. Any such statement, if so recorded, or a certified copy of the record thereof, in so far as the facts stated therein bear on the title to land, shall be admissible in evidence in support of such title in any court in the commonwealth in proceedings relating to such title.

SECTION 6. Every deed presented for record shall contain or have endorsed upon it the full name, residence and post office address of the grantee, and shall also state whether the grantee is married or unmarried. If the statements required are not contained in the body of the deed but are endorsed upon it they shall be entered in the margin of the record. 

Failure to comply with this section shall not affect the validity of any deed or prevent it from being recorded. 

SECTION 7. A conveyance of land, if otherwise valid, shall, notwithstanding disseisin or adverse possession, be as effectual to transfer the title of the grantor as if he were actually seized and possessed of such land, and shall vest in the grantee the rights of entry and of action for recovery of the estate incident to such title. 

14. SHORT FORMS ACT. 

In 1912 (see chapter 502 of the Acts of 1912, now G. L. c. 183, § 8 et seq.) the Legislature passed an act providing new and shorter forms for deeds, mortgages and other instruments of conveyance. That act is commonly referred to as the "Short 

Forms Act," and became effective on January 1, 1913. By setting out in the statute certain stock forms and provisions, and by incorporating certain of these where applicable into all instruments, and by permitting the parties to incorporate others by reference when desired, the forms were reduced in length and a great saving in the space required for recording was effected.

DEEDS. 

29 

(GENERAL LAWS, CHAPTER 183.) SHORT FORMS. 

SECTION 8. The forms set forth in the appendix to this chapter may be used and shall be sufficient for their respective purposes. They shall be known as "Statutory Forms" and may be referred to as such. They may be altered as circumstances require, and the authorization of such forms shall not prevent the use of other forms. Wherever the phrase "incorporated by reference" is used in the following sections, the method of incorporation as indicated in said forms shall be sufficient, but shall not preclude other methods. 

SECTION 9. For the purpose of avoiding the unnecessary use of words in deeds or other instruments relating to real estate, whether said statutory form or other forms are used, the rules and definitions contained in sections ten to twentyeight, inclusive, shall apply to all such instruments executed or delivered on or after January first, nineteen hundred and thirteen.

SECTION 10. A deed in substance following the form en titled "Warranty Deed" shall when duly executed have the force and effect of a deed in fee simple to the grantee, his heirs and assigns, to his and their own use, with covenants on the part of the grantor, for himself, his heirs, executors, administrators and successors, with the grantee, his heirs, successors and assigns, that, at the time of the delivery of such deed, (1) he was lawfully seized in fee simple of the granted premises, (2) that the granted premises were free from all encumbrances, (3) that he had good right to sell and convey the same to the grantee and his heirs and assigns, and (4) that he will, and his heirs, executors and administrators shall, warrant and defend the same to the grantee and his heirs and assigns against the lawful claims and demands of all persons. 

SECTION 11. A deed in substance following the form en titled "Quitclaim Deed" shall when duly executed have the force and effect of a deed in fee simple to the grantee, his heirs and assigns, to his and their own use, with covenants on the part of the grantor, for himself, his heirs, executors, administrators and successors, with the grantee, his heirs, successors and assigns, that at the time of the delivery of such deed the

premises were free from all encumbrances made by him, and that he will, and his heirs, executors and administrators shall, warrant and defend the same to the grantee and his heirs and assigns forever against the lawful claims and demands of all persons claiming by, through or under the grantor, but against none other.

SECTION 12. In a conveyance of real estate the word 

"grant" shall be a sufficient word of conveyance without the use of the words "give, bargain, sell and convey", and no covenant shall be implied from the use of the word "grant". ["A warranty during the life of the grantor was, in the absence of express covenants, formerly implied from the word 'give'." -Crocker's Notes on Common Forms, page 29.]

SECTION 13. In a conveyance or reservation of real estate the terms "heirs", "assigns" or other technical words of inheritance shall not be necessary to convey or reserve an estate in fee. A deed or reservation of real estate shall be construed to convey or reserve an estate in fee simple, unless a different intention clearly appears in the deed.

SECTION 14. When a conveyance or devise of real estate is made to a grantee or devisee to a use intended to be executed by the statute of uses, the word "use" shall be employed in declaring the use; and provisions introduced by the words "in trust", or other expressions that might otherwise create uses, shall be deemed to create trusts and not uses. If no use is declared in a conveyance or devise of real estate, the same shall take effect as if it were expressed to be for the use of the grantee or devisee. [See Carr v. Richardson, 157 Mass. 576, 577.]

SECTION 15. In a conveyance of real estate all rights, easements, privileges and appurtenances belonging to the granted estate shall be included in the conveyance, unless the contrary shall be stated in the deed, and it shall be unnecessary to enumerate or mention them either generally or specifically. [See Dubinsky v. Cama, 261 Mass. 47, and section 71 of these notes.]

SECTION 16. In a conveyance of real estate the words 

"warranty covenants" shall have the full force, meaning and effect of the following words: "The grantor, for himself, his heirs, executors, administrators and successors, covenants with

SECTION 17. In a conveyance of real estate the words "quitclaim covenants' or the words "limited covenants" shall have the full force, meaning and effect of the following words: "The grantor, for himself, his heirs, executors, administrators and successors, covenants with the grantee, his heirs, successors and assigns, that the granted premises are free from all encumbrances made by the grantor,and that he will, and his heirs, executors, administrators and successors shall, warrant and defend the same to the grantee and his heirs, successors and assigns forever against the lawful claims and demands of all persons claiming by, through or under the grantor, but against none other وو

SECTION 20. The following "condition" shall be known as the "Statutory Condition", and may be incorporated in any mortgage by reference: 

(CONDITION.) 

Provided, nevertheless, except as otherwise specifically stated in the mortgage, that if the mortgagor, or his heirs, executors, administrators, successors or assigns shall pay unto the mortgagee or his executors, administrators or assigns the principal and interest secured by the mortgage, and shall perform any obligation secured at the time provided in the note, mortgage or other instrument or any extension thereof, and shall perform mo waste the condition of any prior mortgage, and until such payment and performance shall pay when due and payable all taxes, charges and assessments to whomsoever and whenever laid or assessed, whether on the mortgaged premises or on any interest therein or on the debt or obligation secured thereby; shall keep the buildings on said premises insured against fire in a sum not less than the amount secured by the mortgage or as otherwise provided therein for insurance for the benefit of the mortgagee and his executors, administrators and assigns, in such form and at such insurance offices as they shall approve, and, at least two days before the expiration of any policy on said premises, shall deliver to him or them a new and sufficient policy to take the place of the one so expiring, and shall not commit or suffer any strip or waste of the mortgaged premises or any breach of any covenant contained in the mortgage or in any prior mortgage, then the mortgage deed, as also the mortgage note or notes, shall be void.

DEEDS. 

33 

SECTION 21. The following "power" shall be known as the 

"Statutory Power of Sale", and may be incorporated in any mortgage by reference: 

(POWER.) 

But upon any default in the performance or observance of the foregoing or other condition, the mortgagee or his executors, administrators, successors or assigns may sell the mortgaged premises or such portion thereof as may remain subject to the mortgage in case of any partial release thereof, either as a whole or in parcels, together with all improvements that may be thereon, by public auction on or near the premises, or at such place as may be designated for that purpose in the mortgage, first complying with the terms of the mortgage and with the statutes relating to the foreclosure of mortgages by the exercise of a power of sale, and may convey the same by proper deed or deeds to the purchaser or purchasers absolutely and in fee simple; and such sale shall forever bar the mortgagor and all persons claiming under him from all right and interest in the mortgaged premises, whether at law or in equity. [This section was amended by chapter 104 of the Acts of 1927 by inserting after "by public auction on or near the premises" the following: "then subject to the mortgage, or, if more than one parcel is then subject thereto, on or near one of said parcels".]

SECTION 25. The holder of a mortgage of real estate, or any person acting in his behalf, may purchase at a foreclosure sale unless the contrary is stated in the mortgage. [A similar provision was commonly inserted in the forms of mortgages in use before this statute was enacted. Unless so authorized, equity would not allow the holder of a mortgage containing a power of sale to become the purchaser at a sale under the power. Hall v. Bliss, 118 Mass. 554.]

SECTION 26. Until default in the performance or observ ance of the condition of a mortgage of real estate, the mortgagor and his heirs and assigns may hold and enjoy the mortgaged premises and receive the rents and profits thereof. unless otherwise stated in the mortgage.

[This provision was also commonly inserted in the old form mortgage. Without it, a mortgage being considered between the parties as a conveyance in fee, the mortgagee would have been entitled to possession forthwith.]

SECTION 27. The holder of a mortgage of real estate, or his representatives, out of the money arising from a sale under the power of sale shall be entitled to retain all sums then secured by the mortgage, whether then or thereafter payable, including all costs, charges or expenses incurred or sustained by him or them by reason of any default in the performance or observance of the condition of the mortgage or of any prior mortgage, rendering the surplus, if any, to the mortgagor, or his heirs, successors or assigns, unless otherwise stated in the mortgage. No person other than the holder of the mortgage shall be bound to see to the application of the money arising from such sale. [Also found in substance in the old forms.]

SECTION 28. In an assignment of a mortgage of real estate the word "assign" shall be a sufficient word to transfer the mortgage, without the words "transfer and set over". 

15. DEEDS FORMS IN USE IN MASSACHUSETTS. 

The deeds in common use in Massachusetts are called (1) warranty, and (2) quitclaim. These forms differ in the granting words employed and in the covenants of title. According to the technical rules of the common law, a deed in the quitclaim form would be ineffectual to pass any right to one who was not already seized or in possession of the estate. But our 

Supreme Court, acting upon the principle that although technically an instrument is not adapted to execute the intent of the parties, it shall be held to operate in some other way in order to effect such intent, held that a deed in the quitclaim form should be effectual although the releasee was not in possession. These decisions were incorporated in Revised Statutes of 1836, which provided that "a deed of quitclaim and release of the form in common use in this state shall be sufficient to pass all the estate which the grantor could lawfully convey by a deed of bargain and sale." See G. L. c. 183, § 2.
