MORTGAGES - PRELIMINARY STATEMENT. 

In form, the Massachusetts mortgage differs from a warranty deed by the insertion, before the in testimonium clause, of clauses expressing the mortgage condition and the power of sale. "The power of sale is not a necessary part of a mortgage. 

All that relates to it may be omitted, and there will still be a valid mortgage which may be foreclosed in three years as provided by statute." - Crocker's Notes, page 161. See G. L. c. 244. 

Professor Washburn defined a mortgage as follows: "A mortgage at common law may be defined to be an estate created by a conveyance, absolute in its form, but intended to secure the performance of some act, such as the payment of money and the like, by the grantor or some other person, and to become void if the act is performed agreeably to the terms prescribed at the time of making such conveyance. It is, therefore, an estate defeasible by the performance of a condition subsequent." See also Jones on Mortgages, Vol. 1, page 17. 

17. DIFFERENT THEORIES AS TO THE NATURE OF A MORTGAGE. 

"In New York, Michigan, Wisconsin, California and the great majority of the American States, the mortgage is treated as conferring upon the mortgagee merely a lien on the land, which lien is only personalty in his hands; and as leaving the legal title and estate in the hands of the mortgagor." Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 1050. "In England, Massachusetts, New Hampshire, Maine, Illinois, and probably in some other States, the legal title to the land is transferred by the mortgagor to the first mortgagee, and the mortgagor retains only an equitable interest or estate in the property, his equity of redemption." - Reeves on Real 

Property, Vol. 2, page 1050. 

The Massachusetts theory of a mortgage is best stated by 

Chief Justice Shaw in Ewer v. Hobbs, 5 Met. 1: 66 'The first great object of a mortgage is, in the form of a conveyance in fee, to give to the mortgagee an effectual security, by

the pledge or hypothecation of real estate, for the payment of the debt, or the performance of some other obligation. The next is, to leave the mortgagor, and to purchasers, creditors, and all others claiming derivatively through him, the full and entire control, disposition and ownership of the estate, subject only to the first purpose, that of securing the mortgagee. Hence it is that, as between the mortgagor and the mortgagee, the mortgage is to be regarded as a conveyance in fee, because that construction best secures him in his remedy and his ultimate right to the estate, and to its incidents, the rents and profits. 

But in all other respects, until foreclosure, when the mortgagee becomes the absolute owner, the mortgage is deemed to be a lien or charge, subject to which the estate may be conveyed, attached, and in other respects dealt with as the estate of the mortgagor. And all the statutes on the subject are to be so construed, and all rules of law, whether administered in law or in equity, are to be so applied, as to carry those objects into effect." 

In Crowley v. Adams, 226 Mass. 582, the Court said: "It is settled in this Commonwealth that as between the parties a mortgage is regarded as a conveyance in fee for the protection of the mortgagee, but until foreclosure, the mortgage in equity constitutes a lien for the security of the debt or obligation, subject to which the property can be dealt with as the estate of the mortgagor."

An assignee of a mortgage who is also indorsee of the note secured thereby, holding the same as collateral security, has all the rights, interests and benefits which the original mortgagee had as to all persons other than his assignor and pledgor. 

He is the holder of the record title. He may foreclose the mortgage and convey to the purchaser title to the land. He is the owner so far as concerns the maker of the note. He is the owner of it as to everybody except his assignor and pledgor. 

United States Trust Co. v. Commonwealth, 245 Mass. 75. See also MacFarlane v. Thompson, 241 Mass. 486.

Further light is turned upon the nature of a mortgage by the following statements from the opinion in Ritger v. Parker, 8 Cush. 145: "When a mortgagor or the assignee redeems, he regains his estate just as it existed when he made the mortgage; the oper-

DEEDS. 

39 ation of the mortgage is defeated by force of the condition; he takes the estate with all the incidents and benefits, and subject to the servitudes, to which it was subject when the mortgage was made; and no lease, charge, or incumbrance, made by the mortgagee, can be set up against the claims of the mortgagor. The estate is restored unchanged. "So, if the mortgage is foreclosed, then the estate, which was conditional and defeasible in its creation, becomes absolute; and the incidents, privileges, and covenants attached to it, unchanged by anything which the mortgagor or any other person may have done in the meantime, remain attached to it as if the original conveyance had been absolute." "The defendant on January 7, 1921, was in occupation of the premises described in the record under a lease given by the mortgagor, dated July 1, 1920, for the term of one year. 

But the second mortgage, held by the plaintiff who had the legal title as between the parties, was paramount to the lease. 

Ewer v. Hobbs, 5 Met. 1, 3. The entry on January 7, 1921, by the plaintiff for the purpose of foreclosure of the mortgage, followed by notice in writing to the defendant, that he had taken possession, and that if the defendant cared to remain as a tenant he must in the future pay rent to him, which defendant declined to do, terminated defendant's leasehold, and no eviction or actual dispossession was necessary." Burke v. 

Willard, 249 Mass. 313. 

In Way v. Mullett, 143 Mass. 49, at 56, the Court said: "What is popularly known as a power of sale mortgage is a combination of two instruments, the one a security for a debt or other obligation by a mortgage of property to which the usual incidents of the right to foreclose on the part of the creditor and to redeem on the part of the debtor are attached; the other a power in the nature of a trust to sell the premises and to devote the proceeds to the payment of the debt. The latter is to be executed according to its terms, except so far as its effect or execution may be controlled by the statutes. The donor of the power cannot revoke it, as it is coupled with an interest. The rights of a creditor who is a mortgagee and also the donee of a power are not less distinct because one instrument instead of two is used to state them."

The only practical distinction between the "lien theory" and the "conveyance theory" as to the nature of a mortgage is that under the former the mortgagor retains the legal title and is entitled to possession until foreclosure, while under the latter the legal title passes to the mortgagee and entitles him to immediate possession of the mortgaged premises unless restrained by the terms of the mortgage. The form of mortgage in use prior to the Short Forms Act included the following provision: "And it is agreed that, until default in the performance or observance of the condition of this deed, I and my heirs and assigns may hold and enjoy the granted premises and receive the rents and profits thereof.". See G. L. c. 183, § 26, and comment supra. 

G. L. c. 244, § 9, provides as follows: This chapter shall not prevent a mortgagee or person claiming under him from entering on the land or from recovering possession thereof before breach of condition of the mortgage, if there is no agreement to the contrary, etc.

G. L. c. 206, § 9: If a mortgagee of real estate or an assignee of such mortgagee dies without having foreclosed the mortgage, the mortgaged property and the debt secured thereby shall be personal assets in the hands of his executor or administrator, and shall be administered and accounted for as such; and the executor or administrator shall have the same rights of entry and of action under said mortgage as the mortgagee or his assignee.

The foregoing statute was necessary as a practical matter. 

Without it, under the "conveyance theory," title to the mortgaged property would vest in the heirs at law of the holder of the mortgage if he died intestate, and' in his devisees if he left a will. See Miller and Sons, Ltd., v. Blinn, 219 Mass. 

266, where the Court said: "It follows that until an administrator is appointed no one can enforce the rights of the deceased mortgagee; not the mortgagee, for he is dead; not the person to whom the mortgagee's legal estate in law descends (to wit the administrator of his estate), because no administrator has been appointed. Further, where (as was the case in this suit) the mortgagee was a non-resident, the mortgage cannot be foreclosed until an ancillary administrator is ap-

DEEDS. pointed in Massachusetts. It is settled that the legal estate in the land mortgaged which a deceased mortgagee had does not become vested in a foreign administrator." "A foreign executor or administrator cannot make a valid assignment of a mortgage of land situated in this state." 

Crocker, page 183. However, it is the practice generally to accept discharges of mortgages from a foreign executor or administrator upon satisfactory proof of his appointment by a court of competent jurisdiction. In such a case it should be remembered that a resident of another State, who is the mortgagee under a mortgage of real estate in this Commonwealth, has an interest in the real estate which at his death is subject to a succession tax under St. 1912, c. 678, § 1. Hawkridge v. 

Treas. and Receiver General, 223 Mass. 134. See also Kinney v. Same, 207 Mass. 368.

18. THE STATUTE OF USES. 

The English Statute of Uses was enacted in 1535. It is a part of the common law of this Commonwealth. It was intended to prevent the common practice of conveying land to one, called the feoffee to uses, to the use of another, the cestui The former merely held the legal title, while the latter had the management, control and benefit of the property. 

The cestui had these advantages, moreover, divested of most of the duties, responsibilities, and burdens which ordinarily attached to the ownership of land, as such an interest could not be reached by creditors, nor forfeited for crimes, nor made subject to the claims of a wife, husband or feudal lord. Uses were peculiarly within the control of the courts of equity, and it finally became apparent that those courts had favored such estates too strongly in failing to attach to them the incidents so requisite to the fair and proper employment of land for business and commercial purposes. Accordingly attempts to remedy the evil were made by means of legislation. These culminated in the Statute of Uses by which it was enacted, in substance, that whenever one person was seised of a legal estate for the use of another, the owner of the use (cestui que use) should have also the legal estate in the same quality, manner, form and condition in which he had the use. 

Since the statute was to operate only where one person was seised to the use of another, it was held by the courts of common law that it did not execute any use where the holder of the legal title had no greater interest than an estate for years. 

Example: Land conveyed to A for ten years to the use of B for ten years. The use would not be executed, since A did not have seisin. But if the conveyance was to A and his heirs, or to A for life, for the use of B for ten years, since in

See DEEDS. 43 such case A had the seisin, the statute did operate and transfer the legal estate to B to continue during the same term of years for which he was given the use.

Owing to the construction placed upon the Statute of Uses by the courts, three classes of uses were not executed_thereby. (1) Uses to married women to be enjoyed by them to their sole and separate use. See Richardson v. Stodder, 100 Mass. 

528. In that case, after a devise "in trust to A, for the benefit of B, wife of C, to her sole and separate use, and her heirs and assigns forever" took effect, B died leaving C and their two children surviving, and it was held that B took an equitable estate in fee during coveture, which descended to her children subject to the tenancy by the curtesy of C; that the use was executed in the children by the statute; and that, upon the death of one child under age and unmarried, its share was inherited by the other under Gen. Sts. c. 91, § 1, cl. 6. 

The court said: "The terms of the devise are such as, if B had not been a married woman, would have given her a legal estate in fee; the use declared being executed by the statute of uses. وو (2) It was further determined by the common law courts that, when the feoffee to uses was required to convey the land or to receive the rents and profits and pay them over to the beneficiary, or to perform any other active duties in regard to the property, the use, or trust, was not executed by the statute. Such a settlement created an active trust. And it has been uniformly and correctly held that such a trust was not within the spirit of the statute. To have concluded otherwise would often have resulted in taking the management of property from a competent trustee and placing it in the hands of an infant, a lunatic, or some other incapacitated cestui que trust. See Reeves, page 421. 

(3) It was determined in Tyrrel's case that the statute would not execute a use "limited on a use," i.e., if a use were created on a use, the statute would execute only the first use and would thus vest and retain the legal estate in the firstnamed cestui que use. Concerning this rule Mr. Reeves wrote as follows: "About the time of passing the Statute of Uses,' says 

Mr. Watkins (Watkins on Conveyancing) 'some wise man, in

See.44 

the plenitude of legal learning, declared there could not be a use upon a use. This wise declaration, which must have surprised every one who was not sufficiently learned to have lost his common sense, was adopted and still is adopted, and upon it least chiefly) has been built up the present system of use and trusts.' . . . The use was thus restored, in full vigor, notwithstanding the Statute of Uses; but, in order to retain it, it was now usually necessary to convey the legal estate to one in whom it was not meant to remain, for the use of him in whom it was intended that it should remain, for the use of (or in trust for) the intended beneficiary.' Reeves, page 422. 

It now behooved the Court of Chancery, which was endeavoring to preserve uses, so to deal therewith as to remove the incentive for the enactment of another and possibly a more stringent statute of uses. This was done by giving to the old use a new name, and new and more equitable incidents and characteristics. After the decision in Tyrrel's case, both uses and trusts (where there was some duty to perform) were called trusts. It then became necessary to make the division, which is still retained, of all express trusts into two classes, i.e., active and passive. The passive express trust of today is the old use with its new name; and the active express trust is practically the trust so called before the Statute of Uses. The incidents and characteristics of the use, now called a passive express trust, were also materially changed by the application to it of the maxim that "equity follows the law." By this is meant that, as far as their inherent nature will permit, equity applies to these equitable estates those rules of law by which legal titles and interests are regulated. They may be reached in equity by creditors for the satisfaction of debts. 

It was decided that a husband may have curtesy in the equitable estates owned by his wife. But, when the question arose as to the wife's dower in property held in trust for her husband, it was decided that she should not be endowed of such estates, because presumably she was already provided for by a jointure or marriage settlement, and titles would be disarranged by giving her dower. In most of the states of this country, a widow has always been dowable out of

Sec.DEEDS. 45 equitable estates of her husband, but not in Maine or Massachusetts (Reed v. Whitney, 7 Gray, 533; Lobdell v. Hayes, 4 Allen, 187). — See Reeves, pages 424 to 426. 

19. CONVEYANCES UNDER THE STATUTE. 

Two methods of conveying land which are still of interest were created under the statute, namely, the Bargain and Sale and the Covenant to Stand Seised.

There seems to be some controversy as to whether the Covenant to Stand Seised was in use before the Statute of Uses, but whether it was or not is of no great importance, and it is quite certain that the Bargain and Sale was in use before the statute, although it did not then amount to a legal conveyance but only an equitable one. 

Tiffany, at page 344 of volume 1 of his treatise on Real 

Property, describes Bargain and Sale as follows: 

Before the Statute. -"A bargain and sale was a transaction of the following nature: If the owner of land made an agree ment with a purchaser for the sale to the latter of an estate or interest in the land, and the purchaser paid a pecuniary consideration therefor, but no legal conveyance was made, chancery regarded the vendor as seised of the legal estate merely for the use and benefit of the vendee in accordance with the terms of the agreement." 

After the Statute. — “Uses which, before the statute were, as explained above, created without any transmutation of possession by a bargain and sale, were also by the statute created into legal estates. Thus, if A for consideration of $500 bargained and sold land to B and his heirs, the use created thereby in B was converted into a legal estate in fee simple. A bargain and sale thus operating, by reason of the consideration, to transfer the use or equitable title, and the statute then transferring the legal title to the same person, it became a regularly recognized mode of conveyance, the formalities necessary for the transfer of land at common law, such as livery of seisin, entry, and attornment, being thereby avoided. The effect of the Statute of Uses, indeed, was to enable one to transfer land by a bargain and sale not incorporated in any writing, and the

sec.46 

impolicy of allowing the title to land thus to depend upon a mere oral, and perhaps secret, transaction, led to the enactment, in the same year, of the Statute of Enrollments, which required that every bargain and sale of an estate of inheritance or freehold should be made by deed, to be enrolled within six months after its date in a court or public office." "A covenant to stand seised to uses occurred where the owner of a freehold estate covenanted to hold the legal title in trust for the use of his child, wife, or kinsman; and the trust was enforced in equity but only in case the consideration was blood or marriage. The Statute of Uses executed the use, and thereafter this covenant came to be used as a form of conveyance." - American and English Encyc. of Law, Vol. 9, page 101.

So after the passage of the Statute of Uses, if a use was raised by either of the foregoing methods, the statute carried along the seisin and united it with the use, that is to say, carried along the seisin of the bargainor or convenantor and united it with the use in the bargainee or covenantee. 

The Statute of Enrollments, referred to above, provided that "no estate of inheritance or freehold should be made or take effect in any persons, or any use thereof be made, by reason only of any bargain and sale thereof, except the same bargain and sale were made by writing indented, sealed and enrolled" in the public record office. This statute did not apply to covenants to stand seised and apparently an agreement to stand seised did not require to be put into writing until the passage of the Statute of Frauds. The Statute of Enrollments is no part of the law of this Commonwealth. All our deeds are to be recorded alike.

After the passage of the Statute of Uses and the Statute of Enrollments, a daring conveyancer conceived the conveyance of lease and release under the Statute of Uses. It is recounted that this conveyance was contrived at the request of Lord Norris, to the end that some of his kindred should not know, by any search of public records, what settlement he should make of his estate. The lease and release, it should first be noted, was known to the common law. A lease for years would be made, and the tenant would enter, and he then was in a position to have the freehold released to him by a deed of

Sec.

DEEDS. 

47 release. This was as effectual a method of conveying land as was even the feoffment itself. It was necessary, however, for the tenant to enter and perfect his lease, thereby getting the possession of the land before a valid title to the freehold could be made to him by a release. After the Statute of Uses the necessity of an actual entry to perfect the estate of the lessee was obviated by making a bargain and sale for a year instead of a lease for a year at common law; a use was thereby created in the lessee which was at once executed in possession by mere force of the statute, thereupon a release was made and the conveyance was complete. Thus was avoided both livery of seisin and enrollment of the deed. The Statute of Enrollments did not affect the transaction because that statute made mention only of estates of inheritance and of freeholds: a lease, therefore, for a term of years was exempt from its provisions. 

A form of deed evolved from the old deed of release was generally adopted in this country as a complete and proper method of transferring title to land. See G. L. c. 183, § 2. As above stated, this provision of the statute originated in the Revised Statutes of 1836, and was no more than a statutory declaration of earlier decisions.. 

Mr. Crocker said, in his Notes on Common Forms, page 219, referring to the Statute of Uses: "There are cases where, by reason of this statute, certain words or expressions ignorantly or carelessly used in a deed may give to it an effect totally different from that intended by the party who framed it,” and cited Carr v. Richardson, 157 Mass. 576, and Richardson v. Stodder, 100 Mass. 528. In these cases the expression "in trust for" was given effect as though it had been written "to the use of." 

Note that this situation has been corrected by G. L. c. 183, 14. 

On the other hand, the aid of the Statute of Uses was sometimes intentionally availed of to avoid the rule of the common law that a man could not make any conveyance to himself, and also the rule that a husband could not convey to his wife, nor a wife to her husband. Thus A to X to the use of A and B as joint tenants is good conveyancing, and so is A to X to the use of B, the wife of A. Without the statute, two deeds would

Sec.48 

have been necessary in either case in order to accomplish the same result. But again, in this Commonwealth, recent statutes have encroached upon the domain of the Statute of Uses, and one may now convey to himself and another jointly, and husband may convey directly to his wife or a wife to her husband. 

G. L. c. 184, § 8 (Acts of 1918, c. 93, effective March 26, 

1918): Real estate, including any interest therein, may be transferred by a person to himself jointly with another person in the same manner in which it might be transferred by him to another person.

Acts of 1912, c. 304: Husband and wife may make conveyances of real estate to each other except by way of mortgage, as if unmarried; but no such conveyances shall have any effect, either in passing title or otherwise, until the deed describing the property to be transferred is duly acknowledged and recorded in the registry of deeds for the district where the land lies. 

In effect March 21, 1912.

The last-mentioned statute and R. L. c. 153, § 3 were combined in G. L. c. 209, § 3, which is as follows: Gifts of personal property, and conveyances of real estate other than mortgages, between husband and wife, shall be valid to the same extent as if they were sole, except that no such conveyance of real estate shall have any effect, either in passing title or otherwise, until the deed describing the property to be transferred is duly acknowledged and recorded in the registry of deeds for the district where the land lies. 

In Witherington v. Eldredge, 264 Mass. 166, a husband and wife conveyed through a third person to the husband a parcel of land which they owned jointly. The deed was not recorded until after the wife had been adjudged insane. In support of the contention that the deed was a nulity, it was argued that 

G. L. c. 209, § 3, superseded St. 1912, c. 304, and instead of applying to conveyances of real estate "to each other" is now applicable only to conveyances "between" husband and wife, and that the deed in question, although not directly from the wife to her husband, was nevertheless a conveyance "between" husband and wife, and therefore invalid because the wife became insane before it was recorded. The Court did not agree with the contention, and held that it was not the intention of the

Sec.DEEDS. 49 Legislature in enacting the statutes above mentioned to change the law permitting conveyances of real estate to be made between husband and wife through the medium of a third person. No acts were required by the wife after she became insane. The grantee in recording the deed acted for himself. 

20. METHOD OF CONVEYING A FREEHOLD TO TAKE EFFECT IN THE FUTURE. 

A freehold to commence in the future cannot be conveyed by a deed which derives its effect from the common law. Generally, conveyances operating under the Statute of Uses may be made to take effect in the future. The Massachusetts rule is peculiar. The Court has said that a deed which operates as a bargain and sale can have no effect as a conveyance of a freehold in futuro, but that a deed which may be construed as a covenant to stand seised may have such effect; and further, that the English rule, that a covenant to stand seised requires consideration of blood or marriage, does not hold here, but that a deed on pecuniary consideration alone may well be construed as a covenant to stand seised. Gale v. Colburn, 18 Pick. 397; Trafton v. Hawes, 102 Mass. 533; West v. West, 155 Mass. 317; Ricker v. Brown, 183 Mass. 424.

In the case last mentioned an instrument in the form of a warranty deed by an old man to his housekeeper, in consideration of her past and future services, of "all the residue of my property real and personal that has not otherwise been signed to her for like services, which may be remaining in my name and ownership at the time of my death," was held to be good as a covenant to stand seised to the use of the grantee in fee, subject to the right of the grantor to shift the use during his lifetime. The Court said: "It will be noted that the term 'residue' does not mean what shall remain at his death, but means what had not theretofore been conveyed to the grantee, or, in other words, it means what property he had at the time of the execution of the deed. . . . It is the duty of the courts to give effect to the deed, if possible, rather than to declare it null and void. If there had not been reserved a power to dispose of the property during the lifetime of the grantor there would have been no difficulty. . . . We do not think that the

existence of the power to dispose of the property prevents the application of the principle. Before the statute a use was a purely equitable interest, and might be raised after a limita- . tion in fee, or it might be created 'in futuro' without any preceding limitation, or the order of priority might be changed by shifting uses or by powers; or a power of revocation might be reserved to the grantor or a stranger to recall and change the uses." in Apparently the Massachusetts doctrine has never found a friend among the law writers. Professor Gray attacked it in the following language (Gray on Perpetuities, § 57): “In 

Massachusetts it was early held, by a singular error, that while a future estate could be raised by a covenant to stand seised, it could not be raised by a bargain and sale. "The conveyance, being in effect a bargain and sale, must have all the other requisites and qualities of a bargain and sale. One of these qualities is, that it must be to the use of the bargainee, and that another use cannot be limited on that use; from which it follows that a freehold to commence in futuro cannot be made this mode, as that would be to make the bargainee hold to the use of another, until the future freehold should vest.' 

Welsh v. Foster, 12 Mass. 93, 96. The same law is laid down in Wallis v. Wallis, 4 Mass. 135; Pray v. Pierce, 7 Mass. 381; 

Parker v. Nichols, 7 Pick. 111; Hunt v. Hunt, 14 Pick. 374; 

Gale v. Coburn, 18 Pick. 397; Brewer v. Hardy, 22 Pick. 376.

The fallacy is obvious. It lies in assuming that the use to the bargainee rises immediately upon the bargain and sale; that, under the statute, the legal estate vests immediately in him; and that the interest of the bargainor, until the future event happens, must arise out of that legal estate of the bargainee. 

Whereas, in fact, the use does not arise until the future event, and in the meantime the bargainor retains his original estate. 

It is surprising that the learned Court did not perceive that the objection which it made to a bargain and sale applied equally to a covenant to stand seised. The Massachusetts. doctrine has not been adopted elsewhere, and the error on which it rests has been often pointed out. Even in Massachusetts the practical inconvenience of the doctrine is done away with by the other erroneous doctrine, peculiar to that

DEEDS. state, that a covenant to stand seised can be supported by a pecuniary consideration. Trafton v. Hawes, 102 Mass. 533. 

One error neutralizes the other. A use in futuro can be raised by a bargain and sale."

See to the same effect Goodwin on Real Property at page 361. 

No particular words are necessary to constitute a covenant to stand seised. The words "give and grant" are sufficient. 

Gale v. Coburn, supra. 

The terms "shifting use" and "springing use" are often used as though interchangeable, although in fact each is descriptive of a distinct situation in connection with the Statute of Uses. "The Statute of Uses enacts that the estate of the feoffees to uses shall be in the cestuis que use 'after such quality, manner, form and condition as they had before, in or to the use, confidence, or trust that was in them.' Now, the Court of Chancery having permitted the limitation of a use for life or in tail to arise in futuro, without any preceding estate to support it, and also that a use should change from one person to another by matter ex post facto, though the first use were limited in fee, the Courts of Law, in process of time, admitted limitations of this kind in conveyances to uses; and determined that in such cases the statute would transfer the possession to the cestui que use in the same quality, form and condition, as he had in the use." - Greenleaf's Cruise on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 263.

A springing use is a use limited to arise on a future event where no precedent use is limited, and which does not take effect in derogation of any other interest than that which results to the grantor or remains in him in the meantime. Illustrations.

Greenleaf's Cruise: J M, being seized of land in fee, by indenture declared the use of it to be to himself, and to such wife as the said J M might afterwards marry, for and during their natural lives, and the life of the survivor of them, with remainders over. Afterwards, J M married A and then died, and it was held that A took the estate for her life provided for in the declaration to uses by J M.

13 Reeves: A deed of land to X and his heirs to the use of Y when he marries. When Y marries he acquires the use; and the Statute of Uses then executes it and thus confers upon him the legal estate. [Prior to Y's marriage, the use resulted to the grantor, and the use springs up in Y upon his marriage in derogation of the resulting use which was in the grantor.] 

Tiffany: A conveyance to A to the use of such children of 

B as may survive him. Here a use arises in favor of the cestuis named as soon as they are ascertained by the death of B, which is then immediately executed by the Statute of Uses. "Where a future use is limited as a springing use without any preceding limitation of the use, whether in a conveyance operating with or without transmutation of possession, the whole use results to or remains in the grantor until the springing use takes effect to displace it." Leake's Digest of the 

Law of Real Property, page 352. "In all cases of springing uses the estate remains in the original owner till the use arises; where there is no transmutation of possession, the springing use arises out of the seisin of the covenantor or bargainor; where there is a transmutation of possession, the new use arises out of the seisin of the feoffees." Greenleaf's Cruise, Vol. 2, page 264.

A shifting use is a conditional limitation in a use, that is to say, it is a use which takes effect in substitution for or defeasance of an estate previously created by the same instrument. Illustrations.

Greenleaf's Cruise: A made feoffment to B in fee to the use of X until Y paid $200 to X and then to the use of Y and his heirs. Y paid the $200. Thereupon the use shifted to Y and the Statute of Uses vested the legal title in Y in fee. 

Reeves: An estate to X and his heirs, for the use of Y and his heirs, but if Z returns from Rome then for the use of Z and his heirs. The Statute of Uses, executing the use, bestows the legal estate upon Y and shifts it to Z on his returning from Rome and thus acquiring the use. 

Tiffany: Thus, one may convey land to X to the use of A and his heirs, and, if A fails to pay a certain sum to B at a

DEEDS. 

53 certain time, then to the use of B and his heirs. In such case, the use in A will draw to him the legal title by force of the statute, until the shifting of the use to B, by reason of failure to pay such sum, in which case the legal title will also pass to B.

Digby: A shifting use is where a use has been properly created, and then upon the happening of some specified event the interest first created passes away from the person enjoying it, and vests, partially or wholly, in some other person. For instance, if lands are given to A and his heirs to the use of B and his heirs, but if B die in the lifetime of A then to the use of C and his heirs. Upon the death of B in A's lifetime the use is said to shift to C.

Upon consideration of the foregoing it must become apparent that Professor Gray's criticism of the Massachusetts doctrine with regard to conveyances to take effect in the future is sound.

In other words, a deed of bargain and sale to take effect in the future operates as a springing use, and there is but one use to be considered.

Of this Massachusetts doctrine, Professor Goodwin wrote as follows: "One way of stating the fallacy of this doctrine is this: If, indeed, there can be an immediate use in the grantee, then the purpose of the deed is, of course, defeated because the deed provides that it is the grantor who is to have the immediate use, and that the grantee is to have the use afterwards. Another mode of stating the objection to this doctrine is that of Professor Gray. He says that the fallacy is obvious, that the use in the grantee does not arise until the future event shall occur, that is, for illustration, when the grantor shall die, and that meantime the grantor retains his original estate. Another mode of stating the objection to this doctrine is found in Wyman v. Brown, 50 Me. 139, 157, 158.

The Court in that case says that until the occurrence of the future event the use results to the grantor, and that the use in the grantee is not a use upon a use, but is a use after a use. 

Now, any or all of these modes of stating the objection may be put into one proposition, namely, that the seisin is not moved by the Statute until the occurrence of the future event,

at which time it is united with the use in the grantee.' 

Goodwin on Real Property, page 362. 99 Apparently the Maine Court has produced an unnecessary expression in its "use after a use" and one that may cause some confusion. Possibly it meant to say, and this may be the most logical explanation of the process, that the deed to the person who is to have the estate in the future may take effect as an immediate common law transfer (feoffment) of the seisin to the grantee, to his own use at a certain time in the future, as, for illustration, at the death of the grantor, the use in the meantime results to the grantor and the Statute vests in him an estate for the term of his life, and upon the death of the grantor the use shifts to the grantee in fee. In the case of Abbott v. Holway, 72 Mass. 298, there appeared a deed, which after granting certain land to the grantor's wife, thus proceeded: "This deed is not to take effect and operate as a conveyance until my decease, and in case I shall survive my said wife, this deed is not to be operative as a conveyance, it being the sole purpose and object of this deed to make a provision for the support of my said wife if she shall survive me, and if she shall survive me, then, and in that event only, this deed shall be operative to convey to my said wife said premises in fee simple. Neither I, the grantor, nor the said Clarissa B. Abbott, the grantee, shall convey the above premises, while we both live, without our mutual consent. If I, the grantor, shall abandon or desert my said wife, then she shall have the sole use and income and control of said premises during her life." Then followed the usual habendum, and also covenants of seisin against encumbrances and warranty. It was decided that the deed should be upheld as creating a feoffment to commence in futuro. The Court said in part: "Our statutes provide that 'a person owning real estate and having a right of entry into it, whether seized of it or not, may convey it, or all his interest in it, by a deed. to be acknowledged and recorded as hereinafter provided.' 

Can it be doubted that under such statutes the owner of real estate can convey in the manner prescribed, such part or portion of his estate as he and his grantee may agree, subject only to those restrictions which the law imposes as required

DEEDS. 

55 by public policy, but relieved from the technical doctrines which arose out of ancient feudal tenures, and all the restrictive effect which they had upon alienations? Why prevent the owner in fee simple from agreeing with his grantee (and setting forth that agreement in his conveyance) as to the time when, and the conditions upon which, the instrument shall be operative to transfer the estate from one to the other? "In other words, the mere technicalities of ancient law are dispensed with upon compliance with Statute requirements. 

The acknowledgment and recording are accepted in place of livery of seizin, and it is competent to fix such time in the future as the parties may agree upon as the time when the estate of the grantee shall commence. No more necessity for limiting one estate upon another, or for having an estate (of some sort) pass immediately to the grantee in opposition to the express intention of the parties. "The feoffment is to be regarded as taking place, and the livery of seizin as occurring at the time fixed in the instrument, and the acknowledgment and recording are to be considered as giving the necessary publicity which was sought in the ancient ceremony. The questions, did anything pass by the conveyance, if so, what, and when, are to be determined by a fair construction of the language used, without reference to obsolete technicalities. The instrument will be upheld according to its terms, if those terms are definite and intelligible, and not in contravention of the requirements of sound public policy. "We are at liberty, then, to give to the language used by the grantor in a deed, its obvious meaning, without invalidating the deed, to say that it shall operate as the parties intended, and carry an estate to commence in futuro if they so agree, without the necessity of resorting to any subterfuges under which the estate thus created to commence in futuro may be recognized as existing only by way of remainder or by virtue of some imputed covenant to stand seized. "We may give its fair and just effect to one (a deed)

framed, as this is, to convey an estate in fee simple to the grantee, to commence at the decease of the grantor, provided the grantee outlives him; and the true effect seems to be that of a feoffment under which the execution and record of the deed operates in the same manner as livery of seizin made at the time of the grantor's decease." 

Of the foregoing case, Professor Goodwin said (page 363): "Of course, we know that statutes work wonders, but we think this an unnecessary aberration from elementary principles."

That the Statute of Uses and the rules of law flowing therefrom are still of practical importance is illustrated by the following case which recently was before the Land Court: ... 

By warranty deed dated August 26, 1885, C, "in consideration of love, goodwill and affection and five dollars paid by W (my son)", conveyed an undivided half interest in a parcel of land to W, "his executors and administrators, forever, to hold in trust for the sole use and benefit of my daughter, S, and her my said daughter's children. . . . The other undivided half part of the above described premises having been heretofore deeded by me to the said W in his own right. This deed to take effect at my the said C's decease and not before, meaning herein to convey the reversion after my death and nothing more. To have and to hold the aforegranted premises to the said W in trust as above for the sole use and benefit of the said S and her children and to his executors and administrators forever." C deceased in 1895. W deceased in 1901.

S deceased, intestate, in 1911 leaving five children, one of whom deceased in 1914, leaving as his heirs-at-law his widow and three children. The Probate Court appointed G as trustee under the deed to W, and G petitioned that Court for license to sell the property, which license was granted. G also petitioned the Land Court for registration of the title. The title was examined for the Land Court, and the examiner expressed the opinion that the petitioner had a good title and proper for registration. The title was registered and apparently the following conclusions were reached, although no decision was written, the case being uncontested.

DEEDS. 

57 

1. That under the decision in Ricker v. Brown, 183 Mass. 

424, the deed took effect as a covenant on the part of the grantor to stand seised of the real estate therein described to his own use during his lifetime and thereafter to the use of the grantee, and that upon the decease of the grantor the Statute of Uses executed the use so raised and vested the legal title in the grantee, W. 

2. That the statute having so operated could not operate to vest the legal title in S and her children because, if a use was raised for them, it was a use upon a use. 

3. That S took only an equitable life estate and that her children took vested equitable remainders. See Rich v. Rogers, 14 Gray, 174. 

4. That the use in the deed of the word "forever" in connection with the trust indicated that it was not the intention that any trust or use should result to the grantor; that the deed taken in connection with the fact that the grantor had previously conveyed the other undivided half of the same estate to W in his own right indicated that the grantor intended that S and her children should eventually take in fee the half conveyed in trust for them; and that W took in trust an estate commensurate with the interest to which his beneficiaries were entitled, although words of inheritance nowhere appeared in the deed.

After registration, the trustee, acting under the license of the Probate Court, sold and conveyed the estate, and then petitioned the Court for instructions as to distribution of the proceeds of the sale. After notice to all parties claiming under 

C, W and S, the Probate Court directed the trustee to distribute the fund among the living children of S and the heirs of one of her children who had deceased. 

The decree of the Land Court registering the fee in G may appear to run counter to the decision in McElroy v. McElroy, 113 Mass. 509, where it was said: "The word 'hefrs' can no more be dispensed with, in the grant of an equitable estate in fee, than in an ordinary conveyance at common law." There is, however, another class of cases where the omission of the word "heirs" has been held not to restrict to a life estate an interest

in the proceeds of real estate created by an instrument under seal. See Dorr v. Clapp, 160 Mass. 538. Moreover, it was certain that the trustee had a title of some sort to the estate conveyed by the deed because four of the children of S were living, and undoubtedly the Probate Court had power to order a sale. Upon the petition for the sale the whole family was cited and no one came in to object. Every one again had notice of the registration proceedings and no objections were presented. In short, every one who could claim an interest under C or W or S, however the trust deed was construed, had twice been given a day in court, and all were content that the title should be registered and the estate sold. 

21. DEEDS TO USES WHAT FORMS ARE ADAPTED. 

"Where a use is limited upon a use, it is held that the statute executes only the first use, while the second is void at law and only to be enforced in equity as a trust. Hence if in a deed of bargain and sale, which operates in all cases by raising a use which the statute executes, a use is limited to a party other than the grantee, it will be a use upon a use, and as such not executed by the statute. A conveyance in the form of a deed of bargain and sale should therefore never be adopted where the intention is to take advantage of the effect of the Statute of Uses in the manner we have been considering. It has, however, been decided that, even though the words 'bargain and sell' are contained in a conveyance to uses, the Court will, in order to effectuate the intent of the parties, and for the purpose of allowing the use limited to be executed by the statute, construe the deed, not as a deed, but as a feoffment. 

See Thatcher v. Omans, 3 Pick. 521, 530; Stearns v. Palmer, 10 Met. 32, 35; Brooks v. Jones, 11 Met. 191, 192; Durant v. 

Ritchie, 4 Mason 45, 69. But though the words 'bargain and sell' will not work any positive evil, it is of course preferable to employ only words proper to a feoffment,—for instance, ‘give, grant, enfeoff and convey.' The statutes (R. L. c. 127, § 2) provide that a deed of quitclaim and release shall be sufficient to convey all the estate which could lawfully be conveyed by a deed of bargain and sale.' The statutes, however, do not

DEEDS. provide that such a deed shall operate in the same manner as a deed of bargain and sale, and as a release does not in English laws derive its effect from the Statute of Uses, but is a conveyance at common law, a deed using the words commonly inserted in a quitclaim deed—'remise, release, and forever quitclaim' — would seem to be perfectly proper as a conveyance to uses." Crocker's Notes on Common Forms, page 224.

GRANTOR. 

If a deed purporting to convey a right of way is executed as grantor by the wife of the owner of the land to be subjected to the easement and is joined in by the husband releasing "unto the grantee and her heirs and assigns all right of and to curtesy and all other rights in the above mentioned premises so far as may be necessary to give full force and effect to the preceding grant by my wife," it is void as a conveyance of the easement. Daly v. Donovan, 258 Mass. 226.

One of several tenants in common cannot as against his co-tenants make a sale by metes and bounds of a portion of the common land. Sale on execution, which is in the nature of a statutory conveyance, stands upon the same basis as a conveyance by the owner. However, a conveyance by metes and bounds, whether by personal deed or statutory transfer, by one tenant in common of portion of the common estate, although of no effect against the consent of his co-tenants, operates after partition by way of estoppel to transfer the title. The grantee gets nothing unless on partition the share of the grantor should happen to include the parcel described by metes and bounds in the deed. The grantor loses by estoppel and release all his interest in the parcel so described if

DEEDS. 

61 it should happen to be set off to him. Cressey v. Cressey, 215 Mass. 65.

For the provisions of the Uniform Partnership Act, chapter 486 of the Acts of 1922, relative to the right of a partner to convey partnership property, see section 26. 

G. L. c. 156, § 42, requires that the sale, lease or exchange of all the property and assets of a corporation, including its good will, be authorized by a vote of two-thirds of each class of stock outstanding and entitled to vote. See P. Berry and 

Sons, Inc. v. Central Trust Co., 247 Mass. 241. By reason of this statute, conveyancers commonly require such a vote authorizing the sale of all the real estate of a corporation, although it may own and retain personal property. "The conveyance of real estate by the Connecticut corporation, being regular in form and under the corporate seal is presumed to be regular. Lamson v. Coulson, 234 Mass. 288, and cases cited at page 292. Nevertheless, that is only a rebuttable presumption, and it is open to proof that it was unauthorized." O'Brien v. O'Brien, 238 Mass. 403. "It seems that the deed of an insane person, who is under guardianship, is absolutely void. Wait v. Maxwell, 5 Pick. 

217, 220. If an insane person is not under guardianship, his deed is voidable only and not void." - Crocker, page 8.

The statement that a deed of an insane person under guardianship is absolutely void has generally been accepted as law, although the decision in Wait v. Maxwell, supra, concerned a deed given by a person while non compos mentis, but not under guardianship, although a petition for appointment had been filed in the Probate Court and a decree entered thereon which was void for lack of notice. The Court said that the deed in question conveyed a seisin to the grantee, defeasible by the grantor, her heirs or devisees, when entry should be made to avoid it, and also said that if the grantor had been actually under guardianship when she made the deed, it would have been otherwise, as the decree and letters of guardianship would have taken from her all capacity to convey. 

In Allis v. Billings, 6 Met. 415, it was held that a deed conveying land, executed by a person when non compos mentis, is voidable only, and not void, and may therefore be ratified by

him when he is of sane mind. The Court remarked that the term "void" as applicable to conveyances has not at all times been used with technical precision, nor correctly distinguished from "voidable," it being frequently introduced where the purpose is nothing further than to indicate that a contract was invalid and not binding in law. Continuing, the Court said: "But the distinction between the terms 'void' and 'voidable,' in their application to contracts, is often one of great practical importance; and whenever entire technical accuracy is required, the term 'void' can only be properly applied to those contracts that are of no effect whatsoever; such as are a mere nullity, and incapable of confirmation or ratification." 

In Brewster v. Weston, 235 Mass. 14, the Court said: "It is well settled in this Commonwealth that the deed of an insane person is ineffectual to convey a title to land, good against the grantor, or his heirs and devisees, unless it is confirmed by the grantor himself when of sound mind, or by his legally constituted guardian, or by his heirs or devisees. On principle there can be no sound distinction between the right of the guardian of an insane person to avoid a deed as against the grantee of the ward, and one to whom the land has been subsequently conveyed. By a parity of reasoning, the right of a subsequent purchaser for value to hold the land would seem to be no greater than that of the original grantee who took title without knowledge of the grantor's mental incapacity. The absolute and paramount right of infants and insane persons to avoid their contracts may be exercised against an innocent purchaser for value from the grantee, otherwise the law would fail to afford that measure of protection to the mentally helpless and incompetent which their condition justly requires. 

Hovey v. Hobson, 53 Maine, 451."

In Brewster v. Weston, supra, the Court also said: "While some confusion has arisen by reason of the use of the terms 'void' and 'voidable,' it is settled in this Commonwealth that the deed of an insane person is not void, but voidable, and may, after the grantor is restored to his right mind, be adopted and ratified." No distinction was made between the deed of an insane person under guardianship and of one not under

DEEDS. 

63 guardianship. See Hermanson v. Seppala, Adv. Sh.

1930, page 1427. 

Foss v. Twenty-five Associates, 239 Mass. 295, was a bill in equity brought on behalf of Foss by the conservator of his property, to enjoin the foreclosure of and to procure the cancellation of a mortgage of real estate delivered to defendant by the plaintiff at a time when his property was under conservatorship. The decree of the trial court, which was affirmed on appeal, was that the mortgage was "null and void, because of the fact that Foss was under conservatorship at the time of the delivery." The Court said: "It is the settled law of this 

Commonwealth that the deed of an insane person is voidable and the fact that the other party to the transaction acted fairly and without knowledge of the want of mental capacity does not affect the right of the guardian of the insane person to have the instrument annulled. Brewster v. Weston, 235 Mass. 14.

The same principle applies when a conservator has charge of the property of another. The person whose property is placed under the control of a conservator cannot sell or mortgage it while the appointment continues. The deed and contract are voidable, although the purchaser acted in good faith. 

The mortgage and note were voidable, and as no valid title passed to the defendant, the decree must be affirmed." In the case last referred to, the short approach to the decision arrived at would have been a statement that the mortgage was void. As this was not done it seems to be a fair inference that the Court was going on the theory that the deed of an insane person, whether under guardianship or not, is voidable but not void. 

The deed of a minor is voidable not void. Hovey v. Hobson, 53 Maine, 451; Ready . Pinkham, 181 Mass. 351. In the lastmentioned case the owner of a lot of land agreed to sell it to a minor and to build him a house upon it. The minor was to pay to the vendor a certain sum in cash and to give a mortgage for the balance of the purchase money to another person who was to furnish the money from time to time for building the house. These things were done, and the minor gave an assignment to the vendor of all the money to be furnished under the

mortgage. When the house was completed the minor moved into it, then, coming of age, conveyed it to the plaintiff, telling him that there was a mortgage upon it which he did not consider valid, because he made it when a minor. The defendant, having acquired the mortgage, proceeded to foreclose it, and the plaintiff brought a bill in equity to restrain him from doing so. Held, that the transaction was all one contract, and that the minor could not affirm part of it without ratifying the whole, and, having kept the land, could not avoid the mortgage given for the purchase money.

An infant's right to avoid a contract is personal to the infant, and, generally, his guardian cannot avoid. But in Benson v. Tucker, 212 Mass. 60, it was held that the appointment by an infant of a broker to buy and sell stocks for him might be disaffirmed by notice from his guardian to that effect. And it has been decided that the deed of an infant may be avoided after he becomes of age by a guardian appointed over him as a spendthrift. Chandler v. Simmons, 97 Mass. 508. If the infant dies before reaching his majority, his legal representatives or his heirs may exercise his right of avoidance. 

A deed of land and all the covenants therein made by a married woman before St. 1874, c. 184, are void if her husband did not join in the deed or assent thereto in writing. Wing v. 

Deans, 214 Mass. 546. See also Nickerson v. Nickerson, 235 

Mass. 348, where the Court said: "As to the land set off to the widow, . . . the reversion . . . belonged to the six children as tenants in common; and while their respective shares could be disposed of and the title transferred by a deed in proper form, it was the settled law of this Commonwealth at the time the conveyances were made (1865) that the separate real estate of a married woman could not be conveyed by her deed. At common law, the husband during coverture had full title to the rents and profits of the wife's real estate. had a freehold estate which he might convey. . . ceptions not here material, prior to St. 1874, c. 184; Gen. Sts. c. 108 (see now R. L. c. 153), the conveyance of land by deed of a married woman during her coverture transferred no title. 

The instrument was void and of no effect in law or equity." He

For discussion of the earlier acts enabling a married woman to deal with her separate estate, see Smith v. Bird, 3 Allen, 34.

23. DISSEISIN OF GRANTOR. 

See G. L. c. 183, § 7, which re-enacted St. 1891, c. 354.

Prior to this statute it was held that a disseisee could not, without entry and delivery of a deed on the land, convey any title which would be valid as against the disseisor and those claiming under him. Joyce v. Dyer, 189 Mass. 64. "When it is said that the deed of one, who is disseised, is void, it is intended only that it is inoperative to convey legal title and seisin, or a right of entry, upon which the grantee may maintain an action in his own name against one who has actual seisin. It is not void as a contract between the parties to it. The grantee may avail himself of it against the grantor by way of estoppel; or by suit upon the covenants; or may recover the land by an action in the name of the grantor. Although he has no right of entry, yet if by any lawful means he comes into possession, he may then avail himself of the title of his disseised grantor, and by uniting that to his own present possession, defeat recovery by the intermediate disseisor. And his title will also be made good against any one attempting to set up a deed from his grantor subsequent to his own." Farnum v. Peterson, 111 Mass. 148, 151.

In a suit in equity brought by a mortgagee of certain land, where defendant contends that plaintiff's title is defective because the deed under which his mortgagor claimed title had no seal affixed to it, but it appears that the mortgagor took possession of the property under the defective deed and lived in the only house upon the land at the time he made the mortgage, this is evidence that such mortgagor had become a disseisor of the whole premises and had a right to convey by mortgage whatever title he had as disseisor, and, on proof of a confirmatory deed from the grantor named in the defective deed to the mortgagor's grantor, executed after the hearings in the suit had begun, it could be found that the plaintiff was entitled to enforce whatever rights belong to a mortgagee. 

Bon v. Graves, 216 Mass. 440.

24. CONSIDERATION. 

Actual want or failure of consideration is not good ground, in the absence of fraud, for the avoidance of a deed by the grantor or by a subsequent purchaser from him. Beal v. 

Warren, 2 Gray, 447. It is only in the case of deeds which derive their effect from the Statute of Uses, as a bargain and sale or a covenant to stand seised that a consideration should be shown. Bartlett v. Bartlett, 4 Allen, 440.

It is settled in Massachusetts that the clause in a deed, acknowledging payment of the consideration, is a recital merely, which affords only prima facie proof of the facts, and may be controlled and rebutted by parole evidence. Clapp v. Tirrell, 20 Pick. 247; Cardinal v. Hadley, 158 Mass. 352. Consequently, it may be proved by oral evidence that the real consideration was greater or less than that named, or altogether different from it. "... it is believed that, however the cases may conflict, they all agree, in effect, in this: that it is not competent to prove that no consideration was paid, where one has been acknowledged in the deed, for the purpose of impeaching the validity of the deed, unless it is for the purpose of establishing fraud against the grantor. The true doctrine is stated in 

Grout v. Townsend, 2 Hill, 554, 557, that where a deed acknowledges the receipt of a consideration, the grantor and all claiming under him are estopped from denying that one was paid. They may disprove the payment for the purpose of recovering the consideration money, but they cannot do so for the purpose of destroying the effect and operation of the deed." -Washburn on Real Property, Vol. 3, page 328.

In Wilkinson v. Scott, 17 Mass. 249, it was held that an action will lie for the grantor of land against his grantee for a part of the consideration expressed in the deed to have been paid, which the defendant, by mistake, failed in fact to secure or pay. The Court said: "It is not a case within the statute of frauds: because it is not a contract for the sale of lands. That contract was executed and finished by the deed.

This is only a demand for money, arising out of that contract." 

In Boynton v. Rees, 8 Pick. 329, the Court said: "We also

DEEDS. think that the objection to the instrument (a grant of a right to build a dam), because no consideration is mentioned in it, is invalid. The deed itself imports a consideration, and to avoid it as a voluntary conveyance fraudulent against subsequent purchasers, the party objecting must prove that no consideration was given." 

25. RESULTING USE. 

"Before the Statute of Uses, if a person had conveyed his lands to another without any consideration, or declaration of the use of such conveyance, he (the grantor) became entitled to the use of the lands so conveyed. This doctrine was not altered by the statute, and therefore it became an established principle that where the legal seisin and possession of lands is transferred by any common-law conveyance, and no use is expressly declared, nor any consideration or evidence of intent to direct the use, such use shall result back to the original owner of the estate, for where there is neither consideration nor declaration of uses, nor any circumstances to show the intention of the parties, it cannot be supposed that the estate was intended to be given away." - Greenleaf's Cruise on Real 

Property, Vol. 1, page 326.

Our deeds in common use before the Short Forms Act carried a recital of consideration paid by the grantee, and the habendum was "his own use and behoof forever." See Gould v. Lynde, 114 Mass. 366.

In the short form deed, while it does not appear who paid the consideration, the form carries the language "for consideration paid," and, although the habendum, with its declaration that the conveyance is to the grantee's own use, is omitted, this omission appears to be taken care of by the final sentence of section 14 of chapter 183 of the General Laws. 

26. GRANTEE. "It is not essential to the validity of a grant that the grantees should be named; but if not named, they must be ascertained by description so as to be distinguished from all others, and any uncertainty in this respect will render the grant void."

Thomas v. Marshfield, 10 Pick. 364. In that case a grant to the inhabitants of a certain unincorporated neighborhood was held void because "the neighborhood is not defined with sufficient certainty, and its exact limits have not been ascertained."

In Hall v. Leonard, 1 Pick. 27, a grant to "the heirs of A. 

B.," he being living, was held void. 

In Byam v. Bickford, 140 Mass. 31, a deed to the "South 

Chelmsford Hall Associates" was held to be a good deed to the persons (as tenants in common) who constituted an unincorporated society which was known by that name. The number of members of the "South Chelmsford Hall Associates" was not large, there was no uncertainty as to their identity, and they were, in fact, associated for the purpose of erecting a hall on the land described in the deed in question. 

The rule in Byam v. Bickford should not be extended. See 

Land Court Decisions, page 55, and Hamblett v. Bennett, 6 Allen, 140. In the latter case the Court said: "The Circle (referring to the grantee named in a conveyance of land) was not a corporate body capable in its collective capacity of taking any estate in land. And though a grant to the Circle might, under some circumstances, be construed as a grant to the individual members of which it was composed, yet there seems to us nothing in the evidence which would authorize a jury to infer that such a grant was made. The Circle was a changeable and fluctuating body. The purpose to create a tenancy in common would be extraordinary and improbable. "9 It is common to insert the words "and his heirs and assigns" after the name of the grantee in a long form deed, but it is unnecessary, and was so even before the Short Forms Act. 

The habendum is the portion of a deed which should declare the quantity of the estate granted, whether for years, for life, in fee, etc., and if that refers to the "heirs" of the grantee, the deed passes a fee in all the estate described in the premises. 

Crocker, page 29.

It is provided by chapter 62 of the Acts of 1917 (G. L. c. 183, § 6) that every deed shall state the post-office address of the grantee, and whether the grantee is married or unmarried, but the omission of such information does not render a deed invalid.

DEEDS. 

69 G. L. c. 184, § 7: A conveyance or devise of land to two or more persons or to husband and wife, except a mortgage or a devise or conveyance in trust, shall create an estate in common and not in joint tenancy, unless it is expressed in such conveyance or devise that the grantees or devisees shall take jointly, or as joint tenants, or in joint tenancy, or to them and the survivor of them, or unless it manifestly appears from the tenor of the instrument that it was intended to create an estate in joint tenancy.

Where three persons, who owned a parcel of land as tenants in common, conveyed it to a purchaser who gave them a promissory note payable to their order and secured by a mortgage on the land, and the three died at different times and the amount due on the note was paid to the executor of the will of the one who died last, who thereupon discharged the mortgage, such executor cannot retain the whole of the money as property of the survivor, but must account for it in equity to the representatives of the other two payees, because, in spite of the exception of mortgages in G. L. c. 184, § 7, the title of the mortgagees must be held to have been that of tenants in common and not of joint tenants. Park v. Parker, 216 Mass. 405. 

In Dunn v. Lerman, 241 Mass. 555, the Court remarked that "it was held in Ready v. Kearsley, 14 Mich. 215, that a deed to a person or to his heirs is in the alternative, and that if he is dead, the title vests in his heirs or devisees." 

In Westminster National Bank v. Graustein, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 701, in discussing the contention of the defendant that the promissory note sued upon was illegal because it was secured by a mortgage of real estate in violation of the Federal statutes regulating national banks, the Court said: "In Kerfoot v. Farmers' & Merchants' Bank, 218 U. S. 281, 286-287, the Court said: 'In the absence of a clear expression of legislative intention to the contrary, a conveyance of real estate to a corporation for a purpose not authorized by its charter, is not void, but voidable, and the sovereign alone can object. 

Neither the grantor nor his heirs nor third persons can impugn it upon the ground that the grantee has exceeded its powers. 999 "A conveyance to partners will vest the estate in them as

tenants in common; but where an estate has been purchased with partnership funds and for partnership purposes, it will be considered in equity as held on an implied trust for the payment of the partnership creditors and for the settlement of the partnership accounts between the partners themselves, and until these objects are accomplished one partner cannot convey a good title to his own undivided share of the estate to one who takes with notice of the trust, nor, in case of his decease, will his widow, or heirs, or devisees, take any interest except subject to this trust." - Crocker, page 90. See Priestly v. Treasurer, etc., 230 Mass. 452.

The common law, as stated above, has been affected somewhat by the Uniform Partnership Act, so called, chapter 486 of the Acts of 1922. 

Section 8, subsection (3): Any estate in real property may be acquired in the partnership name. Title so acquired can be conveyed only in the partnership name. 

Subsection (4): A conveyance to a partnership in the partnership name, though without words of inheritance, passes the entire estate of the grantor unless a contrary intent appears. 

Section 25, subsection (2), paragraph (b): A partner's right in specific partnership property is not assignable except in connection with the assignment of the rights of all the partners in the same property.

Paragraph (d): On the death of a partner his right in the specific partnership property vests in the surviving partner or partners, except where the deceased was the last surviving partner, when his right in such property vests in his legal representative. Such surviving partner or partners, or the legal representative of the last surviving partner, has no right to possess the partnership property for any but a partnership purpose. 

Paragraph (e): A partner's right in specific partnership property is not subject to dower, curtesy, or allowances to widows, heirs, or next of kin.

Apparently, since the passage of this act, a conveyance of real property to partners for partnership purposes creates in effect a joint tenancy instead of a tenancy in common. This new tenancy is styled in the act "tenancy in partnership."

DEEDS. 

71 Section 10 of the act contains the following provisions as to the conveyance of real property of a partnership. (1) Where title to real property is in the partnership name, any partner may convey title to such property by a conveyance executed in the partnership name; but the partnership may recover such property unless the partner's act binds the partnership under the provisions of paragraph (1) of section nine, or unless such property has been conveyed by the grantee or a person claiming through such grantee to a holder for value without knowledge that the partner, in making the conveyance, has exceeded his authority. (2) Where title to real property is in the name of the partnership, a conveyance executed by a partner, in his own name, passes the equitable interest of the partnership, provided the act is one within the authority of the partner under the provisions of paragraph (1) of section nine. (3) Where title to real property is in the name of one or more but not all the partners, and the record does not disclose the right of the partnership, the partners in whose name the title stands may convey title to such property, but the partnership may recover such property if the partners' act does not bind the partnership under the provisions of paragraph (1) of section nine, unless the purchaser or his assignee is a holder for value, without knowledge. (4) Where the title to real property is in the name of one or more of all the partners, or in a third person in trust for the partnership, a conveyance executed by a partner in the partnership name, or in his own name, passes the equitable interest of the partnership, provided the act is one within the authority of the partner under the provisions of paragraph (1) of section nine. (5) Where the title to real property is in the names of all the partners a conveyance executed by all the partners passes all their rights in such property.

Occasionally the examination of a title discloses a deed to "A B, Trustee," without any explanation as to who A B is trustee for and without any declaration as to his powers or duties as such. Such a title commonly and properly is held to be unmarketable.

For such a case and such a decision,

see Cleval v. Sullivan, 258 Mass. 348. In that case, after objection made to the title, the alleged trustee executed and recorded a declaration of trust which stated that he purchased and held the property for the benefit of two corporations which were named. It was decided that the "trustee" did not have a "clear title" as called for by the agreement to sell which he had executed, and the proposed purchaser recovered his deposit. It is stated, however, in the opinion, that the declaration of trust executed by the trustee was not before the Court, and that the evidence was not reported in full.

It is conceivable that the decision would have been different if the terms of the trust and the evidence had been brought into the record. If the two corporations named were content, and also the person who conveyed to the "trustee," the Court might well have determined that the title was marketable, provided, of course, that the declaration of trust contained an adequate power to sell. It is arguable and there is some authority for the view that if A conveys to "B, Trustee" without more, and a person who desires to purchase the property applies to both the grantor and grantee for information and they agree as to the terms of the trust, or, as is often the case, A replies that he knows nothing about it, that he drew the deed in that form because B requested him to, such a purchaser is protected if the terms of the trust so disclosed to him empower B to sell and convey. However, this view is not the accepted one and as a result such titles are commonly taken to the Land Court for registration (as was done in the Sullivan case) where, after hearing all the facts, the Court can adjudicate as to the terms of the trust, if there is a real trust, and the powers of the trustee. Numerous cases have been before the Land Court wherein it developed that no trust in fact existed, the alleged trustee having taken title in such a manner hoping thus to prevent the property from being attached successfully by his creditors. A few years ago an attempt was made to cure this sort of a situation by legislation and to protect a purchaser in good faith from such "trustee," but the bill did not survive action by the committee which had it in charge.

DEEDS. In recent years there have been before the courts many cases involving conveyances of land to a husband and wife as tenants by the entirety." The following notes on this subject were prepared by Arthur V. Getchell, Esq., and are printed here with his permission: "The status of the tenancy by the entirety in Massachusetts has been so clarified by a series of recent decisions of our Supreme Judicial Court that a review of the cases bearing on such tenancies may be helpful alike to the student and to the practicing attorney or conveyancer. "In general it may be stated that this form of tenancy, when once created, is governed by the rules of the common law, unchanged by statute, the only effect of our legislative enactments being to overcome the common law presumption that a conveyance or devise to husband and wife created in them a tenancy by the entirety, so that now there is a presumption. that a tenancy in common is intended. Hoag v. Hoag, 213 

Mass. 50, 52. "At common law a conveyance of equal interests to two or more persons created in them, prima facie, a joint tenancy, and if the grantees or devisees were husband and wife, they took as tenants by the entirety. Shaw v. Hearsey, 5 Mass. 521.

By St. 1783, c. 52, it was enacted that the 'principle of survivorship shall no longer be in force in this commonwealth.' 

This act was repealed by St. 1785, c.. 62, which enacted that any conveyance or devise to two or more persons will create an estate in common unless it manifestly appears that a joint tenancy is intended; but conveyances and devises to husband and wife were specifically excluded from this provision. statute remained in this form until St. 1885, c. 237, when conveyances to husband and wife were included, thus making them presumptively tenants in common. And this is the law today under G. L. c. 184, § 7, that a conveyance to husband and wife (except a mortgage or devise or conveyance in trust) will create a tenancy in common unless it manifestly appears that. a joint tenancy is intended.

The "The statute of 1885, c. 237, did not abolish either joint: tenancies or tenancies by the entirety, nor in any way change:

the common-law characteristics of either. Hoag v. Hoag, 213 

Mass. 50, 52. "In Pray v. Stebbins, 141 Mass. 219, at 222 and 223, it is said that the 'statutes enabling a married woman to receive, hold, manage, and dispose of real and personal property in the same manner as if she were sole, cannot . . . be construed to apply to the estate by entireties of husband and wife, We think it appears that the Legislature intended that this peculiar tenancy should be preserved as it existed at common law.' "The same words which will ordinarily create a joint tenancy under G. L. c. 184, § 7, will, if the grantees or devisees are husband and wife, create a tenancy by the entirety (Hoag v. Hoag, 213 Mass. 50, 53; see also Shaw v. Hearsey, 5 Mass. 

521), if the husband and wife are named as such or known to be such to the grantor (Woodard v. Woodard, 216 Mass. 1); and in McLaughlin v. Rice, 185 Mass. 212, it was held that extrinsic evidence that grantees were husband and wife was admissible. Thus a deed to husband and wife, habendum to the grantees as joint tenants in joint tenancy, and to the survivor of them and their and such survivors heirs and assigns, to their own use and behoof forever,' will create a tenancy by the entirety. Hoag v. Hoag, 213 Mass. 50. An estate by the entirety may exist in personal property. Marble v. Treas. & 

Rec. Gen., 245 Mass. 504; Boland v. McKowen, 189 Mass. 

563; Phelps v. Simons, 159 Mass. 415. "Where the intention is unmistakably shown, husband and wife can hold real or personal property, even at common law, in joint tenancy as distinguished from an estate by the entirety. 

Woodard v. Woodard, 216 Mass. 1. "At common law, if land was granted or devised to three persons, two of whom were husband and wife, the husband and wife would, prima facie, take one-half, to hold by the entirety, and the third person would take the other half. Shaw v. Hearsey, 5 Mass. 521. "A tenancy by the entirety can exist only in husband and wife.
