Thus a deed to two persons who are not husband and wife, habendum to them as tenants by the entirety and not as tenants in common, will create a joint tenancy. Morris v.

DEEDS. 

75 McCarty, 158 Mass. 11. And a decree of divorce will dissolve a tenancy by the entirety and by operation of law create a tenancy in common in the former husband and wife. Bernatavicius v. Bernatavicius, 259 Mass. 486. "If an estate in common has vested in a man and woman before marriage, their subsequent marriage will not change the estate into a tenancy by the entirety. Walsh v. Young, 110 

Mass. 396, 398. "Since G. L. c. 184, § 7, expressly excepts mortgages, a note and mortgage executed to a husband and wife and their heirs. creates an estate by the entirety entitling the wife, surviving her husband, to collect as against the executor of the husband. 

Boland v. McKowen, 189 Mass. 563. See also Draper v. Jackson, 16 Mass. 480; Appleton v. Boyd, 7 Mass. 131. "Under St. 1918, c. 93 (now G. L. c. 184, § 8), and St. 1912, c. 304 (now G. L. c. 209, § 3), a man may convey to himself and his wife jointly, and such a deed will create an estate in joint tenancy, and not a tenancy by the entirety. Ames v. 

Chandler, 1929 Adv. Sh. page 133. In that case a man conveyed certain premises from himself as grantor to himself and his wife as joint tenants and not as tenants in common,' and the Court held that this created in them a joint tenancy, and not a tenancy by the entirety. (The Court in that case holds. that a man cannot convey to himself and his wife as tenants by the entirety, and adds by way of dictum: 'Doubtless [he] might convey land to himself and his wife as tenants in common.') 66 'A man may make a valid conveyance of his interest in a tenancy by the entirety to his wife through a third person. 

Donahue v. Hubbard, 154 Mass. 537. "Once a tenancy by the entirety is established, it is subject to all the rules of the common law. This is well set forth in Bernatavicius v. Bernatavicius, 259 Mass. 486, 487, as follows: 'The nature of a tenancy by the entirety is thoroughly established by our decisions. It is founded on the common law doctrine of the unity of husband and wife as constituting in law but one person. A conveyance to a husband and wife as tenants by the entirety creates one indivisible estate in them both and in the survivor, which neither

can destroy by any separate act. Both husband and wife are seised of such an estate per tout et non per my as one person, and not as joint tenants or tenants in common. Alienation by either the husband or the wife will not defeat the right of the survivor to the entire estate on the death of the other. 

There can be no severance of such estate by the act of either alone without the assent of the other [Palmer v. Treas. & Rec. 

Gen., 222 Mass. 263; Marble v. Treas. & Rec. Gen., 245 Mass. 

504], and no partition during their joint lives [Pease v. Whitman, 182 Mass. 363], and the survivor becomes seised as sole owner of the whole estate regardless of anything the other may have done. The tenancy by the entirety is essentially a joint tenancy modified by the common law theory of the unity of husband and wife. They do not take by moieties but by entireties. The characteristics of a tenancy by the entirety at common law continue unaffected by the modern statutes designed to ameliorate the rights of married women at common law and to render more flexible and individual the property rights of husband and wife.' "Statutes relating to the separate rights of married women have not changed the common law rights of the husband in such estates.' Raptes v. Pappas, 259 Mass., 37, at 38, and cases cited. "The right of a wife as tenant by the entirety cannot be affected by a statute, passed subsequently to the conveyance to her, reducing such estates to tenancies in common. Pease v. Whitman, 182 Mass. 363. "During coverture, right to possession is exclusively in the husband. Voigt v. Voigt, 252 Mass. 582. The husband may make a lease good against the wife during coverture, and thus determine a tenancy at will under the wife. Pray v. Stebbins, 141 Mass. 219." (In Cunningham v. Ganley, 1929 Adv. Sh. page 1211, it was held that a lease executed by a married woman, the title to the premises being in her and her husband as tenants by the entirety, was invalid as against the husband, he being "entitled to the rents and profits.") "A tenancy by the entirety may be taken on an execution

DEEDS. issued against the husband, and the purchaser at the sale on execution may maintain a writ of entry against the husband for possession and title, and against the wife for possession but not title. If the wife survives the husband, she will have an absolute title with the right to possession; if the husband survives the wife, the purchaser's title will be absolute. Raptes v. Pappas, 259 Mass. 37. "The wife, however, has no interest which she can sell, since at common law the deed of a woman under coverture was void; and there is no interest in the wife which is subject to attachment. Licker v. Gluskin, 1929 Adv. Sh. page 127. "It has been held that, if an estate is held by husband and wife as tenants by the entirety, and, on a conveyance by her husband, the wife joins in the deed solely to release her right of dower (even though the release is nugatory as such, there being no right of dower in such an estate), this will not defeat her estate in the land if she survives her husband. Wales v. Coffin, 13 Allen, 213; Pierce v. Chace, 108 Mass. 254, 258. "Under St. 1909, c. 490, Part IV, § 1, as amended by St. 

1912, c. 678, § 1, subjecting to a tax all property passing by intestate succession, a widow was not subject to a tax on her interest in a tenancy by the entirety on the death of her husband. 'When a tenancy by the entirety is created, the husband and wife take the estate as one person, and they take but one estate. In view of the nature of such an estate, on the death of either husband or wife no beneficial interest accrues to the other by survivorship so as to create succession, and so no part of the estate was subject to the tax. 

Upon the death of the testator no estate in the property in question passed to his widow. It belonged to her from the time when the tenancy by the entirety was created. In the event that she survived her husband, upon his death she took no new title by survivorship, but held under the deed by virtue of which she was originally seized of the whole.' Palmer v. Treas. & Rec. Gen., 222 Mass. 263, 265. Subsequently to this decision St. 1916, c. 268, § 1, subjected to a succession tax any interest which shall arise or accrue by survivorship in any form of joint ownership in which a deceased joint

owner had contributed any part of the property or its purchase price. This statute (now G. L. c. 65, § 1) has not been construed by the Supreme Judicial Court. "The Federal Estate Tax, however, is specifically imposed on the estate of a decedent to the extent of the interest held jointly or as tenants in the entirety by the decedent and any other person.' 

27. GRANTING WORDS. 

The granting words of the warranty deed are "give, grant, bargain, sell and convey." "Give and grant" are common law words of conveyance, and were used from the earliest times in deeds of feoffment and in deeds of grant. "Bargain and sell" invokes the assistance of the Statute of Uses. The granting words of a quitclaim deed are "remise, release and forever quitclaim.'

A warranty during the life of the grantor was, in the absence of express covenants, formerly implied in a feoffment from the word "give." Dow v. Lewis, 4 Gray, 468.

Section 14 of S. F. A. provides: "In a conveyance of real property the word 'grant' shall be a sufficient word of conveyance without the use of the words 'give, bargain, sell and convey,' and no covenant shall be implied from the use of the word 'grant.' ووو For cases discussing the effect to be given to certain words of conveyance, see the following cases: in Jamaica Pond Aqueduct Co. v. Chandler, 9 Allen, 159, the word "lease" was held sufficient to convey a base fee; in Wainwright v. Sawyer, 150 Mass. 168, the words "sell and assign" were held sufficient words of conveyance by a son of all his interest in the estate of his father which was held in trust for him under the provisions of the father's will; in Sims . Pierce, 157 Mass. 52, "Betsey Pratt undertook not only to 'lease, demise and let' the estate, but also to 'convey' it. 'Convey' is certainly as strong a word of grant as the words 'sell and assign,' used in 

Wainwright v. Sawyer." In Bridge v. Wellington, 1 Mass. 219, the granting clause was omitted altogether, but there was, following the description, an habendum in fee to the person who was described as having paid the consideration, and this was held a good deed. The editor of the early reports said, in a

DEEDS. note to Bridge v. Wellington, that the deed was clearly void, but it has been cited four times with approval (although not on similar facts). See the three cases above discussed and

Goodman v. Telfer, 230 Mass. 157. The reasoning in Bridge v. 

Wellington is not followed everywhere, but is probably good law in Massachusetts.

In Webb v. Mullins, 78 Ala. 113, it was said: "At common law, a deed must contain words of grant, release or transfer, in order to pass the legal title to the land designed to be conveyed. The title to land can be transferred from one person to another only by apposite and appropriate language. 

It was not the intention of the statute to dispense with the use of any words whatever operative to convey. By the statute the duty is imposed upon the courts to liberally construe the words employed in the conveyance as words of transfer, and give them effect and operation according to the intention of the grantor, to be collected from the entire instrument. 

There must, however, be some words intended as words of conveyance. They cannot be supplied by judicial interpolation."

In Justice v. Soderlund, 225 Mass. 320, which concerned a deed by executors given by virtue of a power to sell contained in a will, the Court said: "In the deed the executors used the words 'remise, release, and forever quitclaim.' They did not use the word 'grant.' Although the instrument was made before St. 1912, c. 502, it was sufficient in form. It was unnecessary to employ this particular word." 

28. DESCRIPTION. 

"The Northrup deed of the 'spring of water' conveyed a fee in so much of the land out of which the spring issued as was necessary for the reasonable use of the spring itself.” 

Tinker v. Bessel, 213 Mass. 74. "It is a general rule of construction that whenever land is occupied and improved by buildings or other structures designed for a particular purpose, which comprehends its practical beneficial use and enjoyment, it is aptly designated and conveyed by a term which describes the purpose to which it is thus appropriated. The grant of a ‘house,' ‘barn,' or ‘mill,

or 'cottage' or 'wharf' is a familiar instance of the use of such terms, and the conveyance in such a form passes by implication and comprehends the land under the structure and the land adjacent thereto so far as necessary to its use and commonly used with it not as an appurtenance but as parcel." 

Harvey v. Sandwich, 256 Mass. 379. "It is a general rule of construction that the grant of a house carries with it title to all the land under the house, including the land on which the building stands and the land under the projecting eaves. ... The stone foundation upon which the brick building rested was a part of the building.' 

Ansin v. Taylor, 262 Mass. 159. 

"We are unable to perceive any question of law in the various requests for rulings presented by the petitioner. They all appear to us to relate to matter of fact. Engineering theories as to the extent of the variation of the compass, or magnetic, north from the true north, and as to the correct method of calculation of courses contained in instruments of divers dates. . . present no question of law. Where a line or lines described by specified courses and distances may fall upon the face of the earth or may be delineated upon a plan, is not a question of law but a matter of fact." Crawford v. 

Roloson, 262 Mass. 527. 

Where the word "about" is used in a deed in connection with a correct statement of distance it is treated as having no effect. Roberts v. Welch, 192 Mass. 278.

The words "all my right, title and interest in and to" are sometimes inserted before the description of the premises, and it has been held that in such cases all subsequent covenants, which in terms apply, according to the usual form, to the "granted premises" are to be construed as referring not to the parcel of land described by metes and bounds, but to the right, title and interest, whatever that may be, of the grantor therein. 

Hoxie . Phinney, 16 Gray, 332. See Crocker, page 34. 

And if the grantor in a deed in which these words are used, has in addition to a certain vested interest a further contingent interest, the grant will operate to pass the vested interest only, and a warranty will be construed as referring to such vested interest alone, and will not extend to the contingent interest or

DEEDS. operate upon it even by way of estoppel. Blanchard v. Brooks, 12 Pick. 47, 65; Wight v. Shaw, 5 Cush. 56, 64. In the latter case, a number of brothers and sisters were tenants in common of a vested remainder in certain land. One of the sisters, during the lifetime of the life tenant, made a quitclaim deed of “all the right, title and estate" which she had in said land. Afterwards, this grantor, by the decease of three of her tenants in common followed by the death of the life tenant, became the owner (by inheritance) with the other surviving tenant in common of three-sixths of the estate. Held, that her deed passed nothing more than the one-sixth to which she was then entitled.

But where, having used terms purporting to convey an estate described by metes and bounds, the grantor added the words "meaning and intending hereby to convey all my right, title and interest in and to" the said estate, it was held that the covenants applied to the land described and not to the grantor's interest therein. Hubbard v. Apthrop, 3 Cush. 419.

Where a deed purported to convey "all the right, title, interest or estate" in a parcel of land, adding "intending hereby to convey all the title or estate in said premises which was conveyed or passed to the grantor by" a certain deed, it was intimated as the opinion of the court that the covenants applied to the title conveyed by the deed referred to, and not merely to the title of the grantor at the time when his covenant was made. Russ v. Alpaugh, 118 Mass. 369.

It was held in Adams v. Cuddy, 13 Pick. 460 (this was a deed of "all the right and title to the land I have in South Boston, formerly a part of the estate of James Blake, deceased"), that where these words (all my right, title and interest in and to) are thus introduced in a deed, the grantee takes a title subject to all prior unregistered conveyances and incumbrances of the grantor, even those of which the grantee has no notice. In that case the deed contained no particular description by metes and bounds of the parcel conveyed, and it has since been held in Woodward v. Sartwell, 129 Mass. 210, 215, and Dow v. Whitney, 147 Mass. 1, 6, that the rule in

Adams v. Cuddy is not to be extended to any deed containing a specific description.

It has been suggested that the reasoning on which this rule is based should apply to all conveyances by quitclaim deeds, which deeds are by statute, "sufficient to convey all the estate which could lawfully be conveyed by deed of bargain and sale,” but Woodward v. Sartwell, 129 Mass. 210; Mansfield v. Dyer, 131 Mass. 200; and Connolly, Petitioner, 168 Mass. 201, 203, appear to dispose of this question, and to show that a quitclaim deed is to be considered just as effectual in this respect as a deed of bargain and sale.

There is no mystery in the rule in Adams v. Cuddy if it be considered from the angle of the description in the deed of grantor's "right, title and interest." Suppose the grantor inherited from James Blake five parcels of land in South Boston, and had previously conveyed one of said parcels to X by a deed properly executed and delivered, but never recorded. 

Title to this parcel passed to X on delivery of the deed. Accordingly, when his grantor makes the later deed of "all his right, title and interest," such description did not include the parcel which he had conveyed to X, because he had remaining no "right, title or interest" to that parcel. As the Court said: "Where a grantee takes by so indefinite a description as the right which the grantor has, he must take the risk of the grantor's right."

In Fitzgerald v. Libby, 142 Mass. 235, the description in a mortgage was: "All the land by me owned and situate in my new city, so called, in said Lowell, being land situate in said.

Lowell westerly of the Boston and Lowell Railroad, and southerly of the Chelmsford Road, so called: for boundaries and description reference is made to deeds to me recorded in said registry of deeds." In fact, the mortgagor had previously conveyed a portion of one parcel by a deed which was not recorded until after the mortgage was recorded, and it was held that such portion did not pass by the mortgage. The 

Court said: "There is no specific description of land, and no specific designation of deeds where a description can be found. 

Where a conveyance is made with no particular description of the land, the words 'all the land by me owned' are more naturally understood to mean 'all the land now owned by me,' which is equivalent to 'all the land which I have not

DEEDS. heretofore conveyed,' and such, we think, is the true construction of the present mortgage.' 

But the Court, having in mind the importance of the recording acts, and the policy of the law that a purchaser of land, by examination in the registry of deeds, may ascertain the title of the seller, held that the rule in Adams v. Cuddy did not apply to let in unrecorded prior conveyances as against a deed of the grantor's "right, title and interest" if the land to be conveyed is therein specifically described.

In order that title to land may pass by deed, it must be described so that it may be identified. Descriptions may be classified as follows: (1) descriptions by metes and bounds; (2) general descriptions which omit any statement of boundaries and distances. Illustration of (2): "The farm where I now live." Hastings v. Hastings, 110 Mass. 280. Such a description as last mentioned calls for extrinsic evidence to explain it, and may as a practical matter render the title unmarketable, but between persons having equal knowledge of the extent of the farm where the grantor lives, such a description is just as effective as a description by metes and bounds. 

Descriptions by metes and bounds may be divided into two classes: (1) Those wherein the premises are described at length, and (2) Those in which such a description is incorporated by reference to (a) another deed or (b) a plan. See Allen v. Taft, 6 Gray, 552, for a case of incorporation of description in a levy on execution by reference to a will, where the land involved was described at length. See also Lovejoy v. Lovett, 124 Mass. 

270; Foss v. Crisp, 20 Pick. 121; Cassidy v. Charlestown Bank, 149 Mass. 325.

In Lovejoy v. Lovett a description by metes and bounds was followed by the language "being the same premises conveyed to me by Ezra Holden, by deed dated May 7, 1829." It was held that the expression quoted, following as it did a description by metes and bounds, was no more than a reference to the source of the grantor's title, and did not have the effect of incorporating the description in the deed referred to. 

In Cassidy v. Charlestown Bank, a deed of land contained in its granting part a clear and exact description, by metes and bounds, of a lot adjoining that of the grantor's, followed by

the clause, "being the same premises formerly conveyed by grantors to O. W. M. by deed dated Sept. 1, 1868, and recorded with S. Deeds, book 937, page 183," which deed correctly described the land intended to be conveyed. Held, that the grantee did not acquire a good title to the grantor's land. 

The Court said: "The deed to Merriam referred to correctly describes the Locke estate. If we could resort to external evidence, it might appear that Locke's intention was to convey the lot owned by him. But we must look for the intent in the deed itself, and if the terms of the deed do not express the intent, it must fail. The description in the granting part of the deed is explicit and unambiguous, and implies an intention to convey the Colesworthy lot. The words descriptive of the estate intended to be conveyed are wholly without ambiguity. 

The subsequent clause, referring to the deed to Merriam, does not create any ambiguity in the prior description, but is repugnant to it. This clause was not inserted for the purpose of describing the lot conveyed." "Where, as in this case, the deed conveys one estate by a particular description, such description must prevail over a subsequent general reference to a prior deed made for another purpose, and such reference must be rejected. Melvin v. Props. of 

Locks & Canals, 5 Met. 15.” 

In Foss v. Crisp, "Seven lot case," where a deed from V to

P, conveying several parcels of land described by metes and bounds, contained the clause, "meaning and intending hereby to convey all the real estate which I derived under the deeds recorded in Suffolk Registry of Deeds [citing several deeds by book and leaf] to all which deeds reference is to be had," it was held that a parcel of land conveyed to V by a deed thus referred to, and not otherwise described in the deed from V to P than by such reference, passed by the deed from V to P. 

Whether or not the language "meaning and intending hereby to convey, etc.," as used in Foss v. Crisp would alone have had the effect of incorporating the descriptions in the deeds thereafter enumerated, has been something of a moot question. The purists insist upon using "meaning hereby to convey and hereby conveying." This question does not necessarily arise. in Foss v. Crisp, as in that case, following the enumeration of

DEEDS. certain deeds, there appeared the language "to all of which reference is to be had." This language would incorporate the descriptions in the deeds referred to without the assistance of the "meaning and intending" expression. See Abbott v. Frazier, 240 Mass. 586, which is referred to in the following section. 

A description so incorporated by reference may build up the prior description (Foss v. Crisp), but it can never cut it down. 

Whiting v. Dewey, 15 Pick. 428. The following rule of construction must be kept in mind in considering these cases: "Where there is a doubt as to the construction of a deed poll, it shall be taken most favorably for the grantee." Melvin v. 

Proprietors, etc., 5 Met. 15. The soundness of this rule without qualification is generally accepted, but in Aldrich v. Bay 

State Construction Co., 186 Mass. 489, the Court said: "The defendant contends that when the true meaning of a written instrument is doubtful it must be construed most strongly against the person using the doubtful language. This doctrine is sometimes applied in favor of a party who has been misled into advancing money. See Barney v. Newcomb, 9 Cush. 46, 56. But it should be applied only in the last resort, when all other rules of construction fail. Boston v. Richardson, 13 

Allen, 146."

It is a general principle that a grant from the sovereign power is to be construed strictly against the grantee. Nothing will be included in the grant except what is granted expressly or by clear implication. Stoneham v. Commonwealth, 249 

Mass. 112.

It has been held that the evidence of the subsequent acts of the parties is admissible for the purpose of determining the effect of a deed, but strictly such evidence is admissible only where an uncertainty exists. In Foster v. Lee, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 943, in deciding that certain language resulted merely in the reservation of an easement, it was said that the construction apparently placed upon the deed by the parties was not competent to prove its meaning because the deed did not present a question of doubt or ambiguity. 

In Pollard v. Ketterer, 221 Mass. 317, one Pollard acquired title to two adjoining hotel properties in Boston known as Hotel Haymarket and Ketterer's Hotel. The buildings had a

66 common partition or party wall, the center of which before the merger in ownership of Pollard was the division line. By deeds of even date and record, Pollard conveyed the properties to different persons. The deed covering Ketterer's Hotel described "A certain parcel of land with the buildings thereon now known as Ketterer's Hotel . . . and bounded," etc. Then followed a description by metes and bounds wherein one bound was given as "by land this day conveyed by me to Frederick 

H. Pollard (Hotel Haymarket)," and which concluded as follows: "be all said above measurements more or less." The fact that the buildings were described as "now known as Ketterer's Hotel" is not referred to in the decision. The deed of the Hotel Haymarket included a bound "by land this day conveyed by me to Alex H. Wood et al. (Ketterer's Hotel),” and the same 'more or less" clause as above. These deeds were executed in 1897, and upon applying the distances given in each to the premises it developed that the deeds gave to the Hotel Haymarket and took from Ketterer's Hotel a strip of land shutting off substantially the entrance from the public street on one side, as well as depriving the last-mentioned property of the support of the common wall, and of four feet of the front rooms on the upper floor. In 1909, the owner of 

Hotel Haymarket brought a writ of entry against the owner of Ketterer's Hotel to recover the strip of land above mentioned. In the Land Court there was a decision for the tenant. 

Demandant appealed to the Superior Court for a jury trial. 

The jury, as directed by the judge, returned a finding in favor of the demandant; and the tenant alleged exceptions. Exceptions overruled. The Court said that evidence offered by the tenant to the effect that the common grantor intended that the boundary line should be coincident with the center of the common wall was inadmissible because the wording of the instruments was unambiguous and all the calls could be satisfied. Also: "The descriptions are by metes and bounds, and the record shows that upon application to the land the measurements give to each grantee the parcel described and conveyed. If the words 'or however otherwise bounded, measured or described' and 'be all said above measurements more or less' are common to each instrument, they are not the equiva-

DEEDS. lent of a general description, for no reference is made to other conveyances or sources of title. . . . But, even if susceptible of such effect, the tenant is not helped, as they would not exclude from the operation of the demandant's deed the parcel included in the particular description." 

Ovans v. Castrucci, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 1393, was a suit in equity to compel defendant to remove a portion of his dwelling house (No. 17 Pratt Street) from the plaintiff's land. The owner of a parcel of land having a frontage of 105 feet on Pratt Street first conveyed the plaintiff's land described as “land and the buildings thereon situated on Pratt Street . . . the building now on the premises now numbered 21 and 23 on said street, bounded and described as follows: Northerly by 

Pratt Street, 75 feet; . . . containing 6,000 square feet more or less be any or all measurements and contents more or less." 

At the time, the house numbered 17 was standing on the remaining lot of the grantor and a division fence separated the lots, leaving to the plaintiff's house a lot 60 feet in width, and to the defendant's house a lot 45 feet in width. The houses were located with clear reference to these lots of 60 and 45 feet in width. Later the common owner conveyed to defendant a parcel of land bounded "Northerly by Pratt Street about 

50 feet... containing about 4,750 square feet more or less be any or all measurements more or less. If the plaintiff was entitled to the full 75 feet called for by his deed, he owned a portion of the house claimed by defendant whose lot would necessarily be reduced to 30 feet in width. 

The bill was dismissed. 22 

Apparently the decision rests upon the following points: 

1. The language "be any or all measurements and contents more or less" appearing in the deed of plaintiff's land, following Blaney v. Rice, 20 Pick. 62. The Court said that this phrase imported into every length of line given in the description the words "more or less." In Blaney v. Rice, it was held that the words of description "85 feet more or less," if there was nothing in other parts of the deed, or the subject to which it applied, to control or explain them, especially where the grant was of a parcel measured out of the grantor's own larger tract, would be equivalent to 85 feet absolutely, but that the words

66 'more or less" may be considered equivalent to a suggestion, that either there is some monument, abuttal or line there, or as an intimation that one is intended to be placed there, to stand as the line intended, in place of the absolute admeasurement. In the instant case, the Court was of the opinion that an inspection of the premises would have shown that the division fence was a monument standing "as the line intended, in place of the absolute admeasurement." 

2. The conduct of the parties. Neither of the two predecessors in title to the plaintiff ever contended that his lot extended beyond the division fence and never occupied any of the land beyond the fence, and defendant rebuilt the fence without objection. 

3. The deed from the common owner to plaintiff's predecessor referred to the "building now on the premises, now numbered 21 and 23 on said street." The Court said: "There is nothing in the deed indicating that a part of the house numbered 17 Pratt Street was included in the conveyance. The curtilage conveyed was the enclosed space surrounding the building numbered 21 and 23 Pratt Street. . . . this would appear to be the dominating part of the description." 

The writer has difficulty in reconciling this decision with that in Pollard v. Ketterer, supra.

Taking up the controlling features in the Ovans case and comparing the facts in the Pollard case it appears: 

1. In the Pollard case, the deeds contained the "more or less" provision and the buildings in question had a common partition or party wall, the center of which before the merger in ownership of the grantor was the division line. 

2. In the Pollard case the deeds were executed in 1897, but the writ was not brought until 1909. Presumably Ketterer's 

Hotel had been occupied in its entirety in the meantime. 

3. In the Pollard case, although not mentioned in the decision, the deed from the common owner to the tenant's predecessors in title, described "A certain parcel of land with the buildings thereon now known as Ketterer's Hotel," etc.

DEEDS. 

29. DOUBLE DESCRIPTIONS. 

The incorporation of a second description by reference, or the not uncommon practice of following a description by metes and bounds by a general description or explanatory clause, results in a double description, and necessitates a determination as to which controls. For an illustration of "general description" mentioned above, see Crabtree v. Miller, 194 Mass. 123, in which in a lease following a brief but certain description of the buildings, the lessor continued, "meaning thereby all the real estate I now own on Washington Street, excepting the building known as the Park Theatre." And the Court said: "The general rule is that where real property is described by a particular and definite description, the addition of an inconsistent general description does not enlarge the grant." 

The practical rule as to the construction to be given to those deeds which contain "double descriptions," if a rule may be gathered from the cases, is to first divide the description into its two parts, giving to each any words or expressions which fairly belong to both. Then if one of the descriptions be exact and certain and the other be uncertain, the more certain will control. But if both of two descriptions are equally certain we are then bound by the rule that a deed is to be construed most strictly against the grantor, and accordingly, we give the grantee the benefit of the description which is most advantageous to him.

In Stefanick v. Fortona, 222 Mass. 83, the owner of a rectangular parcel of land known as the Busby lot, which was bounded southeasterly by Hoosac Street and measured 66 feet on that street and 198 feet in depth, conveyed the northerly portion thereof described as measuring 66 feet by 107 feet. 

The point of beginning at the southeasterly corner of the granted premises was given as a point 91 feet from a stone monument standing in the northerly line of Hoosac Street, the northwesterly and southwesterly corners were described as marked by iron pins, and all angles were given as right angles. 

The description continued: "Meaning to convey the northerly part of the Busby lot 107 x 66 feet." It developed that the surveyor who marked out the lot made an error in fixing the

southeasterly corner of the Busby lot, and placed the stone monument 18 inches north of the true street line. The distance from the iron pins set in the ground at the southeasterly and southwesterly corners of the granted premises to the northerly line of the Busby lot was actually 105.5 feet instead of 107 feet as called for by the deed. It was held that the 91 feet which the first boundary called for ran from the stone monument, not from the true northerly line of Hoosac Street, and that the other three corners were fixed by monuments. Accordingly the deed conveyed a lot 66 feet by 105.5 feet. The Court said: "The particular description of the land conveyed was not enlarged by the concluding words, 'Meaning to convey the northerly part of the Busby lot 107 x 66 feet.' That was but a repetition of the distances stated in the earlier particular part of the description." 

In Abbott v. Frazier, 240 Mass. 586, the boundary line between the lands of plaintiff and defendant was in dispute thus involving title to a narrow gore of land. Three deeds covering a large parcel including plaintiff's land were before the Court. 

These deeds were given by Sayre to Reed. Each contained an express reference to the recorded deed to the grantor, this fact being noted and discussed by the Court. In the deed from 

Reed to the plaintiff, the granted premises were described by metes and bounds referring to a monument located at each corner of the lot (viz., three stakes and one iron pipe), and the description concluded as follows: "Meaning and intending to convey the easterly portion of the Sayre property, SO called." From the fact that this description was discussed in detail as bearing on the contentions of the defendant, one must infer that one of the monuments marking the easterly boundary of the granted premises was located westerly of the easterly boundary of the "Sayre property." 'Sayre property." The Court said: "The recital Meaning and intending to convey the easterly portion of the Sayre property so called,' shows in this deed as in the previous deeds the intention of the grantor to convey her title to this portion vested in her by the deed of Alice P. 

Sayre of October 22, 1918." This gives to the language meaning and intending" full force and effect as incorporating another description without the assistance of any other 66

DEEDS. words, but it is difficult to reconcile this decision with that in the Stefanick case. If the general description in the Stefanick case was controlled by the monuments referred to in the particular description, it is difficult to understand why the general description in the Abbott case was not controlled in like manner. The writer believes that the decision on this point in the Abbott case is sound and that the earlier decision is unsound in this respect. The difficulty may lie in the fact that in the Abbott case the Court seems to have assumed that the general description had the effect of incorporating one of the Sayre deeds by reference, although in fact nothing was said about that deed, the reference being to the "Sayre property so called." This is certainly no more definite than "the northerly part of the Busby lot 107 x 66 feet." Indeed, the language last quoted standing by itself would be a perfectly good description of land, provided the location and dimensions of the Busby lot were known and understood by both parties to the deed as appears from the decision to have been the case. 

30. 

BOUNDARIES. 

The following general rule regarding boundaries was laid down by Judge Gray in Boston v. Richardson, 13 Allen, 146, 154: 

Whenever land is described as bounded by other land, or by a building or structure, the name of which, according to its legal and ordinary meaning, includes the title in the land of which it has been made a part, as as a house, a mill, a wharf, or the like, — the side of the land or structure referred to as the boundary is the limit of the grant; but when the boundary line is simply by an object, whether natural or artificial, the name of which is used in ordinary speech as defining a boundary, and not as describing a title in fee, and which does not in its description or nature include the earth as far down as the grantor owns, and yet which has width, as in the case of a way, a river, a ditch, a wall, a fence, a tree, or a stake and stones, then the center of the thing so running over or standing on the land is the boundary of the lot granted."

31.

BOUNDARIES ON WAYS. 

At first it seems to have been assumed that it was the ordinary intent of the parties to convey to the side line only of a street or way, and this was the rule of construction in the early cases. Tyler v. Hammond, 11 Pick. 193. 

Mr. Crocker states two general rules, as follows: “(1) If a monument, such as a stake or tree standing at the side of a way, is mentioned as the point to and from which the bounding lines of the granted premises run, the whole of the way is excluded. Sibley v. Holden, 10 Pick. 249. (2) If, however, the land is bounded on or by the way without any reference to such a monument, the conveyance is held to extend to the middle line of the way. Newhall v. Ireson, 8 Cush. 595." Mr. 

Crocker's rule No. 1 is now of doubtful value.

In Sibley v. Holden, 10 Pick. 249, the description starts from a physical monument on the side of the road, goes around locus "to said road," and then by the road to the place of beginning. 

There was, in other words, a physical monument at one end of the boundary on the road and none at the other. The court held that the fee and soil of the road was excluded. 

In Phillips v. Bowers, 7 Gray, 21, the description ran to a stake by land laid out for a street, and thence southerly by the street. In this case there was likewise a monument at one end of the boundary on the street but none at the other, and the court held that the fee and soil of the way was excluded. 

The only alternative in these cases would have been a diagonal line from the middle of the street back to a monument at the side. 

In Everett v. Fall River, 189 Mass. 513, the description began on the line of Barnes Street, but at no physical monument, went around the lot to Barnes Street, and thence westerly by said street to the point of beginning, and the Court said the grantee's title extended to the middle line of the street. 

Pinkerton v. Randolph, 200 Mass. 24: In this case the description began on the northerly line of the avenue, and ran westerly by said avenue, and the Court held that this included the fee and soil of the avenue to the middle line. 

And in this last case the Court said that Everett v. Fall

DEEDS. River "must be regarded as directly overruling Sibley v. 

Holden." As a matter of fact, the cases are not exactly alike, as in Sibley v. Holden the description began at a physical monument, while in Everett v. Fall River, and again in Pinkerton v. Randolph, the description commenced on the line of the way, but at no physical monument. These later cases are close to O'Connell v. Bryant, 121 Mass. 557, in which the description began "on the southerly side of the road" and ran "on said road." And, in the latter case, the Court held that the description carried to the center of the road and directed attention to the fact that the clause first quoted did not refer to any fixed monument at the edge of the road. 

A boundary in a deed "Beginning two public ways, "thence running at the junction of" by" one of the ways, carries to the center of the way, although by the lineal measurements stated the boundary would end at the side of the way. Salem v. Salem Gas Light Co., 241 Mass. 438.

Apparently the rule now is that a boundary "on" or "by" a way will necessarily carry to the middle line, notwithstanding the side lines come up to physical monuments in the ground in the side lines of the way, and that no language short of a boundary on or by the line of the way will exclude the fee and soil, unless by a subsequent paragraph following the description the intention not to convey any part of the fee and soil of the way is explicitly stated. 

For many years the conveyancers had gone on the theory that the early rule of construction, whereby a deed of land bounding "on" or "by" a way did not include any part of the way (see Tyler v. Hammond, supra), had been overruled by the later decisions, and that all such boundaries were to be construed as carrying to the middle line of the way regardless of the date when written. Naturally the profession was somewhat disturbed by the decision in the case of Brown v. Peabody, 228 Mass. 52, wherein the Court said: "When these ways were established and before 1851, it was well settled in this Commonwealth that a deed of land abutting upon a way did not convey a fee in any part of the way. 

Tyler v. Hammond, 11 Pick. 193, 213; O'Linda v. Lothrop, 21 Pick. 292, 295. . In determining whether Joseph Poor,

Jr., the original grantor of the lots, intended to convey to any of his grantees a fee in the ways in question, the state of the law when the deeds were given may be considered in determining the meaning and purpose of the words used."

This disturbing theory was modified by the decision in Erickson v. Ames, 264 Mass. 436, wherein the issue was whether or not a partition in 1822 which bounded the lots set off "by" and "on" a private way included the fee and soil of the way. 

The petitioners, who contended that it did not, relied chiefly on the statement in Brown v. Peabody, which is quoted above. 

In the present case, in deciding that the partition included the fee and soil of the way, the Court denied the soundness of the statement referred to as an absolute rule of law. The following quotations from the opinion state the reasoning of the Court: "The significance of words takes color from the time and circumstances in which they were used, and the intent of parties is almost always a matter of fact. . . . But in the more recent decisions the general rule has been repeatedly declared, and must now be regarded as the settled law of the Commonwealth, overruling whatever is irreconcilable in the earlier cases, that a deed bounding land generally on a highway, with no restrictive or controlling words, conveys the grantor's title in the land to the middle of the highway. 

The underlying principle on which they all rest is that the intent of the parties in each instance was ascertained from the words used in the written instrument interpreted in the light of all the attendant facts. . . . Expressions in some of the earlier cases, which bear a contrary aspect, are to be taken as not essential to the point decided and hence not binding upon the court. Those expressions may well have been the justifiable basis of a more or less widespread opinion in 1822 as to the state of the law respecting a case like the present. That view as to the state of the law must be given weight as an important though not decisive factor in ascertaining the intent of the parties to a particular instrument. It has been said that such state of the law must be 'cautiously used.' Hamlen v. Keith, 171 Mass. 77, 79. But it may permit a just result not otherwise possible. Brown v. Peabody, 228 Mass. 52." "The rule by which the mention of a way as a boundary in

DEEDS. a conveyance of land is presumed to mean the middle of the way, if the way belongs to the grantor, is not an absolute rule of law, like the rule in Shelley's case, but is merely a principle of interpretation adopted for the purpose of finding the true meaning of the words used." Crocker v. Cotting, 166 Mass. 183. 

Where a partial release of a mortgage of real estate describes the land to which the release applies as bounded "by" or "on" a private way, the land to the middle of the way is released, if there is nothing in the release to show a contrary intention. 

McCarthy v. Everett, 234 Mass. 231.

In Gould v. Wagner, 196 Mass. 270, the owner of a lot of land 90 feet wide, after having built a passageway 5 feet wide running down a steep hill along the entire easterly side of the lot so as to connect two streets bounding it on the north and south, conveyed the lot by a deed which described it as 85 feet wide and stated the easterly boundary as "on a passageway five feet wide running through the grantor's land." The grantor owned no land east of the passageway. It was held that the deed conveyed the westerly half of the passageway together with an easement of way over the easterly half. Two justices dissented, they being of the opinion that the entire passageway was conveyed by the deed. 

A grant of a lot of land, bounded southerly on a passageway lying between the land conveyed and a house owned by the grantor; described by measurements which exclude the passageway; and made by a deed which contains a provision that the grantee shall have the use of the passageway situated between the land conveyed and the house referred to, and in which reference is made to a plan upon which the lot is drawn as distinct from the passageway, and with courses and distances corresponding with those mentioned in the deed, passes a title only to the side line of the passageway. Codman v. Evans, 1 Allen, 443. 

In Boston Five Cents Savings Bank v. Massachusetts General

Hospital, 255 Mass. 583, the question presented for decision was, Does a deed which bounds the land conveyed on an existing way, although not described as such, and not designated as a way on the accompanying plan, give the grantee title to the middle of the way? This question was a novel one in this

Commonwealth. There is authority in other jurisdictions to the effect that such a deed does carry title to the middle of the way. The decision went off on the grounds that although it appeared that certain easements of passage existed over some part of the lot which the petitioner contended was in fact a way, the trial court failed to find, and the record did not show, what part of the lot was subject to such easements, and the Court said: "Therefore, it does not appear that the southwesterly boundary of the land conveyed is the northeasterly boundary of any part of any existing way. In these circumstances we need not consider the question, whether a deed which bounds land on an existing way, although not described as such, conveys title to the center of the way." 

Although probably unnecessary to the decision, in Welsh v. 

Briggs, 204 Mass. 540, the Court sustained a ruling by the

Land Court to the effect that a deed of a lot of land described only by its lot number as shown on a plan carries title to the middle line of an adjacent way also shown thereon. 

32. WATER BOUNDARIES. 

When land is described as bounding on or by the sea or harbor, the conveyance is held to include the land towards the water as far as the title of the grantor extends. When land is described as bounding on or by a river, brook, etc., the conveyance includes the soil towards the center of the water as far as the grantor owns, and this result is not affected by the fact that the side lines are described as running to and from monuments standing on the bank of the river, etc. Cold 

Spring Iron Works v. Tolland, 9 Cush. 492. Compare the rule in that case with the rule as to ways discussed above. "But when the boundary is by the bank of the river, by the shore of the sea, by the beach or by the flats, the land under the river in the one case and that between high and low water marks in the other is excluded." - Crocker, page 53.

A boundary of land "by the ledgy shore" in a deed in which the grantor reserves to himself the right to take stones from the "ledgy shore and beach" passes the title to low water mark, the reservation showing such an intention on the part of the grantor. Burnham v. Hoyt, 216 Mass. 278.

DEEDS. "Lands bounded by a private dock laid out by the owner of the land covered by the dock and of the land adjoining the dock stand on the same footing as land bounded on a private way laid out by the owner of the land covered by the way and of the adjoining land. A deed of a lot of land bounded on such a way goes to the centre of the way although (1) the land is conveyed as a specified lot on a plan (referred to in the deed) which is delineated as stopping at the side line of the street, and although (2) the length of the side lines of the lot stop at the side line of the way. . . . The lot conveyed in Whitman v. Boston and Maine R.R., 3 Allen, 133, was a single lot bounded on a real canal. A conveyance of a single lot bounded on what was or had been a canal stands on a different footing from the conveyance of all the lots bounding on a private dock in which the original owner has no interest when all the lots have been sold." Wellington v. Cambridge, 214 Mass. 35. "The parties bounded their lot on the east by the line of the public way to its intersection with the line of high water mark on west beach. This being so, there is no legal objection to the monument made by this intersection moving as accretions take place, just as the rest of the high water front line moves, and the judge was right in ruling as he did on this point. He also correctly ruled that this movement could not go further south than the southerly limit of the street as laid out because there could be no intersection of the street line and high water mark below that point." Allen v. Wood, 256 Mass. 343. "A grant bounded by a great pond or lake which is public property extends to low water mark. . . . But this great pond was not public property. . . . We are of opinion that the boundaries in deeds given by him or them, unless a different intent is manifested in the deeds themselves, must be construed like boundaries upon other privately owned ponds, so as to pass the title to the pond, i.e., to the bed of the pond with the existing rights in the waters, as far as the centre of the pond if the grantor's ownership extended so far." Lynnfield v. Peabody, 219 Mass. 322, 336.

33. MONUMENTS. 

In Stefanick v. Fortona, 222 Mass. 83, the Court said: "No rule is better settled than the rule that monuments govern distances." "Whenever known monuments are referred to as boundaries, they must govern, although neither courses nor distances nor the computed contents correspond with such boundaries." 

Davis v. Rainsford, 17 Mass. 207, 210. The Court said, in the same case: "It [the rule] is not, however, inflexible, but, like other rules of law, it must sometimes yield to exceptions. 

These are to be made in cases in which the reason of the rule or principle fails.”

Davis v. Rainsford, supra, is an unsatisfactory decision. The 

Court said: "The only reason given, or which can be given, why monuments are to control the courses and distances in a deed, is that the former are less liable to mistakes. If, then, it appears that no mistake can reasonably be supposed to have been made in this case, no reason remains for the application of the rule. Now it seems to me impossible that such a mistake in the measure, as is supposed by the defendant, could have been made. The first line, according to the deed, is to extend only one foot and three inches. It was measured, without doubt, by a common rule or scale. Who then can believe that the line, actually measured, was in fact three feet and six inches; and that, in measuring a line so very short, a mistake was made of two feet and three inches! Such a supposition, if not altogether impossible, is at least in the highest degree improbable."

But this case turned also upon a reference to a plan drawn upon the back of the deed and upon the fact that the wall of a store (not then erected) which was referred to was subsequently erected in conformity with the plan. 

In Temple v. Benson, 213 Mass. 128, 132, the Court said: "It is a familiar rule in the construction of deeds that, where the land conveyed is described by courses and distances and also by monuments which are certain, or capable of being made certain, the monuments govern, and the measurements if they do not correspond must yield. In its application natural and

DEEDS. 

99 permanent objects, such as streams or rivers and the shore of the sea, or highways or other lands, or artificial landmarks or signs, such as fences, walls, a line, a building, or a stake and stones, are to be treated as monuments or boundaries, and their identity may be established by extrinsic evidence. White v. 

Bliss, 8 Cush. 510, 512. The only exception recognized is where, by strict adherence to monuments, the construction is plainly inconsistent with the intention of the parties as expressed by all the terms of the grant. Davis v. Rainsford, 17 Mass. 207; Murdock v. Chapman, 9 Gray, 156; George v. 

Wood, 7 Allen, 14." "The land of an adjoining proprietor may be a monument. 

The location of a monument which is in dispute may be established by extrinsic evidence. . . . If the monument cannot be found and its location cannot be made certain by evidence, the measurements and other provisions of the deed are controlling. . . . An exception to the rule that courses and distances must yield to monuments would exist in case a strict adherence to monuments would lead to a construction plainly inconsistent with the intention of the parties as expressed by all the terms of the grant." Holmes v. Barrett, Adv. Sh. 

1930, page 75.

In a description of a parcel of land a boundary "by the ledgy shore" names a monument, which controls a distance of "about thirty-five feet, more or less," which falls short of the shore from thirty to twenty feet, especially where the description by any interpretation contains one or more blunders. 

Burnham v. Hoyt, 216 Mass. 278.

Where an agreement in writing to sell and convey real estate describes the property as having a frontage on a certain street of "sixty feet more or less" and as being bounded on its other sides by a certain river and by an alleyway and as being all the property owned by the sellers "at this location,” the statement of the distances being accompanied by the words "more or less" or the word "about" and where the purchase price named is a round sum of money, the street, the river and the alleyway are monuments which control the stated distances, and, if upon a survey of the property it turns out to be of less area than that indicated by the figures in the description

and the frontage on the street turns out to be only about seveneighths of sixty feet, this gives the purchaser no right to have a deduction made from the price. Cashman v. Bean, 226 Mass. 198. 

In Hall v. Eaton, 139 Mass. 217, the description in a deed of land was in part, "running northerly by W. Street 134 feet; thence westerly by land of grantor 60 feet; thence running southerly by a line at right angles to D. Street 125 feet to D. 

Street; and thence easterly by D. Street 61 feet more or less to the point of beginning. Containing 7,770 square feet." It was held that the third bound ran at right angles to D. Street, although this would make the line on D. Street 80.52 feet and the area 9,101 square feet. The Court said: "So far as the question is as to the relative effect to be given to a course and a distance, neither has in itself any advantage over the other as showing a governing intent. Whether the one in a given case shall outweigh the other, as showing the intention of the parties, must depend upon the circumstances existing at the time. It was not merely giving a course to the third line, but it was expressly fixing the shape of the lot. . . . It is true that the given angle requires a longer line than was supposed; but the angle and the shape of the lot, and not the length of the line, appear to have been the controlling considerations." "When a monument is mentioned in a deed, and there is no such monument on the ground, and the parties by consent, at the time or soon after, erect and place a monument, intending it as and for the monument described, it will be so deemed afterwards as if it had been standing at the time." Cleveland v. Flagg, 4 Cush. 76, 81. But see White v. Bliss, 8 Cush. 510.

In this last case there was granted a right to raise a dam and flow land to the height of a hole drilled in a rock, referred to in the deed. It was held that a hole drilled nineteen years afterward by a grantee who had in the meantime conveyed away his interest under the deed, and after a disagreement had arisen, could not be treated as the monument referred to in the deed, although drilled at the place agreed upon by the parties when the deed was made, and that evidence that it was so drilled was inadmissible to show the right to flow.

DEEDS. 101 

34. PLAN. 

Where a plan is referred to as a basis for description, the lines, courses, distances, references to monuments and other particulars appearing thereon are to be as much regarded as the true description of the land conveyed as they would be if expressly recited in the deed. Walker v. Boynton, 120 Mass. 349.

This rule resulted in the academic theory that where land is conveyed by description which refers to a plan, the grantee takes by implication as appurtenant to the land conveyed a right of way over all the streets laid down on the plan, at least so far as the grantor owns such streets. This theory has been modified by later decisions. In Pearson v. Allen, 151 Mass. 79, it was held that the plaintiff to whom land at a seaside resort had been conveyed, with reference to a plan, was not entitled to rights of way in an avenue shown thereon which extended from a point opposite the land of the plaintiff to another avenue, also shown on the plan, which was situated near the ocean, but furnished no access to it (although there was an agreeable view of the sea therefrom), and which furnished access to no public way. "The principle governing the rights of grantees of lots, bounded on a way shown on a plan was established by Regan v. Boston Gas Light Co. 137 Mass. 37, and Pearson v. Allen, 151 Mass. 79, which declared that, in the absence of an express grant, where large tracts of land were plotted into numerous lots bounded on many different ways, a 'grant by implication of an onerous servitude upon other land of the grantor, not necessary for the enjoyment of the land conveyed, is not to be presumed unless such is clearly the intention of the parties;' that a reference to a plan in a deed, although accompanied by its use for description or bounds, does not result in the conveyance of rights not necessary for the enjoyment of the premises, in the absence of an intent appearing to that effect; that the only necessity contemplated in ordinary cases is that of access to public highways; and that the great desirability of a way for purposes of prospect is not a sufficient reason to extend or modify the general rule. In determining the intent, the entire situation at the time the deeds were given must be

considered. For example, whether the ways in question merely existed on paper, or were then constructed on the ground; whether they were then actually used as appurtenant to the granted premises; or whether they were remote or in close proximity. The doctrine of Regan v. Boston Gas Light Co., supra, and Pearson v. Allen, supra, is not limited nor impaired by Boland v. St. John's Schools, 163 Mass. 229, New England 

Structural Co. v. Everett Distilling Co., 189 Mass. 145, Downey v. H. P. Hood & Sons, 203 Mass. 4, and Ralph v. Clifford, 224 

Mass. 58." Prentiss v. Gloucester, 236 Mass. 36. See also 

Wellwood v. Havrah M. A. S. Cemetery Corp., 254 Mass. 350.

In Kaatz v. Curtis, 215 Mass. 311, land was devised by will to the defendant, bounding it upon a private way as shown on a plan, which was referred to by name and date and by the volume and page of its record in the registry of deeds. 

Other adjoining land was sold to the plaintiff by the executor of the will, bounding it on the same private way as shown on the same plan referred to in the same manner. 

The private way as designated on the plan passed over land occupied in part by barns of the defendant. The Court said: "By a well settled rule of construction the plan referred to became incorporated in the deed to the plaintiff and in the devise to the defendant; and the particulars appearing thereon and applicable to these lots are to be regarded as if fully set forth in the instruments under which they respectively derive title. As between these parties the location and dimensions of the private way designated John Street is fixed by the plan. 

As Colby, the owner of the entire tract, saw fit to define John Street on this plan as running through land occupied in part by his barns, the defendant who succeeds to his title is estopped, as against this plaintiff, from denying the location of the way so established." See also Burnham v. Mahoney, 222 

Mass. 524.

In Oliver v. Kalick, 223 Mass. 252, it was held that a line drawn on a recorded plan which was referred to in the conveyances of lots shown thereon, the line being 15 feet from and parallel to the side line of a street, and being marked "Building Line," had the effect of creating a 15-foot setback restriction. The Court said, however, that if in such a case

DEEDS. 

103 the language of the deed was clearly inconsistent with the plan, the deed undoubtedly would govern.

In Lagorio v. Lewenberg, 226 Mass. 464, land was conveyed by deed which described it as shown on a certain recorded plan. On the plan, a strip of land eight feet wide adjoining the granted premises was marked "Right of Way," and although the deed made no reference to the way, it was held that the plan was incorporated into the deed by reference, and that the right of way therein described became appurtenant to the premises conveyed as clearly as if specifically mentioned in the deeds.

To the effect that a plan may be incorporated by reference in a certificate of title of registered land, see Dubinsky v. 

Cama, 261 Mass. 47.

In Larsen v. Dillenschneider, 235 Mass. 56, it was held that land was sufficiently described in an assessment, advertisement of sale for taxes, and a tax deed following the sale by the following language: "South side of Harvey Street, a parcel of land with buildings thereon being lot No. 7 on Block Plan No. 

269, dated April, 1897, in the office of the assessors of the city of Cambridge and containing 4,810 square feet." No metes and bounds were given, and there was no reference to any other plan. The plan designated was not on record in the registry of deeds. 35. 

CLAUSES IMPLYING PROMISE BY THE GRANTEE. 

"When in a deed conveying an interest in real estate there is any clause or proviso, in whatever language expressed, showing an intention of the parties that the grantee should do certain things for the benefit of the grantor, and the grantee accepts the deed and the estate granted thereby, the law implies a promise on his part to perform the things thus provided for." -Crocker, page 78. 

In Plympton v. R.R., 221 Mass. 548, a deed of land to a railroad corporation, which was given in consideration of an agreement of the corporation, recited that agreement as follows: "As a part of the consideration for this conveyance said railroad company, for itself and its successors and assigns,

2 35 agrees to maintain a connection from the line and tracks of said railroad to a certain track now built on a trestle adjacent to the manufacturing establishment on the adjoining land of said grantors, and to maintain said trestle and track thereon so long as said connection does not interfere with the proper development of the railroad company." In an action against the railroad corporation for failing to keep the trestle in repair, there was no evidence that the conditions had changed in any way since the deed was given, and the trial judge found for the plaintiff. Held, that the finding was warranted, the defendant by accepting the deed having bound itself to perform the agreement to maintain the trestle under the conditions named, which still existed. 

In Thayer Foss Co. v. City of Woburn, 269 Mass. 186, it appeared that plaintiff's predecessors in title conveyed a parcel of land to the city of Boston for sewer purposes. The deed to the city contained the following: "It is hereby mutually agreed by us and said City (the assent of the City being evidenced by its acceptance of this instrument), that there shall be no obligation upon the part of ourselves, our heirs and assigns, to keep said sewer upon our said estate in repair and in good order, or to make any compensation for its use." In 

1898 the Commonwealth made a taking of the sewerage system, including the land so conveyed to city of Boston, and in 1921 conveyed to the city of Woburn all its right, title and interest in or to the land acquired by the taking of 1898. In 

1925 the defendant assessed to the plaintiff a tax for the use of the sewer. The plaintiff paid the tax under protest and brought this action to recover the amount so paid. It was held that the city of Boston, although it signed and sealed nothing, was bound by the acceptance of the deed to the agreement therein contained, and that this agreement being a matter of record was also binding in equity upon the defendant city as owner of the sewer.

A common illustration of a promise implied from the acceptance of a deed by the grantee is afforded by the recital, in a deed of an estate sold subject to a mortgage, that the grantee "is to assume" or 'assume and pay" such mortgage. These words in a deed raise an implied promise to the grantor by the 66

DEEDS. 105 grantee that the latter will pay the mortgage when due, or, if it is already due at the time of passing the deed, that he will pay it forthwith or within a reasonable time. 

Where the grantee in a deed assumes and agrees to pay a mortgage on the property conveyed, given by his grantor, he becomes as between himself and his grantor the principal for the payment of the mortgage debt assumed by him, and the mortgagor merely a surety, although this does not bind the mortgagee unless he assents to it, but, if thereafter the mortgagee, without the knowledge and assent of the mortgagor, makes a binding agreement with the grantee extending the time of payment of the mortgage note, the mortgagee by such agreement accepts the grantee as the principal debtor and the mortgagor as a surety and, by the agreement to extend the time of payment contained in the same instrument, made without the knowledge and assent of the mortgagor, discharges the mortgagor from his liability as surety. Codman v. Deland, 231 Mass. 344. "It is well settled in this Commonwealth that a promise made by a purchaser of an equity of redemption, by accepting a deed poll from his grantor, to pay the mortgage debt, is not a promise which can be enforced by the mortgagee by an action at law in his own name, or in the name of the grantor without his consent." Cressy v. Willis, 159 Mass. 249. See also Codman v. Deland, supra. 

In North End Savings Bank v. Snow, 197 Mass. 339, the grantee of an equity of redemption did not assume and agree to pay the mortgage. After maturity the mortgagee made an agreement with the grantee not to foreclose so long as the interest was paid, by reason of which there was delay in foreclosure and the real estate depreciated until its value fell short of the amount due on the note. It was held that the agreement of the plaintiff not to foreclose operated to release the mortgagor to the amount by which, because of such extension, the value of the security fell short of the amount due on the note. The Court said: "After the conveyance of the equity subject to the mortgage, the relation of the defendants to the plaintiff became in a sense that of sureties. . . . But in a case like the present the mortgagors are not regarded as

being, in the strict sense of the word, sureties, but as being such only in the sense that they are entitled to have the security regarded as the primary fund for the payment of the debt and it is only so far as that is affected that they can complain." See Lincoln v. Finkelstein, 255 Mass. 486; Phillips v. Vorenberg, 259 Mass. 46.

Warneke v. Wyszatycki, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 2143, was an action to recover damages for alleged breach of an agreement contained in a deed. Plaintiff conveyed to defendant certain land subject to a first mortgage for $5,500 and to a second mortgage for $3,300. It was recited in the deed that the grantee assumed and agreed to pay these mortgages. Interest due on the second mortgage was not paid when due and the holder foreclosed by sale. Plaintiff purchased the property at the sale for $3,527.65 which was the amount of the second mortgage plus interest and expenses. The Court said: "As the property was sold and bid in by the plaintiff for a sum sufficient to pay the mortgage debt including interest due and expenses of sale, the liability of the defendant to the plaintiff to assume and pay this second mortgage was thereby extinguished. The plaintiff has suffered no damage, and is not entitled to recover."

Where by the terms of the deed the grantee is to "pay," or simply to "assume' "assume" the mortgage, the grantor may recover from him the amount due on the mortgage, if he fails to pay it when due, even though the grantor has not himself paid such amount or any part thereof; but it seems that if the provision of the deed is only that the grantee shall hold the grantor "harmless and indemnified" from the mortgage, the grantor will be entitled to recover only sums actually paid by him. Crocker, page 80. 

In Peterson v. Abbe, 234 Mass. 467, the grantee of an equity assumed and agreed to pay two mortgages, "meaning hereby to assume and pay both of said mortgages and to hold the grantor harmless thereon." The first mortgage was foreclosed by sale, the proceeds of which were insufficient to pay the second mortgage note, and, its holder having recovered and collected the amount of a judgment in an action upon the note against the maker, the former owner of the real estate,

DEEDS. 107 such former owner brought this action against his grantee upon the agreement of indemnity contained in the deed. It was held that the agreement entitled the grantor to be indemnified by the grantee, not only in a sum equal to the value of the security represented by the real estate, but also against personal liability upon the mortgage note, and that the agreement by its terms was severable and an action would lie for separate breaches. The Court said: "If it be conceded that the plaintiff might have brought suit at any time after March 26, 1907, when the balance remaining on the second mortgage was overdue and unpaid, nevertheless he has as surety the option to rely on the defendant's personal undertaking of complete indemnity, and, until obliged to make payment, the statute (of limitations) did not begin to run." This decision seems to turn on the agreement "to hold the grantor harmless thereon" which is referred to as "a contract of indemnity, whereby the defendant expressly engages to save the plaintiff harmless from payment of the second mortgage," and this implies that the decision would have been otherwise if the special indemnity agreement had been lacking. However, in Rice v. 

Sanders, 152 Mass. 108 (cited in Peterson v. Abbe, supra), a similar action, where the agreement was only "to assume and pay," the Court said: "In assuming the mortgage, the grantee undertakes to relieve the mortgagor, not only from personal liability for the debt, but also from all liability under the mortgage." This being so, the additional agreement "to hold the grantor harmless" in Peterson v. Abbe would seem to add nothing to the undertaking. 

36. HABENDUM. 

"To have and to hold, etc." "The office of the habendum in a deed is to declare and fix the nature of the interest or title conveyed by the premises. It may define, enlarge or diminish the estate granted." Pratt v. Sanger, 4 Gray, 84, 86.

Illustrations from Pratt v. Sanger: 

1. "Define.” — If lands are given in the premises to A and his heirs, which would vest a fee, habendum to A and the heirs of his body, this will vest only an estate tail in A.

2. "Enlarge." - An estate granted to A for life, habendum to him and his heirs, vests in A a fee simple. 

3. "Diminish."- Where no estate is limited in the premises, and an express estate for years is declared in the habendum, this will operate to qualify and abridge the general intendment of the premises, by which an estate for life would otherwise have passed.

While the illustration given above under "diminish" is probably sound, it is a fact, broadly speaking, that the habendum may not cut down the grant. 

In Brooks v. West Boston Gas Co., 260 Mass. 407, it appeared that a corporation conveyed by warranty deed a tract of land to L E, his heirs and assigns, and in the description included a grant to "said E" of a certain easement. Habendum: “To have and to hold the above granted premises, with the privileges and appurtenances thereto belonging to the said L E, his heirs and assigns," etc. The Court said: "The respondent, . . . contends that the grant was merely for the life of L E. . . . This contention is not sound. It is based upon the omission of the words 'heirs and assigns' after E's name in the description of the easement granted. Words of limitation have no appropriate place in the descriptive part of a deed. It is the function of the habendum to declare the extent of the estate conveyed." See also Parsons v. N. Y. etc. R.R. Co., 216 Mass. 269. 

Mr. Crocker said, at page 83, "When the habendum is repugnant and contradictory to the premises, it is void, and the grantee will take the estate given in the premises. Thus if lands are given in the premises to A and his heirs, habendum to A for life, the habendum is void because it is utterly repugnant to and irreconcilable with the premises." 

In Simonds v. Simonds, 199 Mass. 552, there was a grant to C S, his heirs and assigns. Habendum to said C S during the term of his natural life, remainder to such of the children of said C S as shall arrive at the age of twenty-one years, their heirs and assigns, to their use and behoof forever. Held, that the fee vested in C S and his heirs, first for the use of himself during his life, and then for the use of such of his children as shall attain the age of twenty-one years; and that,

DEEDS. 109 when all this class had been determined finally, the use would shift to them in fee and be executed by the Statute of Uses. 

John L. Thorndike, Esq., in an article entitled “Conveyances of a Freehold Estate to Commerce in the Future," in 

Mass. Law Quarterly, February, 1918, said that at common law the habendum in Simonds v. Simonds would have been void, but justified it under the Statute of Uses. 

As bearing on the rule stated, that the habendum is void if it is repugnant to the grant, see Land Court Decisions, page 24. 

Here there was a deed given by T D in which the consideration was recited as paid by W and B; the grant was to said "W and B" without words of inheritance, and the habendum was to "said T D and his heirs and assigns." The decision of the Land Court was that the insertion of the grantor's name in the habendum was obviously an error of the scrivener, and must be rejected because it was contrary to the grant. But the 

Court was of the opinion that the intention to convey a fee to the grantees was obvious, and accordingly it retained the words of inheritance in the habendum, annexed the same to the grant, and ruled that the grantees took an estate in fee simple. 

In Goodman v. Telfer, 230 Mass. 157, it was held that a deed which recited a consideration paid by a married woman, in which, in the granting clause the word "said" was followed by the name of her husband, while the habendum ran to her and "his" heirs and assigns, conveyed good title to the married woman. The Court said: "The deed from C to the defendant, while inartificially drawn, vested in her as matter of law a good title to the premises conveyed. A conveyance of land is to be construed so as to vest the title in accordance with the intention of the parties, if such interpretation is not contrary to established principles of law. The consideration for the grant was paid by the defendant, and the habendum to her and 'his' heirs and assigns clearly shows that it was intended that the premises were to be conveyed to her. The word 'his' instead of 'her' manifestly was a clerical error which does not affect the validity of the grant. The habendum explains the granting clause, and as the consideration was paid by the defendant it is plain it was the intention of the parties that the title should vest

in her. Bridge v. Wellington, 1 Mass. 219; Pratt v. Sanger, 4 Gray, 84, 86; Chenery v. Stevens, 97 Mass. 77; Breed v. 

Osborne, 113 Mass. 318; Tibbetts v. Leeson, 148 Mass. 102."

The proper place for the insertion of words showing an intention to create an estate in joint tenancy, etc., or a trust, is the habendum. Example: "To have and to hold the aforesaid granted premises to the said A B and C D, as joint tenants, and to their heirs and assigns, etc." If it be desired to create a trust, after the habendum in the usual form should be added, "but in trust nevertheless for the following uses and purposes. 99 66 For the statutory provision relieving the necessity of using the terms "heirs” or assigns" in order to convey or reserve an estate in fee, see G. L. c. 183, § 13. Of course, in the short form deed, from which the habendum is omitted, any special estate must needs be created by inserting the appropriate language following the grant.

41. Covenants which run with the Land. 37.

RESTRICTIONS. 

G. L. c. 184, § 23, re-enacting St. 1887, c. 418:

Conditions or restrictions, unlimited as to time, by which the title or use of real property is affected shall be limited to the term of thirty years after the date of the deed or other instrument or the date of the probate of the will creating them, except in cases of gifts or devises for public, charitable or religious purposes.
