The provisions of this section shall not apply to conditions or restrictions existing on the sixteenth day of July in the year eighteen hundred and eighty-seven or to those contained in a deed, gift or grant of the commonwealth. 

The term "restrictions" in the foregoing statute refers to those limitations upon the use of real property which are frequently created by agreement for a limited or unlimited period, and which commonly are imposed for the benefit of adjoining property. Among such restrictions frequently imposed are those to the effect that the whole or a definite portion of the premises shall be occupied only in a specified manner, or be devoted only to specified uses, or shall have only specified structures or none erected on them. "Such an agreement may occur in connection with a conveyance of land restricting the grantor as regards the use of the land retained by him, or restricting the grantee as regards the use of the land conveyed."-Tiffany, Vol. 2, page 1426. 

In Flynn v. Caplan, 234 Mass. 516, it was held that a deed. to the city of Boston of land over which the city agreed to construct a roadway and walk, and which contained building restrictions affecting the grantor's adjoining land and recited. that such restrictions "shall continue in force so long as said

§37 roadway and walk shall be maintained by said City of Boston," did not define with exactitude the interval of time over which the restrictions should run; and, therefore, under St. 1887, c. 418, such restrictions were not in force after thirty years from the date of the deed. The Court said: "The evident purpose of the statute was to terminate restrictions that have been in force thirty years, unless it appeared in the instrument creating them that they were to run for a definite term of years, or until the happening of an event certain to occur. 

The word 'unlimited' means without confines, unrestricted, boundless. The city of Boston may maintain the roadway and walk forever or for a lesser period, in accordance with the determination of the city. The restriction may therefore be unlimited as to time because the city may never cease to maintain the roadway and walk; and in any circumstance it is terminable upon the happening of an event not certain to come to pass and hence it is not protected against termination at the expiration of thirty years prescribed by the statute." See also 

Burke v. Metropolitan District Commission, 262 Mass. 70.

Not infrequently restrictions are imposed "forever," or it is provided that land shall "never" be used for a particular purpose. The writer believes that the reasoning in Flynn v. Caplan conclusively and properly establishes that such restrictions expire at the end of thirty years. A restriction imposed "forever" must be "unlimited as to time," and accordingly within the intent of the statute.

The general rule seems to be that a limitation of time expressed in a deed of land imposing restrictions is applicable to all the restrictive provisions therein contained without regard to rhetorical arrangement. See Hill v. Levine, 252 Mass. 513; 

Welsh, Petitioner, 175 Mass. 68; Best v. Nagle, 182 Mass. 495. "The cases heretofore decided by this court relating to equitable restriction are analogous in principle to those relating to easement: each is a property right in the estate to which it is appurtenant." Nash v. Eliot Street Garage Co., 236 Mass. 176. "As the restriction was created by the grantor, the words which he used are not to be extended in their meaning, but are to be interpreted most strongly against him. Welsh, petitioner,

DEEDS. 113 §37 175 Mass. 68. It is not for the interest of the community, nor is it the policy of the Commonwealth, that, as conditions greatly change in our large cities, restrictions put upon land in reference to the quiet of residential streets should continue, when the neighborhood is entirely given up to business, unless they are so expressed as plainly to be binding." Boston Baptist Social Union v. Trustees of Boston University, 183 Mass. 202. 

In case of doubt a clause creating an equitable restriction is to be construed against the grantor and in favor of the grantee's right to have the land free from restrictions. American Unitarian Scy. v. Minot, 185 Mass. 589.

Relief in equity in actions to restrain violation of a restriction is granted only when sought with promptness, and where active diligence has been exercised throughout respecting the matter of complaint. Where a plaintiff has violated the very restriction he seeks to enforce to substantially the same extent and in the same general way as the defendant, and there is no material difference in kind or degree between them, a court of equity will not ordinarily interfere. Loud v. Pendergast, 206 

Mass. 122. See also Balcom v. Normile, 255 Mass. 186.

The rights to which agreements of this nature give rise are commonly termed "equitable easements" by the text writers, and are chiefly within the cognizance of courts of equity, although such agreement may support an action at law between the immediate parties. In the leading case of Whitney v. 

Union Railway Co., 11 Gray, 359, the owner of a tract of land laid it out in lots for dwelling houses, and conveyed one lot with certain restrictions as to the character of the buildings that might be erected thereon. It was held that these restrictions, after the deed had been recorded, might be enforced in equity against an assignee of the grantee, in favor of the original grantor, still continuing to own one of the other lots, and not having been guilty of laches. And the Court said that there could be no doubt that in whatever form such a restraint was placed on real estate by the terms of a grant, whether it was in the technical form of a condition or covenant, or of a reservation or exception in the deed, it was binding as between the grantor and the immediate grantee, and could be enforced

§37 against such grantee by suitable process, both in law and equity. The Court said, further: "The more difficult question, and the one on which the decision of this case must turn, is, to what extent and in what cases are such stipulations binding on those who take the estate under the grantee, directly or by a derivative title? Upon this point the better opinion would seem to be that such agreements are valid and capable of being enforced in equity against all those who take the estate with notice of them, although they may not be, strictly speaking, real covenants, so as to run with the land, or of a nature to create a technical qualification of the title conveyed by the deed. This opinion rests on the principle that, as in equity that which is agreed to be done shall be considered as performed, a purchaser of land, with notice of a right or interest in it, subsisting in another, is liable to the same extent and in the same manner as the person from whom he made the purchase, and is bound to do that which his vendor had agreed to perform. . . . In such cases, although the covenant or agreement in the deed, regarded as a contract merely, is binding only on the original parties, yet, in order to carry out the plain intent of the parties, it will be construed as creating a right or interest, in the nature of an incorporeal hereditament or easement appurtenant to the remaining land belonging to the grantor at the time of the grant, and arising out of and attached to the land, part of the original parcel, conveyed to the grantee. When, therefore, it appears by a fair interpretation of the words of a grant that it was the intent of the parties to create or reserve a right, in the nature of a servitude or easement, in the property granted, for the benefit of other land owned by the grantor, and originally forming with the land conveyed one parcel, such right will be deemed appurtenant to the land of the grantor and binding on that conveyed to the grantee, and the right and burden thus created will respectively pass to and be binding on all subsequent grantees of the respective lots of land." See also Parker v. Nightingale, 6 Allen, 341.

In Massachusetts Institute of Technology v. Boston Society of Natural History, 218 Mass. 189, it was said: "We are not impressed by the argument of the petitioner that equitable

DEEDS. 115 §37 easements or restrictions were not introduced into our law until the decision in 1863 of Parker v. Nightingale, 6 Allen, 341. . . . The reasoning of the opinion in Whitney v. Union Railway, 11 Gray, 359, decided in January, 1860, is against the proposition. And in Parker v. Nightingale, ubi supra, the doctrine in question was applied to a conveyance made in 1822." 

In connection with the statement that such agreements are valid and capable of being enforced in equity against all those who take the estate with notice of them, it should be noted that where such agreement is incorporated in a conveyance duly recorded in the appropriate registry of deeds, all persons take the land affected with constructive notice of the right, and are bound thereby even if they have not actual notice. See 

Riley v. Barron, 227 Mass. 325.

In Clapp v. Wilder, 176 Mass. 332, the owner of two adjoining lots of land, lot A, on which was a store building, and lot B, upon which was a dwelling house occupied by him, conveyed lot A by deed containing the following provision: "And this conveyance is made upon the express condition that said W [the grantee], his heirs and assigns, shall never erect any building nearer the street line of said land than the store building now thereon." After the grantor's death, lot B was conveyed to the plaintiff. Held, on a bill in equity to enjoin W from building an addition to his store on lot A, that the clause in question was a condition, and that upon full consideration of the whole deed in the light of the attendant circumstances the plaintiff had failed to show that this condition was created for the benefit of his land. Three Justices dissented. In deciding the case, the majority of the Court first determined that by reason of the language a conditional fee had been conveyed. It then was said: "The next question is whether this condition was imposed for the benefit of the land now held by the plaintiff. If it was, then it is immaterial whether it be in the form of a condition or restriction so far as respects the right of the plaintiff." The Court also said that this was not a case of a general building scheme, and "it is therefore not included in the class of cases of which Whitney v. Union Railway [supra] and Hopkins v. Smith, 162 Mass. 444 are examples." Then the Court proceeded, in order to determine

§37 whether or not the provision was intended to benefit the grantor's remaining land, to consider in detail "the situation and attendant circumstances." And because it appeared that the grantor was an invalid and was accustomed to sit in the front window of his house and enjoy the view across the front of the lot which he conveyed, it was held that the provision was intended for his personal benefit and not for the benefit of his remaining land where he lived. 

The writer believes that the statement first above quoted is unsound and that it was wholly unnecessary. The Court should not have determined from the language alone that a common law condition was created. (See Cassidy v. Mason, 171 Mass. 507, discussed in the dissenting opinion.) But, the language being apt, and it was, the "situation and attendant circumstances" should have been considered in connection with the language, and, this being done, it seems clear that a common law condition was made out and thereupon the case is done and the provision was not for the benefit of the remaining land. That is to say, if the provision was intended for the personal benefit of the grantor, the language being apt, it created a common law condition and could not be for the benefit of other land; but if it was intended for the benefit of the remaining land, it was not personal to the grantor, did not create a true condition, and should have been construed as a restriction only. Mr. Crocker (page 96) made substantially the same statement to the effect that a condition may be enforced for the benefit of other land. The cases cited do not bear him out if he meant to say what the Court said so plainly in Clapp v. Wilder. A given provision may operate as a true condition and at the same time create an equitable restriction in favor of a person other than the grantor where such conditions are imposed as a part of a general scheme (Wilson v. 

Technology, 188 Mass. 565), but the writer has found no case among those cited by Mr. Crocker which holds that a restrictive provision may operate as a common law condition, and, at the same time, as a restriction for the benefit of the grantor's remaining land. 

In Lodge v. Swampscott, 216 Mass. 260, the Court said: "It seems plain . . . that the restriction was intended for the

DEEDS. 

117 §37 benefit of the two estates; whether to be enforced as a condition through an action in the name of Williams [the grantor of the two parcels of land concerned] or his heirs, or in equity as creating an equitable easement in each estate for the benefit of the other, it is not necessary now to decide." Again, in 

Bessey v. Ollman, 242 Mass. 89, in sustaining a decision of the

Land Court to the effect that a provision sounding in condition did not create a restriction appurtenant to the land of the respondent, the Court said: "When the existence of a servitude over one parcel of land for the benefit of another is asserted, it must appear that it was the intention of the grantor, by inserting in his deed the condition or restriction, to create a servitude or right, which should enure to the benefit of a particular parcel of land, and be annexed to it as an appurtenance." But see the statement quoted from Dana v. Wentworth, 111 Mass. 291, infra.

It does not follow necessarily, in a case where the owner of land conveys a part thereof subject to restrictions and retains other land adjoining or in the immediate vicinity, that such restrictions were imposed for the benefit of the remaining land. 

In Badger v. Boardman, 16 Gray, 559, the first deed, which was of a house and lot, contained a restriction that no shed or outbuilding at the rear of the house should ever be built any higher than the one then existing. Subsequently the same vendor sold his other and adjoining lot to another purchaser, who sought to restrain the first vendee from increasing the height of the shed. But the court in equity refused to grant the relief asked for, because there was nothing in the deeds or circumstances of the case to show that the restriction as to the defendant's building was intended to inure to the benefit of the plaintiff's land. The Court said: "There is an entire absence of any language in the deeds under which the parties claim, from which it can be fairly inferred that the restriction in the deed to the defendant against erecting his building above a certain height was intended to inure to the benefit of the estate now owned by the plaintiff. The restriction is in the most general terms, and no words are used which indicate the object of the grantor in inserting it in the deed. . . . Generally, when such a right or privilege is reserved, the purpose intended to be

accomplished by it is stated in the conveyance or can be gathered from a plan referred to therein, or from the situation of the property with reference to other land of the grantor. All parties then take with notice of the right reserved and the burden or easement imposed. But the conveyances in the present case contain no such clause, nor is there anything in the terms of the grant, or in the circumstances surrounding the parties when it was made, to lead to an inference in favor of the claim set up by the plaintiff. . . . We are therefore' of opinion that the clause in the deed to the defendant, creating the restriction on the enjoyment of his estate, must be construed as a personal covenant merely with the original grantor." 

In Lock v. Hale, 165 Mass. 20, A, who owned and occupied as a residence land on a certain street, conveyed land on the opposite side of the street to B by a deed containing the provision that thereafter only buildings of a certain class should be built thereon, and that dwelling houses should not be placed nearer to it than a certain distance. It was held that such provision did not create an easement or servitude in favor of the premises occupied by A as a residence at the date of his deed to B. The Court said: "A's residence was on the opposite side of the street, and there is nothing in the deed, or in the manner of using or occupying the respective estates, or in their situation, which in terms or by necessary or reasonable implication shows that the provision was designed to subject the property that was conveyed to B to an easement in favor of the premises where A lived. . . . It probably was or would be an advantage to the premises where A lived that houses built on the opposite side of the street should be set back a certain distance from the street line, etc. But we cannot by reason of that fact alone construe the provision as subjecting the lot conveyed to an easement or servitude in favor of the premises occupied by A." In Peck v. Conway, 119 Mass. 546, the Court said: "The question whether such an easement is a personal right, or is to be construed to be appurtenant to some other estate, must be determined by the fair interpretation of the grant or reservation creating the easement, aided, if necessary, by the situation of the property and the surrounding circumstances." In this

DEEDS. 119 case E conveyed a triangular piece of land adjoining his homestead subject to a restriction that it should never be built upon. The Court said, further: "It is difficult to see how E would have any interest in restricting the use of the land sold, except as owner of the house lot which he retained. The nature of the restriction implies that it was intended for the benefit of this lot. A prohibition against building on the land sold would be obviously useful and beneficial to this lot, giving it the benefit of better light and air and prospect; this is its apparent purpose, while it would be of no appreciable advantage for any other purpose. The fair inference is that the parties intended to create this easement or servitude for the benefit of the adjoining estate. We are therefore of the opinion that it was not a mere personal right in E, but was an easement appurtenant to the estate which he conveyed to the plaintiff." 

In Hill v. Levine, 252 Mass. 513, premises were conveyed subject to certain restrictions. Then followed in separate paragraphs the provisions: "The rest of the land shown on said plan is to be conveyed subject to the same restrictions;" and "The grantee may re-divide said land into lots but no lot shall have a frontage of less than fifty feet." It was held that the provision as to frontage was a personal agreement between grantor and grantee, and the fact that it was not in the group of restrictions to be included in other deeds tended to show that it was not intended for the benefit of other lot owners, and it was said: "Unless it appears that a right or privilege is reserved for the benefit of the remaining lots of a grantor, in the deed itself or from a plan or from the situation of the property with reference to other land of the grantor, the grantees of other lots cannot enforce them.”

In Skinner v. Shepard, 130 Mass. 180, it was held that certain provisions in a deed took effect only by way of restriction; and that the restriction as to building, in the absence of evidence that it was imposed for the benefit of other land, must be construed as a personal covenant merely with the grantor, which his heirs could not enforce after his death. 

Storey v. Brush, 256 Mass. 101, was a bill in equity to enforce restrictions. It was found that a large parcel of land including locus was divided into building lots, and that restric-

tions were imposed on the several lots as a part of a general scheme for the benefit of the several grantees. The Court said: "Before the final decree the plaintiffs had conveyed to different grantees all the land originally owned by them as trustees. . . . As trustees, although no longer owners of any part of said original tract, they still had such an interest in the subject matter of this suit that they have a right to enforce the restrictions for the benefit of their grantees.' 99 

In Dana v. Wentworth, 111 Mass. 291, the Court said: "The clause in the deed from the plaintiff to Nudd [predecessor in title to defendant], restricting building on the land thereby conveyed, is in the form of a condition; as a condition, it can only be taken advantage of by the grantor, and at common law; and this bill is not framed to enforce a forfeiture for breach of condition. The plaintiff does not show that since that conveyance she is the owner of any land which may be affected by a disregard of the restriction. She is not therefore entitled to ask a court of equity to enforce the restriction for her own benefit." So far as the writer is aware, this decision in Dana v. Wentworth has never been overruled. See Orenberg v. Johnston, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 2273. 

But if all the lots in a development are sold subject to uniform restrictions, it warrants a finding by a court of equity that there was a general scheme of improvement, and that each owner has the right as against every other owner to require the observance and enforcement of such restrictions. 

Whitney v. Union Railway Co., supra. Such an arrangement is commonly called a "general building scheme." Being founded in equity, if the neighborhood changes in the course of years so that it would be inequitable and oppressive to longer enforce such rights, a court of equity will deny relief other than to allow the plaintiff to recover such money damage as he may prove. Jackson v. Stevenson, 156 Mass. 496. The change in the neighborhood concerned in the case of McArthur v. Hood Rubber Company, 221 Mass. 372, was so complete that the Court went further than in Jackson v. Stevenson and said that here the restrictions had ceased to exist. See also 

Massachusetts Institute of Technology v. Boston Society of

Natural History, 218 Mass. 189, and Loud v. Pendergast, 206 

Mass. 122.

DEEDS. 

121 The Legislature gave the Land Court power to register title to land freed from restrictions when the enforcement thereof would be inequitable. See Acts of 1915, c. 112, § 2. In Riverbank Improvement Co. v. Chadwick, 228 Mass. 242, decided in 1917, that statute was held unconstitutional on the grounds that it deprived respondents of rights in real estate for a private use contrary to the security afforded by articles 1 and 

10 of the Declaration of Rights. In case No. 6709 in the 

Land Court, George Luther Lincoln et al., Petitioners, the 

Land Court, in a decision filed on February 7, 1918, said: "There has been a material development in the law in this state in regard to equitable restrictions since the early cases of registration of title above referred to, which were in and prior to the year 1900. These changes have only accentuated however the practice in this court. . . . . namely, that the only question that can be determined in registration proceedings is whether restrictions do or do not exist as matter of law. With partial changes in the character of the neighborhood, or general disregard or violation of restrictions this court has nothing to do." 

But see chapter 387 of the Acts of 1931, - An Act conferring upon the Land Court jurisdiction in equity of suits to quiet or establish the title to land or to remove a cloud from the title thereto and relative to the general law and equity powers of said Court.

Slight difference in the restrictions in the different deeds, and the fact that one or two lots were sold without any restrictions, will not be sufficient to remove the case from the general rule, and the failure to impose restrictions on a few lots simply takes those lots out of the general scheme. Hano v. Bigelow, 155 Mass. 341; Bacon v. Sandberg, 179 Mass. 396; Hartt v. 

Rueter, 223 Mass. 207. In the case last mentioned, equitable restrictions were imposed upon thirteen lots of land adjoining a private street. 

It was held that the fact that the restrictions imposed on three of the lots situated at the end of the street, which had no outlet at that end, differed from restrictions imposed on the other ten lots in not requiring, like those restrictions, that dwelling houses should be set back 25 feet from the street, but permitting houses to be built up to the line of the way

on those three lots, did not affect the character of the restrictions on the other ten lots or render such restrictions less binding.

In Storey v. Brush, 256 Mass. 101, it was held that the fact that no general plan showing a large tract of land developed and sold was recorded did not prevent the creation of a general scheme of restrictions. 

But it has been held that there is no evidence of a general scheme where only five or six out of twenty-one lots were conveyed by deeds containing restrictions. Roak v. Davis, 194 Mass. 481. See also Webber v. Landrigan, 215 Mass. 221, and St. Patrick's Association v. Hale, 227 Mass. 175.

In Webber v. Landrigan, supra, a parcel of land was divided by the owner into six lots. Three of these lots he conveyed, each subject to the restriction that all buildings thereafter erected thereon should be set back on a line with the building then thereon. He conveyed two other lots with the restriction that all buildings erected thereon should be set back at least 10 feet, excepting that a circular front might project 2 feet and 6 inches farther. The sixth lot he conveyed last, subject to no restriction. None of the deeds referred to any plan showing that the lots were subject to restrictions, and none of them expressed any intent to benefit the other lots by the restriction. Held, that no general scheme was established on which to found a right in the owner of one of the lots to enforce in equity the restriction imposed on the holder of another lot by his deed or the deed of a predecessor in title.

It should be noted, however, that even though the lots included in a larger parcel are sold subject to restrictions which vary considerably, if a substantial number of adjacent lots are conveyed subject to uniform restrictions, it might well be held that a general scheme had been created as to those lots. 

In this manner two or three independent general schemes might be created in one large tract of land. 

In Goulding v. Phinney, 234 Mass. 411, a corporation, the owner of a tract of land, by its deeds of numerous lots, created a general building scheme, and it was held that the right of the owner of one lot to maintain a suit in equity to enjoin an adjoining owner from violating the restrictions was not

DEEDS. 123 affected by a deed from the corporation to the defendant purporting to release its rights.

But while sales are going on and the grantor continues to hold a part of the land, if a purchaser of a single lot desires to protect himself against any objectionable use of such remaining land he must require a stipulation to be inserted in his deed to the effect that the unsold lots shall be held and sold subject to similar restrictions. McCusker v. Goode, 185 Mass. 607. See 

Beekman v. Schirmer, 239 Mass. 265.

It is well settled that an easement in the nature of an equitable restriction cannot be imposed upon land by parole. Sargent v. Leonardi, 223 Mass. 556, citing Sprague v. Kimball, 213 Mass. 380. See G. L. c. 183, § 3.

In Sprague v. Kimball, the Court said that if the front building line, with any language indicating the nature of the restrictions, had appeared on the plan referred to, the defendant would have been estopped to deny an implied grant with covenants coextensive with the scope of the plan, or, if by any appropriate wording of the deeds it appeared that the remaining lots as they were sold should be subject to the restrictions, the statute would have been satisfied.

In Beekman v. Schirmer, supra, defendant at different times purchased two lots of land. The first lot was restricted to one single dwelling house with outbuildings, including a private greenhouse. The second lot was restricted so that "only a single dwelling house . . . with such outbuildings, including a private stable or garage, . . . shall be erected," etc. Held, that defendant's holdings, even if separately purchased, constituted an entire estate which he could occupy and develop to the same extent as if he had acquired the whole tract of land under one deed which recited all the restrictions, and accordingly he was entitled to place his residence on his first lot and a private greenhouse on the second. But see Watters v. Blatt, 249 Mass. 340, which appears to be contra, but in which Beekman v. Schirmer is cited with approval. 

A provision in a deed of one of six city building lots on which the erection of a uniform block of six private dwelling houses is anticipated that "the front line of the house to be erected on the lot" shall be set back from the line of the

Qwas held it does molate street as marked and laid down on a certain plan creates a restriction relating only to the house to be built at that time. 

American Unitarian Society v. Miņot, 185 Mass. 589. See also 

Davidson v. Sohier, 220 Mass. 270. But in McLaughlin v.

Eldredge, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 577, which concerned a restriction that "no building other than dwelling houses, etc., shall be erected upon the land," it was said that plaintiff's contention, that he was entitled to the relief sought because the word "erected" in the restriction applied only to the first building, could not be maintained. 

In Small v. Parkway Auto Supplies, Inc., 258 Mass. 30, it was held that two pumps, a gasoline tank and a concrete structure for the purpose of changing the oils of automobiles did not violate a restriction that no "buildings" should be erected or maintained.

The use of a building as a tea room, where food and bever- He-ages are sold to be consumed on the premises, does not violate a restriction against use for "mercantile" purposes. Merrymount Co. v. Edwardes, 263 Mass. 282.

In Tripp v. Fay, 264 Mass. 516, it was held that a restriction that "no buildings other than dwelling houses (which word shall include club houses) . . . shall be erected," etc., was not violated by the erection of a club house, although it was intended to include a cafeteria, barber shop, tailor shop and smoke shop, all inside the club house, for the use of members. But see Doyle v. Wheeler, 265 Mass. 256.

The right to enforce an equitable restriction is wholly extinguished by a failure to enforce it for twenty years, if others during that period have used the land which was subject to the restriction in a manner inconsistent with the existence of the restriction. Smith v. Price, 214 Mass. 298. See also Wilson v. Middlesex Co., 244 Mass. 224.

In Carr v. Riley, 198 Mass. 70, the restrictions considered were that "no buildings other than dwelling houses shall. be erected, placed or used upon the said land. . . . No building erected. . . shall be used as an apartment house, family hotel or flats, or in design or construction be fitted for occupancy by more than one family." A woman claiming under a deed containing such restrictions lived with her sister in a

DEEDS. 

125 dwelling house on the land, the internal structure of which was fitted for occupancy by only one family. They were physicians and carried on a hospital there, one of the rooms being fitted up as an operating room. It was held that the restrictions in question had not been violated. See also Stone v. 

Pillsbury, 167 Mass. 332, and Prest v. Ross, 245 Mass. 342.

These cases direct attention to a distinction between a provision as to the character of a building which may be erected or maintained, and a restriction as to the use to which buildings may be put. They appear to establish the rule that under restrictions similar to those quoted above a building once erected as a dwelling house and fitted for occupancy by one family may be used for other purposes than as a single family dwelling house, provided, of course, that such use is not otherwise expressly prohibited, and provided that no structural change is made inside or out which renders the building unsuitable for use as a single family dwelling house, and provided, further, that the person who uses the building for purposes other than as a single family dwelling house also resides therein.

In Powers v. Radding, 225 Mass. 110, a restriction that "but one dwelling house shall be erected" was construed as defining the use, and not merely the form of the structure, and as prohibiting anything but single family dwelling houses. This decision somewhat clouds the rule above stated, but is not generally considered as contra to the decisions above cited. Moreover, it should be noted that this decision rests in part on the proximity of the land involved to the grounds of the United States Armory at Springfield, “with the reasonable probability that they would perpetually remain open and be carefully and artistically cared for." 

For a case where land was conveyed subject to the restriction that no grocery business should be carried on on the granted premises, which restriction was held to be unenforceable against a subsequent owner of the granted premises, see 

Shade v. M. O'Keefe, Inc., 260 Mass. 180, cited in section 41.

38. RESERVATION AND EXCEPTION. 

"The operation of an exception in a deed is to retain in the grantor some portion of his former estate, which by the exception is taken out of or excluded from the grant; and whatever is thus excluded remains in him as of his former right or title, because it is not granted. A reservation or implied grant vests in the grantor in the deed some new right or interest not before existing in him." Ashcroft v. Eastern R.R., 126 Mass. 196. "Whether a particular provision is an exception or a reservation does not depend upon the use of the word 'reserving' or 'excepting,' but upon the nature and effect of the provision itself." Stockwell v. Couillard, 129 Mass. 231. See also 

Kronoff v. Worcester, 234 Mass. 254. 

In Churchill v. Harris, 257 Mass. 499, the owner of land on which was a farmhouse, a bungalow and a spring from which pipes ran to the cellar of the farmhouse, where a pipe had been connected with the pipe from the spring and laid to the bungalow, sold the farmhouse and the land in which the spring was by a deed containing the following provision: "Reserving also to the grantor and his heirs and assigns onehalf of the spring of water which now runs to the buildings on said premises." The Court said: "The recital in the deed that the grantor reserves to himself and his heirs and assigns 'one-half of the spring of water which now runs to the buildings on said premises' is a reservation. The effect of the reservation is to create an easement in the spring appurtenant to the bungalow and the land upon which it is situated. The reservation in the deed by the plaintiff to himself of one-half of the spring results in the plaintiff and the defendants being the owners in fee as tenants in common of so much of the land out of which the spring issues as is necessary for the reasonable use of the spring itself, as well as a right to one-half of the water. . . . The plaintiff is entitled to take water from the spring in any reasonable and convenient manner." 

Inasmuch as an interest in land was retained by the plaintiff, it would seem that the provision quoted from the deed should have been considered as an exception of an interest in the land out of which the spring issued and of one-half the

DEEDS. 127 water. As the court pointed out, the plaintiff, in the absence of an expressed right of access, would have a right of way by necessity to enter upon the land in a reasonable manner to obtain the water, and this right would as well attach to an estate excepted from the deed as to one arising in the grantor by way of reservation. The existing right to have the water carried in the pipes could also, as a matter of law, be the subject of an exception.

Before the Short Forms Act, if a fee was conveyed and a reservation was made to the grantor without words of inheritance, a life estate only was reserved. But see G. L. c. 183, § 13. The rule was different, however, in the case of an exception. No words of inheritance were necessary. The grantor having a fee, whatever estate was excepted by him from the operation of the conveyance would remain in him in fee. 

In White v. Shippee, 216 Mass. 23, the owner of a farm containing a soapstone ledge conveyed a right to mine for soapstone for life to one who did not record the deed for eight years, and three years later conveyed the farm, without referring to the mining grant, to another person, who three years later conveyed to another by a deed containing the following words: "The said premises, subject to a mortgage . . . which, together with the Soapstone Ledge is excepted from the sale." 

There was no evidence that either of the grantees of the farm knew of the previous conveyance of the right to mine. It was held that the quoted words were not a mere reference to the encumbrance consisting of the life estate in the ledge, but were an exception of the ledge from the conveyance. 

An existing easement or quasi-easement may be the subject of an exception. In Simpson v. B. & M. R.R. Co., 176 Mass. 

359, the railroad company filed a location which divided the farm of S into two parts, and later took from him a deed of the strip of land required for its tracks, in which deed appeared the following provision: "Reserving, however, a crossing or right of way to be maintained and kept in repair at the expense of said railroad corporation, and to be thirty feet wide, etc." The Court said: "Whatever may be the law elsewhere, it is settled in this Commonwealth that an easement may be created by way of exception or reservation, and as an excep-

tion may be created by words of reservation, little reliance can be placed upon the language used. The clause in the deed could not operate by way of an exception, because it was a new right of way. Nor could it operate as a reservation beyond the life of the grantor because of the absence of the word 'heirs.'. . . The easement created by the deed continued only during the life of the grantor." See also Foster v. Smith, 211 

Mass. 497, and Childs v. Boston and Maine R.R. 213 Mass. 91.

In Bean v. French, 140 Mass. 229, the owner of a large tract of land conveyed a portion of it, "reserving, however, to myself the privilege of a bridle road in front of the house." This was held to be a reservation and not an exception on the ground that the effect of the clause was to create a right or easement not before existing, and the right which the grantor had to pass and repass over any part of his estate while he held the whole of it was held not to be an existing right of way over the part sold.

In Bowen v. Conner, 6 Cush. 132, two tenants in common (plaintiff and defendant in the case) of a lot of land entirely surrounded by lands of other persons except on one side which bounded on a road, made a division thereof by mutual releases. 

The plaintiff, to whom the back part was assigned, in his release of the front part to the defendant inserted the following language: "Reserving forever a right of way over a street which the grantee is to make from the northwest corner of said granted lot to the road etc." It was said that the language of the deed above quoted secured a right to the plaintiff and his assigns as owners of the rear lot. This implies that the right so created was in fee, although such a decision was not strictly necessary because the original parties to the partition were before the Court. The Court laid stress on the fact that had there been no express reservation, the plaintiff would have had a way of necessity by necessary implication, and then said that the right plainly intended by both parties to be secured to the plaintiff could legally be secured in the manner adopted in the deed, treating the right reserved as an exception. 

The reasoning in support of the last statement was that prior to the deeds the plaintiff as tenant in common had a right to pass over every part of this land at his pleasure, and that

DEEDS. 

129 when he conveyed the front lot he restricted himself from any further right to pass over the whole and every part, and limited himself to the strip 30 feet wide specially described, and that this was a part of the right previously enjoyed, and this he had excepted out of the grant. 

This reasoning appears to be at odds with the decision in Bean v. French, supra. The way claimed by the plaintiff was not in existence when the deeds were given, and accordingly it is difficult to see how the right therein could be the subject of an exception. Unless the case is taken to turn upon the relation of the parties or upon the fact that a right of way by necessity would have been created without any reservation, it seems that it can be justified in the light of later decisions only by saying that the court was leading up to the doctrine of "equitable easement arising from contract" which was later set up in Childs v. Boston & Maine Railroad, 213 Mass. 91.

If a grantor in his deed reserves a right of way over the granted premises for the benefit of the owner of adjoining land, who is a stranger to the deed, such reservation will not of itself create any right or easement in the owner of such adjoining land. Murphy v. Lee, 144 Mass. 371, 374; Haverhill Savings 

Bank v. Griffin, 184 Mass. 419; Kronoff v. Worcester, 234 Mass. 254. 

39. COVENANTS. 

Note the form of the covenants of title in the long form warranty and quitclaim deeds printed in section 15. The short form warranty deed is expressed to be "with warranty covenants," without stating the same at length, and the meaning and effect of a conveyance "with warranty covenants" is set forth in G. L. c. 183, § 16. Likewise, "quitclaim covenants," which are set forth in G. L. c. 183, § 17, are incorporated in the short form deed by reference. Note that the statutory form has it "free from all incumbrances made by the grantor," instead of "made or suffered," as in the long form deed. "The covenants of seizin, of right to convey, and against incumbrances, are broken, if at all, at the moment of the delivery of the deed, and cannot in any event pass to the heirs or assigns of the grantee." - Crocker, page 113.

In Whitney v. Dinsmore, 6 Cush. 124, the Court said: "If there has been any breach of the covenant against incumbrances, it was broken immediately on the delivery of the deed, and so became a chose in action which by law was not assignable."

It should be noted, however, that G. L. c. 184, § 22, provides as follows: Whoever conveys real property by a deed or mortgage which contains a covenant that it is free from all encumbrances shall, if it appears by a public record that an actual or apparent encumbrance, known or unknown to him, exists thereon, be liable in an action of contract to the grantee, his heirs, executors, administrators, successors or assigns, for all damages sustained in removing the same.. 

In Gallison v. Downing, 244 Mass. 33, the Court said: "The provisions of G. L. c. 184, § 22, have no pertinency to the facts here disclosed. . . . The plaintiff has not removed the incumbrances. They continue to exist and have been established by the decrees in the equity suits. The plain words of that section confine the right of recovery to instances where damages are sustained in removing incumbrances, as, for example, by paying mortgages, extinguishing liens, or redeeming from tax sales."

In Dyer v. Scott, 253 Mass. 430, it was held that the word "encumbrance" in the statute has reference to something that encumbers a legal title, and that the statute was not intended to require a grantor who can show a good title by adverse possession to pay his grantee or assigns the expense incurred in making a good title of record. It was also held that the words "public records" include records in the registries of probate. "The covenant of warranty, on the other hand, runs with the land conveyed, and any heir or assignee of the grantee, to whom the estate may afterwards come, is entitled, whether the heirs and assigns of the grantee are or are not mentioned in the covenant, to maintain an action upon it." - Crocker, page 113.

But there can be no continuing breach of the covenant of warranty. Ladd v. Noyes, 137 Mass. 151. "This covenant will not run with the land so that an action can be brought upon it by an assignee of the grantee, unless the grantor is, at the time of his conveyance, seized of the premises

DEEDS. 

131 conveyed. Slater v. Rawson, 1 Met. 450, 456; Bartholomew v.

Candee, 14 Pick. 167. As to what seizin is necessary in order that the covenant may attach, see Slater v. Rawson, 6 Met. 

439." - Crocker, page 124. Quere, was this rule affected by 

St. 1891, c. 354, now G. L. c. 183, § 7? "The covenant against incumbrances is broken if a third person at the time of the conveyance had a right to or interest in the land granted, which diminished the value of the absolute interest in such land, while it was consistent with the passing of the fee by the deed."— Crocker, page 118.

Illustrations: The existence of a mortgage upon the granted premises has been held to be a breach of this covenant (Wyman v. Ballard, 12 Mass. 303; Tufts v. Adams, 8 Pick. 547); or a public town way (Kellogg v. Ingersoll, 2 Mass. 97); or a private right of way over the same (Harlow v. Thomas, 15 Pick. 

66; Wetherbee v. Bennett, 2 Allen, 428); or the right of a third person to cut and remove standing timber therefrom (Spurr v. Andrew, 6 Allen, 420); or an inchoate right of dower therein (Shearer v. Ranger, 22 Pick. 447; Bigelow v. Hubbard, 97 Mass. 195, 198; Harrington v. Murphy, 109 Mass. 299); or a tax which had been assessed upon the premises, though it had not been committed to the collector (Cochran v. Guild, 106 Mass. 29). And, as under the Massachusetts statute taxes on real estate are assessed as of the first day of April (May 1 prior to 1910), they become an incumbrance on that date.

In order to constitute a breach of the covenant of warranty it is necessary that there should be not only a want or defect of title, but an actual eviction or ouster, or what in law is deemed equivalent thereto. 

In Kramer v. Carter, 136 Mass. 504, it was stated: "It may be said in general that any adverse assertion of a paramount right by which the covenantee is deprived of the enjoyment of the granted premises, or any part of them, or to which he yields, as by giving an equivalent for the paramount estate, will be such an eviction as will constitute a breach of the covenant of warranty. The voluntary payment of a mortgage upon demand shows a breach of that covenant. Sprague v. 

Baker, 17 Mass. 586. So the purchase by an assignee of the grantee of an equity of redemption sold on execution against

the grantor. Whitney v. Dinsmore, 6 Cush. 124. So the recording of a certificate of entry by a mortgagor for condition broken. Furnas v. Durgin, 119 Mass. 500. In these cases, and in others which might be cited, there was no ouster; there was nothing more than the assertion of a paramount right adversely to the right claimed by the tenant, with a yielding to that right by payment of money to procure its extinguishment, or the mere assertion of the right by an act in derogation of the title claimed." 

It will be deemed equivalent to eviction if the covenantee consents to dispossession by one having a paramount title. 

But in such cases the burden will be upon the covenantee to show that the title to which he yields is good, whereas in case of an eviction by force of a judgment at law, with notice of the suit to the warrantor, the judgment itself, unless obtained by fraud, will be plenary evidence of the validity of such title. 

Hamilton v. Cutts, 4 Mass. 349, 352.

In Gallison v. Downing, 244 Mass. 33, it was held that an action for breach of a covenant of warranty is brought prematurely if the breach relied on is a restriction against the maintenance of a building on the land and the plaintiff, while he refuses to yield to a claim based on the restriction and waits to bring his action against the covenantor until in a suit in equity to enforce the restriction an injunction pendente lite has been issued against him, nevertheless brings it before a final decree against him has been entered. 

One who has been duly vouched in to defend a title which he has covenanted to warrant and defend will be bound by the result of the suit, establishing the adverse title, although he did not appear therein, and although it was decided upon an agreed statement of facts, in which a fact was misstated, which if correctly stated would have defeated the adverse title, provided, such statement was agreed to in good faith and without collusion. Chamberlain v. Preble, 11 Allen, 370.

As to what constitutes a breach of a limited covenant of warranty against persons claiming by, through or under the grantor, see Tarbell v. Page, 155 Mass. 256, and Weeks v. 

Grace, 194 Mass. 296. In the first case a second mortgagee conveyed part of the mortgaged premises by a quitclaim deed which did not refer to the mortgages and in which he war-

DEEDS. 

133 ranted against all persons claiming through or under the grantor, and subsequently acquired the first mortgage. Held, that he was not precluded by his prior conveyance from obtaining title under the first mortgage and enforcing it against his grantee. In the latter case it was decided that the appropriation of private property for a public use under the right of eminent domain is a proceeding in rem, and the title acquired is an independent one not derived from that of the owner of the land. Therefore the existence of an easement which was taken by a city under the right of eminent domain in a strip of land for the purpose of laying and maintaining a sewer is not a breach of covenant in a deed of the land warranting the title against the lawful claims and demands of all persons claiming by, through or under the grantor. (Two justices dissenting.) 

Neither did the building and maintaining of a public sewer through a part of the granted premises constitute a breach of the covenant against incumbrances, as the action of the public authorities was neither made nor suffered by the grantor. 

The granted premises. By these words are meant, not the parcel of land described by metes and bounds, but the interest in such parcel which the deed purports to convey. Note earlier statements as to the effect of the words “right, title and interest" before the description, section 28 of these notes. "So if the parcel of land described in the granting part of the deed is there stated to be subject to a Lortgage or other incumbrance, the 'granted premises' referred to in the covenants are to be deemed to be the parcel of land subject to the mortgage or other incumbrance, and consequently the covenantor will not make himself liable on account of the incumbrance by failing to mention it as an exception under his covenants." - Crocker, page 114, citing Brown v. South Boston 

Savings Bank, 148 Mass. 300.

But the fact that in a covenant some of the incumbrances are mentioned while others are not may show that it was intended that "granted premises" should refer to the land simply without regard to the incumbrance. Ayer v. Philadelphia Face 

Brick Co., 157 Mass. 57, 58, 60.

In the case last mentioned a second mortgage of land recited that the land was conveyed subject to a "certain right of

drainage, a certain easement and the mortgage hereinafter named," and the grantor covenanted that he was seized in fee of the "aforegranted premises," that they were free from all incumbrances "except a certain mortgage," describing it, "the right of drainage and the easement aforesaid," and that he would warrant and defend same "against the lawful claims and demands of all persons except the right of drainage and the easement aforesaid." Held, that the covenant of warranty included the first mortgage. 40. 

ESTOPPEL. 

A covenant of warranty will so operate by way of estoppel or rebutter in cases where the warrantor at the time of making the deed has no good title, but afterwards acquires one, that such title, when acquired by him, will inure to the benefit of the covenantee or his heirs or assigns. White v. Patten, 24 Pick. 

324; Ayer v. Brick Co., 157 Mass. 57; Knight v. Thayer, 125 Mass. 25, 27; Huzzey v. Heffernan, 143 Mass. 232.

In Cole v. Raymond, 9 Gray, 217, the Court said: “It is a well-established rule of law that although a deed as a present conveyance transfers only the title which the grantor then has, yet if it is a deed in fee, with warranty, it has a further operation as a covenant real running with the land by which the grantor and his heirs are bound to make it good, so that if the grantor has no good and sufficient title to the estate, yet if he or they afterwards acquire a good title, it forthwith inures to the benefit of the grantee, to the same extent as if the grantor and warrantor had had the same good title at the date of the grant and warranty, to operate by way of estoppel, if the action be brought in such form that it may be pleaded by way of estoppel; otherwise, by way of rebuttal to the claim of any one bound by such warranty." 

In Ayer v. Philadelphia Face Brick Co., supra, the first mortgage was foreclosed and the title came by mesne conveyances to the person who gave the second mortgage, and it was held that he was estopped to set up his title so acquired against the second mortgage given by himself, or, to express the matter in another way, the second mortgage which was cut off by the

DEEDS. 

135 foreclosure of the first was revived as a valid first mortgage by the acquisition of the title by the second mortgagor. 

Huzzey v. Heffernan, supra, is the reverse of this. In this case A mortgaged land to B subject to a prior mortgage to a bank, and excepted from the covenant of warranty in the second mortgage any claims under the prior mortgage. The bank foreclosed the first mortgage, and, under the power of sale contained therein, sold the land to C, who subsequently conveyed the land to the bank by whom it was again conveyed to A, and afterwards by mesne conveyances was acquired by D. 

Held, that B could not maintain a writ of entry against D to recover the land.

An adverse and exclusive possession of land for a period of twenty years is a good bar to a writ of entry to recover the same, although the demandant's title may have been derived through mesne conveyances from the tenant; nor is the tenant estopped by his covenants of warranty in the deed to his original grantee from setting up a subsequent title acquired by disseisin. Stearns v. Hendersass, 9 Cush. 497. Here it was. pointed out that the foundation of the rule of estoppel or rebutter rests upon the fact that the person conveying with warranty had not the title he professed to convey, and was liable therefor upon his warranty. But such was not the case here. 

It was a good title that was conveyed, and there was no breach of any covenant, and, of course, no ground for estoppel against the tenant by reason of his covenant with warranty.

In the case of Whitman, Petitioner, Land Court Decisions, page 9, the question was whether, by the giving of a quitclaim deed, an estoppel is created so that title subsequently acquired by the grantor will enure to the grantee. The Court referred to Mr. Crocker's statement touching the matter which was (page 133): "Where the grantor warrants only against himself and those claiming under him, it would seem that he will not be estopped from setting up a paramount title afterwards acquired by him," and expressed the opinion that such a limitation is not justified, either by the cases cited by Mr. Crocker or by the principle of law which underlies them. After referring to numerous cases decided in this Commonwealth and elsewhere, the Court said: "In a full warranty deed there may

be a limited grant, coupled with an unlimited warranty. In such cases the estoppel arises under the covenant alone. In a quitclaim deed the force of the covenant, apart from the necessary effect of the conveyance, will not be sufficient of itself to create an estoppel by virtue of which a subsequently acquired paramount estate will enure to the grantee. In Jones on Real Property, section 992, it is said that 'aside from the estoppel arising from the conveyance of a particular estate, only a warranty deed operates to transfer an after acquired title of the grantor. A quitclaim deed cannot have that effect.'

The phrase 'aside from the estoppel arising from the conveyance' is clearly intended to qualify both statements. In the case at bar the estoppel arises from the grant. . . . Monahan [grantor] is estopped by his grant.” 

41. COVENANTS WHICH RUN WITH THE LAND. 

In Boston Ice Co. v. Potter, 123 Mass. 28, A, who had bought ice of B, ceased to take it on account of dissatisfaction with B, and contracted for ice with C. Subsequently B bought C's business and delivered ice to A, without notifying him of his purchase until after the delivery and consumption of the ice. Held, that B could not maintain an action for the price of the ice against A. And the Court said that this was so for the reason that there was no privity of contract established between the plaintiff and defendant.

There is, however, a class of cases having to do with agreements concerning real property wherein actions at law are maintained by a plaintiff against a defendant with whom he has no contractual relation. An illustration of this is furnished by the ordinary party wall indenture by which the owners of adjoining parcels of land agree that the first to build may erect the dividing wall half on the land of each, and that such wall shall be a party wall and that the adjoining owner, when he uses the same for a building on his land, shall pay to the owner of the estate for which the wall was first erected a proportional part of the cost of the wall. There is no difficulty in enforcing this agreement if the wall is used by the second estate while the parties to the agreement are still the owners of the parcels of land described therein. Between these two there is privity

DEEDS. 137 of contract, but if the party who built the wall has conveyed to X, and if the other party to the agreement has conveyed to Y, and Y has used the wall, clearly there is no privity of contract between X and Y. Nevertheless, in such a case X may recover from Y in an action at law the sum which Y's predecessor in title agreed to pay in the event that he should use the wall, and the courts justify such an action on the theory that there is between the parties "privity of estate," and such an agreement is called a "Covenant running with the Land." 

Savage v. Mason, 3 Cush. 500.

It is believed that this topic presents as much of technicality and more unsatisfactory decisions than any other single subject connected with the law of real property. No attempt will be made to state more than an outline of the fundamental rules and to call attention to some of the modern developments of those rules.

The great landmark of the law on this topic is Spencer's 

Case (Queen's Bench, 1583), 5 Co. 16a, included in Vol. 2 of

Gray's Cases on Real Property. Although this case concerned an agreement between landlord and tenant, the "resolutions" stated in the opinion have been referred to repeatedly in cases involving agreements between parties standing in other relations. The "resolutions" of Spencer's Case are condensed and discussed ably in Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 835. 

Generally speaking, covenants in leases run with the land. 

Examples: By the lessor, to renew the lease; by the lessee, to pay taxes; not to use the premises for any unlawful purpose. 

Recent Massachusetts cases touching upon covenants in leases are Lamson v. Coulson, 234 Mass. 288; Taylor r. Kennedy, 228 Mass. 390; Watts v. Bruce, 245 Mass. 531; Saxeny v. 

Panis, 239 Mass. 207; Marsylax v. Fox, 260 Mass. 127.

The writer believes that much of the confusion attaching to this subject has been caused by the failure generally of the courts and text writers to distinguish between covenants in leases and covenants contained in other forms of conveyance and agreement affecting real property, and suggests that the Massachusetts Court has actually held to such a distinction, although the application of the theory is not always easily understood.

66 In brief, it is said that in order that a covenant may run with land, there must be "privity of estate" between the parties; and the covenant must "touch or concern" or extend to the support of" the land as to which privity of estate exists, and not be merely collateral to it. For example, A, owning a parcel of land, conveys a portion of it to B, bounding it on the remaining parcel by a line running through the middle of a party wall. Assuming for the moment that there is then "privity of estate" between the parties as to the wall, it undoubtedly would follow that a covenant by A to maintain and keep in repair the whole wall without contribution from B would be held to "touch or concern" the wall, and that it would run with the land of both parties. But if A also covenanted to keep in repair a wall on other land of B which A never owned, and in which he never had any interest, such a covenant would be merely a personal contract and would not run. 

In Spillane v. Yarnalowicz, 252 Mass. 168, it was held that a covenant by a mortgagor of real estate to keep the premises insured for the benefit of the mortgagee does not run with the land, and a grantee of the mortgagor who does not assume the mortgage is not bound, in the absence of a further contract with the mortgagee, to perform such a covenant. 

Covenants in conveyances in fee naturally fall into two classes: (1) covenants for title and (2) other covenants. This distinction is necessary because, as will appear, the theory of 'privity of estate" as applied to these two classes is not the 66 same.

Again, in considering the rights or interests which may attach to and run with land, the following classification should be observed: (1) legal easements; (2) covenants which run with the land at law; (3) equitable easements or covenants which run with the land in equity. 

Legal Easements.

As the general subject of "Easements" has not yet been considered, an example may be of assistance. A owns a parcel of land and B owns the adjoining parcel. A grants to B in fee, as appurtenant to B's land, a right of way over A's

DEEDS. 

139 land, neither making any agreement or covenant relating thereto. This creates a legal easement appurtenant to B's land which goes with it to subsequent owners, whether or not mentioned in later conveyances. 

Covenants which Run with the Land at Law. 

Before proceeding to discuss "privity of estate," which is where the greatest difficulty lies, we may properly refer to one other special rule of law which seems to be well established in Massachusetts.

In section 35 it was pointed out that the grantee in a deed poll, by accepting the deed, binds himself to perform such acts as by its terms are to be performed by him, and that his grantor may have an action at law against him for damages resulting from his failure to perform. The next step is to inquire whether such agreements run with the land. Considered strictly as covenants, in this Commonwealth they do not.

In Martin v. Drinan, 128 Mass. 515, it was held that an agreement, by the grantee in a deed poll, to keep in repair a building on adjoining land of the grantor, not being under the seal of the grantee, was not a covenant, and would not sustain an action against her by the assignee of the grantor. 

In Kennedy v. Owen, 136 Mass. 199, it was held that a stipulation in a deed poll, that the grantee, his heirs and assigns, should make and maintain a fence between the granted premises and adjoining land of the grantor, was not a covenant, and would not sustain an action by a subsequent owner of the adjoining land against a purchaser from the grantee in the deed poll. The Court said: "It is also clear that the stipulation is not technically a covenant running with the land. The grantee sealed nothing. It is plain that an agreement not under seal cannot, technically speaking, run with the land. . . . It (referring to the stipulation above stated) appears to be rather a mere personal obligation, imposed upon and assumed by the grantee, and binding upon him and his legal representatives as an implied contract entered into with the grantor, not amounting to a covenant, or a charge upon the land, but an obligation,

which, if enforceable at all against purchasers, is to be enforced against them by a Court of Equity alone. . . ." See 

Whittenton Manuf. Co. v. Staples, 164 Mass. 319, at page 328.

The reverse of the foregoing case is Bronson v. Coffin, 108 

Mass. 175. This case concerned a deed made by Timothy G.

Coffin to a railroad company which conveyed a strip of land bounded on each side by lands retained by Coffin, and afterwards granted by his devisees to the plaintiff, which deed contained the clause: "I, the said T. G. C., hereby covenant that

I and my heirs and assigns will make and maintain a sufficient fence through the whole length of that part of the railroad which runs through my farm; this covenant of maintaining the fence to be perpetual and obligatory upon me and all persons who shall become owners of the land on each side of the railroad." This agreement was held to create a covenant running with the land. The Court said, in part: "The necessary conclusion is, upon principle and authority, that the terms of Coffin's deed conveyed to his grantees an interest in the nature of an easement in his adjoining lands, and thus created a sufficient privity of estate between them and his assigns to support the covenant to maintain the fence as a covenant running with the lands adjoining." 

See Thayer Foss Co. v. Woburn, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 2145, discussed in section 35. Here it was expressly pointed out that an agreement in a deed poll was not a covenant running with the land, but was binding in equity on the successors in title of the grantee in favor of the grantors and their successors in title. 

In Orenberg v. Johnston, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 2273, land was conveyed by the Harvard Church by deed which contained the following: "The grantee consents and agrees as part of the consideration hereof to keep the tower clock in its present position or substantially so or to erect it in some other public and conspicuous place in Charlestown to be approved by the City of Boston." After this conveyance the church had no other real estate to which any easement, or right in the nature of an easement or servitude, could be appurtenant as a dominant tenement. It was held that this provision did not prevent the grantee from conveying a good

DEEDS. 

141 and clear title free from all encumbrances, and it was said that the fact that there was no other land to which the promise concerning the clock could be annexed made it unenforceable, either at law or in equity, against any successor in title to the grantee, and that the alternative obligation to erect the clock in some other public place was one which obviously had no relation to the real estate conveyed. 

Now to consider briefly the meaning of "privity of estate." 

In Hurd v. Curtis, 19 Pick. 459, the several owners of mills drawing water from the same stream by means of the same dam entered into an indenture in which, for themselves, their heirs, administrators and assigns, respectively, they covenanted with each other, and their respective heirs, administrators and assigns, that they would erect and use wheels of a certain construction and limited power in their respective mills. It was held, that there was no privity of estate between the parties to the indenture, and consequently that the covenant did not run with the land and bind the grantee of one of the mills. 

The Court said: “To make a defendant liable to an action of covenant, there must be a privity between him and the plaintiff. As there is no privity of contract between the plaintiff and defendants, it follows that the defendants are not liable in this action, unless there is privity of estate between them. 

Where such a privity exists between the covenantor and covenantee, and the covenantor assigns his estate, the privity thereby created between the assignee and the other contracting party renders the former liable on all such covenants as regulate the mode of occupying the estate, and the like covenants concerning the same. And so if the covenantee assigns his estate, his assignee will have the benefit of similar covenants. These covenants are annexed to the land and run with it. But if there is no privity of estate between the contracting parties, the assignee will not be bound by, nor have the benefit of, any covenants between the contracting parties, although they may relate to the land he takes by assignment or purchase from one of the parties to the contract. In such a case the covenants are personal and are collateral to the land. "Covenants for title may be considered as an exception to the general rule, and the reason for the exception is very strong,

for nothing can be more manifestly just than that the party who loses his land by a defect of title should have the benefit of the covenants which were intended to secure an indemnity for the loss. Such a covenant is dependent on the grant, is annexed to it, as part and parcel of the contract, and runs with the land in favor of the assigns of the grantee or covenantee; but there is no exception to the rule, that no covenant will run with the land so as to bind the assignee to perform it, unless there were a privity of estate between the covenantor and covenantee."

Mr. Tiffany, in his treatise on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 

1410, says: "Privity of estate, however, as between a grantor and grantee in fee simple has a meaning different from that which it has as between lessor and lessee, and their successors in interest. In the latter case, privity of estate means the simultaneous ownership by both parties of estates in the land, while in the former case it can mean merely succession in ownership. It was occasionally used in the latter sense by 

Coke, and perhaps other early authorities, particularly in connection with the law of warranty, and conceding the necessity of privity of estate in order that the burden of a covenant, not contained in a lease, may run with the land, it was reasonable to regard this requirement as satisfied by the succession in ownership which was included under this designation by the earlier writers. "In at least two states the conveyance of an estate in the land, as distinguished from the grant of an easement therein, has been held not to furnish the privity of estate necessary to the creation of a covenant which will run with the land, the theory being, apparently, that a mere succession in interest is not sufficient for this purpose, but that the simultaneous existence of two distinct interests in the land, in the covenantor and covenantee respectively, is necessary." 

In Norcross v. James, 140 Mass. 188, 191, the Court, referring to covenants in deeds other than covenants for title, said: "But the expression 'privity of estate' in this sense is of modern use, and has been carried over from the cases of warranty, where it was used with a wholly different meaning." 

In section 39 it was pointed out that the covenant of war-

DEEDS. 143 ranty in a deed from A to B runs with the land. There is, of course, privity of contract between A and B. Suppose that 

B conveys to C, and there then occurs a breach of the covenant. C may recover against A in an action at law on the covenant. Now, since there is not privity of contract between 

A and C, we must bring in the doctrine of "privity of estate," but the privity of estate which is thus required is privity with the original covenantee, not with the original covenantor, in other words, merely the "succession in ownership" mentioned by Tiffany. 

In Morse v. Aldrich, 19 Pick. 449, the Court said: "To create a covenant which will run with the land it is necessary that there should be a privity of estate between the covenantor and covenantee. [Citing English cases.] In these cases, and in most cases on the same subject, the covenants were between lessors and lessees, but the same privity exists between the grantor and grantee where a grant is made of any subordinate interest in land; the reversion or residue of the estate being reserved by the grantor, all covenants in support of the grant, or in relation to the beneficial enjoyment of it, are real covenants and will bind the assignee."

In Bronson v. Coffin, supra, the Court said: "In order to make a covenant run with the land of the covenantor and bind his heirs and assigns, the covenantee must, according to all the authorities, have such an interest in that land as to amount to a privity of estate between the parties to the covenant. In this 

Commonwealth, at least, it is not necessary that their relation should be that of landlord and tenant; but an interest in the nature of an easement in the land which the covenant purports to bind, whether already existing, or created by the very deed which contains the covenant, constitutes a sufficient privity of estate to make the burden of a covenant to do certain acts upon the land, for the support and protection of that interest and the beneficial use and enjoyment of the land granted, run with the land charged." See also Norcross v. James, supra, at page 191. 

It should be noted that the rule in Bronson v. Coffin, supra, while it appears to be good law in Massachusetts (see N. Y. C. & H. R. R.R. v. Clarke, 228 Mass. 274) is open to some ob-

jection. In Hurd v. Curtis, supra, it was said that "the stipulations in the indenture cannot be construed as grants and covenants at the same time." Apparently Bronson v. Coffin runs close to this, and it might well be argued that if the agreement concerning the fence be construed as a grant, then there was no covenant, but if, on the other hand, the language be construed as a covenant, then there was no grant, and that if there was not a grant there could be no easement, and if no easement, there was no privity of estate. 

The writer believes that, aside from covenants for title, the Massachusetts decisions are satisfied by defining "privity of estate" as "Mutual interests in the land which is the subject of the agreement." The interest of one party may be exclusive for the time being if the other is entitled to a certain interest at a future time, as where A has an estate for years and B has the reversion; or the interests may be identical in point of time while differing in quality, as where A owns the fee in a parcel of land and B has a right of way or other easement therein; or the interests may be identical as to both time and quality, as where the dividing line between lands of A and B is the center line of a passageway used in common by both of them, or where such dividing line is the center of a party wall. 

As bearing on the above, it should be noted that it has been held in Massachusetts that a covenant created in connection with an easement was held to run even though not entered into till after the grant of the easement. See Morse v. Aldrich, 19 

Pick. 449.

Much has been written and said concerning the distinction between "benefits" and "burdens" of covenants as running with the land. See Tiffany, Vol. 2, page 1405, and Lincoln v. Burrage, 177 Mass. 378. Apparently the English jurists have been unwilling to allow "burdens" to run with the land of the covenantor. In Massachusetts, it is clear that burdens do run if there is privity of estate between the parties, and the covenant touches or concerns the estate in which the privity exists. See Bronson v. Coffin, supra; King v. Wight, 155 Mass. 444; Whittenton Mfg. Co. v. Staples, 164 Mass. 319, 327. 

Mr. Tiffany also said (Vol. 2, page 1415): "The intention

DEEDS. 

145 of the parties to the covenant that it shall run with the land is occasionally referred to as a consideration of importance in determining whether it does run, but in the great majority of cases no reference is made to this matter, the question whether the covenant runs being regarded as one to be determined by the consideration whether it touches and concerns the land. 

The correct rule appears to be that the parties to the covenant may, by indicating an intention to that effect, prevent the covenant from running, although it is such that otherwise it would run, while if the covenant is one which does not touch and concern the land, the parties cannot make it run by indicating an intention or desire that it shall run."

It is believed that the foregoing statement is sound in the light of Massachusetts decisions, at least so far as covenants running with the land at law are concerned, although it is a fact that our Court has referred to an express provision that a covenant should be deemed to run with the land as supporting its conclusion that it did run. See Savage v. Mason, 3 Cush. 

500. No doubt other such references may be found. They go along with the similar remark, which is often inserted for the same purpose, to the effect that the covenant purports to bind "heirs and assigns." This latter has been brought over from the lease cases and relates to the second resolution in Spencer's case, which was that a covenant concerning a thing not in esse could not run with the land unless assigns were mentioned. But it should be noted that in King v. Wight, supra, the Court said: "The fact that it (an instrument) does not purport in terms to create covenants running with the land is not of much moment. 

The effect of the instrument is to be gathered from it as a whole,' and in Morse v. Aldrich, supra: "If then the covenant in question runs with the land, it is clear that the defendants are liable; and it is immaterial whether the heirs and assigns of the covenantor are named in the covenant, or not." 

Maine v. Cumston, 98 Mass. 317, appears to run somewhat contra to Kennedy v. Owen, supra, and to stand quite alone. 

This was an action of contract for the cost of half of a division wall. The city of Boston executed an agreement to convey a lot of land to A, which agreement provided that, so long as the lot should remain unoccupied, he should allow the owner

of the adjoining lot to build one-half of a partition wall on A's lot; that, if this was done and A should later use the wall, he should pay to the proprietor so building a proportional part of the cost of the wall; and that A, if he built first, should erect one-half the wall on the adjoining lot. A assigned the agreement to B, who built accordingly, and the city conveyed the lot to B by a deed containing a repetition of the above conditions. B later conveyed to C and C to the plaintiff.
