Later the city executed an agreement to convey the adjoining lot to the defendant, subject to conditions similar to those above mentioned. Defendant, after the conveyance by C to plaintiff, built a house, and in so doing used that portion of the wall of plaintiff's house which had been built on the adjoining lot. Thereafter the city, at defendant's request, conveyed the lot on which he had built to H, and defendant never had a deed thereof. Judgment for plaintiff. This went on the theory that the plaintiff under his deed had a right to build and maintain the whole wall as part of his house, until some one claiming under the common owner built on the adjacent lot and used the wall. When that took place, the plaintiff's property in that part of the wall on the adjacent lot ceased, except so far as he might claim a right or easement of support in it as a partition wall, and he became entitled to recover for half the cost of its erection. The Court said: "Not having signed and sealed the deed (meaning, of course, the agreement), he (defendant) is not liable for breach of covenant; but by accepting the deed he assumes the performance of the condition or stipulation, from which the law will imply a promise on which an action may be maintained." Distinguish Standish v. Lawrence, 111 Mass. 111, and Richardson v. Tobey, 121 

Mass. 457, and see comment in Bronson v. Coffin, supra, at page 184. See also Joy v. Boston Penny Savings Bank, 115 

Mass. 60, and Lincoln v. Burrage, 177 Mass. 378. In the latter case the Court referred to Maine v. Cumston as one of the cases which has gone the farthest in establishing privity of contract between parties to the suit and in implying a contract in favor of the plaintiffs simply on the ground that a contract was made with some one else.

Perhaps it would have been more consistent if the Court had

DEEDS. 

147 decided in Maine v. Cumston, supra, that the plaintiff could not recover in an action at law, but that the defendant was liable in equity as was suggested in Kennedy v. Owen, supra. 

Covenants Running with the Land in Equity.

Covenants running with the land in equity commonly include only restrictive covenants. In equity, the rule, generally speaking, is that assigns who take with notice are bound. Tulk v. 

Moxhay, Chancery, 1848, 2 Phil. 774; Gray's Cases, Vol. II.

Under our registry system, the recording of a covenant or agreement affecting land works constructive notice upon all who thereafter acquire title thereto. Peck v. Conway, 119 Mass. 546. 

It was held by a majority of the Court in Whittenton Manufacturing Company v. Staples, 164 Mass. 319, that a stipulation in a deed poll of a mill site, for the use of which and four others on the same stream the grantor had built a reservoir dam, that the grantee and his assigns should pay one-fifth of the flowage damages caused by the dam, imposed an obligation in the nature of an easement or servitude upon the estate, which could be enforced in equity, though not as a personal obligation, against a subsequent grantee. The Court also pointed out that in this Commonwealth such a provision in a deed poll does not constitute a technical covenant. Cases of this character, and, indeed, cases which result in the enforcement of other than equitable easements, are not common. 

There is another sort of incumbrance created by a deed poll, which runs with the land, which will be mentioned now, although perhaps the matter falls more properly under the topic of Easements. 

In Bailey v. Agawam National Bank, 190 Mass. 20, a deed by the owner of two adjoining lots of land conveying one of them contained this provision: "A passageway is to be kept open and for use in common between the two houses ten feet in width, five feet of said passageway to be furnished by said H (the grantee) and five feet by me from land lying east of the land here conveyed." There was no passageway there before the deed was made. Held, that this provision was not a reservation nor an exception, but was a contract imposing a

right in perpetuity for the benefit of adjoining lands which could be enforced in equity against any one taking with notice of it, and so created an incumbrance on the granted land. 

The Court does not name this species of interest, but in Childs v. Boston & Maine Railroad, 213 Mass. 91, we find a similar interest labeled “a right in the nature of an equitable restriction over land of the defendant arising by contract, which in effect established a quasi easement respecting the perpetual use and enjoyment of real estate." A more convenient designation appears in the head note, namely, "an equitable easement founded on contract."

In Childs v. Boston & Maine Railroad, in 1846 the owner of a farm conveyed by deed to a railroad corporation land partly within the railroad location of the grantee which divided the farm of the grantor. The deed contained at the end of the description the following: "And it is understood and agreed that the said Company are to make me a good and sufficient crossing etc." A crossing was constructed, which was used by the grantor until he conveyed his farm to another, who thereafter used the crossing until it was closed by the successor of the railroad corporation. It was held that the clause quoted could not operate as an exception because it created a new right of way not before used or existing, the burden of which was placed upon the railroad company; that it could not operate as a reservation in fee because of the omission of the word "heirs;" that it could not operate as a covenant running with the land because the grantee sealed nothing; but that it did create an equitable easement founded on contract which could be enforced in equity.

In Apsey v. Nash, 229 Mass. 77, the Court said: “It is plain that Bailey v. Agawam National Bank, 190 Mass. 20, is authority only for the rule that a court of equity will specifically enforce against a promisor for value, and all others having actual notice thereof, a written agreement to maintain in perpetuity a passageway over land of the promisor for the use of land sold to the promisee at the delivery of the agreement; and it is equally plain that the rule of that case does not extend to promises and agreements arising by implication merely. 

Sprague v. Kimball, 213 Mass. 380."

DEEDS. 149 It also seems advisable here to mention two Massachusetts cases dealing with restrictions on land especially for the purpose of directing attention to the tendency to bring into decisions in equity the technical rules which were evolved and declared in actions at law.

In Norcross v. James, 140 Mass. 188, K conveyed to F a quarry bounded by other land of K with a covenant as follows: "And I do for myself, my heirs, executors and administrators, covenant with the said F, his heirs and assigns, that I will not open or work, or allow any person or persons to open or work, any quarry or quarries on my farm," etc.

By mesne conveyances A became possessed of the quarry conveyed to F and B of the surrounding land referred to in the covenant, and A brought a bill in equity to restrain B from quarrying stone on his own land. The bill was dismissed. The 

Court, after discussion of the rules relating to covenants which run with the land, said that notwithstanding the fact that the covenant in question was found among the covenants for title, it purported to create a pure negative restriction on the use of the land, and it was taken as intended to do so for the benefit of the land conveyed. It was then stated that the question was whether or not the covenant was of a kind which the law permits to be attached to land. "For equity will no more enforce every restriction that can be devised, than the common law will recognize as creating an easement every grant purporting to limit the use of land in favor of other land. The principle of policy applied to affirmative covenants applies also to negative ones. They must 'touch or concern,' or 'extend to the support of the thing' conveyed. .. They must be 'for the benefit of the estate.' . . . Or, as it is said more broadly, new and unusual incidents cannot be attached to land, by way either of benefit or of burden. In what way does it [the covenant] extend to the support of the plaintiff's quarry? It does not make the use or occupation of it more convenient. It does not in any way affect the use or occupation; it simply tends indirectly to increase its value, by excluding a competitor from the market for its products. . . . The scope of the covenant and the circumstances show that it is not directed to the quiet enjoyment of the dominant land. Again, this covenant • •

illustrates the further meaning of the rule against unusual incidents. If it is of a nature to be attached to land, as the plaintiff contends, it creates an easement of monopoly, - an easement not to be competed with, and in that interest alone a right to prohibit an owner from exercising the usual incidents of property. . . . We think that, if this covenant were regarded as one which bound all subsequent owners of the land to keep its products out of commerce, there would be much greater difficulty in sustaining its validity than if it should be treated as merely personal in its burden. Whether the latter is its true construction, as well as its only legal operation, and whether, so construed, it is or is not valid, are matters on which we express no opinion." 

Shade v. M. O'Keefe, Inc., 260 Mass. 180, was a bill in equity to enjoin defendants from conducting a grocery store. 

The land belonging to both plaintiff and defendant formerly belonged to the same persons who, in 1902, conveyed defendants' land to their predecessor in title by deed containing the following provision: "The above described premises are conveyed and accepted subject to the following restriction, that a grocery business shall not be carried on in any building which may be erected on said premises. This restriction shall continue for a period of ninety-nine years and shall be for the benefit of the adjoining property of the grantors." Later, the grantors conveyed their remaining land to one Felton who deceased intestate, the plaintiffs being his heirs at law. At the time when the first deed was given, and at the date when the bill was brought, there was a grocery store in operation on the plaintiff's land. The bill was dismissed. It was said that the deed containing a stipulation by words of restriction imposed an implied contract on the grantee, which was enforceable as between him and his grantors at law for damages, or in equity to restrain its breach without showing actual damages, and "An action cannot be maintained against an assignee of the promisor or in the name of an assignee of the promisee. 

Martin v. Drinan, 128 Mass. 515." Of course, an action at law would not lie except between the parties to the instrument. 

The agreement could not run with the land at law because, as was said in Kennedy v. Owen, 136 Mass. 199, "The grantee

DEEDS. 

151 sealed nothing." The Court then said that the present suit could not be maintained unless it appears from the terms of the deed and the circumstances attending its execution that the restriction "touches or concerns the estate of the grantee, is not wholly collateral to it, and imposes and carries with it a grant of an easement or quasi easement in the land owned by the defendant, for the benefit of the land of the covenantee," and cited a Virginia case and four Massachusetts cases, including Clapp v. Wilder, 176 Mass. 332. The decision in the latter case was that a certain provision in the deed before the Court created a common law condition and not merely a restriction, and that the plaintiff had failed to show that the condition was created for the benefit of his land, and therein it was said: "There is no language in the deed expressly stating that this condition was inserted for the benefit of the remaining land. It would have been easy to say that it was so inserted if that had been the intention of the grantor." The grantor did so express his intention in the Slade case. It is difficult to accept Clapp v. Wilder as authority for the statement following which it was cited in the Slade case. The other three Massachusetts cases cited were actions at law. 

The opinion continues: "The implied promise of the grantee not to carry on a grocery business in any building which may be erected on said premises, like the covenant in Norcross v. 

James, supra, does not make the use or occupation of the land more convenient. It does not in any way affect the use or occupation. It simply tends to increase its value by excluding a competition from the market operated or to be operated on the retained land. In a word, the restriction is in the nature of a collateral easement which cannot be annexed to the land. 

The case in principle cannot be distinguished from Norcross v. 

James, supra, and is governed by it." some. 

From a conveyancer's viewpoint, these decisions are trouble- The reference to the technicalities of the common law, the citation of cases which were actions at law, and the rules stated, all seem to make for uncertainty as to what manner of restrictions will or will not "run with the land." Of course, these cases are not to be taken as deciding generally that restrictions against a use to which the land to be benefited is

being put will not run with the granted premises. If this were so, if the owner of a parcel of land with buildings thereon sold an unoccupied portion of his land subject to the restriction that no buildings should be erected thereon, such restriction would not be enforceable against the assignee of his grantee. 

Again, it seems that the decisions are not to be taken as establishing the rule that restrictions against a particular mercantile use will not run with land. If this were so, the decision in the Slade case would necessarily have been the same, although the grantor's remaining land had been occupied by a fine residence. It is hoped that it will develop that the decisions in question are applicable only to restrictions against a mercantile pursuit similar or substantially similar to the business which is being conducted on the premises sought to be benefited, and that this rule rests mainly upon the fact that to hold otherwise would be to give assistance to an easement of monoply which would be objectionable under the rule against unusual incidents. 

See, however, Sheff v. The Candy Box Inc., Adv. Sh. 1931, page 425, where a different rule was held applicable to a restrictive provision inserted in a lease.

42. DATE.

In the absence of evidence to the contrary, the presumption is that a deed was executed on the day on which it bears date, and the fact that it was not acknowledged until a later day does not affect this presumption. Smith v. Porter, 10 

Gray, 66.

Evidence is admissible to show that a deed was delivered at a time subsequent to that of its date. Fairbanks v. Metcalf, 8 Mass. 230, 240. 

"But so far as the date of a deed is referred to in the body of it as fixing the time for the performance of any act by the grantor or grantee, — for instance, in a mortgage, the time of payment of the sum of money thereby secured, it seems that such date cannot be varied by oral evidence." - Crocker's Notes, page 143. 43.

SIGNATURE. 

In this Commonwealth, sealing without signing is not a sufficient execution of a conveyance of land. G. L. c. 183, § 3; 

Hutchings v. Byrnes, 9 Gray, 367. 

A deed signed with the grantor's name in his presence and by his request, though by a stranger, is sufficiently well executed. Finnegan v. Lucy, 157 Mass. 439, 440; Gardner v. 

Gardner, 5 Cush. 483.

In Bartlett v. Drake, 100 Mass. 174, it was held that a person whose name was subscribed to a deed by his wife in his absence recognizes and adopts the signature as his own by acknowledging the deed before a magistrate.

When the grantor in a deed is a corporation, the in testimonium clause and signature should be substantially as follows: "In witness whereof the said corporation has caused its corporate seal to be hereto affixed and these presents to be signed in its name and behalf by M. N., its treasurer, this first day of, etc. The 

Corporation, by 

M. N., Treasurer. (Seal.) "In witness whereof the said Corporation has caused these presents to be signed by C. D., its president, and its corporate seal to be hereto affixed. C. D., President," and a seal, was held a good execution in Haven v. Adams, 4 Allen 80. "In witness whereof the said corporation by C. D., its 

Treasurer, has hereto set its name and seal. C. D., Treasurer 

Corporation," and a seal, was held good in 

Hutchings v. Byrnes, 9 Gray, 367.

But in Brinley v. Mann, 2 Cush. 337, "In witness whereof 

I, the said C. D. in behalf of the said corporation and as its 

Treasurer, have hereto set my hand and seal. C. D., Treasurer of 

Corporation," and a seal, was held not to be a good execution by the corporation. See St. Patricks Religious &c. Assoc. v. Hale, 227 Mass. 175. 

Where a sealed instrument is to be executed for a party by his attorney, such instrument should be written exactly as if it were to be executed by the party himself, except that the in testimonium clause may be altered to read as follows: "In witness whereof I, the said A. B. by C. D., my attorney hereto duly authorized (by letter of attorney, dated, etc., and recorded, etc.), hereunto set my hand and seal this first day of, etc." 

The proper form of signature is "A. B." by "C. D." Other forms may be valid, the material points being that the deed should purport to be the deed of the principal and not the deed of the agent, and that the name of the principal should in some way appear in the signature. 

A deed purporting to be the deed of A. B. and signed “C. D. for A. B." was held to be well executed as the deed of A. B. in Mussey v. Scott, 7 Cush. 215. 

But a deed beginning "I, C. D.," or "I, C. D. as attorney for A. B.," or "I, C. D. by virtue of a power of attorney from 1

DEEDS. 

155 

A. B.," and signed "C. D." or "C. D., attorney for A. B." will not be good as the deed of A. B. Copeland v. Mercantile 

Insurance Co., 6 Pick. 198. 

44. SEAL.

A writing not under seal will not, even as between the parties to it, be effectual to pass a freehold interest in real estate. 

Stewart v. Clark, 13 Met. 79. "It seems that for the seal of an individual it is necessary that there should be affixed to the deed a wafer, wax, gummed paper or some other tenacious substance capable of receiving the impression of a seal upon it, and that a mere impression on the paper of the deed, or a scrawl, will not be sufficient. 

See Bates v. Boston & N. Y. C. R.R. Co., 10 Allen, 251, 254; 

Commonwealth v. Griffith, 2 Pick. 11, 18; Bishop v. Globe 

Co., 135 Mass. 132, 137." - Crocker, page 145. 66 'For the seal of a corporation a distinct, visible and permanent impression of the corporate seal upon and into the substance of the paper on which an instrument is written, without the addition of any wafer or other substance, is sufficient." Crocker, page 145, citing Hendee v. Pinkerton, 14 

Allen, 381. 

In Hendee v. Pinkerton, decided in 1867, and which concerned a mortgage executed on January 1, 1853, it was held that an impression of the seal of a corporation stamped upon and into the substance of the paper upon which the instrument was written which was designed to be sealed, was a good seal, although no wax, wafer or other adhesive substance was used.

This decision made no reference to chapter 223 of the Acts of 1855, which provided that "The impression or stamping of the established seal of any corporation, upon any legal instrument executed by such corporation, shall henceforth be taken and held to be the seal of such corporation, and valid in law to the same extent that an impression of such seal would be if made on wax or wafer attached to such instrument." 

G. L. c. 155, § 6 provides that a corporation "may have a corporate seal, which it may alter at pleasure." The form of seal is commonly provided for in the by-laws, but it is a fact

that there are a few corporations in Massachusetts which have never adopted an impression seal, and which accordingly have been obliged to use a wafer. 

Several persons may seal by one seal, but in such case it is proper, though not absolutely necessary, that the in testimonium clause should read thus: "have hereunto set our hands and our common seal." Tasker v. Bartlett, 5 Cush. 359, 364.

The seal upon a bond or deed is none the less the seal of the one who executes it, whether he affixes it before or after his signature, or whether he adopts as his seal one which already had been affixed to the instrument by another, or is affixed to it at his request by another after the signature has been made. 

The material question is whether he has adopted it as his seal and delivered the writing as a sealed instrument. George N. 

Pierce Co. v. Casler, 194 Mass. 423.

If an instrument, which requires a seal, is by accident or mistake executed without one, a court of equity may grant relief by compelling a seal to be affixed or otherwise. Gaylord v. Pelland, 169 Mass. 356, 359.

After many attempts to relieve the necessity of affixing a seal to instruments conveying or affecting the title to real property, the following statutes were enacted in 1929:

Acts of 1929, c. 107, § 2: Section six of chapter one hun dred and eighty-two of the General Laws, as amended by section five of chapter two hundred and ninety of the acts of nineteen bundred and twenty-six, is hereby further amended by adding at the end thereof the following new paragraph: 

An association or trust may have a seal, which it may alter at pleasure, and which may be used by the trustee or trustees of such association or trust as his or their seal. An impression of a seal purporting to be the seal of such an association or trust shall be sufficient for all purposes without the use of a wafer or wax.

This legislation was sponsored by a firm of attorneys which deals extensively with bond issues and various forms of securities. They complained that they frequently came upon instruments under seal executed by the trustees of an association or trust using an impression seal purporting to be the seal of the association or trust instead of individual wafer seals. This was

DEEDS. 

157 a situation which did not commonly arise in conveyancing. 

Occasionally the declaration of trust under which an association or trust dealing in real estate was organized would provide for a seal, and in such a case, upon an instrument affecting real estate, it was common practice to have the trustees use both the impression seal and individual wafer seals. 

Acts of 1929, c. 377: SECTION 1. Section seven of chapter four of the General Laws, as amended in clause twenty-ninth by section one of chapter one hundred and seven of the acts of the current year, is hereby further amended by striking out said clause twenty-ninth.

SECTION 2. Said chapter four is hereby further amended by inserting after section nine the following new section:- Section 

9A: In any written instrument, a recital that such an instrument is sealed by or bears the seal of the person signing the same or is given under the hand and seal of the person signing the same, or that such instrument is intended to take effect as a sealed instrument, shall be sufficient to give such instrument the legal effect of a sealed instrument without the addition of any seal of wax, paper or other substance or any semblance of a seal by scroll, impression or otherwise; provided, that if in any case the seal of a court, public office, public officer or corporation is expressly required by the constitution or by statute to be affixed to a paper, the word "seal" shall mean either an impression of the official seal upon the paper or an impression on a wafer or wax affixed thereto. The word "person" as used in this section shall include a corporation, association, trust or partnership.

This act was first drafted with the intention of relieving the necessity of using a seal only in the case of instruments affecting real estate, but, before its passage, it was broadened out and made applicable to "any written instrument.” 

It was enacted as an emergency law and was approved March 11, 1929.

Chapter 4 of General Laws is entitled "Statutes." Section 7, which includes 37 clauses, has the following preamble: "In construing statutes the following words shall have the meanings herein given unless a contrary intention clearly appears. 

Clause 29, as amended by section 1 of chapter 107 of the Acts of 1929, which was repealed by chapter 377 of the Acts of

1929, was as follows: "If the seal of a court, public office, public officer or corporation is required by law to be affixed to a paper, the word 'seal' shall mean either an impression of the official seal upon the paper or an impression on a wafer or wax affixed thereto." The provision concerning seals of corporations therein contained was all that remained of chapter 223 of the Acts of 1855, quoted above. 

Substantially the provisions of said clause 29, so far as concerned courts or public offices, appeared as clause 15 of section 6 of chapter 2 of the Revised Statutes of 1836, this section being applicable expressly, as was section 7 of chapter 4 of the General Laws, in the construction of statutes. When the General Statutes were compiled in 1860, chapter 223 of the Acts of 1855 was repealed, and a provision concerning the seal of a corporation in much the same form as it appears in General Laws as above stated was merged with the provisions relative to the seal of courts or public offices in clause 15 of section 7 of chapter 3.

No satisfactory reason has been found for thus repealing the general provisions of chapter 223 of the Acts of 1855 and writing a provision which doubtless was intended to have the same effect into a section which was expressly dedicated to construction of statutes.

Shortly after its enactment, it was suggested that chapter 377 of the Acts of 1929 was lame because the proviso, which included and referred to the seal of corporations, rendered it necessary in connection with any written instrument purporting to be under seal, but in fact bearing none, and executed by a corporation, to consider whether there was any express constitutional or statutory provision requiring that the seal should actually be affixed. So far as the writer is aware the only case of such an express requirement which has come to light is the statutory provision concerning certificates of stock. 

See G. L. c. 156, § 33, as amended by chapter 375 of the Acts of 1929.

It was also urged that the act completely destroyed so far as common law was concerned (if in fact this result did not follow from the compilation of General Statutes) the provisions of chapter 223 of the Acts of 1855, and it was also urged

DEEDS. 159 that, if this theory was sound, a corporate instrument which in terms did not purport to be a sealed instrument could not be given effect as such, even though the seal was impressed thereon, provided no wax or wafer was used.

It was also suggested that the provisions of the act that "the word 'seal' shall mean either an impression of the official seal upon the paper or an impression on a wafer or wax affixed thereto" was weak because to say that a word shall mean thus and so may well imply that it has no other meaning, and it is a fact that some Massachusetts corporations have never adopted an impression seal.

To meet the objections noted above the following amendments were enacted as sections 128 and 129 of chapter 426 of the Acts of 1931.

SECTION 128. Section nine A of said chapter four, inserted by section two of chapter three hundred and seventy-seven of the acts of nineteen hundred and twenty-nine, is hereby amended by striking out all after the word "otherwise;" in the eighth line down to and including the word "thereto" in the fourteenth line and inserting in place thereof the following:; but the foregoing shall not apply in any case where the seal of a court, public office or public officer is expressly required by the constitution or by statute to be affixed to a paper, nor shall it apply in the case of certificates of stock of corporations.

SECTION 129. Said chapter four is hereby further amended by inserting after section nine A, inserted by section two of chapter three hundred and seventy-seven of the acts of nineteen hundred and twenty-nine, the following new section:- 

Section 9B. The impression or stamping of the established seal of a court, public office, public officer or corporation upon a paper shall be taken and held to be the seal of such court, public office, public officer or corporation, and valid in law to the same extent that an impression of such seal would be if made on wax or wafer attached to such paper. 

In Capitol Amusement Co. v. Gallagher, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 

1823, the Court said: "Opposite the names of the signers there is a bracket enclosing the letters 'L.S.' The agreement contains no recital that it is sealed by, or bears the seal of, the

persons signing the same, or is given under the hand and seal of the persons signing the same, or that it is intended to take effect as a sealed instrument. It has no seal of wax or other adhesive substance impressed or affixed upon or to it. As the contract declared on was not an instrument under seal, U. S. 

Drainage & Irrigation Co. v. Medford, 225 Mass. 467 (see, relative to seals and sealed instruments, St. 1929, c. 377, § 2), the plaintiff, not named in the agreement, can enforce it if in fact the plaintiff was the undisclosed principal.” 

45. "SIGNED, SEALED AND DELIVERED IN PRESENCE OF." 

A deed was always valid without attesting witnesses. Dole v. Thurlow, 12 Met. 157, 166.

It is now provided by G. L. c. 233, § 68, re-enacting St. 

1897, c. 386, that the signature to an attested instrument or writing, except a will, may be proved in the same manner as if it were not attested. Prior to this statute an attesting witness might often be the cause of trouble, inasmuch as the law then required that, upon proving the deed, he should be called or his absence accounted for, even though the person whose signature was attested was himself present and competent to testify. Crocker, page 147. 

A deed of land, which purports on its face to have been "signed, sealed and delivered in the presence of" a witness whose name is signed, is presumed, in the absence of anything appearing to the contrary, to have been executed on the day of its date and to have been delivered. Hall v. Sears, 210 Mass. 

185. But such recital of delivery will not have the effect to exclude parol evidence that there has been no delivery. 

Faunce v. State Mut. Life Ins. Co., 101 Mass. 279. 

Where the grantor signs by mark his signature should always be witnessed. It is customary for the justice or notary who takes the acknowledgment to sign also as attesting witness in such a case in this form: "Witness to mark of A B, James Smith.”

DEEDS. 

161 

46. NOTE OF ALTERATIONS. 

"It is important to note in the attestation clause all material alterations or interlineations which may have been made in the body of the instrument, for if made by the grantee after the execution and delivery of the deed, they affect its validity, and the fact that they are noted affords a presumption that they were duly made before execution. If, however, the alterations are immaterial, — that is, such as do not change the legal tenor and effect of the instrument, it is not necessary that they should be noted, for whenever made, whether before or after the execution of the instrument, they are without effect." 

Crocker, page 148. 

No alteration in a deed, made after the estate conveyed has vested in the grantee, can divest or invalidate his title. Carr v. Frye, 225 Mass. 531. In this case it was held that if the grantee in a completed deed of land, after the deed is delivered to him but before it is recorded, causes his name to be erased as grantee and that of his wife to be substituted therefor, and thereafter the deed is recorded, his estate is not divested but remains his property and is not transferred to his wife, and, although the record title stands in her name, the Land Court properly may grant a petition by him for registration of the title in his name. "The instruments may become invalid, so that no action can be maintained upon the covenants contained in them, and yet the titles which have been acquired under them remain unaffected. When a person has become the legal owner of real estate, he cannot transfer it or part with his title, except in some of the forms prescribed by law. The grantee may destroy his deed, but not his estate." Cheeseman v. Whittemore, 23 Pick. 231, 234.

In Macurda v. Fuller, 225 Mass. 341, it was said: "The instrument (a deed with the name of the grantee in blank) delivered to Fuller was without validity and the direction to him to fill the blank space with the name of George A. Tower conferred on him no legal right because the filling in of such a blank created a substantial part of the deed itself, stood on

the same footing as signing and sealing, and could be authorized only by a power under seal."

In Long v. Lowrey, 243 Mass. 414, it was held that where, after execution of a deed, the name of the grantee was erased, and the name of a different grantee inserted without re-signing by the grantor, the grantor, who was present and consented, was estopped to question the validity of the deed, and that the instrument on its face being duly executed and delivered by the grantor, the substituted grantee, who took it in good faith, had the right to rely upon it. 

47. ACKNOWLEDGMENT. 

For general statement of the history and theory of the law relative to the acknowledgment of deeds, see Pidge v. Tyler, 4 Mass. 541. 

G. L. c. 183, § 29: No deed shall be recorded unless a certificate of its acknowledgment or of the proof of its due execution, made as hereinafter provided, is endorsed upon or annexed to it, and such certificate shall be recorded at length with the deed to which it relates; but this section shall not apply to conveyances from the United States.

SECTION 30. The acknowledgment of a deed or other written instrument required to be acknowledged shall be by one or more of the grantors or by the attorney executing it. The officer before whom the acknowledgment is made shall endorse upon or annex to the instrument a certificate thereof. Such acknowledgment may be made 

(a) If within the commonwealth, before a justice of the peace, notary public or special commissioner. (b) If without the commonwealth, in any state, territory, district or dependency of the United States, before a justice of the peace, notary public, magistrate or commissioner appointed therefor by the governor of this commonwealth, or, if a certificate of authority in the form prescribed by section thirty-three is attached thereto, before any other officer therein authorized to take acknowledgments of deeds. (c) If without the United States or any dependency thereof, before a justice of the peace, notary, magistrate or commis-

DEEDS. 163 sioner as above provided, or before an ambassador, minister, consul, vice consul, chargé d'affaires or consular officer or agent of the United States accredited to the country where the acknowledgment is made; if made before an ambassador or other official of the United States, it shall be certified by him under his seal of office.

SECTION 31. The acknowledgment by a married woman may be taken in the same form as if she were sole, and without any examination separate and apart from her husband.

SECTION 32. The law relative to the acknowledgment and recording of deeds shall apply to letters of attorney for the conveyance of real estate.

SECTION 33. Whenever, under clause (b) of section thirty under section forty-one, a certificate of authority is required to be attached, there shall be subjoined or attached to the certificate of proof or acknowledgment a certificate of the secretary of state of the state where the officer taking the acknowledgment resides, under the seal of such state, or a certificate of the clerk of a court of record of such state in the county where said officer resides or where he took such proof or acknowledgment, under the seal of the court, stating that said officer was, at the time of taking such proof or acknowledgment, duly authorized thereto in said state, and that said secretary of state or clerk of court is well acquainted with his handwriting and verily believes the signature affixed to such certificate of proof or acknowledgment is genuine.

It is good practice, where deeds are acknowledged in this country but outside the Commonwealth, to require the certificate of authority described in section 33 above, but most conveyancers will accept an acknowledgment before a notary public in another State, provided he affixes his notarial seal, although they frown upon acknowledgments before a justice of the peace in another State, and this in spite of the decision in Close v. 

Martin, 208 Mass. 236, in which it was held that deeds affecting land in the Commonwealth, acknowledged in 1894 and 1898 before a justice of the peace in Maine in accordance with the requirements of Pub. Sts. c. 120, § 6, were entitled to be recorded and recognized here without authentication of the office and authority of the officer taking the acknowledgment.

Into the statutory provisions there has been read the rule that an instrument must be acknowledged before an official who is not a party thereto and who is not the husband or wife of a party. It was held in Judd v. Tyron, 131 Mass. 345, that a certificate of the entry of a mortgagee for the purpose of foreclosing a mortgage, sworn to before himself as a justice of the peace, was invalid. The Court said: "It is as contrary to elementary principles of justice to allow a magistrate to administer the requisite oath to such a certificate of his own entry under a mortgage to himself, as to permit him to take in his official capacity a deposition in a suit to which he is a party, or an acknowledgment of a deed to himself, or a recognizance for a debt 'due to him personally."

Though a deed is neither acknowledged nor recorded it will be effectual to pass the title as against the grantor, his heirs and devisees, and persons having actual notice of it. As between the parties, title passes on delivery. Dole v. Thurlow, 12 Met. 

157. See to the same effect Cooper v. Monroe, 237 Mass. 192, wherein a deed was acknowledged before the grantee. 

A person executing a deed for a corporation should acknowledge it to be "the free act and deed" of the corporation. 

Whenever a deed is acknowledged by an agent he should acknowledge it to be the free act and deed of his principal. 

For statutory forms of acknowledgment see Appendix to chapter 183 of G. L., forms 13, 14 and 15.

Formerly, where land was conveyed by a married woman by a deed in which her husband joined, it was common practice to allow the husband to acknowledge the instrument. This practice had judicial sanction in Hayden v. Peirce, 165 Mass. 359, in which it was held that even though the husband joined only in the testimonium clause in token of his release of all right to curtesy, his acknowledgment was sufficient. This was on the theory that having released his curtesy right, however accomplished, he was a grantor within the meaning of the statute relative to acknowledgments. The Revised Laws of 1902 abolished curtesy at common law. In Downey v. King, 201 Mass. 59, the 

Court said that a husband had no interest in the lands of his wife except in such as she died seized of. Thereupon the conveyancers concluded that if the husband had no interest to convey, he could

DEEDS. 165 not be a grantor within the meaning of the statute, even though he did join in the deed, and that accordingly an acknowledgment in such cases by the husband was ineffective. Whether or not the statute of 1915 creating a new right of curtesy in a husband renewed his power to acknowledge the deed of his wife in which he has joined to release such right, the practice of allowing him to acknowledge has not been revived. 

It is sometimes argued that if a deed is in fact recorded without acknowledgment or upon an improper acknowledgment, it is nevertheless recorded and has the benefit of the recording acts. The safer view is that expressed by Mr. Crocker at page 

262, as follows: "And it has been held accordingly that if a deed is recorded without having been properly acknowledged or proved, such registration will be a mere nullity." Pidge v. 

Tyler, 4 Mass. 541, 547.

For statutory provision for the "proof" of an unacknowledged witnessed deed before a court of record, see G. L. c. 183,

SS 34-41. 

48. DELIVERY. 

It is requisite to the validity of a deed that it be delivered by the grantor himself or by his attorney. 2 Blacks. Com. 306.

Considerable information as to the law concerning delivery appears in footnotes to Maynard v. Maynard, 10 Mass. 455.

As between the parties to a deed, title passes on delivery. 

Dole v. Thurlow, 12 Met. 157. "A deed may be delivered to a party by words without any act of delivery, as if the writing sealed lieth on the table, and the feoffor or obligor saith to the feoffee or obligee, go and take up the writing, it is sufficient for you, or it will serve the turn, or take it as my deed, or the like words, it is a sufficient delivery. If, however, a party throws a writing on a table and says nothing, and the other party takes it up, this does not amount to a delivery, unless it be found to be put there with the intent to be delivered to the party, or to be taken up by him." Mills v. Gore, 20 Pick. 28, 36.

If the owner of real estate executes and acknowledges a deed of it and hands the deed to the grantee, who at once returns it to him, and it appears that the owner intends to retain control

Sec. 166 

of the deed with the power and right to change the disposition of the property during his lifetime, and does not intend that the deed shall become operative either as to title or enjoyment until his death, the mere manual delivery of the deed is ineffectual to convey title; and if the owner dies intestate without a further delivery of the deed, the property passes to his heirs at law. Tewksbury v. Tewksbury, 222 Mass. 595. 

A deed of land to the grandson of the grantor, executed in the presence of a witness, in consideration of "love and affection," was found after the grantor's death in a chest owned by him, which, with its contents, was given by the grantor's will to the grandson. The grantor retained possession of the land and of the chest and its contents until his death. The executor of the will delivered the deed to the grantee, who recorded it. 

Held, in a writ of entry to recover possession of the land, that these facts failed to show a delivery of the deed in the grantor's lifetime. Parrott v. Avery, 159 Mass. 594, 595.

Authority to deliver a deed to the grantee may be given to an agent by parol. Macurda v. Fuller, 225 Mass. 341.

The delivery of a deed by the grantor to a register of deeds for record will not of itself amount to a delivery to the grantee. 

Barnes v. Barnes, 161 Mass. 381, 383. Such delivery, however, if made in accordance with a prior request of the grantee that it should be so delivered, has been held to be a good delivery to the grantee. Shaw v. Hayward, 7 Cush. 170.

A deed may remain undelivered although it has been recorded, and a delivery may be established after such record. 

The issue of delivery is ordinarily one of fact. This question depends on the acts done and the intent with which they are performed. Murphy v. Hanright, 238 Mass. 200.

Evidence is admissible to show that a deed was in fact delivered at a time subsequent to its date. Fairbanks v. Metcalf, 8 Mass. 230, 240.

It seems that a deed, which was acknowledged five months after its date and was recorded eleven months after its acknowledgment, in the absence of other evidence of the time of its delivery, must be taken to have been delivered on the day of its acknowledgment. Mighill v. Rowley, 224 Mass. 586. "It is well settled in this Commonwealth that the delivery of

Sec. DEEDS. 167 a deed is not complete and effectual without an acceptance by the grantee, or by some one authorized to represent him, or who assumes to represent him, and whose act of acceptance is afterwards ratified." Meigs v. Dexter, 172 Mass. 217, 218.

Where a man makes a deed of real estate to a woman about to become his wife, and with the knowledge and consent of the grantee retains the deed in his possession without making any manual delivery of it, and the grantee at the request of the grantor executes and delivers a mortgage of the property to him, whereupon he has the deed and the mortgage recorded, and later the grantee takes possession of the property and assumes control of it with the apparent consent of the grantor, the assertion of title by the grantee in her act of executing and delivering the mortgage is an acceptance of the deed by her, and the acceptance of the mortgage by the grantor shows his intention that the deed should take effect. Blackwell v. Blackwell, 196 Mass. 186.

By statute, the record of an instrument affecting title to land is conclusive evidence of its delivery in favor of purchasers for value without notice who claim thereunder. G. L. c. 183, § 5, re-enacting St. 1892, c. 256. 

A deed may be delivered to a third person as an escrow, that is to say, with instructions to deliver it to the grantee at some future time, or upon the performance of some condition. The title of the grantee is not complete until the delivery of the deed to him, but, upon such delivery, takes effect as of the time when it was delivered by the grantor to the third person, notwithstanding that in the meantime the grantor has become incapable of making a deed. Foster v. Mansfield, 3 Met. 412, 415.

It was said in Daggett v. Simonds, 173 Mass. 340, that, if the maker of a promissory note delivered it to a person in her employ with instructions to deliver it to the payee, provided the maker died without recalling it, the note in the meantime to be held by the employee as a third person, as if she were not in the service of the maker, and with an intention on the part of the maker that her employee's possession should be for the benefit of the payee, there was a good delivery in escrow, and the employee had authority to deliver the note after the death of the maker; but if the maker intended merely to put the

Sec. 445 note in the custody of her servant in the same way as much of her other property was in the servant's custody, and wished it to be held under her direction without a change of possession, there was no delivery, and the authority of the servant terminated with the death of the testatrix.

If a person executes a deed of land, and places it in the hands of A with directions to keep it during the grantor's life and on his death to deliver it to the grantee, A holds it as agent of the grantor, and not as agent of the grantee, and the grantor may revoke it at any time. Hale v. Joslin, 134 Mass. 310.

In Landry v. Landry, 265 Mass. 265, the owner of land executed and acknowledged a deed thereof to his son, which he gave to L with instructions to deliver it to the grantee only upon the grantor's death. The grantee was informed of the execution of the deed and the terms of the escrow. On the following day, without the knowledge of the grantor, L caused the deed to be recorded. In due course the recorded deed was mailed to the grantee who retained it without notice to or knowledge of the grantor until a dispute arose and the grantee asserted the rights of an owner under the conveyance. The Court said: "The unauthorized recording of the deed by L vested no rights in the defendant (grantee). The findings . . . showed that there was no effectual or valid delivery of the deed to the defendant, and no intention of passing a present interest in it by the plaintiff (grantor), and that the title was not to vest in the defendant until the grantor's death. It follows that the transaction was an attempt to make a testamentary disposition of the real estate and was in violation of the statute of wills. The making and delivery of the deed to L were in the nature of a testamentary act; such act could be revoked by the grantor at any time and was so revoked by the bringing of the present bill.” 

49. RECORDING. 

See G. L. c. 183, § 4. The object of that statute is to protect those taking title bona fide from a prior unrecorded deed of which they have no notice. Reading Coal & Iron Co. v. Boston, 211 Mass. 526. See also Wilcox v. Foster, 132 Mass. 320; 

Potter v. Aiden, etc., 125 Mass. 97; and George v. Wood, 9 

Allen, 80.

DEEDS. 169 In George v. Wood it was held that a mortgagee of land may release a portion thereof from the operation of the mortgage without impairing his security upon the remainder, provided he has no actual or constructive notice of the existence of a right of any other part thereof to exemption from contribution; and the record of a subsequent conveyance of such other part is not constructive notice to him thereof. The Court said: "We are not aware of any adjudged case in which the record of a deed has been held to be constructive notice to any other persons other than subsequent purchasers, or those claiming title under the same grantor."

In Wilcox v. Foster it was held that if a mortgage of land, after being assigned, is discharged by a mistake of the mortgagee, the assignee, although he has not caused his assignment to be recorded, is entitled in equity to have the discharge canceled as against a person claiming under a subsequent mortgage, which was in terms subject to the prior mortgage and was executed before that mortgage was discharged; and as against a person claiming under a mortgage made after the discharge, who knew of the fact of the mistake, although he was advised by counsel before taking his mortgage, that the prior mortgage could not be reinstated; and also as against an assignee of the mortgage last executed, who took the assignment, in ignorance of the fact of the mistake, after the note secured by the mortgage assigned was overdue. The Court said: "The failure of Hawes to record his assignment could only be availed of by subsequent assignees of the same mortgage. Registry of an assignment is not necessary as against the mortgagor or his subsequent grantees."

It was held in Bates v. Norcross, 14 Pick. 224, that the registry of a deed of land executed by a grantor who had no right to the premises, is not constructive notice to the true owner that such conveyance has been made, and that the registry of a deed is constructive notice only to after-purchasers under the same grantor. The Court said: "To hold the proprietors of land to take notice of the records of deeds, to determine whether some stranger has without right made conveyances of their lands, would be a most dangerous doctrine, and cannot be sustained with any color of reason or authority."

In an action for goods sold to a person alleged to have been the defendant's agent, where defendant contends that the purchaser was its lessee and not its agent, it may put in evidence a lease for more than seven years of its farm to such person, although such lease is not recorded as required by G. L. c. 183, § 4, and therefore is good only between the parties and persons having actual notice of it as affecting the right to the possession of the land. Potter v. Aiden Lair Farm Association, 225 Mass. 97. "The Court said: "Between the parties the lease was valid. 

The purpose of the statute was to protect subsequent purchasers against prior and unrecorded conveyances; it was not passed to protect those who claim no right or title in the premises conveyed by the unrecorded lease." 

Where defendant's counsel, acting for him in negotiations preliminary to a purchase of real estate, was furnished a copy of the vendor's lease to plaintiff and information as to a subletting by plaintiff, and made for defendant an examination of the title and represented him in passing papers, his knowledge of the lease and of the subletting was defendant's knowledge. 

Lincoln v. Sauer, 243 Mass. 201.

If one purchases real estate in good faith for value, in ignorance of an infirmity in the title, the validity of his title will not be affected by what he afterwards learns respecting such infirmity. Flannagan v. Keefe, 250 Mass. 118. "However the law may be elsewhere it is settled in this Commonwealth that an attaching creditor stands in the position of a purchaser for value, and, as a deed duly recorded takes precedence of a prior deed unrecorded, so an attachment, when duly made, has the effect of a prior purchase and takes precedence of a prior unrecorded deed." Hillside Co-operative 

Bank v. Cavanaugh, 232 Mass. 157. 

As to the meaning of "actual notice," see Hughes v. Williams, 218 Mass. 448. In this case it was held that in order to prove that a creditor, who in an action for the collection of his debt caused an attachment of land standing in the name of his debtor to be made, had knowledge of the fact that the debtor before the attachment had delivered a deed of the premises to another, it is not necessary to prove positive knowledge on his part, but intelligible information of the fact, conveyed to him

DEEDS. 

171 either orally or in writing from a source which ought to be heeded, is evidence upon which such knowledge can be found. 

See Rathesky v. Piscopo, 239 Mass. 180.

If the holder of title to land delivers a deed of it to one who does not record the deed, but who at once delivers a deed of the premises to another, and it does not appear that record title was retained in the first holder for the purpose of securing the land from attachment, an attachment of the land made in an action against the first grantee, as land of the first grantee standing in the name of the record holder of the title is ineffectual, because at the time of the attachment the land described was not the land of the debtor. Piantadori v. Nally, 233 Mass. 158.

An assignment under seal by the owner of real estate of rent to become due under the provisions of a lease of the premises. for a term of less than seven years, is effectual, after notice to the lessee, against one who, without notice of the assignment and before notice was given to the lessee, purchased and received conveyance of the premises from the owner. Winnisim-. met Trust, Inc. v. Libby, 232 Mass. 491.

A writ, upon which an attachment of real estate in the southern district of Essex County was made, according to the record of attachment in the registry of deeds was returnable in the Superior Court in Suffolk County, although in fact it was returnable in Essex County. The writ was not entered in Suffolk 

County and a certificate to that effect was filed in the registry of deeds and was noted there in the margin of the record of the attachment. The writ was entered in Essex County. No certified copy of the writ was deposited in the registry of deeds. 

Held, that these facts constituted no notice of the attachment to one who purchased at a sale upon execution issued in a subsequent action in which the real estate was made subject to a later attachment. Trojanowski v. MacLachlan, 240 Mass. 225.

In a suit in equity by a mortgagee of real estate for the cancellation of a discharge of the plaintiff's unpaid mortgage which had been entered by mistake on the margin of the record in the registry of deeds under R. L. c. 127, § 34, as amended by St. 

1908, c. 149, it appeared that two of the defendants were attaching creditors of the mortgagor, who had attached the real

estate in actions of contract after the apparent discharge of the mortgage on the record and without knowledge of the mistake, and it was held that the bill must be dismissed as to the defendant attaching creditors, whose liens took precedence of the plaintiff's mortgage. Waltham Co-operative Bank v. Barry, 231 

Mass. 270.

A forged instrument gains no validity from being recorded in the registry of deeds, and a bona fide purchaser of land which would be affected by it if it were genuine gets no better title by relying on it as recorded. Breed v. Gardner, 187 Mass. 300. 

G. L. c. 183, § 53: If a deed purports to contain an absolute conveyance of land, but is made defeasible by a deed, bond or other instrument, the original deed shall not be thereby affected, as against any person other than the maker of the instrument of defeasance and his heirs and devisees and persons having actual notice of it, unless such instrument is recorded in the registry of deeds for the county or district in which the land to which it relates is situated. And see section 51 of these notes. 

G. L. c. 203, § 1: No trust concerning land, except as may arise or result by implication of law, shall be created or declared unless by an instrument in writing signed by the party or by the attorney of the party creating the trust. 

G. L. c. 203, § 2: If a trust concerning land is created or declared by such instrument, the recording of the instrument in the registry of deeds for the county or district in which the land lies shall be equivalent to actual notice to every person who claims under a conveyance, attachment or execution thereafter made or levied. 

G. L. c. 203, § 3: No trust concerning land, whether implied by law or created or declared by the parties, shall defeat the title of a purchaser for a valuable consideration and without notice of the trust, nor prevent a creditor who has no notice of the trust from attaching the land or from taking it on execution in like manner as if no such trust existed. 

In Somers v. Brewer, 2 Pick. 183, where a grantee obtained a deed by imposition and fraud upon the grantor, and without consideration, and entered under the deed, which was duly acknowledged and recorded, and afterwards conveyed the land

DEEDS. 173 to a bona fide purchaser for a valuable consideration without notice of the fraud, it was held that such purchaser had a valid title against the first grantor.

It is a matter of common knowledge that there is an advantage to be gained by speedily recording a deed in the registry of deeds for the county or district where the land lies. The conveyancer understands this advantage to be that when he has examined the records and has found a proper conveyance to A B, and no conveyance from him, and has procured the execution and delivery of a proper deed by A B, and has caused the same to be recorded, then the grantee in said deed will hold the land as against any earlier grantee from A B who has not recorded the deed to him, provided, of course, that the later grantee has not actual knowledge of the earlier conveyance. 

In such a situation, which presents no mystery, and in general with regard to questions of priority of record of instruments affecting real property, one hears the conveyancers saying that a certain instrument of conveyance is or is not within the protection of the Recording Acts. In fact, the Recording 

Acts consist of a measure of common law, as to which see Somes v. Brewer, supra, being an equitable doctrine recognized both at law and in equity, and the statutory expressions of that doctrine quoted above which go to meet certain common situations in conveyancing.

The original certificate of the register of deeds as to the time when a mortgage deed was received and recorded, is conclusive as between the mortgagee and a creditor who has attached the land. Tracy v. Jenks, 15 Pick. 465. Such certificate is also conclusive evidence that the whole deed was recorded, even if a part of it is in fact omitted on the record. Adams v. Pratt, 109 Mass. 59.

The following cases have recently been the subject of discussion. In Trull v. Bigelow, 16 Mass. 406, a case on writ of entry, the following deeds were before the Court: 

No. 1. S to G. Dated November 10, 1812. Recorded November 17, 1812.

No. 2. G to Tenant. Dated March 16, 1816. 

No. 3. S to C. Dated November 12, 1812. 

Chad notice of deed No. 1. 1812. 

Duly recorded. 

Recorded November 13,

No. 4. C to J. Dated February 26, 1813. Duly recorded. J had no knowledge of deed No. 1.

No. 5. J to Demandant. Dated March 3, 1813. Duly recorded. De mandant knew of deed No. 1. 

Judgment for Demandant. Note that deed No. 3 was recorded before No. 1. J, if he examines the record, finds deed 

No. 3 to C and looks no further. If he had carried on four days more he would have found deed No. 1. It was held that J held title under C purged of the knowledge which vitiated it in the hands of C. This is a sound principle, otherwise it would be necessary in examining a title to consider the schedule of each owner from the date when he acquired title down to the date of passing papers. See Morse v. Curtis, 140 Mass. 112. The estate of J being indefeasible, the demandant, notwithstanding his knowledge, stepped into the shoes of J. 

In Flynt v. Arnold, 2 Met. 619, A made a deed to B, who at the same time gave back a mortgage to A. Before the deed or mortgage was recorded, B conveyed the land to C who had knowledge of the mortgage. After the deed to B (recorded 

May 6, 1837) and his mortgage to A (recorded May 1, 1837) were recorded, C mortgaged the land to D who assigned his mortgage to E. Neither D nor E had any notice of the mortgage to A beside that implied from the record. Held, that E could not hold the land against A. This decision does not conflict with that in Trull v. Bigelow, supra. Although the 

Court, through Chief Justice Shaw, questioned the soundness of the earlier decision, the decision in the case at bar was put squarely on the grounds that if the demandant claimed under an unrecorded deed, so did the tenants, with the decisive difference that the mortgage, under which they claimed, was not recorded till after that of the demandant. That is to say, the tenants, in order to make out their defense, were obliged to rely on the deed from A to B, and the mortgage from B to A was recorded before the deed. There is here no hardship for a conveyancer. If an examination was made for the purpose of determining the validity of the tenants' mortgage, the deed to B would be found. Then, of course, the examiner would consider the schedule of B from the date of the deed by which he acquired

DEEDS. 175 title, and thereby the mortgage given by B to A would be discovered.

For the statute permitting the recording of an affidavit relative to a person's married or unmarried status, kinship or lack of kinship, or of the date of his birth or death, which relates or purports to relate to the title to land, see chapter 227 of the 

Acts of 1924. 

If a deed or other writing affecting land has been recorded in a registry of deeds or registered in a registry district of the land court, a copy thereof, duly certified by a proper official, may be recorded or registered in any county or registry district wherein the original might properly have been recorded or registered, and when so recorded or registered shall have the same effect as a record or registration of the original instrument; provided, however, that no such certified copy of such a record or registration, except of a declaration of trust as provided for in section seventytwo of chapter one hundred and eighty-five, shall be entitled to registration in any registry district of the land court until the same has first been approved for the purpose by a judge of the land court. (Chapter 80 of the Acts of 1931, amending section 

13 of chapter 36 of the General Laws, as amended by chapter 130 of the Acts of 1926 and by chapter 267 of the Acts of 1930.)

51. EQUITABLE MORTGAGES. 

In determining whether a transaction constitutes a mortgage, the substance and not the mere form of the instrument is to be regarded. It is of no consequence that it is called by a wrong name. The effect of the instrument will ascertain its legal character. Lanfair v. Lanfair, 18 Pick. 299, 304.

Thus a deed, otherwise in the usual form of a mortgage, in which the words "then this deed shall be void" were omitted, was held to constitute a valid mortgage. Steel v. Steel, 4 Allen, 417, 419. "The rule has long been established in this Commonwealth that if a conveyance absolute in form is given as security for a debt, it will be construed to be a mortgage no matter how strongly the language used may indicate that the parties intended to prevent any right of redemption or reconveyance." 

Burns v. Hunnewell, 217 Mass. 106. 

An absolute deed with a separate instrument of defeasance will constitute a mortgage. Bayley v. Bayley, 5 Gray, 505.

In the last mentioned case the Court said: "The statute law recognizes as mortgages, subject to all the incidents and pro-

MORTGAGES. 

179 visions affecting mortgages, not only those made by a common deed of mortgage, but also such as are made by a conveyance, with a separate deed of defeasance. . Though it be ever so strongly expressed that the estate shall be absolute, if the money is not paid at the day fixed such stipulation would be void. It does not depend upon the intent of the parties because it is an intent contrary to the rules of law, which the law will not carry into effect." See Southwick v. Bigelow, 237 

Mass. 299.

But such an instrument of defeasance must be delivered at the same time with the deed and as part of the same transaction. Trull v. Skinner, 17 Pick. 213.

If the instrument of defeasance is not under seal, it will not in law constitute a mortgage; an equitable mortgage will be created, however. Eaton v. Green, 22 Pick. 526. 

As to the necessity of recording such an instrument of defeasance see G. L. c. 183, § 53, section 49 ante.

A leading case on equitable mortgage is Campbell v. Dearborn, 109 Mass. 130. There it was held that a bill in equity, to declare the plaintiff entitled to redeem land which the defendant holds by an absolute conveyance from him, may be maintained upon parol proof that he bought the land with money borrowed from the defendant, and, though he executed his absolute deed intelligently, yet both parties understood that it was intended as security for the loan. The Court said: "The parol evidence is admitted, not to vary, add to or contradict the writings, but to establish the fact of an inherent fault in the transaction or its consideration, which affords grounds for avoiding the effect of the writings, by restricting their operation, or defeating them altogether. This is a general principle of evidence well established and recognized both at law and in equity." "A conveyance, although absolute in form, may be shown by oral proof to have been made in trust or by way of security. 

There is nothing in the statute of frauds which prevents this result. Campbell v. Dearborn, 109 Mass. 130." Jennings v. 

Demmon, 194 Mass. 108. See also Alexander v. Grover, 190 

Mass. 462, at 465, and O'Brien v. Hovey, 239 Mass. 37. 

In Hassam v. Barrett, 115 Mass. 256, Wells, J., who wrote

the opinion in Campbell v. Dearborn, said in part: "But the theory of that decision (Campbell v. Dearborn) is that dealings between borrower and lender of money, or debtor and creditor, conducted by requiring an absolute deed for security, and a renunciation of all legal right of redemption, are so significant of oppression, and so calculated to invite to or result in wrong and injustice on the part of the stronger towards the weaker party in the transaction, as in themselves to constitute a quasi fraud against which equity ought to relieve, as it does against the strict letter of an express condition of forfeiture. The grounds of relief being purely equitable, it may and should be refused if the equitable considerations upon which it rests are wanting." 

In Clark v. Seagraves, 186 Mass. 430, the Court said: "The grounds on which the grantor of an absolute deed of real estate can go into equity and show that it was in fact given to secure a debt, is fraud. See Campbell v. Dearborn, 109 Mass. 130, particularly pages 137 and 142."

In Eaton v. Green, 22 Pick. 526 (1839), it was held that the 

Court had no jurisdiction over suits in equity for the redemption or foreclosure of equitable mortgages, but since that date the equitable powers of the Supreme and Superior Courts have been extended to cover such actions. See Campbell v. Dearborn, supra, at pages 141, 142, and Clark v. Seagraves, 186 Mass. 430.

Where the owner of land conveys it by a deed absolute in form to one who receives it under a parol agreement that the grantee shall hold it to secure the payment of a debt due him from the grantor, such agreement may be discharged without an instrument in writing by a subsequent parol agreement which leaves the title absolute in accordance with the terms of the deed. Sears v. Gilman, 199 Mass. 384.

Where, in consideration of a loan of money, the borrower delivers to the lender an instrument in writing by which he agrees that, as soon as he secures an interest in certain real estate, the equity of redemption of which then belongs to his trustee in bankruptcy, he forthwith will make a mortgage conveying such interest to the lender, this instrument is a mere executory agreement and creates no equitable lien upon the real estate; and, if the borrower subsequently acquires the con-

MORTGAGES. 

181 templated interest, the lender cannot set up such instrument to establish an equitable charge on the real estate as against a creditor of the borrower who has brought a suit in equity to reach and apply such equitable interest to the payment of the debt to him. Dudley v. Nickerson, 214 Mass. 274. 

52. THE MORTGAGE NOTE. 

It is not necessary that a note or other collateral personal security for the debt should be given with the mortgage. Rice v. Rice, 4 Pick. 349; Cook v. Johnson, 165 Mass. 245. In the latter case the Court said: "It is well settled that there may be a mortgage without personal liability on the part of the mortgagor for the debt which the mortgage secures. The mortgagee may agree to look to the land for payment, and may remain liable to account to the mortgagor for any surplus which he receives over and above his debt, and the estate conveyed may remain subject to redemption, although the mortgagor is under no personal liability to the mortgagee. The want of mutuality is not destructive of the character of the transaction as a mortgage." "If the instrument be made as a security for the payment of a debt, or the performance of a duty, it is a mortgage." Lanfair v. Lanfair, 18 Pick. 299.

Not infrequently a mortgage is given to secure an agreement to support the mortgagee during his lifetime. See Marsh v. 

Austin, 1 Allen, 235. In this case it was held that the administrator of the grantee of a mortgage given to secure an agreement for the support of the grantee and his wife during their lives, is entitled to foreclose the mortgage for breach of condition occurring both before and since the grantee's death, although the grantee's widow is living and does not appear in the suit.

But in practically all cases where a mortgage is given to secure the payment of money, such mortgage is accompanied by a promissory note as evidence of the debt. In such a case, upon default, and in the absence of an agreement to the contrary, the holder of the mortgage may sue on the note, and is not required first to foreclose the mortgage. And the holder of the mortgage may proceed concurrently with a suit on the

Sec 52 note and with foreclosure proceedings up to the point where he secures payment of his debt from one source or the other. Of course, if he should commence an action on the note, and, while such action was pending, should exercise the power of sale and sell the land for enough to pay the debt then due him, this would preclude him from proceeding with the action on the note. 

Where the holder of a mortgage exercises the power of sale and does not receive a sum sufficient to satisfy the debt, he may apply the sum so received to the payment of the note and maintain an action thereon for the unpaid balance. Phillips v. 

Vorenberg, 259 Mass. 46; City Institution for Savings v. Kelil, 262 Mass. 302.

It has been held that mere inadequacy of the price at which the mortgaged premises have been sold by the mortgagee will not afford a sufficient ground for setting aside the sale, if the mortgagee has complied with the terms of the power and has acted throughout in good faith. See Turansky v. Weinberg, 211 Mass. 324.

But if by reason of mismanagement the premises do not bring a fair price at the sale, this fact will serve as a defence to an action by the mortgagee to recover the balance due on the note, but the burden of proof is on the mortgagor. Vahey v. 

Bigelow, 208 Mass. 89. In the latter case, which was an action against the maker of a note secured by a power of sale mortgage of real estate to recover a balance alleged to be due after applying towards the payment of the note the proceeds of the sale, it appeared that there had been a literal compliance with the power of sale as to advertising the sale; that there were ten persons present at the sale, but that the only bid was one of $7,200 made by one Richards who acted throughout as attorney for the plaintiff; and that the fair market value of the property was $10,000. Judgment for the plaintiff. 

The fact that an action upon a note secured by a mortgage is barred by the statute of limitations does not affect the validity of the mortgage securing the payment of the note. The presumption that a witnessed promissory note has been paid when it has been due for more than twenty years is a presumption of fact which can be rebutted by any legal evidence

MORTGAGES. 

183 

Sec. 52 showing that the note has not been paid. Jenkins v. Andover 

Seminary, 205 Mass. 376. "The general rule is familiar that an assignment or transfer of a mortgage debt carries with it an equitable right to an assignment of the mortgage." Barnes v. Boardman, 149 Mass. 

106 at 114, citing Sturtevant v. Jaques, 14 Allen, 523, and 

Morris v. Bacon, 123 Mass. 58. "The debt is always the principal and the mortgage is merely the accessory. If the debt is assigned without the mortgage, the mortgage is held in trust for the assignee." 

Sturtevant v. Jaques, supra. 66 Weinberg v. Brother, 263 Mass. 61, was a bill to reach and apply a note secured by a mortgage of real estate. A and B conveyed the real estate to P, who gave the mortgage in question to the defendant as a straw man." Before the bill was brought, defendant assigned the note and mortgage to A in accordance with a previous understanding between the parties. 

The assignment was never recorded. Bill dismissed. The Court said: "Upon the facts found, the plaintiff could not, by a bill to reach and apply, deprive the owners of their interest in the note. The mortgage is merely security for the note. As the note had been transferred to the real owner, the defendant would hold the mortgage in trust for the owner, even if there had been no assignment of it."

On a bill in equity to redeem a mortgage, it is competent for the holder to show that the mortgagor had orally agreed that the mortgage might be held as security for advances beyond the amount originally secured by it, the doctrine being that he who seeks the aid of a court of equity must do equity. Joslyn v. 

Wyman, 5 Allen, 62.

Such oral agreement could not be set up against a subsequent mortgagee, or against an attaching creditor; nor could it be set up against the mortgagor or his assignee in a suit at law, but it may be in equity. Upton v. National Bank of South Reading, 120 Mass. 153.

A mortgage of land and the note secured by it, which were given by the wife of a person arrested upon an indictment to indemnify one who entered into a recognizance for such hus-

band's appearance, can be transferred by its holder, before his obligation under the recognizance is discharged, to a third person at the request of the mortgagor as security for a loan from such third person to the mortgagor, so as to give such third person the same rights against a prior mortgagee that he would have had if the mortgage assigned to him had been made to him originally to secure the loan from him. Bon v. Graves, 216 Mass. 

440. But it was held in Flye v. Berry, 181 Mass. 442, that one who, knowing that a mortgage has been fully paid before its maturity, takes the satisfied mortgage by assignment as security for a new debt of the mortgagor, whatever may be his equitable rights against the mortgagor, has no right to maintain a bill to redeem or to restrain foreclosure against the holder for value of a prior mortgage given by the same mortgagor upon the same property.

One who takes an assignment of a mortgage after the maturity of the note which it was given to secure, will take a title subject to all defenses which the mortgagor might have set up against the original mortgagee, even though no suggestion of any such defense appears upon the note or record. 

Vinton v. King, 4 Allen, 562; Fish v. French, 15 Gray, 520 (lack of consideration). 53. 

ASSIGNMENT OF MORTGAGE. 

"The proper technical words of an assignment are ‘assign, transfer, and set over.' But the words 'give, grant, bargain and sell,' or any other words which show the intention of the parties to make a complete transfer, will amount to an assignment.' Crocker, page 177. 

See G. L. c. 183, § 28.

The following form of assignment was commonly used before the passage of the Short Forms Act: 

KNOW ALL MEN BY THESE PRESENTS that a certain mortgage given by to dated A.D. 1 

Deeds, book and recorded with page in consideration of dollars paid by
