185 See +53 the receipt whereof is hereby acknowledged, do hereby assign, transfer, and set over unto the said the said mortgage deed, the real estate thereby conveyed, and the note and claim thereby secured.

To have and to hold the same to the said and heirs, and assigns, to their own use and behoof forever; subject nevertheless, to the conditions therein contained and to redemption according to law.

In witness whereof, this hereto set hand and seal day of

A.D. 19 

The statutory form of assignment is holder of a mortgage from page Witness recorded with to 

Deeds, book dated assign said mortgage and the note and claim secured thereby to hand and seal this day of (Seal.) 

The Real Estate thereby Conveyed. -Where the assignor has an interest in the mortgaged premises distinct from his interest as mortgagee, such distinct interest will not be affected by an assignment in the usual form. Barnstable Savings Bank v. 

Barrett, 122 Mass. 172. See also Merritt v. Harris, 102 Mass. 326, 328. 

And the Note and Claim thereby Secured. Even though the mortgage note is not delivered to the assignee of the mortgage, these words in an assignment will have the effect of transferring the note as against any subsequent indorsee thereof, unless such indorsee is a bona fide purchaser for value and without notice of the prior assignment, and, if the note purports on its face to be secured by mortgage, this fact alone will be sufficient to affect the indorsee with such notice. Strong v. 

Jackson, 123 Mass. 60, 62; Murphy v. Barnard, 162 Mass. 72.

In the latter case it was held that one who purchases from the mortgagee a mortgage which the latter has previously sold and transferred to another by an assignment duly recorded takes with constructive notice of want of title in his vendor; and although the mortgage and note are in the possession of his vendor, and are delivered with the assignment, the second purchaser takes no better title than that of his vendor, and

must reassign and deliver up the note and mortgage to their true owner. It was also held that a mortgagor who, relying merely upon his own supposition that the mortgage is still owned by the mortgagee, who has in fact sold it and has no authority from its owner to collect the principal, after the note has become overdue and without the production of the note, makes payments of principal to the mortgagee, is not protected as against the owner of the note by the fact that at the time of payment the mortgage note was in the possession of the mortgagee in his office in another city. But it was properly conceded that the plaintiff was not charged with constructive notice of the recorded assignment of the mortgage, and George v. Wood, 9 Allen, 80, was cited. See section 49, ante.

In Westminster National Bank v. Graustein, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 701, it was held that plaintiff, who claimed title to a note secured by mortgage under an assignment of the mortgage which included in terms an assignment of the note which was delivered to the plaintiff, had sufficient title to the note to enable it to maintain suit thereon, although the note was not endorsed by the payee. 

City National Bank v. Roberts, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 445, was an action on a demand note which contained, in addition to a promise to pay a certain sum of money, numerous agreements with regard to collateral security. Within two days after execution the note was indorsed by the payee and delivered to plaintiff as collateral security for an obligation of the payee. Later, without the knowledge of the assignee, the maker paid to the payee the amount due on the note. The validity of the defense of payment depended upon the negotiability of the note. The 

Court discussed its terms in the light of the Negotiable Instruments Act, held that the note was negotiable, and said: “Payment to extinguish a negotiable note must be made to a holder in due course. G. L. c. 107, § 142. The person paying has a right to demand that the note be exhibited to him and on payment be delivered to him (§ 97). Arnold v. Dresser, 8 Allen 

435; Legg v. Vinal, 165 Mass. 555, 556. One who pays without seeing it or demanding its production is not acting with reasonable prudence. See Wilbur v. Turner, 5 Pick. 526." But see 

Springfield National Bank v. Jeffers, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 455,

MORTGAGES. 

187 where it was held in a suit on a similar note that there was evidence tending to show that the plaintiff, the assignee of the note in question and other notes as collateral security, by its conduct had authorized the payee to accept payment of the note, which evidence should have been given to the jury. 

A warranty deed (Ruggles v. Barton, 13 Gray, 506), a quitclaim deed (Southwick v. Atlantic Insurance Co., 133 Mass. 

457), or even a mortgage (Murdock v. Chapman, 9 Gray, 156) of the mortgaged premises, given by the mortgagee will operate as an assignment of his mortgage. 

Even before the Short Forms Act, it was held that if a mortgagee of land, by a single instrument duly executed under seal and acknowledged, makes an absolute assignment of the mortgage debt and of the mortgage, such assignment is sufficient to vest in the assignee the full legal title of the mortgagee to the mortgaged premises, although no words of inheritance are used. Barnes v. Boardman, 149 Mass. 106.

An agreement to give or assign a mortgage is an agreement to convey an interest in land and, to be enforceable, must be in writing. Linsky v. Exchange Trust Co., 260 Mass. 15. 54. 

DISCHARGE OF MORTGAGE. 

Originally the form of discharge in common use was 

KNOW ALL MEN BY THESE PRESENTS 

that the mortgagee named in a certain mortgage given by dated A. D. 1 

Deeds, libro and recorded with folio, hereby acknowledges that it has received from the mortgagor named in said mortgage, full payment and satisfaction of the same; and in consideration thereof it hereby cancels and discharges said mortgage, and releases and quitclaims unto the said and heirs and assigns forever all interest acquired under said mortgage in the premises thereby conveyed. 

The use of this form, which acknowledged payment by, and discharged the mortgage to, a named person, often resulted in the following error on the record. The mortgagor having con-

veyed the land to another subject to the mortgage, the new owner would later pay the mortgage and the mortgagee would thereupon execute a discharge in the form above, acknowledging payment by and discharging the mortgage to the mortgagor who had parted with his equity of redemption. This situation on the record suggested the possibility that such a discharge might be held to operate as an equitable assignment of the mortgage to the original mortgagor. The conveyancers were inclined to ignore these situations, especially if some time had elapsed, which view was supported by a decision of the Land Court in the case of Morris, Petitioner, Land Court Decisions, page 60, where the cases are collected and discussed, except in cases. where the equity had been conveyed subject to the mortgage which the grantee, by the terms of the deed to him, assumed and agreed to pay. In the latter situation, a discharge to the original mortgagor required explanation. 

Chapter 294 of the Acts of 1907 provided that “a mortgage may be discharged by a written acknowledgment of payment or satisfaction of the debt thereby secured, or of the conditions therein contained, etc." This provision was carried into chapter 149 of the Acts of 1908, and thereunder the following form was evolved:

KNOW ALL MEN BY THESE PRESENTS 

that a certain mortgage given by to dated A.D. 1 and recorded with the page 

Registry of Deeds, book do hereby acknowledge that have received full payment and satisfaction of the debt thereby secured and of the conditions therein contained, and in consideration thereof do hereby cancel and discharge said mortgage. 

G. L. c. 183, § 54: A mortgage may be discharged by an entry acknowledging satisfaction thereof, made on the margin of the record of the mortgage in the registry of deeds and signed by the mortgagee, his executor, administrator, successor or assignee, and his signature witnessed by the register of deeds or by the assistant register of deeds, or by some person employed in the registry of deeds designated by the register for that purpose by a writing which shall be recorded in said registry; and such

MORTGAGES. 

189 entry shall have the same effect as a deed of release duly acknowledged and recorded. One of two or more joint holders of a mortgage may so discharge it or he may discharge it by a deed of release duly acknowledged and recorded. A mortgage may also be discharged by a written acknowledgment of payment or satisfaction of the debt thereby secured, or of the conditions therein contained, signed and sealed by the mortgagee, his executor, administrator, successor or assignee. Such instrument shall have the same effect as a deed of release, shall be valid if executed by one of two or more joint holders of a mortgage and may be recorded when duly acknowledged or on proof of its execution in accordance with sections thirty-four to fortyone inclusive.

The statutory form of discharge is holder of a mortgage from corded with to 

Deeds, book acknowledge satisfaction of the same.

Witness hand and seal this dated re- , page day of (Seal.)

By the strict performance of the condition of the mortgage it will become ipso facto discharged, and no written release will be needed except as evidence of the facts. And where the condition is for the payment of money at a time certain, its payment at or before the day will of itself discharge the mortgage. But upon payment after the day, the title will not revest in the mortgagor without further proceedings. - Crocker, page 190.

A mortgaged land to B to secure payment of several notes payable at different times, and afterwards mortgaged the same land to C. After this second mortgage, and before either of the notes, which were secured by the first, fell due, B took from A an absolute deed of the mortgaged land, with covenants of warranty, etc., in full satisfaction and discharge of said notes and of another note due to him from A, and all the notes were thereupon given up to A; but the mortgage was not formally discharged. Held, that B's estate, as mortgagee, was defeated, and that if C's mortgage was valid, he had a complete and adequate remedy at law by writ of entry against B, and therefore that he could not, as second mortgagee, maintain a bill in equity against B to redeem the land. Holman v. Bailey, 3 Met. 55.

DEEDS, MORTGAGES AND EAsements. 

In Depon v. Shawye, 263 Mass. 206, it was held that a sale for the purpose of foreclosing a mortgage, which was perfect in its compliance with formal requisites for such a sale, was invalid for the reason that the mortgagor having tendered to the mortgagee, on the day of maturity, the correct balance due on the mortgage, the mortgagee's title came to an end and with it his power to sell. It was said that even if it should be assumed that, in consequence of our laws in regard to the recording of deeds, the mortgagee might convey a title although he did not have one, he could not do so to a purchaser with notice, which was the fact in the case at bar, the purchaser at the sale having been present when the tender was made. As to the right generally of a purchaser at foreclosure sale, see section 59 post. 66 Where, however, a mortgage was paid before the day on which it was due, and no discharge or release was placed on record, and the mortgagee afterwards, but before the maturity of the mortgage note, assigned the mortgage for value to a bona fide holder, it was held that in the hands of the latter the mortgage could be enforced in full against a bona fide second mortgagee, who did not know of the existence of the first mortgage; and it was said that the rule would have been different if the first mortgage had been paid on or after the day when it fell due. 

Watson v. Wyman, 161 Mass. 96, 99." Crocker, page 190. 

In Watson v. Wyman, the Court said: "Payment of the mortgage note on the day when it falls due is performance of the promise, and very possibly would discharge the note even as against one who took it for value and without notice later on the same day. But payment before the day, or a satisfaction like that in the present case, is a defence which binds only the party receiving payment and those who stand in his shoes." "Where a mortgagor has been in uninterrupted possession of the mortgaged premises for twenty years, the law will presume that the debt has been paid and the mortgage discharged, unless there is evidence of part payment of principal or interest within that time, or other positive and unequivocal evidence that the debt has not been paid." Kellogg v. Dickinson, 147 

Mass. 432.

For statutory provision for obtaining a decree of court (Superior) in effect discharging a mortgage in cases where the

MORTGAGES. 

191 mortgagor and those claiming under him have been in uninterrupted possession of the mortgaged premises for twenty years, without having recognized the existence of the mortgage, see R. L. c. 182, § 15. Jurisdiction was transferred to the

Land Court in 1904 (Acts of 1904, c. 448). The act was amended by chapter 533 of the Acts of 1913. See G. L. c. 240, 

§ 15, as amended by chapter 20 of the Acts of 1924. The Land 

Court has prepared for the bar a statement of the rules and procedure under this process.

In Crowley v. Adams, 226 Mass. 582, a petitioner in the Land 

Court for registration of title to two parcels of land claimed title under a person who, in behalf of M, purchased the two parcels at sales in foreclosure of mortgages alleged to have been assigned to M. One of these mortgages was so assigned more than thirty-five years after the time limited therein for the performance of the condition thereof, and the other was assigned more than thirty-two years after the expiration of the time for performance of that mortgage. The trial judge found that from the lapse of time and the disappearance or non-existence of the note and mortgage relating to the first parcel, the various transactions as to the second parcel, and the undisputed possession of the premises by the mortgagor's successor in title and his heirs for more than twenty years before the commencement of this attempt to obtain title to the land, the presumption of payment had not been overcome, and dismissed the petition. The 

Supreme Court, affirming the decision of the Land Court, said: "It is plain, however, that if at the date of the assignments to M the debts secured had been paid, as the judge found, there was no default or breach of condition, and under such circumstances the foreclosure in each instance, whether by entry or by sale under the power, did not pass a good title either to the person entering or to the purchaser even if the purchaser bought for value and in good faith, or to any subsequent purchaser claiming under him without notice."

For a case where the presumption of payment was rebutted, although no payments of principal or interest had apparently been made during the twenty years between the date of a mortgage and the date of its assignment, see Bertram v. Wilbur, 246 

Mass. 377.

55. MERGER. 

As a general rule, the assignment of a mortgage to the owner of the equity of redemption in the mortgaged premises will operate to extinguish the mortgage title by merger, and no valid assignment of such mortgage to a third person can subsequently be made. Carlton v. Jackson, 121 Mass. 592. And a conveyance of the equity to the holder of the mortgage will necessarily have the same effect. But where a mortgagee purchases the equity and gives up the mortgage note, the title is not to be deemed to have been merged or extinguished so as to enable a third party to set up an intervening title or incumbrance. New England Jewelry Co. v. Merriam, 2 Allen, 390; 

Farwell v. Cotting, 8 Allen, 211. And where the mortgage comes into the possession of the owner of the equity charged with a trust, there is no merger, even though the trust is an oral one. Bailey v. Wood, 211 Mass. 37.

It is commonly said by the text writers that whether merger takes place or not is purely a matter of intention. The Massachusetts rule, and the true rule on principle, seems to be that whether merger takes place or not depends not upon the actual intention of the parties, expressed or unexpressed, but upon whether there is or is not an intervening interest or right to keep the mortgage and the equity of redemption apart. See 

Land Court Decisions at page 43.

In the case last mentioned it appeared that one Burnham, assignee of a mortgage, took a release of the equity by deed which contained the following recital: "this conveyance shall not operate to merge the title acquired by the assignment of the mortgage." There was nothing to show any interest or estate intervening between the mortgage title and the equity of redemption. The Land Court declared the Massachusetts rule to be as above stated, and said further, "In the case at bar there is no reason for the interposition of equity to stop or change the ordinary course of the legal title. The mortgage title and the equity of redemption came together in the same person. There being no intervening interest to keep them apart, they merged of necessity."

MORTGAGES. 

193 In Cheffee v. Geageah, 253 Mass. 586, under agreement with a mortgagor and her mortgagee, for the purpose of negotiating a sale and for the protection of both parties, one Greene received a deed of the equity from the mortgagor and also an assignment of the mortgage from the mortgagee. Later, as ordered by a decree in equity, Greene reconveyed the land to the mortgagor subject to the mortgage. The action was a writ of entry. The demandant claimed under a sheriff's deed which purported to convey all the title and interest of the mortgagor as of a date following the reconveyance to her by Greene. The tenant claimed under a foreclosure of the mortgage. The trial judge found that it was not the intention of the parties to merge the absolute title conveyed by the mortgage and the deed of the equity, unless the proposed sale was consummated; and that until that occurred Greene held the title on an oral trust in favor of the mortgagor, which could not merge and unite with the title afterwards, under the assignment of the mortgage which he held on another trust in favor of the mortgagee. 

Judgment for tenant. Demandant appealed. Judgment for tenant affirmed. The Supreme Court said: “The finding that there was no merger of title in Greene is final. We cannot revise findings of fact made in the Land Court. Day v. Boulle, 234 Mass. 25. The question whether there was a merger depended upon the interest of Greene in taking the assignments, and the intent of the parties. . . . The Court also found that 

Greene held the title under an oral trust. Upon that finding there was no merger of the title in Greene by virtue of the deed to him and the assignment of the mortgage. Bailey v. 

Wood, 211 Mass. 37, 45, 46."

Of course, the decision is sound, although the reasoning may seem to be opposed to what has been said above. Undoubtedly the Court meant to say that the parties intended that Greene should hold the equity on a trust for the mortgagor, and the mortgage on a trust for the mortgagee. This being so, as a matter of law no merger could result regardless of any further intention.

56. REDEMPTION. 

For statutory provisions see G. L. c. 244, §§ 18-34. 

SECTION 20. If the mortgagee or person claiming or holding under him has had possession of the land, he shall account for rents and profits, and be allowed for all amounts expended in reasonable repairs and improvements, for all lawful taxes and assessments paid and for all other necessary expenses in the care and management of the land. A balance of such account, if due from him, shall be deducted from the debt due on the mortgage; if due to him, shall be added to the debt, and paid or tendered as such. "If nothing was due the mortgagee at the time of foreclosure, the sale made under the power of foreclosure was invalid. Rogers v. Barnes, 169 Mass. 179. The plaintiff, therefore, was entitled to a reconveyance upon paying such sum as might be found due on an accounting under G. L. c. 244, § 20. The facts found justified the conclusion that such expenditure as had been made upon the premises, although greater than ordinarily would be allowed, MacFarlane v. Thompson, 241 Mass. 486, had been authorized by the mortgagor." Rehrig v. Inman, 258 Mass. 431.

Having assigned his mortgage as collateral, a mortgagee entered, collected rents and made expenditures in completing a second story and rebuilding a cesspool, and advertised the estate for sale under the power in the mortgage. On the question of accounting, it was held that, inasmuch as defendant had no legal right to exercise the power of sale, the mortgagor should not be required to pay the expenses incurred by defendant in advertising the sale, but that it would be inequitable not to require the plaintiff to reimburse the mortgagee for reasonable expenditures made by him in good faith which were a benefit to the property, although he had no right to make entry under the statute. 

MacFarlane v. Thompson, 241 Mass. 486. "Plaintiff contends that she has the rights of a junior encumbrancer and is entitled to be subrogated to the rights of the senior mortgagee. If it be assumed in her favor that she, as holder of a part of the bond issue secured by the second mortgage, is entitled to redeem, still she has not paid the first mortgage; and if she could be found to have tendered payment, the

MORTGAGES. 

195 offer was made upon condition that the mortgage be assigned, a condition which a junior mortgagee redeeming has no right to impose." Linsky v. Exchange Trust Co., 260 Mass. 15.

There is no fixed rule of law limiting the compensation to which a mortgagee in possession of real estate may be entitled for his services in the care and management of the mortgaged premises. Barry v. Dow, 240 Mass. 419. A mortgagee in possession ordinarily is entitled to fair pay for his services in managing the property. Barry v. Harlow, 242 Mass. 159.

One interested in the estate of a deceased mortgagor of real estate only as a creditor who, without making an attachment of the real estate, has brought a suit in equity to enforce his claim, has no right to redeem the real estate from the mortgage nor to maintain a suit to set aside a foreclosure sale for alleged failure to give notice thereof and because of inadequacy of the price for which it was struck off to the purchaser. Talbot v. Gingras, 246 Mass. 356.

An attempted foreclosure by sale was defective by reason of insufficient notice, and the mortgagor brought a suit in equity to redeem, in which, in 1892, a decree was made that plaintiff be allowed to redeem upon paying specified sums of money to the holder of the mortgage within forty-five days. The mortgagor failed to pay as ordered, and thereupon a final decree was entered dismissing the bill. In 1917 the mortgagor brought a bill in equity to obtain a conveyance of the property to him on the alleged ground that there had been no valid foreclosure, and it was held that the final decree dismissing the former bill after the plaintiff had failed to pay the amounts due within the time prescribed effectually foreclosed the mortgage, and thereafter his rights to redeem were lost. Dennett v. 

Atkins, 232 Mass. 301.

A wife may maintain a bill in equity to redeem, from a mortgage, real estate in which she has an inchoate right of dower (Fitcher v. Griffiths, 216 Mass. 174), but such right gives her no right to maintain a bill to redeem her husband's real estate from a mortgage made by him in which she did not join. Ryder v. Brockton Savings Bank, 235 Mass. 476, and 238 Mass. 52.

In a case where a married woman is so entitled to redeem, if she pays the holder of the mortgage, she is entitled to the note

and mortgage held by him. In equity these may be kept alive and enforced for her benefit. Even at law they would be kept alive in her hands, and, after the decease of her husband, she could enforce them against his estate, and during his life she may transfer a good title to third persons who may enforce them against him directly. Fitcher v. Griffiths, 216 Mass. 174.

The purchaser of a husband's right of redemption at a sale in foreclosure of a junior mortgage of real estate in which the mortgagor's wife did not release her inchoate right of dower, is entitled, in a suit by him to redeem from a prior mortgage upon the premises, only to a decree discharging that mortgage upon payment of the principal and proper interest charges and expenses, and is not entitled to an assignment of the mortgage, because that would give him a right superior to the wife's inchoate right of dower. Ellis v. Sullivan, 241 Mass. 60. "The intervening plaintiff (holder of a second mortgage) contends that he has a right to redeem after the auction sale and before the conveyance is made. If a foreclosure sale is fairly conducted and there is no defect in the proceedings, the right of the intervener to redeem is gone when the contract of sale was made with the purchaser at the auction. . . . Beal v. Attleborough Savings Bank, 248 Mass. 342, is not in conflict, and is not an authority for the contention that the right to redeem continues until the conveyance is made. In that case the point decided was that the mortgagee who had entered to foreclose and who sold the property under the power in the mortgage deed was accountable to the mortgagor for the rents received by the mortgagee between the date of the foreclosure sale and the time the conveyance was made. In the course of the opinion it is said that the mortgagor has an equity of redemption until conveyance is made under the power of sale. . . . Until the contract of sale is completed the mortgagor has an equity in the property, but it is not strictly accurate to designate this interest as an equity of redemption.. The mortgagor, after a valid auction sale under a power of sale in the mortgage deed, has no right to redeem, although he has an ownership of the equity." White v. Macarelli, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 1379.

McKnight v. Winkler, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 1529 was a bill in equity whereby the plaintiff, owner of land through the fore-

MORTGAGES. 

197 closure of a third mortgage, sought to restrain the holder of a second mortgage from foreclosing. After the first mortgagee had begun foreclosure proceedings, defendant, in order to protect his title, paid the first mortgagee, $457.25, which included sums on account of principal and interest due under the first mortgage and expenses in connection with the foreclosure proceedings. It was held that defendant was entitled to be paid the sum of $457.25 paid by him, with interest, before the mortgage referred to in the bill should be discharged or redeemed, and it was said that plaintiff had had the benefit of the amount paid by defendant to the first mortgagee, and in equity and good conscience should reimburse defendant before he becomes entitled to redeem.

57. Foreclosure of Mortgage. 

58. Foreclosure by Entry. 

59. Foreclosure of Mortgage by Exercise of the Power of Sale 60. Foreclosure of Mortgage by Action. 

57. 

FORECLOSURE OF MORTGAGE. 

There are various methods by which a mortgage may be foreclosed upon breach of condition, namely: 

1. By entry. 

2. By exercise of the power of sale which is commonly inserted in the mortgage. 

3. By action. (a) Writ of entry. (b) Bill in equity. 

58. FORECLOSURE BY ENTRY. 

G. L. c. 244, §§ 1, 2 provides as follows: "A mortgagee may, after breach of condition of a mortgage of land, recover possession of the land mortgaged by an open and peaceable entry thereon, if not opposed by the mortgagor or other person claiming it, . . . and possession so obtained, if continued peaceably for three years, shall forever foreclose the right of redemption.

If an entry for breach of condition is made . . . a memorandum of the entry shall be made on the mortgage deed and signed by. the mortgagor or by the person claiming under him, or a certificate, under oath, of two competent witnesses to prove the entry shall be made. Such memorandum or certificate shall within thirty days after the entry, except as provided in section seventy of chapter one hundred and eighty-five (registered land), be recorded in the registry of deeds for the county or district where the land lies, ... Unless such record is made, the entry shall not be effectual for the purposes mentioned in the preceding section." 

In Fletcher v. Cary, 103 Mass. 475, at 478, the Court said: "If the entry be duly recorded, it is wholly immaterial whether the owner of the equity of redemption had actual knowledge of it or not. An entry upon a small portion of a large tract

MORTGAGES. 

199 is sufficient. An entry on one of several lots included in the same mortgage is also sufficient, though they may be quite remote from each other. It is no valid objection to such entry that it was made in the night time, and was intended to be secret. Ellis v. Drake, 8 Allen, 161. Permitting the mortgagor to continue in the occupation of the premises is also held not to defeat the operation of an entry for foreclosure. 

The rule of law as now held seems to be that the entry by the mortgagee for condition broken, in the presence of two witnesses, and a certificate thereof duly sworn to before a justice of the peace, and duly recorded, are all that are necessary to effect a foreclosure. Since the provision in the Rev. Sts. c. 107, § 2, for recording the evidence of the entry of the mortgagee, this must be considered constructive notice, by which all persons may ascertain the relation which the mortgagee holds to the property; and the mortgagor, and all claiming under him, are conclusively prevented from holding adversely to his paramount right."

But after entry for breach of condition of a mortgage of real estate, duly certified and recorded, and the lapse of three years, the mortgagor, in defense of a writ of entry to recover possession of the estate, may show by parol evidence that there has been no breach of condition. Pettee v. Case, 77 Mass. 478. 

G. L. c. 244, § 35: If, after the foreclosure of a mortgage not containing a power of sale, the person entitled to the debt recovers judgment for any part thereof on the ground that the value of the land mortgaged at the time of the foreclosure was less than the amount due, such recovery shall open the foreclosure, and the person entitled may redeem the land, although the three years limited therefor have expired, if suit for redemption is brought within one year after the recovery of such judgment.

This statute originated in the Revised Statutes of 1836, before the adoption of power of sale mortgages, and was then applicable to the "foreclosure of any mortgage." It was carried in substantially the same form into Public Statutes, c. 181, § 42, and was amended by chapter 203 of the Acts of 1896 by adding the words "but nothing in this section shall apply to a foreclosure under a power of sale mortgage."

If, after a mortgagee, upon breach of the mortgage conditions, had made an entry for the purpose of foreclosure, he did not record the certificate required by the statute until thirty-two days after the entry, but remained in possession of the land, collecting rents and profits for more than three years, and then made another entry, recorded the required certificate within thirty days thereafter, and also duly sold the property in foreclosure under a power of sale contained in the mortgage for an amount which, with the rents and profits received since the first entry, was insufficient to satisfy the mortgage note, he may maintain an action for the balance still due upon the note, the first entry and possession for three years thereafter not operating to discharge the note. Fitchburg Co-operative Bank v. Normandin, 236 Mass. 332.

In Mitchell v. Wright, 234 Mass. 458, it was held that a foreclosure by sale under the power contained in a mortgage was not opened by the later action of the purchaser at the sale in making an entry under a prior mortgage on the same property, and it was said that the only possible effect of such entry was by way of further assurance of title, or as the assertion of a right paramount to intervening incumbrances, if any. 

59. FORECLOSURE OF MORTGAGE BY EXERCISE OF THE POWER OF SALE. 

For statutory provisions see G. L. c. 244, §§ 11-17. 

66 . In Sedgewick v. Lafin, 10 Allen, 430, it was held that a mortgage of land to an individual, his successors and assigns forever," conveyed only a life estate. This was a writ of entry by the executor of the mortgagee to foreclose the mortgage, and it was held that as the estate granted terminated with the mortgagee's life, the plaintiff had no title on which to maintain the writ. The Court suggested, however, that the power of sale, if it had been executed by the mortgagee, would have transmitted an estate in fee, and said, "A power to dispose of land in fee may be and often is given to a tenant for life." Under the present statute undoubtedly a mortgage in the form above noted would convey a fee simple to the mortgagee. See G. L. c. 183, 

§ 13.

MORTGAGES. 

201 $59 It should be noted here that the method of foreclosing by exercising a power of sale is entirely distinct from foreclosure by entry. At the present time a mortgagee who is obliged to foreclose commonly proceeds under both methods. If he does so, and any technical defect in his sale develops, he has the advantage of his entry and possession which, under the statute, will ripen in three years.

Power of sale mortgages are not founded primarily on any statute, but are a matter of contract between the parties. They were evolved in order to meet the increase of business transactions requiring loans and the desire to have a more speedy process of foreclosing than was furnished by suit or entry. 

For the statutory power of sale which is incorporated by reference in the form of mortgage now in common use, see G. L. c. 183, § 21. Conditions and agreements in addition to those set forth in the statutory condition (section 20) may be inserted in a mortgage otherwise in the statutory form, and the power, which refers to a breach of the "foregoing or other condition," may be exercised in case of default thereunder. In American 

House Hotel Co. v. Hemenway, 237 Mass. 180, the added provision was that "in case any default in the condition of this mortgage shall exist for more than thirty days the entire mortgage debt shall become due at the option of the holder hereof," and it was held that this agreement, which the Court referred to as "somewhat drastic," was within the scope of the statutory condition and power. In Greene v. Richards, 244 Mass. 495, the added condition provided for the construction of two bathrooms in the building covered by the mortgage. 

Mr. Crocker said: "So far as the author is aware, the first reported case recognizing the existence of mortgages with powers of sale is Howard v. Ames, 3 Met. 308, decided in 1841." 

In 1857 a statute was passed providing in substance that the mortgagee in a power of sale mortgage might upon breach do all the things required by the power, and should within thirty days after selling the property cause a copy of the notice of sale and an affidavit setting forth his acts to be recorded; and that, if it should appear by such affidavit that he has in all respects complied with the power in the mortgage, the affidavit

859 or a certified copy of the record of the same should be admitted as evidence that the power of sale was duly executed. 

It next occurred to the Legislature to do something for the protection of the mortgagor, and by Acts of 1877, chapter 215 (in effect June 12, 1877), it was provided that notice of the sale should be published once a week for three successive weeks, the first publication to be not less than twenty-one days before the day of sale, in some newspaper published in the town where the mortgaged land lies. Chapter 75 of the 

Acts of 1882 provided that if there was no newspaper published in the town, then any newspaper published in the county might be used. 

In the case of Rose v. Fall River Five Cents Savings Bank, 165 Mass. 273, which concerned the foreclosure of a mortgage in the town of Dighton, Bristol County, publication of notice was made in the "Fall River News," a paper published in the said county. The statute then in force required the publication of notice of foreclosure in a "newspaper, if there is any, published in the city or town wherein the mortgaged premises are situated." A newspaper called the "Dighton Rock," which had the same contents as a newspaper called the "Fall River Advertiser," but a different heading and date line, was printed in Fall River, and a few copies were sent to Dighton to regular subscribers or for sale and distribution. Held, that the newspaper described was not published in Dighton within the meaning of the statute, and that accordingly the publication in the "Fall 

River News" was sufficient.

Then came Acts of 1912, chapter 360: Whenever notice, advertisement or publication in connection with the foreclosure of mortgages or sale of real estate for non-payment of taxes or under execution is required by law, or otherwise, to be made or published in a newspaper printed or published in a town, city or county within the commonwealth, such notice, advertisement or publication shall be sufficient for all purposes if made in a newspaper printed or published, or which shall by its title page purport to be printed or published, in such town, city or county, and which shall have a circulation therein. 

When the General Laws were compiled a similar provision as to foreclosure sales was inserted in section 14 of chapter 244,

Dec 

MORTGAGES. 

203 and apparently what remained of the 1912 act was carried in a slightly different form into clause 8 of section 6 of chapter 4, which also provides that when publication is required in a newspaper published in a city or town, it shall be sufficient, when there is no newspaper published therein, if the publication is made in a newspaper published in the county where the city or town is situated. In Ellis v. Lyford, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 227, the validity of a sale of land in Sudbury on execution was before the Court. Section 28 of chapter 236 provides that notice of such sales must be published in a newspaper published in the town where the land lies. There was no newspaper published in Sudbury. There was a paper printed in Hudson which was circulated in Sudbury. The Sudbury edition bore at the top of the first page the name "The Sudbury News." At the top of the first column on page 4 appeared: "News-Enterprise, Editions in Hudson, . The News, Editions in Maynard, Acton, Sudbury, 11 Church Street, Hudson. . . . Entered at the Post

Office at Hudson, Mass. . . "There was another paper called "Sudbury Enterprise," printed in Marlborough. No evidence was introduced as to the circulation of this paper in Sudbury. 

It bore at the top of the first page the name "Sudbury Enterprise," and just below, "The Weekly Enterprise." On page 4 at the top of a double column appeared: "The Weekly Enter prise Office: 1 Liberty Street, Marlboro, Mass. Published at Marlboro, Mass. . . . Entered as Second Class Matter . . 

at the Post Office at Marlboro, Mass. Maynard, Concord, Sudbury, Acton, Bedford." The sheriff elected to ignore these chain papers and published in the "Framingham News." 

In deciding that the publication was sufficient, the Court said: "We are of opinion that neither of these papers purported by its title page to be printed or published in the town of Sudbury and it is manifest that no newspaper was actually printed or published in that town. In these circumstances the advertisement of sale was properly published in a newspaper in the adjoining town of Framingham." Since the enactment of the 1912 act, it has been assumed by the conveyancers that the word "title page" referred to the first page of a paper only, and that the use of such a paper as is described in the act was permitted but not required. The latter

5ac204 

view was approved by the Court so far as foreclosures were concerned in Brown v. Learmouth, 228 Mass. 417.

After the publication of Ellis v. Lyford in the Advance Sheets, the opinion was altered by striking out the language above quoted and by inserting the following: "Although G. L. c. 4, § 6, cl. 8, permitted the publication in the 'Sudbury Enterprise' or in the 'Sudbury News,' it was not required to be published in either of those newspapers, but inasmuch as no newspaper was actually printed or published in Sudbury, the publication in the 'Framingham News' was sufficient." If the original statement which ran contra to the general understanding of the statute had been allowed to stand, it would have seriously affected and perhaps would have completely destroyed the safeguard which was intended by the act of 1912, for the reason that there is probably no one newspaper in the Commonwealth that does not disclose somewhere within its sheets the location of its home office and where it is actually printed. 

The most frequent errors in foreclosures by sale are failure to publish the first of the three successive notices at least twenty-one days before the day of sale, and failure to record the affidavit within thirty days after the sale as required by the statute. The first-mentioned defect makes the whole proceeding void; the second, while it renders the title under the foreclosure unmarketable, is not fatal. It is true that such an affidavit is not admissible in evidence, but in court proceedings the facts therein set forth may be proved by testimony, and under the provisions of G. L. c. 233, §§ 46–51, it is possible by proceedings taken before two justices of the peace to perpetuate testimony, and a deposition taken as provided by the statute and recorded in the proper registry of deeds is admissible in evidence under certain circumstances as set forth in section 51. 

But if either or both of these defects occurred more than twenty years ago, then proof of possession under the mortgage will make a good title on the authority of Stevens v. Dedham Institution for Savings, 129 Mass. 547, which holds that equity will not allow a mortgagor a longer period than twenty years within which to redeem.

Although the failure to record the affidavit of sale within thirty days, as required by G. L. c. 244, § 15, does not render

Sax. 205 

MORTGAGES. 

the foreclosure deed void, one claiming title under the foreclosure could not convey "good and clear record title,” as she had contracted to do, since resort to extrinsic evidence that the power of sale was properly exercised would be necessary in order to establish that the foreclosure was valid. O'Meara v. Gleason, 246 Mass. 136.

Under a mortgage with a power of sale in the usual form a mortgagor cannot compel the mortgagee to exercise the power; it is for his benefit and he can exercise it or not as he sees fit. 

Dennett v. Atkins, 232 Mass. 301; Tourtellotte v. Saulnier, 267 

Mass. 361.

One who takes an assignment of a mortgage as collateral security for an obligation may exercise the power of sale in case of default. MacFarlane v. Thompson, 241 Mass. 486; U. S. Trust 

Co. v. Commonwealth, 245 Mass. 75.

Many titles resting on foreclosure of mortgages have been rendered unmarketable because of failure to observe the rule that in exercising the power of sale the terms of the power must be strictly complied with. A foreclosure sale should not be considered as a sale of the land described in the mortgage subject to such incumbrances as may then exist, but as the exercise of a power given at a previous date to sell the estate described in the mortgage upon the happening of certain events. See the case of Seldner, Petitioner, Land Court Decisions, page 39.

A power of sale in a mortgage of land, which authorizes the mortgagee to advertise and sell at auction the mortgaged premises, including all equity of redemption in the mortgagor, does not authorize a sale of the equity of redemption separately; and if the notice of sale states only that the mortgagor's equity of redemption will be sold, it is insufficient, and a sale of the premises under such a notice is invalid and passes no title to a purchaser which will bar the right of redemption. Fowle v. 

Merrill, 10 Allen, 350.

A sale of land, under a power contained in a second mortgage, of the entire estate, free from incumbrances, is invalid. Donohue v. Chase, 130 Mass. 137. In this case the Court said: "A proper execution of the power of sale contained in the mortgage requires the mortgagee to sell all he is entitled to sell under it. 

For the same reason that he has no power to sell less than

See.59 the whole interest, he has no right to sell a greater interest than the mortgage gives him authority to sell." See also Dearnaley v. Chase, 136 Mass. 288.

The common practice of selling subject to "all unpaid taxes, tax liens and other municipal assessments, if any," is accepted on the grounds that such incumbrances are paramount liens of which all persons must take notice, and concerning the existence of which there can be no question. 

In Donohue v. Chase, supra, it was also said that "no question is made that the notice of sale was in conformity to the terms of the power therein contained. The only question presented relates to the validity of the sale itself," followed by a recital of the evidence of what the auctioneer stated at the sale. Nothing was said about the form of the deed given to the purchaser. 

This suggests that the "validity of the sale2 is to be determined from what takes place before and at the sale, and that, if proceedings have been regular up to the time when the property is knocked down to the highest bidder, the form of the deed thereafter given to him is immaterial. This view has not been generally accepted, however (see the case of Seldner, Petitioner, supra, in which the finding that the foreclosure of a third mortgage was bad rests upon the fact that the deed described the land but did not set forth the prior mortgages, although they were properly set forth in the notice), but it is believed that there has always been a tendency to accept in the deed a variance from the power, provided the prior steps were in proper form. 

It was attempted by section 439 of chapter 257 of the Acts of 1918 (G. L. c. 244, § 14) to lessen the number of defective foreclosure sales arising from failure to observe the foregoing rules. 

This amendment set up a statutory form of notice and provided that "the premises shall be deemed to have been sold, and the deed thereunder shall convey the premises, subject to and with the benefit of all restrictions, easements, improvements, outstanding tax titles, municipal or other public taxes, assessments, liens or claims in the nature of liens, and existing encumbrances of record created prior to the mortgage, whether or not reference to such restrictions, easements, improvements, liens or encumbrances is made in the deed." It seems probable that many

Sec. .

MORTGAGES. 

207 of the defects in foreclosure proceedings at which this statute was aimed originate in a fear that the mortgagee in selling may assume some personal responsibility if he fails to sell and convey subject to all existing incumbrances whether mentioned in the mortgage or not. The form of notice provided encourages the seller to conform to his power, and presumably that portion of the statute above quoted was intended to further bolster up his courage so that he would not feel obliged to set out in his deed all manner of incumbrances appearing at some time in the chain of title but not referred to in the mortgage. 

The statutory notice conformed to the notice then in common use with the following direction in the blank intended for description of the property, viz.: "(Description exactly as in the mortgage, including all references to title, restrictions, encumbrances, etc., as made in the mortgage.)". This form has assisted the situation, but it has not proved a cure-all, and the conveyancer is sometimes in a difficult position when it comes to reciting incumbrances in such a notice, first, because of the fear that a notice which conforms exactly to the mortgage may be in fact misleading because of changes in circumstances after the execution of the mortgage, and may be prejudicial to the rights of the owner of the equity because it may discourage bidding, and that such a situation may render the sale voidable at the election of such owner. For example, a prior incumbrance mentioned in the mortgage may have been discharged. 

In such a case to follow the statutory form and to advertise the estate for sale subject to such incumbrance might work to the disadvantage of the equity owner. However, the statutory form of notice calls for strict adherence to the mortgage as written, and presumably a mortgagee who follows that form will be protected so far as the owner of the equity is concerned, although a statement of the facts as they then existed might have made the sale more attractive to possible purchasers. 

This view is borne out by a statement in Russell v. Bon, 221 

Mass. 370, decided in 1915 before the statutory form of notice was adopted, in which case the notice literally followed the description of the premises found in the mortgage, as also the provisions therein contained that the conveyance was "subject to a first mortgage for $10,000 and a second mortgage upon

Sec.59 which there now remains unpaid the sum of $3,350." The notice continued: "Said premises will be sold, . . . subject to said first mortgage for $10,000, to said second mortgage upon which there now remains unpaid the sum of $3,350 (the undersigned is informed that said second mortgage has been reduced by payments on account thereof)." In fact, at the time of the notice there was due only $1,100 on the second mortgage. The owners of the equity brought a bill in equity to set aside the sale. The trial court dismissed the bill. The plaintiffs alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court sustained the exceptions and adjudged that a decree be entered declaring the sale “invalid and void." The reasoning was that the parenthetical statement that the second mortgage had been reduced by payments on account thereof, when read with the statement in the mortgage deed and repeated in the notice that "there remains unpaid the sum of $3,350," reasonably might be interpreted to mean that the debt originally secured by the second mortgage was in excess of $3,350, and that by reason of payments there remained unpaid the sum of $3,350, and that possible purchasers might have been misled by the notice to the belief that the second mortgage stood as security for a debt larger by $2,250 than it was in fact, and that such belief might well have discouraged attendance at the sale.

The Court also said, this being the statement above referred to, "Before exercising the power of sale the mortgagee was held to strict observance of all express and implied conditions precedent contained in the mortgage or imposed by law. He was under no legal obligation to ascertain whether the first or second mortgage had been paid in whole or in part; much less was it his duty to make any statement in the advertisement of sale with reference thereto. It sufficed to state the facts as found in the instrument creating the power. Other statements he made at his peril."

It is not clear as to the weight given to the parenthetical statement in the notice. The reasoning above referred to implies that the decision turns on that statement, but later reference to a request for a ruling by the plaintiffs suggests that the decision would have been the same had that statement not appeared. It is difficult to see why it should not have been the

Ses 5'

MORTGAGES. 

209 same. It would seem that the damage was done when it appeared that the premises were subject to "a second mortgage upon which there now remains unpaid the sum of $3,350," and yet that was the language of the mortgage. Under the statute and the form of notice now prescribed thereby, an exact copy of the description in the mortgage, "including all references to title, restrictions, encumbrances, etc., as made in the mortgage," is a 'sufficient notice of the sale," and it seems that the addition of a parenthetical statement like that appearing in Russell v. Bon should not be considered as discouraging bidders and thus rendering the sale voidable. It might be taken to mean, as the 

Court said in that case, "that there was reason to believe that the second mortgage debt was something less than $3,350." It could not reasonably be taken to mean that the debt was more than that sum which was the amount stated in the mortgage. 

Notwithstanding the statutory form of notice, it is believed that many capable conveyancers would hesitate to follow that form literally in a case like Russell v. Bon, quoting the language of the mortgage referring to the prior mortgages, without stating in some manner that there had been a reduction in the amount secured by the second mortgage. It is suggested that this might be safely done by saying: "the undersigned is informed that payments have been made on the second mortgage above mentioned which have reduced the principal sum secured thereby below said sum of $3,350." 

Goldman v. Damon, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 1573, was an action of contract in which there was judgment for defendant. 

Plaintiff alleged that defendant, holder of a third mortgage on real and personal property of plaintiff, became the purchaser at foreclosure sale thereunder, bidding $6,000 above a first mortgage for $28,000 and a second mortgage represented at the sale as securing $10,650; that in fact but $5,000 was due on the second mortgage, as defendant well knew, and consequently he owes plaintiff the difference, $5,650. The third mortgage (statutory form) was drawn subject to "a second mortgage upon which there is now due the sum of $10,650." It was found that the foreclosure was duly advertised, and that the notice set out as required by G. L. c. 244, § 14, the description of the premises, "exactly as in the mortgage, including all references to 

---

See.59 title, restrictions, encumbrances, etc., as made in the mortgage," and that the amount due on the third mortgage in accord with its terms was $6,050. At the sale, when the auctioneer read from the notice a statement of the second mortgage "on which there is now due, $10,650," a person who was present interrupted and suggested that this was an error and that the amount then due on the second mortgage was $6,350. The auctioneer conferred with the defendant and then said, "No, that is right; $10,650 is correct." The notice, at the plaintiff's request, was again read and the auctioneer made the same statement as to the amount due on the second mortgage. It was again suggested to him that the amount of the second mortgage had been reduced. The auctioneer again stated that the amount of the second mortgage was $10,650, and called for bids. The only bid made was that of the defendant, who became the purchaser. 

The Court said that all a mortgagee can sell is the interest in the property conveyed by the mortgage; that upon such a sale the former owner of the equity becomes entitled to any surplus, which he may recover from the mortgagee by action at law; that the amount bid at the sale is the only criterion of the value where the action is at law, and "If through fraud, collusion or mistake in the proceedings the price obtained is not a true criterion of the value, the remedy is in equity to set aside the sale. 

Obviously nothing is due if no valid sale has been made. 

Consequently, if he sues for a surplus, he affirms the validity of the sale so far as it concerns him. These fundamental conceptions are not changed if the holder of the mortgage becomes himself the purchaser. . . his bid at the sale stands like that of any bid of a third person; it is his offer for what he will pay for what is there for sale. . . . If the plaintiff believed that more was sold at the sale than was legally possible . . . his remedy was in equity to set aside the proceedings, and either redeem or let another sale be made. If he wished the sale to stand, he must take it as a sale at a price of $6,000 of what legally could be sold. No surplus then appears.” Another difficulty which sometimes confronts the conveyancer is that the mortgage to be foreclosed does not disclose incumbrances of which he has knowledge, as, for instance, restrictions. 

Of course, he must not advertise the sale subject to such re-

See.211 

MORTGAGES. 

strictions; to do so would render the sale invalid. But he must disclose the fact in some manner in order to bind the purchaser because the statute provides that "no purchaser at the sale shall be bound to complete the purchase if there are incumbrances, other than those named in the mortgage and included in the notice of sale, which are not stated at the sale and included in the auctioneer's contract with the purchaser." This implies that the mortgagee is permitted to instruct the auctioneer to state such incumbrances at the sale, and the writer believes that the courts would so hold, but it goes without saying that the mortgagee makes such statements at his peril. Such statements, if they are incorrectly made, may invalidate the sale if the equity owner attacks it seasonably. Moreover, referring to the rule in 

Russell v. Bon, it is difficult to support a statement as to such an incumbrance made at the sale, if it is settled that such a statement inserted in the notice would render the sale voidable. If the latter would discourage attendance at the sale, the former would probably discourage bidding at the sale.

In Antonellis v. Weinstein, 258 Mass. 323, the holder of a third mortgage properly advertised a foreclosure sale thereunder subject to two prior mortgages, and became the purchaser at the sale for a sum far in excess of his mortgage. In a suit to recover the surplus above the sum due to the third mortgagee, it was held that he could not be heard to say that he intended to include the first and second mortgage in his bid, because it must be assumed as a matter of law that the mortgagee intended to sell the interest of the mortgagor and his own interest in the property under the power, citing Feuer v. Capilowich, 242 Mass. 

560. In the same case the Court said: "The notice of sale for the foreclosure of mortgages, prescribed by G. L. c. 244, § 14, provides that the premises shall be sold subject, among other things, to ‘existing encumbrances of record created prior to the mortgage.' e."" The language quoted does not appear in the prescribed form; indeed, it seems that the form was devised in order to discourage the insertion of prior incumbrances not stated in the mortgage. The language quoted is found in the provisions of the same section defining the effect of foreclosure deeds. See Chute v. Cronin, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 2365.

It might appear from the decision in Bernstein v. Shain, 263

See159 Mass. 188, that it was considered that G. L. c. 244, § 14, obliges the use of the form of notice therein prescribed, but of course this was not intended, as the section expressly provides that "nothing herein shall be construed to prevent the use of other forms." 

In Way v. Dyer, 176 Mass. 448, it was held that a mistake in an advertisement of sale under power in a mortgage, in overstating the amount due on a prior mortgage, cannot affect a purchaser in good faith and for a valuable consideration after the foreclosure and without any actual or constructive knowledge of the error. See Silva v. Turner, 166 Mass. 407 and Stevenson v. Hano, 148 Mass. 616. See also Rogers v. Barnes, 169 

Mass. 179, discussed infra.

It has occurred to the writer that the difficulties above noted in connection with the foreclosure of mortgages may be greatly relieved by taking a little more thought in the drafting of the mortgages themselves. Usually the person drawing the mortgage is familiar with the chain of title and has information as to what incumbrances do or may exist. This being so, he should so draft the description of the premises that, if the mortgage is later foreclosed, it will be possible to follow the form prescribed by G. L. c. 244, § 14" (Description exactly as in the mortgage, including all references to title, restrictions, encumbrances, etc., as made in the mortgage)", and thereby leave little to be told. 

The duty of one acting under a power of sale in a mortgage is to use that reasonable degree of effort and diligence to secure and protect the interests of the mortgagor, the owners of the equity of redemption and junior lienors, to the observance of which he is bound by the obligation of good faith. Bon v. Graves, 216 

Mass. 440. In this case the acts of the first mortgagee complained of were that in advertising the sale he gave notice for the shortest period permitted by law in a newspaper of limited circulation, although five other newspapers circulated in the neighborhood to a greater extent, and, although he knew that two neighbors wished to buy portions of the land, gave them no notice of the sale, even when one of them asked about it, and gave a second mortgagee known to him no notice of the sale, and at the sale the first mortgagee and those in his interest were the only persons present, and the premises were sold to him

Sex 

MORTGAGES. 

213 for less than three-fifths of their market value, and the Court said that all the acts, taken together, showed a failure to use good faith in executing the power, although there had been a technical compliance with its terms, and although none of the circumstances standing alone would show bad faith. 

For other cases discussing "good faith," see Taylor v. Weingartner, 223 Mass. 243; Manning v. Liberty Trust Co., 234 

Mass. 544; Porter v. Porter, 236 Mass. 422; Clapp v. Gardner, 237 Mass. 187; Flynn v. Curtis & Pope Lumber Co., 245 Mass. 

291; Gadreault v. Sherman, 250 Mass. 145.

A question is sometimes raised as to the amount of cash deposit which the mortgagee may properly require to bind the sale. In Model Lodging House Association v. Boston, 114 Mass. 

133, 139, the Court said: "The objection that the notice is defective because it did not state that one of the terms of sale would be that one hundred dollars should be paid down to bind the bargain, cannot prevail. The mortgagee had the right under the power to advertise the sale as a sale for cash, and to require the whole price bid to be paid at the sale." In Williams v. Van 

Dam, 246 Mass. 61, one of the allegations in a bill to reopen a foreclosure sale under a mortgage given to secure $1,100 was that defendant discouraged bidding by requiring a cash payment of $1,000. It was said that the sale could have been reopened in a suit by the mortgagor in his lifetime and redemption decreed on proof of allegations similar to those of the present bill, because of misconduct in the execution of the power which was admitted by a demurrer. The amount of deposit required was not specifically referred to in the opinion. However, it should be said that notice that a very large deposit will be required is generally frowned upon as suggesting bad faith. See Kavolsky v. Kaufman, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 2293. "Where property, real or personal, is conveyed by a debtor to his creditor, with a power to sell and dispose of it, and apply the proceeds to the payment of the debt, the creditor, in executing such power, becomes the trustee of the debtor, and is bound to act bona fide, and to adopt all reasonable modes of proceeding, in order to render the sale most beneficial to the debtor, like any other agent, factor, or trustee to sell. Like other trustees he cannot himself directly become the purchaser,

Accordingly it came to be the custom to include in a mortgage this language: "And it is agreed that the grantee, his executors, administrators, or assigns, or any person or persons in his or their behalf, may purchase at any sale as aforesaid, etc." contrary No such agreement appears in the statutory mortgage, but see 

G. L. c. 183, § 25. which grounder that the holder of a mastgage as any person acting in his behalf may purchase unless the When the agreement above quoted appeared in a mortgage, the holder of the mortgage was in a position to purchase in his the own name, and, under the power, to make a valid deed to himself as purchaser. Hall v. Bliss, 118 Mass. 554, 559. See also Field v. Gooding, 106 Mass. 310.

But in cases where the mortgage contained no agreement that the holder of the mortgage might purchase, and he did, directly or indirectly, this would not render the sale void, but voidable only at the election of any party whose interests have been prejudiced. Burns v. Thayer, 115 Mass. 89, 93.

And the right to avoid the sale will be lost if it is not asserted within a reasonable time. Learned v. Foster, 117 Mass. 

365, 369. In the latter case it was held that thirteen years was not a reasonable time. "Where a third person has in good faith purchased at the sale, the mortgagee may purchase of him. His trust is ended with the sale. But if there was a previous arrangement between him and the purchaser for a reconveyance, the trust may still attach to him, and the title he has acquired will be voidable. The presumption is in favor of the mortgagee that he has fulfilled his trust until the contrary is shown." - Jones on Mortgages, Vol. 2, § 1876.
