But even if the records in the registry of deeds show a perfect execution of the power, a bona fide purchaser for value at the sale, or a subsequent purchaser from him, will not acquire a good title if there has not been a default in the performmort.

See

MORTGAGES. 

215 ance of the condition of the mortgage. Rogers v. Barnes, 169 

Mass. 179, 183, 184. See also Montague v. Dawes, 12 Allen, 397. In Rogers v. Barnes the Court said: "The law goes a great way in protecting the title of a purchaser for value without notice or knowledge of any defect in the power of the vendor to sell, and his title is not to be affected by mere irregularities in executing a power of sale contained in a mortgage, of which irregularities he has no knowledge, actual or constructive. 

The argument certainly is strong that a bona fide purchaser for value ought to be protected in his title by what appears in the registry of deeds, in the absence of knowledge to the contrary; but the argument, we think, is stronger that a mortgagor should not be deprived, without his knowledge and assent, of his equity of redemption by a sale under a power contained in a mortgage which authorizes a sale only in case of a default, when there has been no default." See also Crowley v. Adams, 226

Mass. 582, and Depon v. Shawye, 263 Mass. 206, discussed in section 54, ante; Rehrig v. Inman, 258 Mass. 431.

In Montague v. Dawes, 12 Allen, 397, it was held that a tender duly made by the mortgagor after maturity, but not followed by a bill to redeem, did not affect the title to a bona fide purchaser claiming title by mesne conveyances from the purchaser at a sale under the power in the mortgage. 

When one sells the property of another by virtue of a power, the law implies an obligation that he will account for and pay over the proceeds of the sale. The mortgagee under a power of sale mortgage obtains his power to sell from the mortgagor; but if before the sale the mortgagor has parted with his title to another person, who at the time of the sale stands in the relation of owner, the law, independently of any contract in the power, makes it the duty of the seller to pay the surplus to such owner. Mattel v. Conant, 156 Mass. 418.

And such grantee or mortgagee may recover such surplus in an action for money had and received. Cook v. Basley, 123 

Mass. 396. But the remedy of an attaching creditor is by bill in equity to reach and apply. Hardy v. Beverly Savings Bank, 175 Mass. 112.

The long form mortgage commonly provided that "out of

the money arising from such sale, the mortgagee shall be entitled to retain all sums then secured by this deed, rendering the surplus, if any, to me or my heirs or assigns." 

No such provision appears in the short form mortgage, but see 

G. L. c. 183, § 27. 

In Knowles v. Sullivan, 182 Mass. 318, the plaintiff, at a sale to satisfy a mechanic's lien, purchased a parcel of land subject to a mortgage. The mortgage was then foreclosed, the proceeds of the sale being more than sufficient to satisfy the mortgage debt. Afterwards the plaintiff received a deed pursuant to the lien sale, and brought action for money had and received against the mortgagee to recover the surplus proceeds of the foreclosure sale. Judgment for plaintiff. The Court said that the difficulties hinted at in Cook v. Basley, supra (see Hardy v. Beverly 

Savings Bank, supra), did not seem to arise in the case at bar.

This was due to the fact that the plaintiff had acquired legal title to the premises.

The validity of a sale is affirmed by bringing an action at law to recover a surplus. See Goldman v. Damon, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 1573, supra.

As a practical matter a mortgagee who has in his hands a surplus after sale should run the record to date in order to determine whether there is any one other than the mortgagor in a position to claim it. Although there is some dispute on this point, the writer is of the opinion that in such a situation the mortgagee is not bound to examine the record in order to protect himself. The claimant, be he grantee, mortgagee or attaching creditor, should bring his claim to the attention of the holder of the surplus. This is settled as to an attaching . creditor. See Hardy v. Bank, supra. The writer is of the opinion that the same rule applies to grantees or mortgagees of the mortgagor. In order to be in a position to recover from the holder of a surplus, they should bring their claims to his attention before he has paid over to the mortgagor. If the holder of the surplus knows that the mortgagor has conveyed or mortgaged his equity, he pays to the mortgagor at his peril. But the record of such a deed or mortgage is not constructive notice to the holder of the surplus. See section 49.

Not infrequently the holder of the mortgage makes the mis-

MORTGAGES. 

217 take of bidding more than the amount to which he is entitled, with the result that he finds himself called upon to account for a surplus. In Antonellis v. Weinstein, 258 Mass. 323, the real owner of a third mortgage on which was due $750 and $36 interest, the mortgage standing of record in the name of a straw man, bid at foreclosure sale $9,050, which he intended to include $8,100 due on a first and a second mortgage. It appearing that the holder of the mortgage had no power to sell except subject to the two prior mortgages, it resulted that he had a substantial surplus which was reached by judgment creditors of the mortgagor. See also Feuer v. Capilowich, 242 Mass. 560.

In Weiner v. Slovin, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 547, the holder of a second mortgage who had foreclosed and purchased at the sale for a sum which included the amount due on the first mortgage, refused to complete the sale by executing and delivering a deed to himself. The holder of a third mortgage brought a bill in equity to compel defendant to complete the sale. Defendant pleaded the Statute of Frauds. The bill was dismissed on the grounds that the failure of the auctioneer to make a memorandum of the purchase and sale as required by the statute rendered the sale a nullity, and that it therefore had no binding force or effect upon any one. See Pilok v. Bednarski, 230 

Mass. 56.

In Crane v. White, 215 Mass. 478, at a foreclosure sale, before the bidding, the auctioneer stated that the property would be sold "subject to interest," and it was held that the holder of the mortgage who purchased must be taken to have bought with an agreement on his part to pay the interest then due on the mortgage, and that in accounting with the mortgagor or his assigns he was not entitled to be credited with the amount due as interest at the time of sale. 

In Dennett v. Perkins, 214 Mass. 449, the holder of a first mortgage made six unsuccessful attempts to sell the property at foreclosure sale. Under those sales he received as deposits and retained sums aggregating $3,100. At the seventh sale the holder of the mortgage purchased the property at a price covering the full amount due him. It was held that the mortgagee held the surplus derived from the forfeitures of deposits at the previous sales for the benefit of the owner of the equity of redemption.

If a foreclosure sale has been fairly conducted, the trust relation of the mortgagee to the mortgagor ceases, and if the mortgagee becomes the owner by purchase at such a sale, he cannot be held to account for a profit made on a later sale by him. 

Steiner v. Schrank, 253 Mass. 551.

In the absence of special agreement the mortgagor and those claiming under him are not entitled to any notice other than the usual published notice. Dyer v. Shurtleff, 112 Mass. 165, 170. The notice published must contain the name of the party who holds the mortgage and who makes the sale. Roche v. Farnsworth, 106 Mass. 509. In that case the Court said: " we think there was a defect in the notice that rendered the sale invalid. It describes the mortgage as being made by S. to F., but does not name the present owners of the equity, who are the plaintiffs, nor the assignee of the mortgage, who is the defendant Farnsworth. It purports to be made by order of the assignee, but does not name him, and is signed by no one. 

These are obscurities that are inconsistent with the degree of clearness that ought to exist in such an advertisement. The mortgagee sells under a power. The form used in the mortgage does not require him to notify the holder of the equity of the intended sale, but it is not an unreasonable strictness to require him to state what property he proposes to sell, and who proposes to make the sale, and who advertises it for sale." 

Where the notice stated that all the parcels described in the mortgage, four in number, would be sold, when in fact one of the parcels had been released from the mortgage, the sale of the other parcels was held invalid. Peoples Savings Bank v. 

Wunderlich, 178 Mass. 453. In Chace v. Morse, 189 Mass. 

559, the Court suggested that the sale in the case above cited was voidable and not void.

The mortgagee may, in the exercise of a reasonable discretion, adjourn the sale from time to time. Dexter v. Shepard, 117 

Mass. 480, 485; Taylor v. Weingartner, 223 Mass. 243. In the latter case it was held that it could not be ruled as matter of law that notice of a two weeks' adjournment should have been published in a newspaper, where ample notice was given of the time originally fixed for the sale, and the adjournment was made by a proclamation of the auctioneer at that time.

MORTGAGES. 

219 Of course, if a sale is held after adjournment, the affidavit of sale should set forth in detail the facts connected therewith, and the manner in which notice thereof was given. 

In Antoine v. Commonwealth Trust Co., Adv. Sh. 1929, page 

425, an action of tort for the alleged wrongful and negligent foreclosure of a chattel mortgage, at the sale, which was held at · eleven o'clock, there were from fifty to seventy-five persons present. The auctioneer offered the property as a whole, but received no bids. He then announced that he would sell the items separately and secured a bid of $125 for one, and $50 for another. There was then an intermission until 12.45. At that time plaintiff's counsel objected to proceeding, on the ground that a number of persons who had been present had left. The auctioneer announced that he proposed to sell all the property for a lump sum, and that he had a bid of $5,250. There being no other bids, the property was knocked down for that sum. 

The Court said: "The temporary postponement of the sale violated no rights of the plaintiff. Rowley v. D'Arcy, 184 Mass. 

550. . . . The refusal of the auctioneer to adjourn the sale after the bid of C ($5,250) had been made was not improper. It was a matter resting in the sound discretion of the auctioneer."

It is customary, where an adjournment is desired, to have a licensed auctioneer make the announcement. No good reason for this theory occurs to the writer. The office of an announcement is to convey information. If the adjournment is stated in a manner which can be generally understood by those present, the identity or official authority of the speaker would seem to be immaterial. 

Test of a Foreclosure by Sale. Unless "yes" may be answered to each of the following nine questions, the foreclosure is defective: 

1. Is the grantor and affiant the same person appearing as mortgagee in the mortgage, or an assignee of said person under assignment duly recorded? 

2. Is the description in the foreclosure deed, and in the notice of sale, the same as in the mortgage less any parcels which have been released from the operation of the mortgage? (Theoretically the notice and deed should describe the premises remaining at the time subject to the mortgage. It seems, how-

ever, to be considered the better practice to describe the whole premises as in the mortgage and then to add, "there is excepted from the above-described premises the following described parcels which have been released from the operation of said mortgage." Note form prescribed in section 439 of chapter 

257 of the Acts of 1918, now G. L. c. 244, § 14.) 

3. Does the affidavit allege a default which, so far as appears of record, may, under the terms of the original mortgage, then exist, and sale at public auction to the highest bidder? 

4. Is the notice printed in a paper published (if there be any) in the town or city where the land lies, and if there be no such paper, in a paper published in the county where the land lies? 

5. Is the notice published in accordance with the requirements of the original mortgage? 

6. Is the notice of sale and the deed subject to the same incumbrances, if any, as stated in the original mortgage? (In answering this question a statement that the sale is to be made subject to any unpaid taxes or other municipal liens, or similar language, may be disregarded.) 

7. Is the place of sale as appearing in the notice authorized by the original mortgage, and does the affidavit show that the sale was made at the time and place advertised at public auction? 

8. Was the date of the first publication twenty-one days before the day of sale, and was the notice published once each week for at least three successive weeks? 

9. Was the affidavit of sale recorded within thirty days from the day of sale?

It was held in Beal v. Attleborough Savings Bank, 248 Mass. 

342, that a foreclosure sale in the usual form is a mere contract for a sale; that the sale is not concluded until the deed has been delivered; and that accordingly, in the absence of special agreement, the mortgagor is entitled to rents accruing between the date of the sale and the day of delivery of the deed. See comment on this case in White v. Macarelli, Adv. Sh. 1929, page 

1379, noted in section 56.

In Parker v. La Plant, 254 Mass. 595, it was held that the statutory form of mortgage, with the provisions of G. L. c. 183,

MORTGAGES. 

221 § 19 relating thereto, precludes the mortgagor, after foreclosure, from assigning to a third person the right to cancel the insurance and to receive return premiums from the insurer. 60.

FORECLOSURE OF MORTGAGE BY ACTION. 

(a) Writ of entry. See G. L. c. 244, §§ 3-8: 

"In Massachusetts and Maine, instead of possession obtained by entry, the mortgagee may recover possession by writ of entry, declaring on his own seisin, stating that it is in mortgage, and if it appears that he is entitled to possession for breach of the condition, the court on motion of either party awards a conditional judgment, if the defendant be the mortgagor or any one claiming under him, that if he within two months after the judgment pays to the plaintiff the sum due on the mortgage with interest and costs the mortgage shall be void; otherwise that the plaintiff shall have his execution for possession. If but part of the mortgage money is due, or the condition of the mortgage be for the doing of any other thing, the terms of the judgment are varied as the case may require. 

The action may be brought by an assignee of the mortgagee, and after his death by his executor or administrator. It may be brought against whoever is tenant of the freehold, and the mortgagor may in all cases be joined as defendant, whether he then has any estate in the premises or not; but he is not liable for costs when he has no estate, and makes no defence to the suit. Possession obtained in this way must be continued for three years to foreclose the right of redemption." -Jones on 

Mortgages, Vol. 2, § 1277. 

"A writ of entry to foreclose a mortgage differs from an ordinary writ of entry, and is in the nature of a bill in equity, and governed by the same rules as to parties and relief." 

Cochran v. Goodell, 131 Mass. 464. "Upon a writ of entry to foreclose a mortgage made to secure the payment of money, the amount due is to be ascertained according to equity and good conscience, and by the same rules as on a bill in chancery to redeem the same mortgage." 

Holbrook v. Bliss, 9 Allen, 69, 76. See also Freeland v. Freeland, 102 Mass. 475.

By Acts of 1904, chapter 448 there was transferred from the Superior Court to the Land Court jurisdiction over "writs of entry, under the provisions of chapter 179 of Revised Laws.” 

This, however, did not transfer to the Land Court jurisdiction over actions for foreclosure of mortgages on a writ of entry. (b) Foreclosure of mortgage by bill in equity: 

In Hallowell v. Ames, 165 Mass. 123, the plaintiff sought on a bill in equity to foreclose a mortgage which did not contain a power of sale. The bill alleged default in payment of the mortgage debt, and prayed that the land conveyed by the mortgage might be sold under the direction of the Court, and the proceeds of the sale applied to payment of the mortgage debt and interest. Defendant demurred to the bill for want of equity. Demurrer sustained and bill dismissed.

The Court said that Pub. Sts. c. 181 (R. L. c. 187, now G. L. c. 244) provided elaborate statutory provisions for the foreclosure and redemption of mortgages, which fix the rights of mortgagors and mortgagees; that this chapter purports to settle the rights of the parties to a mortgage in regard to foreclosure, and it should not be held that by a resort to equity a mortgagee can obtain a foreclosure in a shorter time than the statute prescribes; and that no case in this Commonwealth is known in which a sale has been decreed by a court of equity so as to accomplish a foreclosure in less than three years, in the absence of special provisions in the mortgage authorizing a sale. This plaintiff, said the Court, had a plain, adequate and complete remedy at law.

But the foregoing decision has since been cited for the proposition that the existence of special facts requiring equitable relief may be a sufficient basis for jurisdiction in equity in the foreclosure of mortgages, and facts sufficiently special to that end were found to exist in Old Colony Trust Co. v. Great 

White Spirit Co., 178 Mass. 92, in which it appeared that the mortgage created a trust with elaborate provisions for the protection of the rights of the parties in case of a breach of condition, and the property conveyed included nearly all the property of the mortgagor consisting of real estate in two counties and a variety of articles of personal property on the premises in each of the two counties.

MORTGAGES. 

223 The war measure known as the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act was held to be a special circumstance sufficient to give the equity courts jurisdiction to foreclose power of sale mortgages. Hoffman v. Charlestown Five Cents Savings Bank, 231 Mass. 324; Morse v. Stober, 233 Mass. 223; John Hancock

Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Lester, 234 Mass. 559; Great 

Barrington Savings Bank v. Brown, 239 Mass. 546. See also

DEFINITIONS. 

20 63 An easement is a right of making use of the land of another for a definie purpose, not inconsistent with a general right of property in the owner. gross, 

Easemens may be divided into two classes: (1) easements in were the easement is not connected in any way with any landelonging to the person having the easement; and (2) easements appurtenart, - those connected with land. m If A as wner of a piece of land has a right, appurtenant to his land, t use in some way the land of B, there are two tenements or states, as it is expressed. A's land is called the dominant enement or state, and the land of B, which is subject to the servitude, is called the servient tenement or estate.

The general principle is that a man who owns land subject to an easement has the right to use his land in any way which is not in onsistent with the easement. Atkins v. Boardman, 2 Met. 4; Burnham. Nevins, 144 Mass. 88.

In Atk s v. Boardman, which is a leading case, it was held that a pasageway about five feet in width, running from Washington Seet to rear land owned by the plaintiff, might be

EASEMENTS. 

225 arched over by the owner of the front land. The Court held that, by the true construction of the grant under which the plaintiff claimed, he acquired merely the right of "a suitable and convenient footway to and from his dwelling house, of suitable height and dimensions to carry in and out furniture, provisions and necessaries for family use, and to use for that purpose wheelbarrows, hand sleds and such small vehicles as are commonly used for that purpose in passing to and from the street to a dwelling in the rear, through a foot passage, in a closely built and thickly settled town." It was therefore adjudged that the owner of the fee might build over the way in a manner which did not render it unfit for such purposes. 

See Sargeant v. Traverse Bldg. Trust, 267 Mass. 490.

In the case of Schwoerer v. Boylston Market Association, 99 

Mass. 285, it was clear that the passageway could not be built over, because the grant to the plaintiff expressly provided that it should not be "subject to have any fence or building erected thereon," and because the other parts of the deed and the facts of the case showed that the intention of the parties was that it should be in the nature of an open court or street. 

In Brooks v. Reynolds, 106 Mass. 31, the passageway was expressly declared to be "for light and air;" and it was held that it could not be covered in whole or in part. 

Where, at the time of an express grant by deed of a right to use a certain private way as a common passageway, it appears that the way was laid out by the common grantor for the purpose of selling lots belonging to a certain estate in order to settle the estate, that it was not in a thickly settled district and was used only by persons who collected ashes and garbage, no easement that the way should be kept open to the sky for light, air, prospect or any other purpose of convenience can be added to the grant by implication. Duncan v. Goldthwait, 216 Mass. 402. 

The owner of the fee in one-half of a passageway in the town of Brookline, which provides a convenient means of communication between a much used street and a populous residential district on a hill, and which is subject to an easement of use "for all the purposes for which such passageways now are or at any time hereafter may be commonly used in said town of Brookline," has no right to build a structure over his half of

the way nor to construct buildings which will drop snow, ice or water from their roofs upon it. Newburyport Inst. for Savings v. Brookline, 220 Mass. 300.

The owner of the fee and soil of a passageway may excavate below the surface thereof, if thereby he does not interfere with the rights of passage of the owners of a dominant estate. Siegel v. Starzyx, 238 Mass. 291. 

The owner of a farm, through which the owner of an adjoining farm has a defined right of way, not only may fence the sides of the way, but also may erect and maintain at his own expense such gates or bars across the ends of the way as are reasonably necessary for his own protection in the pasturage of cattle or other proper use and enjoyment of his own property. 

Ball v. Allen, 216 Mass. 469.

In a petition filed in the Land Court under G. L. c. 185 for registration of the petitioner's title to certain land therein described, the petitioner claimed "as appurtenant to" the land the right to use a certain passageway, the entire fee of which was owned by him, "for passageway purposes . . . in common with others entitled thereto, and the right to arch and build over that portion of said passageway the full width of which lies within the lines of the premises herein before described, subject to the use for passageway purposes for a convenient height above the ground in favor of those entitled thereto in common with the petitioner." Held, that the Land Court had jurisdiction not only to register the title, but also to determine the extent of the easement claimed by the petitioner. Levenson v. Ciampa, 251 Mass. 379. . 

62. EASEMENTS IN GRoss. 

Easements are distinguished from profits à prendre by the law writers, and, indeed, the more common definition of an easement has it that it is a right in the land of another "without profit." "A profit à prendre is a right to take some profit from another man's land, illustrations of which are the right to feed cattle on another's land, the profit consisting of the grass which the cattle eat off the land; also such rights as to take gravel or stone or minerals from another's land. Profits

EASEMENTS. 

227 à prendre can always be in gross, and frequently they are appurtenant." Goodwin, page 413. 

"A right to profits à prendre though in gross is assignable. 

It so far partakes of the nature of an estate in the land itself as to be treated as an inheritable and assignable interest." 

Jones on Easements, section 39. “A man may have a way in gross over another's land, but it must, from its nature, be a personal right, not assignable nor inheritable; nor can it be made so by any terms of the grant any more than a collateral and independent covenant can be made to run with land." Washburn on Easements, page 11. 

The Massachusetts rule appears opposed to the preceding statement. "In Massachusetts an inheritable and assignable incorporeal right, such as a right of way or to draw water, may be granted in gross; that is, so as to exist independently of any particular parcel of land owned by the grantee, and so as to be enjoyed by the grantee and his heirs in connection with any estate which he or they may own or acquire, and to be capable of assignment or conveyance by him or them in gross. Goodrich v. Burbank, 12 Allen, 459, 461; Owen v. Field, 102 Mass. 

90, 108; Bowen v. Conner, 6 Cush. 132, 137." - Crocker, page 32.

As to a right to take water, the law seems certain. See 

Goodrich v. Burbank and Owen v. Field, supra. But the rule of law that a way in gross may be granted or reserved in Massachusetts appears to lie in dictum rather than in decision. 

Such rule has been so often stated that it doubtless would be followed. See Bowen v. Conner and Goodrich v. Burbank, supra; also White v. Crawford, 10 Mass. 183, 187, and American Tel. & Tel. Co. v. McDonald, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 2203.

Professor Washburn, as appears above, disagreed with the Massachusetts view. His idea was that a right in gross to take water might be supported on the theory that such right is a species of "profit à prendre," although in Goodrich v. Burbank the Court said that this was not so, but he considered the rule unsupportable so far as ways were concerned.

An easement created by a deed may be found to be appurtenant to land also conveyed by the deed, although it is not

declared in express words to be so appurtenant. Brooks v. 

West Boston Gas Co., 260 Mass. 407.

An easement is not presumed to be personal unless it cannot be construed fairly as appurtenant to some estate. Dennis v. 

Wilson, 107 Mass. 591. 63.

EASEMENTS DISTINGUISHED FROM LICENSE. 

“An easement always implies an interest in the land in or over which it is enjoyed. A license carries no such interest.' - Washburn on Easements, page 6. "A license is technically an authority given to do some act, or a series of acts, on the land of another, without passing any estate in the land, such as a license to hunt in another's land, or to cut down a certain number of trees." Cook v. Stearns, 11 Mass. 533, 537.

A license to enter upon land, and remove trees therefrom, passes no interest in the land, and though not in writing, is valid, notwithstanding the statute of frauds. Whitmarsh v. 

Walker, 1 Met. 313.

The owner of a building, in which there was a hall, agreed orally with A to permit him to use the hall for dancing parties on the afternoon of four holidays named, at a stipulated price for each afternoon. Held, that the contract was for a license, and not for the sale of an interest in land, within the statute of frauds; and that A could maintain an action against the owner of the building for breach of contract. Johnson v. Wilkinson, 139 Mass. 3. "A license is a permission or authority to enter the land and do certain acts, the parties not intending to convey any interest in the land, and it is well settled that such a license need not be in writing, under the statute of frauds. Thus a license to enter land and to cut timber, or to gather the growing crops, is valid, though not in writing. Whitmarsh v. Walker, 1 Met. 

313. So an agreement for a seat in a theatre is a license merely. McCrea v. Marsh, 12 Gray, 211; Burton v. Scherpf, 1 Allen, 133." 

In Morse v. Copeland, 2 Gray, 302, the owner of a mill privilege gave the owner of lands flowed thereby an oral license to

EASEMENTS. 

229 erect a dam on the land of the licensee so as to exclude the water from a portion of the servient estate, and also to dig a ditch across the land of the licensor to drain the water from part of the licensee's land; and under this license the dam was erected and the ditch dug. Held, that the licensor could revoke the license to dig the ditch, even after the expiration of twenty years, but could not revoke the license to build the dam. "The authorities show that the rule sometimes laid down in the books, that a license executed cannot be countermanded, is not applicable to licenses, which if given by deed, would create an easement, but to licenses which, if given by deed, would extinguish or modify an easement.” (nat revocable) 

A woman who owned certain real estate agreed orally to sell and convey it, free from all incumbrances except certain taxes, to one who agreed to permit to her the use of a certain way over the land sold to a garden on other land of hers whenever she requested it. As performance of her oral agreement, the owner tendered a deed containing an express reservation of the desired way to herself, "her heirs and assigns, forever." It was held that the permission to be given in accordance with the oral agreement would have created at most a revocable license; that the words of the deed created an easement; and that the deed offered was not in accordance with the agreement. Mason v. Albert, 243 Mass. 433. 64. 

EASEMENTS HOW ACQUIRED. 

An easement being an interest in land can be acquired only by grant, express or implied.

A deed giving a right of way over land of the grantor adjacent to the parcel conveyed must be so construed in determining the use to which the way is to be put as to carry out the intent of the parties to it as manifested by it in the light of the circumstances. Briggs v. Connors, 243 Mass. 1.

Where land is owned by two or more tenants in common an easement cannot be imposed on the land by a deed of one of them. Silverman v. Betti, 222 Mass. 142.

In order that an easement not expressly granted may be created by a deed by implication, it is commonly necessary to

show that a measure of necessity, which goes beyond mere convenience, attaches to its existence. See sections 71 and 72. See also Apsey v. Nash, 229 Mass. 77, and Union National Bank of Lowell v. Nesmith, 238 Mass. 247.

One apparent exception to this rule is expressed in Tobey v. 

Taunton, 119 Mass. 404, where the Court said: "When a grantor conveys land bounding on a street or way, he and his heirs are estopped to deny the existence of such street or way, and the right thus acquired is not only coextensive with the land conveyed, but embraces the entire length of the way as it is then actually laid out or clearly indicated and prescribed." 

In Ralph v. Clifford, 224 Mass. 58, the Court said: "When a grantor conveys land bounded on a street or way he and those claiming under him are estopped to deny the existence of such street or way, and the right of way of the grantee and his successors in title therein includes the entire length of the way as then actually laid out or clearly defined. This is the contention of the plaintiffs and it is fully sustained by the authorities, and applies as well to a contemplated way if clearly indicated as to an existing street."

But as the only reference in the deed to an alleged private street was that a boundary of the lot was described as beginning on an avenue "at the corner of a street," when in fact there was no street and no indication of a street, and the only indication that a street ever was contemplated was the fact that two posts were placed by the grantor on the line of the avenue about forty feet apart, which posts remained there only a short time, it was held that there was nothing to estop one, who owned the adjoining land under a subsequent deed from the same grantor, from denying the existence of a way over his land. 

It is believed that the statement from Ralph v. Clifford quoted above has been generally accepted as controlling whether the granted premises are described as bounded "on" or "on the line of" a way. See Driscoll v. Smith, 184 Mass. 221. This was a bill in equity to restrain defendant from obstructing a private way leading from plaintiff's land, conveyed to him by defendant, to the highway. It appears that the deed bounded the granted premises in part "on the northerly line of Smith Street," and the 

Court said: "The defendant does not contend and could not

EASEMENTS. 

231 successfully contend that he is not estopped by the boundary in the deed to deny that there was a street called Smith Street over which the plaintiff had a right of passage. The only question would seem to be, therefore, how far Smith Street extended at the date of the plaintiff's deed." It does not appear whether the plaintiff had any other means of access to the highway and the question of necessity is not discussed. But in the same volume, 184 Mass., at page 452, there is reported the case of McKenzie v. Gleason. This was an action of tort for the obstruction of a way. Plaintiff was owner of two parcels, acquired at different times, one being situated on the southerly side of a private way and the other on the northerly side. The larger lot, located on the southerly side of the way, was first conveyed to plaintiff by deed in which the northerly bound was given as running “by said road." At the date of this deed, the grantor owned the fee and soil of the road as well as the land of defendant and the land on the northerly side of the road later acquired by plaintiff. 

The parcel on the northerly side of the road was conveyed to plaintiff by deed in which the southerly bound was given as running "by the northerly side of said private road." Judgment for plaintiff. There can be no question as to the soundness of the decision so far as the plaintiff's lot on the southerly side of the road is concerned, and this, it may be argued, was all that was necessary to the decision. However that may be, the Court referred to the description of the northerly lot, said that it gave the plaintiff no title to any part of the way, and said further: "Plaintiff . . . claims that by the description in each deed, whether bounded by the side of the road or by the way itself, he gained an easement in the road as it then was for the purpose of travel. . . . The instructions of the presiding judge at the trial adopted this view. By the description of the second lot the road was excluded, and the defendant was not estopped to deny its existence." Driscoll v. Smith, supra, was not cited except by the reporter in a footnote. 

In Wood v. Culhane, 265 Mass. 555, the description in a deed began at a point in a public way "where it meets the southerly line of a 40-foot way." Thence the description ran on the public street, thence around the lot "to the southerly line of the 40foot way before mentioned; thence turning and running south-

easterly on the southerly line of said 40-foot way 209.74 feet to the point of beginning." The trial court found that none of the deeds in Mrs. Culhane's chain of title contained any mention of or reference to any rights in the forty-foot way; that there was no intention at the time of the conveyance to Mrs. Sullivan (predecessor in title to defendant) to create any rights in the forty-foot way for the benefit of the Culhane lot; that no rights in the way had been acquired by user or otherwise; and that the deeds of this lot conveyed no rights in the way. The Supreme 

Court said: "It is manifest that this particular description, which begins at the southerly line of the way, runs around the lot to the southerly line again, and then turns and runs on the southerly line to the place of beginning, excluded the way, and that the grantor retained the fee in it." There can be no doubt as to the soundness of the latter statement.

The Court said also: "In view of the finding that 'No rights in the 40-foot way, so far as the Culhane lot is concerned, have been acquired by user or otherwise and the deeds of this lot convey no rights in said way,' it is plain that the defendants derive no title in the way by express grant, nor can any easement over it be held to exist by implication.” 

The basis for the finding that "there was no intention to create rights in the way" is not clear. If this "finding” be taken as a ruling of law as to the proper construction of the deed, it perhaps might be supported by the decision in McKenzie v. 

Gleason, supra, but the latter case was not cited in the opinion. 

This decision seems to run contra to Driscoll v. Smith and to what is believed to be the popular understanding of the statement above quoted from Ralph v. Clifford. It may well be that the decision in Wood v. Culhane was influenced by the fact that for thirty years there had been some sort of a fence between defendant's land and the way.

If land was described in a deed as bounded on two private ways, and rights in one way were expressly granted, there is some authority for the view that the deed should be construed as expressing the intention of the grantor that no rights in the other way should attach to the premises. See Prentiss v. Gloucester, 236 Mass. 36, at 54.

This rule as to ways by implied grant or, if you prefer, by

EASEMENTS. 

233 estoppel, seems to be distinct from the theory of easements created by reference to a plan (see section 34). The rule in the latter case being that a plan referred to for description is thereby incorporated into the description employed, it is not strictly necessary to use the expression "implied grant" in such cases, but it is commonly so used as a convenient expression. 65. 

ACQUISITION OF EASEMENTS BY PRESCRIPTION. 

In section 64 it was stated that an easement can be acquired only by grant, express or implied. It is true that an easement may be gained by prescription, that is to say, by use, but prescription is based upon the presumption of a lost grant. 

For a learned discussion of the history of the law regulating the acquisition of title to land, and of easements in or over land, by adverse user, see Edson v. Munsell, 10 Allen, 557. "It is universally conceded that the prescriptive period is measured by the period fixed by the statute of limitations, which in most of the states of this country and in England is twenty years.' Goodwin, page 418. دو 

G. L. c. 187, § 2: No person shall acquire by adverse use or enjoyment a right or privilege of way or other easement from, in, upon or over the land of another, unless such use or enjoyment is continued uninterruptedly for twenty years. 

As to acquisition of easements over registered land, see G. L. c. 185, § 53, as amended by chapter 117 of the Acts of 1921, quoted in section 11. See also Dubinsky v. Cama, 261 Mass. 47. "The use and enjoyment of what is claimed (by prescription) must have been adverse, under a claim of right, exclusive, continuous, uninterrupted, and with the knowledge and acquiescence of the owner of the estate in, over, or out of which the easement prescribed for is claimed, and while such owner was able, in law, to assert and enforce his rights, and to resist such adverse claim, if not well founded." Washburn on Easements, 4th Ed., page 

150. See Powell v. Bragg, 8 Gray, 441.

In Dana v. Valentine, 5 Met. 8, where one objection to defendants' title by prescription to maintain a nuisance was that until lately the plaintiffs had suffered no damages, and therefore could not interpose to prevent its continuance, it was said

that it was clear that where a party's right of property is invaded, he may maintain an action for the invasion of his right, without proof of actual damage. 

"The doctrine that a lost grant is the basis of prescription has been severely condemned by various writers. But as we have seen, it still continues to be the law in England, and it is the prevailing doctrine in the United States, including Massachusetts. But when it cannot be applied, prescription may be based upon analogy to the statute of limitations. See Attorney- 

Gen. v. Revere Company, 152 Mass. 444." - Goodwin, page 419.

In the case last mentioned, an action was brought in equity to restrain the defendant from lowering the waters of a great pond. Defendant had used the waters of the pond for more than twenty years, but a lost grant could not be presumed because Nullum tempus occurrit regi, — No length of time can bar the king. But there was a twenty-year statute of limitations binding on the Commonwealth as well as individuals, and it was held that by analogy to that statute a prescriptive right had grown up in the defendant which was good against the 

Commonwealth. In Attorney General v. Boston and Albany 

Railroad, 246 Mass. 292, it was held that the right of the public to such relief against a railroad so constructed as to obstruct a public road is not barred by mere lapse of time, and it was said: "The decision in Attorney General v. Revere Copper Co., 152 Mass. 444, is confined to the gaining of prescriptive rights with respect to property owned by the public under a statute of limitations which puts the property rights of the public on the same basis as those of individuals.” 

In Bucella v. Agrippino, 257 Mass. 483, it was held that the mere fact that one of several successive owners of property, who used a passageway over adjoining property under a claim of right, purported to convey that right of way with his land at a date before there had been twenty years' adverse use under a claim of right, did not prevent the acquisition of title by adverse use by any one who thereafter used the passageway under a claim of right for more than twenty years. The Court said: "The theory of a lost grant is a legal fiction intended to meet the difficulty of contemplating the creation of a valid title in land by something other than a deed. . . . The language from

EASEMENTS. 

235 

Atkins v. Bordman, 2 Met. 457, 465, 476, . . . is not authority for the proposition that, where a deed is put in evidence and it appears that the grantor was without power to convey the easement purported to be granted, a prescriptive right to the easement cannot be made out from other evidence and other facts." 

In Truc v. Field, Adv. Sh. 1930, page 81, plaintiff's premises had been supplied for over sixty years with water from a spring on defendant's land, and the plaintiff claimed the right to have the flow continued. No deed in the chain of title to either estate contained any reference to the right to take water. There was evidence that for many years defendant and her predecessors had claimed the right to give or withhold the water; that payments had been made from time to time for the use of the water; that the water was cut off for a time; that in 1887 the then owner of plaintiff's premises acknowledged in writing that his right was dependent on the permission of the owner of the spring farm; and that in 1908 there was a written agreement permitting the taking of water for five years. Plaintiff contended that a grant of the water right to him or his predecessors must be presumed. The Court said: "It is conceivable that before 1860 the use had been so long enjoyed, . . . that a title to an easement by prescription had been acquired; but we do not think that we are bound to presume conclusively that a grant of the easement has ever existed, or to hold that evidence which seriously weakens belief in the existence of grant and right cannot be considered because of such presumption.

A legitimate inference with regard to the beginning of a use may be drawn from events which happen after even long after the actual circumstances attending that beginning have been forgotten or lost through lapse of time.” 

• • • In White v. Chapin, 12 Allen, 516, the Court quoted from 

Smith v. Miller, 11 Gray, 145, as follows: "A right to an easement of that kind (drainage) in the land of another may undoubtedly be acquired by the actual enjoyment of it, provided that the enjoyment is adverse, uninterrupted and of sufficient continuance and duration," and then said: "In the present instance, we suppose there is no question as to the existence of all these qualities except that of adverse use. . . . Wherever

1 there has been the use of an easement for twenty years unexplained, it will be presumed to be under a claim of right and adverse, and be sufficient to establish a title by prescription and to authorize the presumption of a grant, unless controlled or explained; and it is incumbent upon the owner of the land to prove that the use of the easement was under some license, indulgence or special contract inconsistent with a claim of right by the other party."

The writer believes that the foregoing statement is unsound as a general proposition of law, and that the presumption of a grant does not arise until the claimant has offered evidence which goes to establish the necessary elements. The concluding paragraph of the opinion in Barnes v. Haynes, 13 Gray, 188, recognizes the necessity of the proof of the required elements in order to create the presumption of a grant. In Stearns v. Janes, 12 Allen, 582, the Court said: "The actual use of an easement, as of right, for twenty years without interruption creates the presumption of a grant." In Bullukian v. Franklin, 248 Mass. 151, at 155, it was said: "The mere fact of user by the public for the period required to establish a public way raises no presumption that such use is adverse." "The burden is upon the plaintiff (who claimed a right of way by prescription) to prove all facts essential to the establishment of a right of way by prescription, one of which is, that the use must have been under a claim of right." Bartlett v. The Roosevelt, Inc., 258 Mass. 494.

Where evidence is produced which raises the presumption of a grant, if the other party relies upon the fact that the acts done were permissive, the burden is on him to rebut such presumption. Barnes v. Haynes, supra; White-v. Chapin, 12 Allen, 516; Daw v. Lally, 213 Mass. 578.

In order to prove "claim of right" it is not necessary to show an express claim by the person using the easement, nor an express admission of the right by the owner of the land. Blake v. 

Everett, 1 Allen, 248. It may be inferred, provided the circumstances are sufficient to warrant such an inference. McCreary v. Boston and Maine Railroad, 153 Mass. 300. See Truc v. 

Field, supra.

Because the occupation was continuously open and adverse,

EASEMENTS. 

237 the defendant's prescriptive title is not disturbed by the fact that during the period there were different owners of both the dominant and servient estates. Matthys v. First Swedish Baptist Church, 223 Mass. 544.

In Massachusetts it is impossible to acquire an easement of light and air by user. See G. L. c. 187, § 1; Tidd v. Fifty Associates, 238 Mass. 421; Hampe v. Elia, 251 Mass. 465. For discussion of a grant of such an easement, see Kesseler v. Bowditch, 223 Mass. 265.

An easement, acquired by prescription, to the use of the entrance, landing places and stairways in a building, which exists as distinct from any right of way, granted or acquired without reference to the building, over the land upon which the building is erected, is lost when the building is destroyed by the intentional act of the owner of the servient estate. tional Bank of Lowell v. Nesmith, 238 Mass. 247. 

66. ELEMENTS OF PRESCRIPTION. 

Union Na- See the statement by Professor Washburn quoted in section 65, and compare with comment on the elements of "adverse possession," section 6. (a) “Adverse, under a Claim of Right.” 66 Adverse use means a use under a claim of right, as distinguished from a use which was permitted." Sprow v. Boston and Albany Railroad, 163 Mass. 330, 339.

If the user was begun by permission, the legal presumption is that the use continued to be permissive. Hall v. Stevens, 9 Met. 418. This was a case involving title by adverse possession, but it is believed that the same rule is applicable to cases of prescription. See also Daw v. Lally, 213 Mass. 578. Of course, a use commenced by permission may later be exercised adversely and under a claim of right. If this change occurs, the period of user necessary to establish the right begins to run from the time when the character of the user was altered. 

If the use of a way is under parol consent given by the owner of the servient tenement to use it as if it were legally conveyed, it is a use as of right. Ashley v. Ashley, 4 Gray, 197. A parol

gift of the right to use another's land is a good foundation for a user which in twenty years will ripen into a prescriptive right. 

Sumner v. Stevens, 6 Met. 337. (b) "Exclusive." "In Maine and Maryland the word 'exclusive' above, has been taken literally, so that if any other person than the owner of the estate claimed to be dominant exercises a user, the user of the owner of the estate claimed to be dominant is not exclusive, and he cannot acquire a prescriptive right. Washburn on 

Easements, 97. But the better view is, and it is so held in 

Massachusetts, that the word 'exclusive' means that the claimant must rely exclusively upon his own user or that of those under whom he claims, and not upon the user of third parties, in order to create, as appurtenant to his land, an easement by prescription." Goodwin, page 422.

In Kilburn v. Adams, 7 Met. 33, it was said that where a tract of land attached to a public building is designedly left open for convenience and ornament, the passage of persons over it, in common with those for whose use it is appropriated, is in general to be regarded as permissive, and not adverse, and that although an adjacent proprietor may make such use of the open land more frequently than another, yet the same rule will apply, unless there be some decisive act indicating a separate and exclusive use, under a claim of right. The Court said also: "A regularly formed and wrought way across the ground, paved, macadamized, or gravelled, and fitted for use as a way from his own estate to the highway, indicating a use distinct from any use to be made of it by the proprietors, would, in our opinion, be evidence of such exclusive use and claim of right. So would any plain, unequivocal act, indicating a peculiar and exclusive claim, open and ostensible, and distinguishable from that of others." 

See also Curtis v. Angier, 4 Gray, 547. (c) "Continuous, Uninterrupted." 

See G. L. c. 187, § 2, quoted in section 65. "Whatever breaks the continuity of the possession and enjoyment of an easement, whether by a cessation to enjoy it, or by any act of the owner of the servient tenement, destroys alto-

EASEMENTS. 

239 gether the effect of the previous user; and this is an interruption within the meaning of the Rev. Sts. c. 60, § 27 (now G. L. c. 187, § 2)." Pollard v. Barnes, 2 Cush. 191. This case involved a claim of right to pile boards upon lands of another. It had been enjoyed from 1822 to 1846, except from 1829 to 1834, during which period no such use was made of the land, and this omission was held to be a voluntary interruption of the user which prevented the acquisition of the easement claimed. 

Dana v. Valentine, 5 Met. 8.

An easement may be gained by prescription although long intervals occur between the dates of user, provided the use is continuous as needed. For illustration, the use of a way for the purpose of cutting and hauling hay, which naturally would be used only during the haying season, if continued annually for twenty years, would doubtless ripen into an easement. 

In Powell v. Bagg, 8 Gray, 441, it was held that if the owner of land, while on the land, forbids the owner of adjoining land to enter thereon, and orders him off while there for the purpose of repairing an acqueduct under claim of an easement by user, such verbal orders, though unaccompanied by further acts, are admissible as evidence of an interruption of the easement. The 

Court said: "This is not a case where title to land is claimed by adverse possession, and where the true owner is disseised. If such disseisin continues for the requisite period, the presumption of a grant will arise, and a mere verbal prohibition to occupy the premises may not be sufficient without an entry to rebut that legal presumption. The owner, in that case, would still be disseised. But the title to an easement by adverse user stands on different ground. The owner remains in possession of the premises; there is no disseisin; the title rests chiefly on his acquiescence in the adverse use, and evidence which disproves such acquiescence rebuts the title to the easement." But see Bucella v. Agrippino, 257 Mass. 483, 487.

If an obstruction to an unauthorized way is put up before the time of prescription has run, the running of the time will be interrupted, although the obstruction is torn down very soon, and the use of the way resumed. Brayden v. N. Y., N. H., and 

H. R.R. Co., 172 Mass. 225. The Court said: "A landowner in order to prevent that result (acquisition of a right of way) is

not required to battle successfully for his rights; it is enough if he asserts them to the other party by an overt act, which, if the easement existed, would be a cause of action." 

In Dorntee v. Lyons, 224 Mass. 256, it was held that the use of a passageway was not interrupted by a temporary obstruction by means of barrels and planks placed there by a stranger without authority from or ratification by the owner of the land. 

G. L. c. 187, § 3 provides a method for preventing the acquisition of easements by user. This is accomplished by posting a notice of the owner's intention to prevent the acquisition of easements on the premises for six successive days, or, as against a particular person or persons, by personal service, and by recording in the registry of deeds a certificate by an officer qualified to serve civil process, that such notice has been posted or served by him. (d) "Knowledge and Acquiescence of the Owner.” "The foundation of the establishment of a right by prescription is the acquiescence on the part of the owner of the servient tenement in the acts which are relied upon to establish the easement by prescription. This makes it necessary that he should know of those acts or be charged with knowledge of them, if he did not in fact know of them. The point has been settled in cases where a party has undertaken to establish a prescriptive right to maintain drains or other appliances under the ground.” 

Gray v. Cambridge, 189 Mass. 405, 418. "To acquire a right of way over the land of another by prescription, the user must be adverse, that is, under a claim of right, and it must be acquiesced in by the owner of the land. 

It is not necessary that the claim of right be expressly made, or that the acquiescence be declared. . . . If the user is open, continuous, and of such a character that it may reasonably be inferred to be adverse, it has been held that the knowledge of the owner of the land may be inferred from these circumstances, and we think that if the use is adverse, and is so open and notorious that the owner of the land ought to have known of it, his acquiescence may be presumed, even if it is not found that he actually knew of it." Deerfield v. Connecticut River Railroad,

EASEMENTS. 

241 

144 Mass. 325 at 338. See also Bigelow Carpet Co. v. Wiggin, 209 Mass. 542; Matthys v. First Swedish Baptist Church, 223 

Mass. 544. "Acquiescence" must not be confused with "permission."

The former term is descriptive of the failure of the owner of land to take any steps to interrupt a user begun under claim of right. To say that a user is enjoyed by "permission" of the owner negatives completely the idea of "claim of right." 

67. DISABILITIES. 

"It is an essential element of the use necessary to give a title by prescription, . . . that it should be with the acquiescence of the true owner having the power to grant or interrupt the easement. . . . The ground upon which prescription has generally been put in this Commonwealth has been the presumption of a previous grant or agreement which has been lost by lapse of time. . . . But a grant cannot be presumed against a person legally incapable of making it. . . . An insane person cannot make a binding grant of his real estate." Edson v. Munsell, 10 Allen, 557. “If the owner of the land by reason of insanity or other disability, is incapable of acquiescing, . . . a prescriptive right is not acquired." Deerfield v. Connecticut River Railroad, 144 

Mass. 325, 338.

In Allis v. Moore, 2 Allen, 306, it was held that if an owner of land has been disseised, his subsequent insanity does not prevent the disseisor's title from maturing by twenty years' adverse possession.

In Currier v. Gale, 3 Allen, 328, the Court cited Allis v. Moore with approval and said: "The same rule must for the same reason prevail in relation to easements or other rights acquired by prescription, or to titles established and confirmed by open, adverse possession." 

In Ballard v. Demmon, 156 Mass. 449, it was said: "Since the decision of Allis v. Moore, 2 Allen, 306, and of Currier v. Gale, 3 Allen, 328, and the opinion in Edson v. Munsell, 10 Allen, 557, we think it must be considered that the acquisition of an easement by adverse use follows the analogy of the acquisition of

title by adverse possession; and that a disability arising after the adverse use has commenced, and has become known to the owner of the servient estate, does not suspend the acquisition of the right, or extend the time necessary to acquire it." Apparently the disability here referred to was the fact that the land came into the possession of lessees of the owner. The decision seems to have been influenced by the statute enabling the owner, although not in possession, to interrupt the user by notice (now G. L. c. 187, § 3). 

But in Melvin v. Whiting, 13 Pick. 184, it was held that an easement may be acquired by user, where the land has descended to an infant heir, if the time of the user during the life of the ancestor, added to that after the heir came of age, amounts to twenty years. In other words, the period of disability was deducted in computing the period of adverse user. 

Melvin v. Whiting does not appear to have been expressly overruled. On the question whether the language above quoted from Ballard v. Demmon was intended to have such an effect, it should be noted that in Ballard v. Demmon and Currier v. 

Gale the disabilities, tenancies in the one case, and coverture in the other, were voluntary; that in Edson v. Munsell the disability existed when the use began; and that in Allis v. Moore the person setting up disability was himself in possession when the adverse use began, and had been in a position to bring action during a period of nine years before the disability occurred.

A husband cannot acquire by prescription a right of way over land of his wife who is living with him on adjoining land of his own. Graves v. Broughton, 185 Mass. 174. "The owner of the servient tenement cannot avoid the effect of twenty years' adverse user by showing that he was out of the country and so did not in fact know of the acts. A servient owner in that condition comes within the second branch of the rule that he is chargeable with knowledge." Gray v. Cambridge, 189 Mass. 405, 418. Compare with the statutory provision as to real actions, G. L. c. 260, § 25 (section 10).

EASEMENTS. 

243 68. 

DEDICATION. 

To the statement that an easement can be acquired only by grant, two exceptions should be noted. Such an interest in land may be created without a grant by (a) dedication and (b) substitution. 

Dedication is defined to be an act by which the owner of the fee gives to the public, for some proper object, an easement in his lands. Boone on Real Property, section 139. "A dedication is properly only to the public use; there can be no dedication, properly speaking, to private uses.' Washburn on Easements, page 203, 4th edition. See Prentiss v. 

Gloucester, 236 Mass. 36, 54. "To authorize a dedication does not require the existence of a corporation to whom it is made, or in whom the title should vest. It may be valid without any specific grantee in esse at the time, to whom the fee could be granted. And in this respect it forms an exception to the general rule of transferring or creating an interest in lands, as it may be done without a deed, and without any person competent to accept the grant as grantee. The public is an ever-existing grantee, capable of taking a dedication for public uses." - Washburn on Easements, page 202. 66 'Ways by prescription and ways by dedication rest upon entirely different principles. The first is established upon evidence of user by the public, adverse and continuous for a period of twenty years or more, from which use arises a presumption of a reservation or grant and the acceptance thereof, or that it has been laid out by the proper authorities, of which no record exists. The second is created by the permission of gift of the owner, and upon the acceptance of such gift by the public authorities it becomes a way, and the owner cannot withdraw his dedication." Commonwealth v. Coupe, 128 Mass. 63. "There is not, however, any particular formality required to constitute the dedication of a way. Any act is sufficient which clearly indicates an intention to dedicate it. It may be done by writing, by parol, or by acts in pais, and even acquiescence in the use of such way by the public may be sufficient. Nor

can any conveyance though with warranty, by the owner of the soil, affect a dedication once made." - Washburn, page 205. "But, if ample evidence of acceptance appears, the inquiry remains whether the association had dedicated the use of the lands to the public. No formalities are necessary. It is settled at common law that the dedication need not be in writing. 

Its validity depends upon the intent and consent of the owner who, of course, retains the fee. It may spring from oral declarations or statements by the dedicator, or by those authorized to act in his behalf, made to persons with whom he deals and who rely upon them; or it may consist of declarations addressed directly to the public. It may also be manifested by the owner's acts from which such intention can be inferred.” 

Attorney-Gen. v. Onset Bay Grove Asscn., 221 Mass. 342. "To constitute a dedication of land to a public use there must first be an intention to do it on the part of the owner. 

And this must be unequivocally and satisfactorily proved. But it may be manifested by writing, by declaration or by acts. 

Dedications have been established in every conceivable way by which the intention of the party could be manifested. Without that, no dedication can take place, and if, for instance, in opening a passageway of a character which might otherwise be deemed a public way, the owner of the land should place a gate at its entrance, by which such passageway may be closed, it would be regarded as evidence negativing the intention to make it a public way. Nor would it become so by the gate being suffered to go to decay, or ceasing to be used. It was accordingly held in Commonwealth v. Newbury, 2 Pick. 51, that there must be a manifest intention to accommodate the public through a man's land, before he shall be held, by implication, to have given it, so that even when, at the first opening of such way, a post only had been put up, which soon after was knocked down, and remained down for twelve years, and the passage had been uninterrupted all that time, it was determined that the owner might maintain trespass against those who used the way." - Washburn, page 209. "The question of dedication is always one of mixed law and fact." Washburn, page 212. 2

EASEMENTS. 

245 "So there may be dedication of a spring of water to public use, or land for a public square in a city, or for a street or public highway, or for a public landing place upon the bank of a river, or for public commons, or for sites for court-houses or other public buildings; and it would seem that 'all sorts of easements and rights to enjoyment of land, whether for use or for pleasure, which may be acquired by an individual by grant or prescription, may also be acquired by the public by actual dedication."" - Washburn, page 215.

In Attorney General v. Abbott, 154 Mass. 323, which involved the dedication of parks, the Court said: "The acceptance of such a dedication at common law need not appear of record, and need not be by the town. The acceptance is by the public at large and the principal thing to show it is use by the public. 

There is no need of a formal grantee. The fee remains in the original owner. No assent of the town is necessary, because no burden is put upon the town,,as in the case of a way. The improvements upon a park thus dedicated are left to be made by those who are interested. The town may take it up, or it may be left to individuals. . . . Whether the easement of a public park could be accepted merely by enjoying an unobstructed view over it of the ocean, need not be considered. Various other acts of use of all the parks are shown sufficiently to show an acceptance of them by the public. Such acceptance need not be very specific." See also Attorney General v. Onset Bay

Grove Association, supra; Mighill v. Inhabitants of Rowley, 224 Mass. 586. "If, in this connection, it is asked what length of time is necessary in order to have a dedication become effectual, it is believed there is no period or term of enjoyment necessary, as in the case of prescription. Length of time may be regarded, when the evidence of a dedication having been made depends upon a user by the public of the thing dedicated. But as all that is requisite to a good dedication is that there should be an intention and an act of dedication on the part of the owner, and an acceptance on the part of the public, as soon as these concur, the dedication is complete. Washburn, page 219. 

"The necessity of an acceptance by the public of a dedicated

way before it can become a public way seems to be admitted as almost an elementary principle. The difficulty lies in what shall be such an acceptance." - Washburn, page 222. 

G. L. c. 84, § 23: Ways opened and dedicated to the public use, which have not become public ways, shall not, except as provided in the following section, be chargeable upon a city or town as a highway or town way unless laid out and established in manner prescribed by statute. "It has been held frequently that cities and towns are not liable for injuries occasioned to people using public parks. 

The ground of these decisions of necessity is that the maintenance of public parks is the function of an agency of government, and not resting in any degree upon the property rights of the municipality." Higginson v. Boston, 212 Mass. 583, 588. "But the limitation of the power of dedicating lands to public uses in Massachusetts under the statute, as well as in other states, seems to be confined to ways, and is adopted for the purpose of avoiding the liability to which towns might otherwise be subjected in case of a want of repair of such ways. 

But the law remains, it would seem, as at common law, in respect to public squares and other subjects of dedication." 

Washburn, page 235. "The provisions of St. 1846, c. 203, re-enacted in Gen. Sts. c. 43, § 82 (now G. L. c. 84, § 23), concerning the dedication of ways, do not apply to ways established by prescription." Commonwealth v. Coupe, 128 Mass. 63, 69; see also Jennings v. Tisbury, 5 Gray, 73. Even before the statute of 1846 mere user by the public was not sufficient acceptance. It was competent evidence, however, as bearing both on the question of dedication and of acceptance. Hayden v. Stone, 112 Mass. 346. See also 

Brown v. Peabody, 228 Mass. 52; Bullukian v. Franklin, 248 

Mass. 151; Scott v. Worcester, 257 Mass. 520. "In this Commonwealth there can be no public way by dedication without an acceptance of it by the public authorities. 

Before the enactment of St. 1846, c. 203, such an acceptance could be shown either by a vote to accept, or by circumstances giving rise to a strong implication, such as frequent and longcontinued use by the public, and repairing, lighting or other significant acts of persons authorized to represent the city or

EASEMENTS. 

247 town in that behalf. Hemphill v. Boston, 8 Cush. 195. While public use may be important as evidence, it is not of itself sufficient to show such acceptance. Since the enactment of the statute above referred to there can be no effectual acceptance without a laying out of a way in the ordinary mode prescribed by the statute." Moffatt v. Kenney, 174 Mass. 311. Prentiss v. Gloucester, 236 Mass. 36, 54.

Even though, in the records of town meetings in 1852, recording the reference to the selectmen of a petition for the laying out of a town way, a report of the selectmen on the subject at a subsequent town meeting and a vote "to accept" the report, there is no record that the laying out with boundaries and measurements had been filed with the town clerk seven days before the meeting as required by Rev. Sts. c. 24, § 69, it may be presumed or inferred after sixty years, especially where it appears that the only owner of land through which the way was to pass was a petitioner for its laying out, that the statutory requirement was complied with. Reed v. Mayo, 220 Mass. 565.

Where a public highway once has been established it remains a public highway until it is discontinued as such by a duly authorized public board or officer, or until the place of its location is given over lawfully to some other purpose. Eklon v. 

Chelsea, 223 Mass. 213. 69.

SUBSTITUTION. 

"It is well settled that the owner of land subject to a right of way may, with the assent of the owner of the dominant estate, substitute on his own land a new way for the old way, and that when the change is actually made and a new way is thus adopted by them, it fixes and determines their respective rights by dedication or by estoppel. Larned v. Larned, 11 

Met. 421; Pope v. Devreaux, 5 Gray, 409; Smith v. Lee, 14. 

Gray, 473; Smith v. Barnes, 101 Mass. 275; Epstein v. Dunbar, 221 Mass. 579." Byrne v. Savoie, 225 Mass. 338. See 

Brooks v. Quinn, 266 Mass. 132.

Professor Washburn said that this rule cannot be sustained either upon principle or authority.

Apparently the Massachusetts rule as to easements by substitution is confined to ways, and it probably originated in con-

nection with the doctrine of dedication. If the owner of land dedicates a way to the public and the public accepts it, the dedication is complete and the way has become a public way. 

Now if the owner of the land closes the way dedicated and dedicates another in its stead, and the public accepts and uses the new way, it is clear that the new way has become public by dedication, and it is no breach of principle to say that the first way has been lost by abandonment. But when we arrive at a similar exchange of ways between individuals, ways in which the public has no interest, then, strictly speaking, the doctrine of dedication is not available. So that if A has an easement of way over land of B, and it is orally agreed that such way shall be closed and a new way substituted therefor, and this is done, and the new way is used by A for a period of less than twenty years, it may well be that A has lost his original way by abandonment; the difficulty lies in explaining how he acquired the new way without a grant. In Byrne v. 

Savoie, 225 Mass. 338, the Court said that such a new way was fixed "by dedication or by estoppel.' 

In Crawford v. Roloson, 256 Mass. 331, the Court said: "The respective owners in title doubtless could orally agree that the deed line should be shifted so that each owner assented to a parol gift to the other of such portion of his own land as lay on the other side of the new boundary line. But no title would pass by force of such agreement unless followed by occupation according to the line adopted, which was adverse and under a claim of right for twenty years or more."

In Les v. Alibozek, 269 Mass. 153, which involved rights in a way by substitution, it was said that if it were assumed that an agreement for substitution was made, the parties did not continue to act in accord with it. The owner of the dominant estate claimed the right to use both the old way created by deed and the new way by oral grant. After the new way was prepared, the owners of the servient estate forbade its further use and later blocked it up. The Court said: "Eventually they (servient owners) removed their obstructions from the new way, but too late to validate the substitution. The evidence is not sufficient to show an intent to relinquish except upon a condition which the defendant did not perform - undisturbed use of the new

EASEMENTS. 

249 way; nor a purpose inconsistent with the further existence of the old way, the plaintiff claimed both rights. The plaintiffs remain legal owners of the old right of way." 

Professor Washburn struggled with the question as to the rights of the owner of a way, who has given up the use thereof in exchange for another (without taking a grant of the latter), to return to the use of the former way in case the use of the new way is later denied to him. The ancient cases which he discusses incline to the view that upon such circumstances the use of the old way may. be resumed. However, it seems to the writer that if there should appear on the part of the owner of the dominant estate an intent to abandon the old way evidenced by act similar to those in King v. Murphy, 140 Mass. 454 (which, the Court said, operated as a present abandonment), then the answer on principle is that he has abandoned forever a legal easement in exchange for a revocable license. No doubt the 

Courts in such cases would be inclined to find that the abandonment had not been made out, especially if it appeared, as commonly it would, that the abandonment was intended to be effective only upon condition that the new way should be provided. However, it seems that in Massachusetts the answer to the inquiry is that if the oral agreement for substitution is made by the parties, and "the change is actually made and a new way is thus adopted by them," the owner of the servient estate cannot thereafter successfully deny to the dominant owner the right to use the substituted way.

Severance of Entire Tract of Land.
