73. Easements - How Extinguished. 

70. 

LOCATION AND EXTENT OF EASEMENTS. 

It is well settled that when an easement is created by deed, but its precise limits and locations are not defined, the location and use of the easement by the owner of the dominant estate acquiesced in by the owner of the servient estate will be deemed to be that which was conveyed by the deed. Kesseller v. 

Bowditch, 223 Mass. 265. This case involved the location of windows in a house which enjoyed an easement of light and air by express grant. It was held that all the windows having been placed in the upper story, and this condition having existed for eighty years, the owner of the dominant estate, at least so long as the house in question remained standing, could not object to the erection, on the servient estate, of a one-story building which would not interfere with the light and air coming to the existing windows. See also Naumkeag Co. v. American Glue Co. 

244 Mass. 506; Dunham v. Dodge, 235 Mass. 367; Brooks v. 

Quinn, 266 Mass. 132. "The law is well settled that if the bounds of a way are not located by the deed which creates it, the parties may fix the location upon the servient premises, and, if they do not, court may do so." Mahoney v. Wilson, 260 Mass. 412. See also 

McKenney v. McKenney, 216 Mass. 248; Burnham v. Mahoney, 222 Mass. 524. "It is settled, however, that where a right of way is granted without fixing its location but there is a way already located at the time of the grant, such way is held to be the location of the way granted." Dunham v. Dodge, supra.

Where a right of way is granted, but the width of the way is not specified, the grantee will be entitled to a way of a con-

EASEMENTS. 

251 venient width for all the ordinary uses for which the way was intended, but such width may be practically determined by the acts of the parties in locating the way at the time of the grant. 

George v. Cox, 114 Mass. 382, 387.

Where a right of way from a farm across an adjoining farm to and from a public way was given by deed without limitation or reservation, and the width of the way was not defined, the easement was to the use of a way of reasonable width; and, in determining what is a reasonable width, the character and configuration of the land, the purposes for which the land and the way were used at the time of the grant, and all the circumstances attending the grant are to be considered. Dunham v. Dodge, supra. In this case, which referred to a way created by grant in 

1887, it was also said that the plaintiff was not entitled to a way of greater width than was reasonable at the time of the grant because of changed conditions.

A strip of land 20 feet wide was laid out by deed to be "forever kept open for a passageway and for light and air and drainage," and it was held that the entire way and not merely a convenient part of it was made subject to easements of passage, of light and air, and of drainage. New York Central R.R. 

Co. v. Ayer, 239 Mass. 70. But it was held in Barrett v. Duchaine, 254 Mass. 37, that a grant of "a right of way over a strip of land lying between two houses and at present used as such" must be interpreted in the light of facts tending to show the nature and condition of the passageway, the manner in which it had been used, and the purpose which the parties had in view. After referring to the findings as to the location of a shrub in the space between the houses at the date of the grant, the maintenance therein for a number of years of a clothes reel and other shrubs and plants planted after the grant, and the erection and maintenance of two fences across the space, each having a gate about three or four feet wide, the Court decided that the defendant was entitled to use the granted way as a foot-way only.

Where a right of way is created by grant which does not in terms limit its scope, it is available for all reasonable purposes in connection with the dominant estate. It is only where a right

of way is acquired by prescription, or is narrowed by the terms of the grant to definite purposes, that the extent of the easement is limited. Parsons v. N. Y., etc., R.R., 216 Mass. 269. 

In Randall v. Grant, 210 Mass. 302, it was held that a right of way, created by express reservation in a deed of land as appurtenant to a back lot, to pass to a public street might be used by one to whom the owner of the dominant estate had sold gravel and sand to be taken from a pit thereon, for the purpose of carting such gravel and sand, although when the right was reserved the back lot was garden and grassland and the sand pit had not been opened, but the Court said that the fact that the defendant had carted sand over the way for a period of eight years without objection would further indicate that the use complained of was intended by the grant. 

In Mahon v. Tully, 245 Mass. 571, it was held that a reservation (in 1865) of “a right of way" without limitation or restriction, warranted the use of the easement by the owner of the dominant estate for access to a newly constructed garage for the storage of four automobiles.

In Crosier v. Shack, 213 Mass. 253, the dominant estate, on which was situated a dwelling house, was conveyed by deed which also granted "the necessary use of a private right of way from said Goose Lane as now used" across remaining land of the grantor. It was held that the words "necessary use" meant not a use by necessity, but such use as was reasonably necessary to the full enjoyment of the grantee's land; that the words 'as now used" were descriptive of the location of the way and not of the nature or manner of its use; and that the use reasonably necessary to the full enjoyment of the dominant premises might vary from time to time, so that the owner thereof was entitled to use the way for driving an automobile as well as for driving a paint cart. 66 

In Cornell-Andrews Co. v. B. & P. R.R., 215 Mass. 381, it was held that where a deed to a railroad corporation of a part of a farm contained a stipulation for a "pass-way" for the benefit of the grantor "over" the railroad, "for farming purposes,' a successor in title to the grantor has no right of way over the land so conveyed for other than farm purposes. See also Berube v. Railroad, 234 Mass. 415.

EASEMENTS. 

253 An electric light company has no right, against the protest of one who owns land running to the middle of a private right of way subject to rights of use in other abutters on the way for purposes of travel, to maintain, in that half of the way belonging to such owner, a pole to carry wires from a public way to a lot adjoining, although the owner of the lot adjoining, with the right of travel over the way, had given to the company permission to maintain a line of poles through the way. Crullen v. Edison 

Co., 254 Mass. 93.

When a right of way to certain land exists by adverse use and enjoyment only, although evidence of the exercise of the right for a single purpose will not prove a right of way for other purposes, yet proof that it was used for a variety of purposes, covering every purpose required by the dominant estate, in its then condition, is evidence from which may be inferred a right to use the way for all purposes which may be reasonably required for the use of that estate while substantially in the same condition. But if the condition and character of the dominant estate are substantially altered, as in the case of a way to carry off wood from wild land, which is afterwards cultivated and built upon; or of a way for agricultural purposes to a farm, which is afterwards turned into a manufactory or divided into building lots, the right of way cannot be used for new purposes, required by the altered condition of the property, and imposing a greater burden upon the servient estate. Parks v. 

Bishop, 120 Mass. 340.

In Baldwin v. B. & M. R.R., 181 Mass. 166, the plaintiff had a right of way by prescription over defendant's land. When the right was acquired there was one dwelling house on the plaintiff's lot. Plaintiff built on the lot two additional houses with two tenements in each, and defendant denied the right of plaintiff's tenants to use the path. The Court reviewed the cases in a learned manner, and held that, inasmuch as the character of the use had not changed, a finding was justified that no additional burden had been imposed on the land of defendant by the increase in the number of persons using the way, and said: "The case is therefore distinguishable from cases like Ballard v. Dyson, 1 Taunt. 279, where it was held that under the peculiar circumstances the use of a way for driving carts and pigs

did not necessarily, as matter of law, show that such use was broad enough to give the right to drive horned cattle, or Cowling v. Higginson, 4 M. & W. 245, where it was held that the proof of a user for farming purposes did not necessarily prove a right of user for the purpose of conveying coals, the products of a mine lying under the farm, or like Atwater v. 

Bodfish, 11 Gray, 150, where it was held that a right to use a way for the purpose of carting wood from a lot when in a wild and uncultivated state could not be extended to a larger use. 

In such cases there is an actual change in the physical objects passing over the road." 

In Greene v. Canny, 137 Mass. 64, the Court said: "Private ways are confined in their use to the purposes for which they are granted, and cannot be further extended by the grantees. 

When intended to give access only to particular premises they cannot be used to reach thereby other lands. Where one has a right of way to a three-acre close, he cannot use it to pass over such close from or to closes beyond, and is a trespasser if he enter upon the three-acre close for such purpose." This case also stands for the proposition that where it is apparent that a way is laid out for the benefit of a certain tract of land, and parts of such land are conveyed to various purchasers, with a right to use such way, although the person making such conveyances retains title to the way, he cannot, against the objection of such purchasers, grant rights therein as appurtenant to other lands adjoining the original parcel, if by so doing he interferes in any way with the easements already created. See also Ball v. Allen, 216 Mass. 469. "In respect to who shall designate the way which is to be used by the grantee, where it is claimed as a way of necessity, it would seem that, if a way had been in use for the benefit of such parcel before conveyance, it would be understood that the same would be continued if reasonably convenient. But if it is to be designated anew, it seems that the right of selecting the place over which it shall be used lies with the owner of the land over which it is to pass, provided, upon request, he shall designate it in a reasonable manner, and he may do it so as to be least inconvenient to himself. Schmidt v. Quinn, 136 Mass. 575; 

Russell v. Jackson, 2 Pick. 574. . . . And in this respect it

EASEMENTS. 

255 seems the law differs between ways claimed by necessity and those claimed by grant, where there is no designation made of the particular part of the tenement in which it is to be exercised. 

In the latter case the selection is left to the owner of the dominant tenement, but he must not make such a selection as would unnecessarily occasion detriment to the servient tenement." 

Washburn, page 262. 

But in Bass v. Edwards, 126 Mass. 445, it was held that where the grantee of land was entitled to a way by necessity over the remaining land of the grantor, the fact that the grantor had for a long time used a particular route to the land conveyed would not exclude him from assigning any other practical and convenient way to the highway. "In the case of a way by necessity the use of a particular way by the grantor before the conveyance, and by the grantee after, may operate as an assignment or designation of this way as a convenient one which would deprive the grantee of the right to use any other route while this was unobstructed. 

Bass v.

Edwards, 126 Mass. 445. After the location of an undefined way has been fixed it cannot be changed except by agreement.” 

Davis v. Sikes, 254 Mass. 540. 

71. EASEMENTS CREATED BY IMPLIED GRANT OR RESERVATION UPON SEVERANCE OF AN ENTIRE TRACT Oof Land. 

"All easements upon land of a stranger, such as rights of way, of drainage, of light and air, etc., which have been used and enjoyed by the grantor in connection with the estate granted, will pass by a conveyance of it, although not in any way mentioned or referred to in the deed." - Crocker, page 

73, citing Brown v. Thissel, 6 Cush. 254. See also Willets v. Langhaar, 212 Mass. 573. 

In Parsons v. Railroad, 216 Mass. 269, it was said: "It is familiar law that easements or other interests in land necessary for its natural uses pass under the general clause granting privileges and appurtenances." In Ziendarski v. Baranowski, 253 

Mass. 422, it was said: "There was no express grant of a right of way over A. The habendum however reads, 'with all the privileges and appurtenances thereto belonging.' The right of

DEEDS, MORTGAGES AND EAsements. 

way therefore passed to the plaintiff as appurtenant to the lands conveyed." See also Hartt v. Rueter, 223 Mass. 207.

In fact, unmentioned appurtenant rights pass with the dominant estate, although the deed thereof makes no general reference to "privileges and appurtenances." This was clearly stated in Dubinsky v. Cama, 261 Mass. 47 at 56. This rule was given statutory force in the Short Forms Act. See G. L. c. 183, § 15. 

Presumably this was done as a matter of precaution because of the statements quoted in the preceding paragraph and similar statements in other cases, and because the common law habendum, with its usual language, “with all the privileges and appurtenances thereunto belonging," was omitted from the statutory form of deeds. 66 'Accurately speaking, a person cannot have an easement over his own land. If he burden a portion or tract of it in favor of another tract or portion, as by draining one piece over the other, or by building a house upon one part in such a manner that it is supported by the other part, he creates what would be an easement if the owner of one parcel of land had a right to enjoy it over the land of another; but it is at most only what some writers call a quasi easement so long as both tenements belong to the same proprietor. If, with things in this condition, the two parts come into the hands of different persons, either by the owner's selling or otherwise transferring the piece which enjoys the right and retaining that upon which the burden rests, or by his conveying the servient parcel and keeping the dominant, an easement may be brought into existence." Reeves on Real Property, Vol. 1, page 168. "But any use which a grantor may have previously made of adjoining land belonging to himself, for the benefit of the premises sold, will not be a sufficient foundation upon which the grantee can base a claim for an easement not mentioned in his deed, unless a continuance of such use is reasonably necessary to the beneficial enjoyment of the premises conveyed." 

Crocker, page 73. "No easement upon premises sold can be taken as reserved by the grantor for the benefit of adjoining premises owned by him, unless by express reservation, or unless such easement 'is

EASEMENTS. 

257 de facto annexed and in use at the time of the grant, and is necessary to the enjoyment of the estate which the grantor retains. And this necessity cannot be deemed to exist if a similar privilege can be secured by reasonable trouble and expense.' 

Crocker, page 75, citing Carbrey v. Willis, 7 Allen, 364, 369 and later cases. 999 The two paragraphs from Crocker's Notes above quoted imply that easements arising by implied grant or reservation upon the severance of an estate stand upon the same footing so far as the quality of "necessity" is concerned, and that such rights attach in either case if the right is "reasonably necessary" to the estate granted or retained. It is doubtful, however, if the 

Massachusetts cases bear out this view; certainly, it is not the law in all jurisdictions.

Judge Jones (Jones on Easements) considered the subject under the following headings: 

1. Easements by implied grant upon severance. (Sections 126 to 135.) 

2. Easements by implied reservation upon severance. (Sections 136 to 142.) 

3. Ways by implied grant upon severance. (Sections 254 to 265.) 

4. Ways of necessity founded on implied grant or reservation. (Sections 298 to 314.)

Topic No. 4 will be discussed in the following section, where it will appear that upon severance of an estate a way may be granted or reserved by implication of law, although no way was in existence at the time when the conveyance was made. 

It seems to the writer that topic No. 3 above is superfluous. 

A right of way is an easement, and, generally speaking, the survival of an existing way upon severance of an estate falls in with other easements under topics Nos. 1 and 2. In all such cases a measure of necessity must attach to the easement in order that it may survive. It should be said, however, that when Judge Jones wrote the courts were inclined to the view that ways by necessity rested upon public policy (see Buss v. 

Dyer, 125 Mass. 287 at 291), a theory which has since been abandoned, at least in Massachusetts (see Orpin v. Morrisson,

230 Mass. 529), and accordingly it may have seemed to him that the law applicable to existing ways upon severance was entitled to be considered under a separate heading. 

Again, Judge Jones' arrangement may have been influenced by the distinction that has been made in numerous jurisdictions between "continuous" and 'non-continuous" easements or quasi-easements. This theory he expressed (section 143) as follows: "A continuous easement is one which may be enjoyed without the intervention of any act on the part of anyone; as á drain, by which surface water is carried overland. A non-continuous easement is one to the enjoyment of which the act of the party is essential, and of this class is a right of way. Both these, the continuous and the non-continuous, may be granted and annexed to the same estate. Upon the unity of title they would both cease to exist as easements. Upon the severance of the estate the continuous would revert and pass by the conveyance, but the non-continuous would not revert or pass but by a new creation." 

Judge Jones opened his topic No. 3 above by saying: "A way is not a continuous easement," and cited Pettingill v. Porter, 8 Allen, 1; Oliver v. Pitman, 98 Mass. 46; Grant v. Chase, 17

Mass. 443. In each of these cases it was held that a way which had been used by the owner of an estate did not pass by a deed of a portion of his land, but the words "continuous" and "noncontinuous" are not used and the decisions do not turn on such reasoning. 

So far as the writer is aware, the Massachusetts court has taken little notice of the technical distinction above referred to, and Professor. Washburn, in his treatise on Easements, following statements similar to those above quoted from Jones, said: "A notable exception to the general rules above stated is found in the decisions of the courts in Maine and Massachusetts. It is held by them that no easement, whether continuous or noncontinuous, apparent or non-apparent, will pass by implied grant or reservation, unless it is one of strict necessity." 

However, in Gorton-Pew Fisheries Co. v. Tolman, 210 Mass. 

402, which concerned a right of way annexed to land which had been conveyed by will, the Court said: "It is necessary also for the creation of such an easement that it should be open and

EASEMENTS. 

259 continuous. And it has been said that the easement of a private way, being used only at more or less frequent intervals without any visible mark upon the ground, is non-continuous and therefore will not pass where other easements, continuous in their nature and being such as would be readily apparent upon inspection, would pass. . . . But these decisions do not apply to this case. We have here a roadway of considerable width, situated in the residential and business part of a city, used as appurtenant both to private residences and to business properties and wharves, running between well defined bounds and actually built or wrought upon the surface of the ground." 

Continuing, Professor Washburn said: "If the grantee can procure the enjoyment of a similar easement, no matter how great the cost, or if the easement is not absolutely necessary to the enjoyment of the granted property, it will not pass. The law on this point is stated by the court in the case of Buss v. 

Dyer, 125 Mass. 287 (1878), in which the easement claimed was a chimney built between two adjoining houses of a block, for the use of both, and connecting with both by stove-holes for pipes. The two houses were conveyed by simultaneous deeds to the plaintiff and defendant by the owner of both houses. 

The defendant afterwards tore down the chimney and refused to allow the plaintiff to rebuild. The court says: 'We are aware that it has been held in some English cases that a deed of premises carries the right to continue to enjoy as easements all privileges or conveniences in and upon adjoining lands of the grantor, which were apparent and that had been used by the grantor in connection with the premises before the conveyance; that the conveyance is a conveyance of the premises "as they are." A leading case to this effect is Pyer v. Carter, 1 H. & N. 

916. Similar doctrine has been held in New York (Lampman v. Milks, 21 N. Y. 505). We do not regard this as a correct view of the law. In this Commonwealth grants by implication are limited to cases of strict necessity;' and cites Carbrey v. 

Willis, 7 Allen, 364, and Randall v. McLaughlin, 10 Allen, 366.

In the later case, however, of Bass v. Edwards, 126 Mass. 445, the court says: 'In order to raise an implication that a certain way passes by a deed, it must appear that the way was de facto annexed to the estate conveyed at the time of the grant, and

that it was reasonably necessary to the enjoyment of the estate.

' It may perhaps be considered still unsettled in Massachusetts whether the proper instruction to the jury should be that the easement must be strictly necessary or reasonably necessary to the enjoyment of the estate granted." 

In fact, Bass v. Edwards, supra, merely decided that where the owner of land fronting on a highway conveys the rear portion, which is surrounded by land of persons other than the grantee, the latter has a right of way by necessity to the highway, over the remaining land of the grantor; and the fact that the grantor had for a long time used a particular route to the rear land will not justify the implication that he intended to convey a right to this way, and to exclude himself from assigning any other practical and convenient way to the highway, it appearing that equally convenient ways could be used over other parts of the grantor's land. Accordingly the language quoted by Washburn from this decision seems to lose some weight. It may be that the Court had in mind to express that in order to raise such an implication it must at least appear that the particular way was reasonably necessary to the estate granted, which was not the case. But the Court did make the statement as quoted by Washburn, and, strange to relate, cited Buss v. Dyer, supra. Bass v. Edwards does not appear to have been cited for the proposition above quoted from that decision. 

Again, in Leonard v. Leonard, 7 Allen, 277, a right of way case which strictly did not involve the severance of an entire estate, the Court gave the grantee the benefit of an existing way over the grantor's other land and said: "The grantor's deed is to be most strongly construed against him; and if an easement properly belongs to the granted premises, and has been used by him in connection with the premises, it ought to pass as appurtenant to the land granted, though it is not mentioned in the deed, and though it subjects his other land to the servitude. The fact that the easement has been thus used and is merely convenient is not sufficient. Its use in connection with the land conveyed must be reasonably necessary. And any easement, as for example a right of way or of drainage, which can be thus regarded, passes as an appurtenance without being named." It should be said, however, that this case concerned a way, that the

EASEMENTS. 

261 decision was probably influenced by the rule requiring only reasonable necessity in order to create a new way by necessity (see the following section), and that the general concluding statement as to other forms of easements was not necessary to the decision. 

Professor Goodwin came to this conclusion: "In Massachusetts the rule is very strict, and it is held that the courts will not raise an implied grant, much less would they, we think, an implied reservation, unless there is a strict necessity. They make an exception in the case of rights of way by necessity, in which case a reasonable necessity is all that is required." Goodwin on Real Property, page 438.

Probably the modern Massachusetts rule is not so inflexible and severe as Goodwin's statement would indicate. 

That our courts will find an implied grant where they would not sustain an implied reservation doubtless is correct. Carbrey v. Willis, 7 Allen, 364. This rests upon the principal that a deed is to be most strictly construed against the grantor. Johnson v. Jordan, 2 Met. 234 at 240. This rule of law is not applicable to the case where the owner of an estate divides it and at the same time conveys the portions to different grantees, and apparently the Massachusetts decisions do not favor the creation of easements by implication in such cases. Johnson v. Jordan, supra; Randall v. McLaughlin, 10 Allen, 366. But in favor of a grantee against his grantor, the Massachusetts rule appears to be that where the owner of land severs the same and conveys a part, such quasi-easements of a substantial and permanent nature as have been enjoyed by such part over the land retained, and which are open and apparent, will pass with the land so conveyed if reasonably necessary to its use and enjoyment. See Gorton-Pew 

Fisheries Co. v. Tolman, 210 Mass. 402; Bond v. Orr, 266 Mass. 475. 

Raynes v. Stevens, 219 Mass. 556, was a bill in equity by a lessee against his lessors to restrain them from building over an open area not included in the property leased to plaintiff so as to deprive him of light and air through his rear windows. The bill was dismissed. The Court said: "The master has found that this right is not 'absolutely necessary' for the enjoyment of the leased premises for the purpose for which they were

leased. It may be that the necessity which would warrant a finding that this right passed to the plaintiff need not be absolute in the sense that it must be completely indispensable. It must, however, be a real necessity, though perhaps only a reasonable one. A mere balance of convenience, a mere advantage which otherwise would be lost, is not enough." 

In Hampe v. Elia, 251 Mass. 465, in an action and on facts quite similar to Raynes v. Stevens, supra, it was pointed out that the lease was not "with the appurtenances or with all rights and privileges thereto belonging which are convenient or essential to the beneficial enjoyment thereof, or any words equivalent or similar thereto," and the Court said: "It is settled in this Commonwealth that an easement of light and air can be acquired only by express grant, by covenant, or by implication where the light or air is actually and absolutely necessary. . . . The grant of these premises, without a grant of the appurtenances or of the privileges and conveniences, did not carry the right to enjoy conveniences in the land of the lessor which were apparent but were not of 'strict necessity."" The 

Court also said: "The case of Raynes v. Stevens, 219 Mass. 

556, . . . rests largely upon terms in the grant which are not present in the lease now under consideration." The reason for this remark is not clear. It seems that Raynes v. Stevens was decided in favor of the defendant on the ground that the plaintiff had not made out even "reasonable necessity," which, the Court suggested, might have been sufficient. In Hampe v. Elia, the rule of "absolute necessity" is squarely laid down as quoted above, but note that in the final paragraph of the decision the Court says that the master did not find that there absolute or reasonable necessity that light and air should continue to pass," and cited Raynes v. Stevens. was an It is suggested that the rule of “absolute necessity" laid down in Hampe v. Elia is applicable, if it is to be followed at all, only to cases involving easements of light and air, which, as heretofore stated, cannot be acquired in this Commonwealth by user, leaving other easements, under proper circumstances, to be tested by the rule of "reasonable necessity."

EASEMENTS. 

263 

72. WAYS OF NECESSITY. 

"If A conveys land to B, to which B can have access only by passing over other land of A, a way of necessity passes by the grant. If A conveys land to B, leaving other land of A, to which he can have access only by passing over the land granted, a way of necessity is reserved in the grant.” Brigham v. Smith, 4 Gray, 297. See also Davis v. Sikes, 254 Mass. 540.

Note that the foregoing rules hold, although no particular way is in existence and used at the time of the conveyance. 

Such a way by necessity rests on the theory of implied grant or reservation. In Nichols v. Luce, 24 Pick. 103, the Court said: "The three different modes of acquiring and holding rights of way in their origin resolve themselves into one. The distinction between them relates more to the mode of proof than to the source of title. They are all derived from the voluntary grant of the proprietor of the fee. Prescription presupposes and is evidence of a previous grant. Necessity is only a circumstance resorted to for the purpose of showing the intention of the parties and raising an implication of a grant. 

And the deed of the grantor as much creates the way of necessity as it does the way by grant. The only difference between the two is that one is granted in express words and the other only by implication." 

In Pettingill v. Porter, 8 Allen, 1, the Court said: "The word 'necessary' cannot reasonably be held to be limited to absolute physical necessity. If it were so, the way in question would not pass with the land, if another way could be made by any amount of labor and expense, or by any possibility. If, for example, the property conveyed were worth $1,000, it would follow from this construction that the plaintiff would not have the right of way over the triangular piece as appurtenant to the land, provided he could have made another way at an expense of $100,000. If the word 'necessary' is to have a more liberal and reasonable interpretation than this, the one adopted by the judge must be regarded as correct. Its effect was, to require proof that the way over the triangular piece was reasonably necessary to the enjoyment of the dwelling house granted." 

Unfortunately the Court has not always adhered strictly to

the statement above quoted from Pettingill v. Porter, which seems to be accepted by the text writers as expressing the Massachusetts rule applicable generally to cases involving ways of necessity, although it is true that in that case there was in existence a way to the granted premises over other land of the grantor. In Feoffees of the Grammar School in Ipswich v. Proprietors of Jeffrey's Neck Pasture, 174 Mass. 572, it was said: "The conditions which give rise to an implication that a way is granted where none is mentioned must create a strict necessity for the way in order reasonably to use the property, and the right of way by necessity will continue only so long as the necessity continues. That the way would be convenient and beneficial is not enough." In this case the Court refused to apply the rule laid down in a few jurisdictions to the effect that if one grants a parcel of land, to which there is access by sea, no implied way of necessity over the grantor's land will attach, but it was suggested that a situation might arise where such access by water might be considered all that the grantee was entitled to. 

In Churchill v. Harris, 257 Mass. 499, the grantor of land on which was located a spring of water which also served a house on his remaining land by means of a pipe laid thereto, by his deed reserved "one half of the spring of water, etc.," but did not expressly reserve the right to maintain the pipe. It was held that the grantor was not only entitled to use the water, but was also owner as tenant in common of so much of the land out of which the spring issued as was necessary for the reasonable use of the spring itself; that he was entitled to take water in any reasonable and convenient manner; and that he had a right of way by necessity to enter in a reasonable manner for the purpose of obtaining it. 

Where lots, separated from a great pond by a strip designated on a plan as a promenade, were conveyed by definite boundaries, though the grantor knew the grantees intended to use their lots for an ice house, and that it would be necessary for them to pass over the promenade to do so, they acquired no implied right to maintain on the promenade an ice elevator, an ice chute, gasoline engine and ditch, excluding others from the

EASEMENTS. 

265 use of the promenade. Home Investment Co. v. Iovieno, 243 

Mass. 121.

A right of way by necessity may be terminated by release of the owner of the dominant estate to the owner of the servient estate. "The law does not give to every owner of land a right of way to it, or prohibit an owner from cutting himself off from all access to it by his conveyance." Richards v. Attleboro R.R., 153 Mass. 120.

It is sometimes said that a way by necessity rests solely upon public policy. However it may be elsewhere, such is not the law in Massachusetts. See Orpin v. Morrisson, 230 Mass. 

529. There the Court said: "A right of way of necessity over land of the grantor is implied by the law as a part of the grant when the granted premises are otherwise inaccessible, because that is presumed to be the intent of the parties. The way is created not by the necessity of the grantee, but as a deduction as to the intention of the parties from the instrument of grant, the circumstances under which it was executed, and all the material conditions known to the parties at the time. The rule has its basis in a construction of the deed with reference to all the facts within the knowledge of the parties respecting the subject of the grant, to the end that their assumed design may be carried into effect. It is founded on the idea that it is the purpose of the parties that the conveyance shall be beneficial to the grantee. 

There is no reason in law or ethics why parties may not convey land without direct means of access, if they desire to do so." 66 'A right of way by necessity ceases as soon as the necessity ceases. Rowell v. Daggett, 143 Mass. 483, 489. 

In Hart v. Deering, 222 Mass. 407, a triangular parcel of land, which was vacant but was suitable for a dwelling house, had as appurtenant a way of necessity over a lot of land which adjoined the base of the triangle and abutted on a highway. 

Thereafter a public way was constructed across the apex of the triangular parcel, giving it an opening thereon 5 feet and 9 inches in width. It was held that as a matter of law under the circumstances shown it could not be found as a fact that an opening on a public way so provided was a reasonably adequate

and convenient means of access which extinguished the right of way by necessity over the adjoining land at the other end of the triangular lot.

By statute, chapter 117 of Acts of 1921, no right of way by necessity shall be implied under a conveyance of registered land. 

As to the right to locate a way by necessity, see section 70. 73.

EASEMENTS - How EXTINGUISHED. 

1. By release under seal. Pope v. Devreaux, 5 Gray, 409. 

2. By merger of the dominant and servient estates in the same person. Atwater v: Bodfish, 11 Gray, 150. But a right of way, appurtenant to land, over and upon adjoining land, is not extinguished by the vesting of both estates in the same person as mortgagee, under separate mortgages, until both mortgages are foreclosed. Ritger v. Parker, 8 Cush. 147. If a person holds one estate in severalty, and only a fractional part of another, the easement is not extinguished. Atlanta 

Mills v. Mason, 120 Mass. 244. An easement is not extinguished by the unity of ownership of the servient estate and of a portion of the dominant estate adjacent to the servient estate, so long as the rest of the dominant estate is owned by another, since the easement is appurtenant to the whole and every part of the dominant estate. Mahon v. Tully, 245 Mass. 571. See 

Les v. Alibozek, 269 Mass. 153. 

3. By abandonment. (a) Non-user. "Mere non-user does not conclusively impair or defeat an easement created by deed. In order to have such an effect there must be an element of adverse use by the owner of the servient estate inconsistent with the continuance of the easement, or acts by the owner of the dominant estate of such conclusive and unequivocal character as manifest a present intent to relinquish the easement or such as are incompatible with its further existence. Whether there has been an abandonment is usually a question of fact." Willets v. Langhaar, 212 Mass. 

573. See also Lagorio v. Lewenberg, 226 Mass. 464; Dubinsky v. Cama, 261 Mass. 47; Arcisz v. Pietrowski, 268 Mass. 140;. 

Les v. Alibozek, supra.

In connection with the foregoing it should be noted that in

EASEMENTS. 

267 order to extinguish an easement the "element of adverse use by the owner of the servient estate" must be maintained for twenty years or more. See paragraph (b) following.. 

In Willard v. Stone, 253 Mass. 555, there was a grant of a right to take water from two springs. The grantee constructed a reservoir with which he connected only one of the springs. 

Fifty-eight years later his successor in title connected the reservoir with the second spring. It was held that the use during the first fifty-eight years of the waters of one spring only did not preclude the owner of the dominant estate from availing himself of the right to use the second spring. At the time of making the connection with the second spring, the grantee's successor in title agreed orally that he would do some filling and draining about the springs, but he did not perform the agreement. It was held that the oral agreement gave the owner of the dominant estate nothing beyond the rights which he already had, and his failure to carry out the agreement did not preclude him from insisting upon his right to use the second spring. The 

Court said: "It is settled that an easement is an interest in land, and that it cannot be renounced or extinguished by a parol agreement between the owners of the dominant and servient tenements.' دو "The rule is, that rights acquired by use may be lost by disuse.' 

Arnold v. Stevens, 24 Pick. 106, at 113. 

"The right cannot have been lost by mere non-user; for the doctrine of extinction by mere disuse does not apply to an easement acquired by deed. Washburn on Easements, 2d edition, page 641, and cases cited. There must be something more than this. In this respect it differs from an easement acquired by prescription." Hayford v. Spokesfield, 100 Mass. 491. 

In Cases on Real Property, Vol. 2, page 390, note, Professor 

Gray said: "In Massachusetts, New York and Pennsylvania it has been held that an easement created by deed cannot be extinguished by mere non-user, in contradistinction to an easement by prescription which it is said can be lost by mere non-user. 

But it has never been decided in either of these states that an easement gained by prescription can be lost by mere non-user. 

Nor has it been held that an easement acquired by deed cannot be lost by abandonment without adverse possession."

Professor Washburn said: "In respect to the effect to be given to a mere non-user of an easement which has been acquired by adverse user or prescription, although the language of some of the cases would imply that if continued for twenty years, it would be of itself an abandonment, it is believed that such non-user is in no case anything more than evidence of an intent to abandon the right; that it never applies when the period of such non-user is less than the period of limitation, and is open to explanation, and to be controlled by evidence that the owner of the easement did not intend to abandon it while omitting to use it." (b) But a non-user for twenty years, united with an adverse use of the servient estate, inconsistent with the existence of the easement, will extinguish it. Chandler v. Jamaica Pond 

Aqueduct Co., 125 Mass. 544; Amee v. B. & A. Railroad Co., 212 Mass. 421. But in Butterfield v. Reed, 160 Mass. 361, it was held that an easement of flowage was not lost by the use of the servient estate by its owner for more than twenty years for cutting hay, digging out muck, enlarging and using springs, and occupying a portion for a henhouse and hen yard. 

In Brooks v. West Boston Gas Co., 260 Mass. 407, the Court said: "In Hancock v. Wentworth, 5 Met. 446, and in Central 

Wharf v. India Wharf, 123 Mass. 567, this court decided that when the act of a third party had rendered the use of the easement impossible, the easement was extinguished. Where the acts which have interrupted the use have been acts of the servient owner, the rule is the same. Jennison v. Walker, 11 Gray, 423; Chandler v. Jamaica Pond Aqueduct Co., 125 Mass. 544, 549; Smith v. Price, 214 Mass. 298; Munroe v. Worthington Pump & Machinery Corp., 245 Mass. 474, 479." This statement taken by itself is misleading. The "act of a third party" appearing in each of the two cases first, cited was the laying out of a highway by the public authorities; and, in each of the second group of cases, it appeared that the acts of the servient owner which rendered the use of the easement impossible had been persisted in for more than twenty years.

In Brookline v. Whiddin, 229 Mass. 485, it was held that a general right to use a private way 50 feet wide, which had been granted to the owner of certain land, was not affected by the

EASEMENTS. 

269 fact that the superintendent of streets of the town where the way was situated, in order to prevent accidents, placed a barrier across the way, thus preventing its use with vehicles, although leaving it accessible to pedestrians, and that such barrier was maintained for more than twenty years. 

The owner of a farm, crossed by a right of way, by maintaining bars across the end of such way for more than twenty years consecutively can acquire a prescriptive right to maintain a barway there instead of a gate. Ball v. Allen, 216 Mass. 469.

As stated in section 61, the general principle is that one who owns land subject to an easement has the right to use his land in any way not inconsistent with the easement. Whether he has used it in a manner inconsistent with the easement, and so extinguished the easement, is a question of fact. Each case must be decided upon the facts appearing. (c) By license from the owner of the dominant estate to the owner of the servient to do, on the servient estate, some act inconsistent with the continuance of the easement. See Dyer v. Sanford, 9 Met. 395.

In the last-mentioned case the Court said: "If the owner of the dominant tenement grants a license to the owner of the servient tenement to erect a wall which necessarily obstructs the enjoyment of the easement, and it is erected accordingly, it may amount to an abandonment of the easement. It is not a release, because it is by parole. But it results from the consideration that a license, when executed, is not revocable; and if the obstruction be permanent in its nature, it does, de facto, terminate the enjoyment of the easement. But the license is for the specific act only; and if, when executed, it is of such a nature as, de facto, to destroy the easement, but is only temporary in its nature, or limited in its terms, then, as the easement is not released, when the obstruction erected in pursuance of such specific license is removed, the owner of the servient tenement cannot erect another obstruction of the same, or of a different kind, without a new license.' وو 

See also Morse v. Copeland, 2 Gray, 302, and Les v. Alibozek, 269 Mass. 153. (d) Acts done by the owner of the dominant estate which are inconsistent with the continued existence of the easement, when

done with the intention to abandon and extinguish it, may operate as an immediate abandonment.

In Dyer v. Sanford, supra, the Court said: "But as by the rules of law an easement is an interest in land, to be acquired and released only by deed, as between the parties respectively, when it is contended that the owner of the dominant tenement has voluntarily abandoned his right, so as, de facto, to withdraw the incumbrance from the servient tenement, without a release from its owner, the proof must go to this extent: first, that the acts relied upon were voluntarily done by the owner of the dominant tenement, or by his express authority; second, that such party was the owner of the inheritance, and had authority to bind the estate by his grant or release; and third, that the acts are of so decisive and conclusive a character as to indicate and prove his intent to abandon the easement. Various illustrations might be given of such conclusive acts of abandonment, as when one takes down the building in which a window was placed, and erects on its site a permanent tenement, so constructed as not to require, or even permit, a window similarly situated; or when one grants an express license to do acts on his own land, the necessary effect of which is to take away or impair the easement permanently, and the acts are done accordingly." 

In King v. Murphy, 140 Mass. 254, one Davis, the owner of a tract of land, sold a portion of it to defendant, reserving a right of way thereover. Later, Davis sold the dominant estate to the plaintiff, but before doing so the end of the way had been closed by a substantial board fence so that it no longer furnished access to the land conveyed to plaintiff. Defendant offered to show that Davis, while owner, said that he should relinquish this right of way when he sold the adjoining property, and that the fence was erected by him in order to abandon the passageway. It was held that this evidence was admissible, and it was said: "If he closed up the way with the intention of abandoning it, this operated as a present abandonment, and it ceased to be a way appurtenant to the plaintiff's lot, and did not pass to him under his deed." In Hayford v. Spokesfield, 100 Mass. 491, the owner of a lot of land having a right of way, acquired by grant, over two passageways leading therefrom,

EASEMENTS. 

271 built a close board fence five feet high across one of these, and a subsequent owner placed palings three or four feet high on top of this fence. It was held that the maintenance of this fence for seven years was not, of itself, an abandonment. The Court said: "So many motives may be assigned for maintaining the fence temporarily, and the structure was so slight and so easily removed, that it was far from being sufficient to prove an abandonment of the easement."

It is sometimes said that an easement is extinguished by a change in the dominant estate, substantially altering the nature of the servitude; but the better rule seems to be that a violation of the right to use an easement does not extinguish it unless it has become impossible, or unless the one entitled to the easement refuses, to restore the premises to their former state. 

See Mendall v. Delano, 7 Met. 176.
